This was by far the most Ontakians I had ever seen and I was quite excited about it. I knew almost nothing about my own people other than what Frank told me, and he wasn’t always reliable.

“Hey, no one else is wearing a face mask and breathing your funky air,” I challenged.

Frank had assured me I was hurting myself by breathing the standard Colmarian air mixture. He said that our native atmosphere contained fluorine gas, a substance which was normally quite fatal, so I never tried it.

“They’re dumb,” he shrugged. “I’m probably the only one here who ever spent significant time on our home world before it was destroyed.”

“Why are they all dressed like they’re going to a perverted funeral?”

“Just a style. But maybe it comes from being a bioengineered species, that were formerly slaves, and now are nearly extinct.”

I considered myself somewhat of a pessimist, but compared to this bunch, I felt like Captain Sunshine Gigglepants.

The dim lights seemed to get even dimmer and I noticed there was a small stage to the side. Three people were up there with instruments and they began playing. I didn’t know if they were trying to emulate the sounds of an orphanage, filled with tone-deaf yodelers, being sucked up into a tornado, but they were spot on.

It took me a long while, but I finally recognized one of the screaming, thrashing “musicians” as Najosa.

She was jumping and kicking and flailing around and yelling into a microphone as she played some kind of instrument.

“Is this native Ontakian music?” I asked Frank.

“No, this is crap. But they don’t play for long, thankfully.”

This was the first I had seen Najosa in sort of normal clothes. She was a lot shorter than I remember. Probably because the Suckface wasn’t a very tall ship so we spent a lot of time sitting or lying down. She was wearing a black synth miniskirt and a black synth, kind-of belt, that covered her breasts. Other than her boots, that was the extent of her wardrobe. She had a lot of tattoos. So many that at first I thought she was fully clothed.

The band continued to rock with great abandon, and the audience, other than Frank and myself, seemed to love it.

I suddenly felt very lame. Which was a new sensation for me.

I was not the coolest guy in the galaxy. I just didn’t care. I was too lazy to care. And I was bulletproof. I mean, even if someone thought I was a dork, what were they going to do about it?

But now I felt like a pretender in a green suit and white tie. Yes, I was an Ontakian, but I wasn’t an angry Ontakian like all these dancers around me. I was more of a hungry Ontakian.

These were technically my people and I felt keenly aware that I wasn’t one of them.

The audience, particularly the large, muscled men with their tow truck necklaces, began jostling with one another. At first it was kind of a mixture of pushing and dancing, but then it got a lot more violent and people were flying through the air.

If these were really Ontakians, I assumed those big guys were combat models like me. But I didn’t have any great desire to punch the person next to me just because the music was terrible.

Someone came barreling into me, head first.

“My flowers!” I yelled.

They were shattered. Cliston worked really hard on those and now I didn’t have a gift for Najosa. Though she didn’t strike me as a glass flower kind of gal now that I saw her native environment.

I was expecting the guy who hit me to apologize once he righted himself, but he stood up, looked at my clothes, and then punched me square in the face.

I was pretty surprised, because my cheek hurt. I’ve been shot in the face more times than I could count. For a person to punch me, and for me to be uncomfortable afterwards, proved this guy was tremendously strong.

I had a policy of not letting people who attacked me go unpunished. It was bad for your reputation. Putting up both my arms, I stomped forward to try and grab hold of the man who hit me.

Before I took two steps, I got hit on the side of my head with what felt like a fire hydrant. I looked over and it was a fire hydrant. It was attached via a chain to someone’s waist and he had swung it like a club.

A table hurtled at me from another direction and shattered against my side.

I turned around to see if my uncle was going to help me in this escalating fight. Frank was gone. I briefly caught a glimpse of him. He was standing on the exact opposite side of the club.

I think he wanted to make sure everyone knew he wasn’t with me. That was Frank.

After a few minutes, I found myself at the bottom of a huge pile of Ontakians. It had taken about five of them to tackle me, but they managed it. One guy who was above me in the pile had a free hand and was twisting my nose.

Remind me never to bring flowers to an Ontakian club.

I started to get worried. There was a real danger of suffocation with this much weight sitting on top of me. Frank was probably piling on furniture or picking their pockets.

With a ferocious roar, I started pushing against the floor with my arms and legs.

The pile shifted and I could stand up. I found the guy who was twisting my nose, and grabbed him by the neck. I pushed his head completely through the thin metal countertop of the bar.

A burly man got in my face and I took hold of his necklace. It seemed to be a bunch of paint cans filled with concrete. I rammed one of the cans into his head over and over until he was a bloody mess and slumped to the floor.

It was around this point that the club displayed considerably less interest in fighting me.

But I’d lost it. I was beyond furious. I ran around the building chasing anyone who got near, but they were fast and I was not.

I went into the bathroom, where I saw someone with his back to me, urinating. I pulled the sink from the wall and smashed it over his head, right as he turned around in alarm.

Returning to the club’s main floor, I got hold of one of the small, synth-clad people and hurled him or her like a glocken token. The person bounced off the opposite wall and lay on the floor.

As I was searching for more people to fight, I was suddenly bathed in blue light.

To my left, a man stood there with what looked like a small pistol. But the barrel emitted a light that illuminated every corner of the club. It created a noise so powerful that I could feel it in the back of my throat.

Everyone was standing clear of the weapon and I knew I was about to get shot.

I covered my face and turned sideways.

Bzow!

I felt the beam hit me and smelled my clothes incinerating.

When it was over, I prepared to fight them, but everyone stood there looking confused. The lights were on. The music had stopped.

“Hank is an Ontakian,” I heard Najosa say using her microphone. “He’s a mutant Ontakian, and a friend of the Society.”

Ontakian plasma weapons, like the one I was just shot with, were of limited value against Ontakians. Which was fortunate. The metal wall behind me was melted and half my clothes had been disintegrated.

Club goers started to file up, one-by-one to shake my hand and congratulate me on…I don’t know. A good dance? Helping remodel the club? Surviving?

“Sorry,” Frank said. “I figured you had it under control.”

“You’re like the worst uncle ever,” I replied.

“Nah. You should have met mine.”





CHAPTER 13




“I got you a gold bracelet. But I lost it in the scuffle,” I lied to Najosa, staring at her diamond-colored eyes, tattoos, and supple body. You’d think as a combat model Ontakian she would be big and hulking, but she was anything but.

We were backstage in the small dressing room. I hadn’t realized, but the other two members of her band were the Ontakians I had taken to the PCC installation. Shella, the female architect, and Nordan, a male technician. They were hard to recognize in their wacky, synth, musician outfits.

“Why would I want a gold bracelet?” Najosa asked.

She really took the fact that she was a combat model seriously.

“It was wrapped around the handle of a Tonsus submachinegun,” I further lied.

“Oh, that was thoughtful. We can look around after the club empties and try and find it,” she said.

“Someone probably took it by now. You know those Ontakians,” I said, pretending I knew anything about Ontakians.

“You were pretty wild in the mosh pit,” Shella said. “You’re not actually supposed to try and kill people.”

“They started it. I got really pissed off. Not sure why,” I said, thinking back.

“We put a tiny bit of daxapronal in our fog machine,” Nordan said.

“What’s that?” I asked.

“It’s a drug that stimulates the enhanced adrenal system in combat Ontakians. But I’ve never seen anyone respond like you did,” Najosa said.

“That’s the stuff those Ontakians used!” I shouted.

I had fought some Ontakians years ago who had been insanely powerful when they huffed a drug. I used it a few times but it essentially made me psychotic. A version of it is what Delovoa extracted to make the formula that was the basis for most of my food.

“Which Ontakians?” Shella asked.

“Forget it. Why would you pump that into the air? You trying to cause a riot?”

“We only put a few drops and…usually, people don’t go running around hitting people with bathroom sinks. The fighters do a little wrestling is all. You aren’t supposed to take it so personally,” Najosa said.

“They tried to plasma me,” I sulked.

“If you’re an Ontakian, a plasma weapon won’t do anything to you. But it did get you to calm down,” Nordan said.

“I’m curious why daxapronal makes you like that,” Najosa said.

“I took some before and it had the same effects. Worse, really. I kind of go crazy and it’s really hard for me to get in control. But I become much faster and stronger. I guess Frank didn’t experience it because he has that stupid breathing mask.”

“It must be your mutations that make the drug work differently,” Najosa guessed.

“How many mutations do you have?” Nordan asked, amazed.

“Watch,” I said.

I reached above my head, concentrated as best I could, and tried to raise my legs. But I fell to the floor.

“That’s great,” he said, unsure.

“No, I can fly. Just not very well. I’m tired.”

They all exchanged glances. It probably sounded far-fetched that someone my size could fly.

“Wait,” I said, and I got back on my feet. “Just watch me this time.”

I tried to relax and forget I was performing in front of an audience. Took some deep breaths. Then I raised my arms and slowly lifted my legs.

“Huh,” Shella said.

I was in the air for maybe a second or two before I touched back down.

“So what do you use that mutation for?” Najosa asked.

“That’s about it: showing off in clubs. I think I’m a bit too heavy.”

“You should go on a diet,” Najosa said hurtfully.

“It’s not that I’m fat. It’s part of my mutation. I’m really dense.”

“I think there’s some fat there,” Nordan replied, poking at my stomach.

“So you want to get something to drink?” I asked Najosa.

“I don’t drink,” she replied.

“You just take drapotol?” I asked skeptically.

“Daxapronal. No, we spray it at the audience and I stand in front of a fan,” she said.

“Alright, so…you want to eat something?”

“I really think you’d be a lot healthier if you ate less. On your ship, you ate as much food as the rest of the crew,” she said.

“It’s my ship.”

“Is it true that you said you were glad of the civil war?” she asked.

“When did I say that? That doesn’t sound like me.”

“When we were flying back. You said the Colmarian Confederation had been evil,” she said.

“I was making chit chat. I don’t really pay attention to the stuff I say. Sure, the Colmarian Confederation was an abomination. But I didn’t wish for all those people to die in the civil war. And it’s not like we ended up any better with the PCC.”

“Exactly. That’s what the Society of the Free and Easy is all about,” Najosa said.

“About what?”

“Our goal is to end the PCC once and for all,” she said.





CHAPTER 14




“I found a woman to date. I think,” I told Delovoa.

“You think she’s a woman or you think you found her?” he asked.

We were flying in the Suckface for the ninth straight hour, monitoring transmissions and looking for the parties responsible for hacking the Dredel Led.

“Think I found her. She’s a good match for me.”

“Fat and stupid?”

“Hah, hah. Her name is Najosa,” I said.

“She know you’re a mutant?”

“She knows I’m an Ontakian. She’s one also.”

“Wow. Small galaxy. There probably aren’t more than fifty thousand of you left and you manage to sex one.”

“Well, we haven’t done anything yet. I’m not entirely sure we’re dating, really.”

Delovoa looked up from his control panel.

“You are the worst when it comes to women. You can punch past a hundred robots, monsters, and aliens, and a pretty smile turns you into a weakling.”

“Look who’s talking. When’s the last time you dated anyone?”

“I go out all the time.”

“Yeah, right. Everyone thinks you’re a weirdo.”

“I have my own method. I call it, ‘making friends.’”

“What’s that involve?”

“Look,” Delovoa said, and he thumbed through his tele. He found a video and showed it to me.

It was Delovoa in his apartment, sitting at a table next to three animatronic mannequins that were dressed up in ridiculous outfits. Delovoa was having lengthy conversations with them as they twitched spastically.

“This is the creepiest thing I’ve ever seen in my life. Who has tea parties?”

“I do.”

“That’s not a tea party. That’s talking to inanimate objects.”

“You’ve clearly never been a little girl,” Delovoa said casually.

“Neither have you!”

I gave back the tele.

“Don’t…don’t show me stuff like this again,” I said. “I have a tough enough time justifying all the crazy stuff you do.”

“So what’s this mystery woman like?” Delovoa asked.

“She’s not a mystery. You can meet her if you want. But you can’t do anything strange.”

“You mean like build you a detector and hang out in your spaceship for hours and hours helping you make fifty million credits?” he asked.

“You’re getting your cut. Don’t get snotty. She’s a combat model Ontakian. Real business. Tough. Smart. Short.”

“You’re talking about Garm? Because that sounds just like her.”

“No. Garm is in the PCC Navy now. She’s practically a traitor.”

Delovoa laughed.

“Traitor to what? Poverty? I work for the Navy when they pay me. I still help fix the Portals for them now and then.”

“I wouldn’t. The PCC is the enemy as far as I’m concerned. They took over our System, wrecked our economy, and scared everyone away. Remember how important Belvaille used to be? Now we aren’t anything.”

“We’re still important. Just because we have the goofy name of Portal Depot #4382 doesn’t mean we aren’t valuable,” he said.

“I guess. But unless the Skim starts working, the city is going to die out.”

“Nah. Belvaille used to be at the edge of the galaxy and it didn’t die. It’s actually quite a nice facility. It was built at the height of the Colmarian Confederation and cost a fortune. It’s probably one of the best space stations in the galaxy.”

“And none of its citizens can vote,” I said.

“Why would you want to vote if you think the PCC is so bad?”

“It’s the fact that they won’t let us that irritates me. Like we aren’t good enough.”

“Well, we aren’t. All that’s left on Belvaille are a bunch of thugs and grifters. It’s not like the pinnacle of society stayed behind when everyone else left.”

“Hey, do you want to hear my spaceship safety speech?” I asked.

“I guess. It’s got to be more interesting than you talking about imaginary girlfriends,” he said.

“Hello, the following procedures are in case of sudden exposure to vacuum.”

“We’d be dead,” Delovoa interrupted.

“Shush. The emergency pressure suits are located to the fore and aft. To activate them, grasp both handles firmly, stretch your arms apart, and depress the triggers. They provide a self-contained breathing helmet with ten minutes of oxygen,” I said.

Delovoa stared at me.

“That’s got to be the dumbest thing anyone ever said. Who is going to save you in ten minutes when you’re floating in space? Do you have instructions on how to put out a fire in case you get too close to a supernova?” he asked.

“It’s one of the new laws. I’m supposed to say it any time someone comes on board,” I replied.

“Well, it’s damn informative. But getting back to the subject of women, why did you and Garm break up? It’s obvious you still like her.”

“What? No, I don’t. As for why, I just felt it was time to move on.”

“Hah! You’re going to pretend that it was you who broke up with Garm? I’ve got three eyes and three brains, not one eye and no brains. She’s so much better looking than you that it’s a quantum improbability that you even went out together.”

“Funny stuff. It doesn’t matter, she’s back to being Adjunct Overwatch. She’s Navy through and through.”

“If she was really the Navy she would have arrested us on any of the hundreds of laws we’ve broken. She’s Garm. Which means she’s more corrupt than we could ever dream of being,” he said.

“She’s still PCC. She had a choice. She could have worked on the Skim with us and instead she chose the other side.”

“Does she get to vote?”

“How should I know?” I asked.

“Hey, shut up a second,” Delovoa said, looking back at his controls. “I’m picking up some transmissions.”

“Is it the hackers?”

Delovoa fiddled with the system.

“No. But they’re unusual. This isn’t a frequency or signal type anyone around here uses. And it’s pretty powerful.”

“Where’s it coming from?” I asked.

Delovoa pointed to the side of the ship.

He then got up and went to the cockpit as I followed.

“Zzzho, what’s out that way?” Delovoa asked, still pointing.

“Not much. Let me check. Um. Hold your arm steady. The closest thing in that general direction is the Rettosian space station, Floloria.”





CHAPTER 15




“This is a pretty good haul,” Delovoa said, as we walked along the streets of Floloria.

“I’m the one hauling,” I grumbled.

I was pulling a large metal cart filled with gear we ransacked from the Rettosian space station. I had taken baubles and paintings, while Delovoa had stocked up on technology.

Delovoa couldn’t pull the cart because he would probably rip off his own arms.

We had docked with the station to try and investigate the strange signals we detected. But in short order we realized that was easier said than done. Floloria was a city. Not nearly as large as Belvaille, but a city nonetheless. Furthermore, Delovoa said the transmission didn’t match the Dredel Led hack.

The city was empty as far as we knew so after a few hours of wandering around, we decided to loot the place. The Rettosians left nearly everything behind when they vacated.

“You should get Cliston here. There were a lot of carpets back in that last apartment, and I can smell your underwear is wearing thin,” Delovoa said.

After giving himself an oversized nose, Delovoa was able to smell with incredible accuracy.

“Cliston is on vacation.”

“I thought he rarely left Belvaille,” Delovoa answered, surprised.

“He’s standing in the corner of my house. But it’s still vacation. He’s like…hyperdriving to the Dredel Led capital city.”

“Don’t use words you don’t understand, Hank. His capital? Maybe he’s the one hacking Thaxmultaerea.”

“No, he got me the job.”

“It’s a perfect cover.”

“None of us get paid unless we stop the hacks. And knowing him, he’s probably making more than both of us.”

“Is Cliston powered off?” he asked.

“Not sure. He’s just standing in the corner and hasn’t moved in a few days.”

“You should have told me! I’d love to examine Cliston. That guy is amazing.”

“If he woke up, he’d kill you. He said all I have to do is walk up to him and yell ‘Cliston’ and he’d be back immediately,” I replied.

“Oh. Then, yeah, I won’t mess with him. Cliston is a bit scary.”

“No kidding,” I said. “He’s travelling across the galaxy while leaning next to my chest of drawers.”

“I smell something,” Delovoa said urgently.

“You’re on a city. Can you be more specific?”

“I smell people.”

“How can you smell anything with your body rotting?” I asked.

“It’s not rotting anymore. I fixed it.”

“How did you ‘fix’ it?”

“What are you, the body part police? Listen, someone’s down that way. A few people.”

“Is their underwear in good condition?”

Despite the tease, I let go of my cart of stolen goods, and took up a more defensive position.

“Don’t be mad that I gave myself superior senses and a superior physique,” Delovoa said.

“Try pulling this cart, Mr. Superior.”

“Shut up, they’re getting close. How did they find us?” Delovoa asked, looking around for cameras or scanners.

“Probably because we’re bickering on an uninhabited space station.”

It took a few more minutes for them to reach us and we kept our mouths shut while they approached.

They were two large men, wearing heavy metallic body armor. I suspected they were like us: looters. But if they weren’t above looting, they weren’t above robbing. And they both carried long, flat rifles with extremely wide barrels. I didn’t recognize the weapons.

As they drew closer, I could tell they were Rettosians. Rettosians had liquid, vibrantly colored, gurgling skin that flowed across their bodies. These men had their faces uncovered and their skin was definitely liquid.

“Hi,” I said, smiling.

“Who are you?” one of them asked.

“We’re Rettosians. Who are you?” Delovoa stated.

We looked more like lizards than we did Rettosians. Despite being a pathological liar, Delovoa wasn’t very good at it.

“We’re the guys who are going to kill you,” the other man answered.

“No one needs to kill anyone. We’re just doing a little shopping and we’ll be on our way,” I said quickly.

“You will be on your way,” the first man said.

They both clicked something on their guns and pointed them at us, looking downright mean.

I pushed Delovoa out of harm’s way and I was hit in the chest by two beams of light.

Nothing happened.

I was about to rush forward when I noticed I was now five feet further away. Then forty feet away from them. Then two hundred. Then I hit a building, flew through the outer wall, through the inner wall, through another outer wall, and rolled to a stop in the middle of the street.

I was aware of it going on, but it literally must have taken only a few seconds. There wasn’t even a pushing sensation or a feeling of acceleration. The only way I knew I was moving was because I saw it.

All the walls on Floloria were thin aluminum, so it wasn’t as if I was pushed through feet of concrete or steel. However, I was still dazed. I took a moment to count my legs, my arms, my fingers, my head, to make sure everything was still there.

My tele rang. Why not? I answered it.

“Some guys came to harass the ship so I had to undock,” Zzzho said.

“They’re harassing us too. How can we reach you if you aren’t docked?” I asked.

“There are emergency berths in different corners of the space station. They can’t cover them all. Meet me at DD33. It’s on the far north, middle part of the city. Should be lots of emergency exit signs nearby,” he said.

“DD33. Sure. Hey, I got another call,” I said.

“I’ll see you there.”

“What the hell was that?” Delovoa asked.

“Did they shoot you?”

“Yeah, but the beams pushed me straight up into the sky. I’m on the latticework on top of the city, maybe two thousand feet high. If I hadn’t grabbed hold, I would have fallen to my death. My nose and ears are bleeding and I puked up a week’s worth of food.”

“Your nose is always bleeding.”

“That’s mucous. What are we going to do?”

“Zzzho got shooed away from the port. He’s meeting us on the north side at an emergency exit. DB30 in the north.”

Delovoa was quiet a moment.

“I just checked. There isn’t a DB30 exit. There is a DD33,” Delovoa said.

“Yeah, that.”

“The details matter, Hank. I don’t want to go to the wrong port and get pushed out into space by some weird repulsion rifles.”

“Sorry, I just got blasted through a building. I’m surprised I’m even talking.”

“I’ll go across this latticework. It has walkways. I’ll meet you there. Tele me when you get close,” he said.

“Okay. I’ll see you at the emergency exit.”

I oriented myself using my tele and began walking. It would take about half an hour to reach DD33—assuming I didn’t get pushed through any more buildings.

It was a shame we’d leave behind all the junk we scavenged, but it wasn’t as if we paid for it.

I felt I had completely recovered from flying through a building when I heard some muted talking. I turned around and saw three figures in the distance. They weren’t the same ones who had shot us. They looked like they were wearing construction clothes, with hardhats and coveralls. I couldn’t tell what species they were because they were too far away, but they might have been Rettosians.

Then an immense bass rumble erupted.

The row of buildings behind me, about five identical, six-story structures, were all missing about a third of their facades. They had been abruptly erased. Or disintegrated. Or something. The buildings were now slowly falling over due to their weight being unsupported.

Falling over in my direction.

It was one thing to get punched through those buildings like a bullet, it was another thing to be buried underneath them.

I began running.

The buildings were all falling simultaneously. I could tell by their shadows that I wasn’t going to make it clear of them.

But the street was not wide enough to accommodate them tipping over, and the structures all smashed into the buildings on the opposite side of the street, making a narrow tunnel for me to run in.

I heard the same bass rumble again.

The buildings I had taken shelter in front of had been split nearly in half! Clearly someone was using a kind of demolition device as a weapon.

Both sets of buildings were now falling, and I was out of hiding places.

Fortunately, I could reach the safety of a street intersection before I was squashed. The structures fell in front of and behind me, but I had a whole cross street of cover.

I decided to hang a right, even if it meant going parallel to the emergency exit. Running in a straight line seemed a bad way to escape a gun that sliced up buildings in a straight line.

I heard the noise and saw several buildings crumble in the distance, but it was far away from my position.

They didn’t get near me again, either because they were incorrectly guessing my current location, or they hadn’t been trying to hit me in the first place. Who knows, maybe I had just been in the wrong place at the wrong time as they were remodeling Floloria.

I picked my way around single buildings and kept heading east and north.

I got an urgent tele from Delovoa.

“Help!” He screamed.

“What?”

“Some kind of beam hit the stairwell going down and severed it. I can’t go up and there’s a thirty-foot drop to the ground. I’m wedged in here but I don’t know how long my legs will hold,” he pleaded.

He gave me his tele coordinates and I hurried to his location as quickly as possible.

He was at the very edge of the city and only about five blocks from the emergency port we had to reach.

The stairwell he was talking about looked like a splinter hanging off a piece of wood at an angle. It was impossible for him to jump backwards and reach the rest of the stairs to the ground.

“You have to catch me,” he yelled.

“You know I’m not a soft surface,” I called back.

“My muscles are cramping and I’m going to fall.”

I wish I had time to go into these buildings and drag out some beds or cots or something. Not sure it would matter at 30 feet. I could jump that distance and land on my head and it wouldn’t hurt—much. But Delovoa wasn’t me.

I had played professional glocken and there was a whole classification of players who caught heavy tokens thrown at them. I had not been one of those players.

It was hard to judge where Delovoa was while looking straight up. The angle of the stairs, and Floloria’s curved, yellow, space station shield played tricks with his orientation.

“Okay,” I said uneasily.

I would have to bend my body to try and cushion his descent.

Delovoa let go.

He was falling. Falling. Falling.

He fell directly behind me and landed in the road with a sickening crack. I had failed to catch, or even touch him, as I had not been lined up correctly.

“Ahhh!” he shrieked.

“Sorry,” I said.

Both his feet were twisted at strange angles and the bone of his right leg was poking out of his skin and pants. The bone was blue.

“You moron! You colossal, fat idiot!”

“I’m sorry. I don’t catch people jumping from latticeworks very often.”

I reached down and picked him up, putting him over my shoulder.

“Stop! Stop! I need a doctor.”

“We have to get out of here. You want to ask those guys who shot us for some first aid?”

I walked toward the emergency port, moving as slowly as I dared.

“Why aren’t you bleeding?” I asked.

“Intelligent…blood control…routing,” he said.

I decided I didn’t want to know more.

Buildings were still falling over, but they were very far from us.

We reached the Suckface and Zzzho disembarked the ship without trouble. Once in space, I exhaled with relief.

People always said space was beautiful. Space was beautiful if you didn’t live in space; if you didn’t spend your entire life in space; if you hadn’t been surrounded for hundreds of years by blackness. To a creature without a butt, a turd must also be a beautiful thing, but you didn’t see me writing epic poems about my last bathroom visit.

There was some medical equipment onboard, which I didn’t know how to use. I didn’t often get sick and if I got physically injured, my body usually healed itself. If I was hurt enough I couldn’t heal, I was likely in a hospital. I basically had zero first aid skills.

But Delovoa knew a lot. He knew enough to create entire bodies. But despite his blood control, he was in shock.

He took some drugs from the kit to ease the pain and he snorted a tiny bit of Zzzho.

Zzzho had the ability to kind of supercharge the brains of biologicals when we inhaled him. It was a bit risky, but not much riskier than compound fractures. And the jolt made Delovoa more competent in dealing with his own injuries.

“Did you know those guys?” I asked Delovoa.

“No. How would I? When I was up on the latticework, I saw there was some kind of large construction equipment.”

“They tried constructing a bunch of buildings at me.”

“That doesn’t make any sense.”

“Deconstructing. Whatever. They were blowing up buildings with something.”

“Why didn’t you shoot them? That’s what you’re good at, right?”

“I’m good at…no, that’s not it.” I was going to say I was good at negotiations, but I didn’t negotiate very well with them. “I think the second group of guys I saw were construction workers. They might not have been after us.”

“The first group who shot us didn’t seem like construction workers. They were wearing body armor. But why would anyone be doing construction on Floloria?”

“Same reason we were looting it. Well, maybe not the same reason. If you could convert that space station to an enormous ship, like a transport, it would be super valuable,” I guessed.

“It would be far too slow. But I suppose you could do things with it. Just the scrap metal would be worth a lot. That still doesn’t explain why they tried to kill us.”

“Maybe they were stealing it,” I said.

“Stealing a space station? From whom?”

I shrugged.

“Do you think they could have been sending the signals we intercepted earlier?”

“I can’t see why. Those signals were going off-station. They were communicating with someone else.”

“Do you think it was that old Rettosian you killed years ago?”

We once encountered an ancient Rettosian scientist on Floloria. He was the one who mutated Belvaille System. Delovoa got mad and shot him—but it was only a clone. So he might still be around somewhere.

“Assuming he’s still alive, he probably left the System with all the other Rettosians. Not much reason to stay around here nowadays.”

“Why did you stay?” I asked Delovoa.

“Because I’m a stupid genius,” he said. “Besides, next to you, I’m the longest-living resident on Belvaille.”

“Is that really something to be proud of at this point?”

“A lot worse places to live. Like any of the thousand or so planets that got eradicated in the civil war.”

“What are you going to do about your injuries?” I asked.

“I don’t know. Who on Belvaille do you think has a nice set of legs I can borrow?”





CHAPTER 16




Someone left a note on my front door:



Meet by the crotch. Tomorrow at 7am.



Even though it wasn’t signed, I knew it was MTB.

No one else would wake up that early, for one. Also, he didn’t like being seen with me because he was a police officer and I was a crook. Though police didn’t do much on Belvaille. Finally, “the crotch” referred to Wallow’s corpse.

People had been terrified of Wallow when he was alive. The non-decomposing remains of the giant were no less scary. Who knew what kind of weird Therezian diseases you might catch standing around him? To actually go between his legs to conduct a meeting, well, we could be sure no one would interrupt us.

I wished Cliston was back. Not only to get his advice, but to make me food. He had prepared some meals before he went on vacation, but I didn’t know how to cook them. He couldn’t just set out things for me to heat up. No, Cliston left complicated dinners all organized and color-coordinated. You probably had to have a doctorate in cookology to understand how to prepare them.

So I was eating undercooked, underprepared slop, that was likely poisonous.

I also couldn’t put on my clothes without Cliston. He had created a very complex wardrobe for me over the years and I could barely tie my shoes without his help.

The next day when I woke up to meet MTB, I decided I would just put on underwear and a bathrobe. I’d carry Gun in my pocket and have a cup of bad coffee.

“You’re late,” MTB barked, once I had reached the dark recesses of Wallow’s upper thighs.

“Not much,” I said.

“Fifteen minutes. You live steps away. My time is important.”

“Why would your time be important? Doing a lot of police work on Belvaille?” I asked sarcastically.

“That’s what I’m here for. A Navy officer is missing.”

I waited.

“What’s he missing? His innocence? His bubble gum?”

“Funny guy. Mr. Boshe told Garm. Garm told me.”

I was annoyed again at Mr. Boshe’s lack of title. Even MTB was technically a police Detective and he didn’t exactly detect much.

“Why does this involve you? Let the Navy deal with it. Or Garm.”

“Garm doesn’t want to. She handed it to me. It’s a big deal,” MTB said.

“Just for one missing sailor? Was he an admiral or something? He’s probably AWOL,” I said, drinking my gross coffee and becoming even more irritated.

“The officer is dead.”

“Then what’s the problem? Tell them he’s dead. Say he hit his head giving food to starving children. They’ll award him a medal posthumously.”

“No. Roll Bungalow did it. The officer got in a fight, started shooting. So they killed him. Then they dissolved his body in acid,” MTB said.

I shook my head.

“That was stupid.”

Roll Bungalow was a gang boss who ran a brothel and hotel. That was a big deal nowadays. Not that he made a lot of money, but he had a job and could provide employment. He and I had some dealings a number of years ago when he was in a different occupation with a different fake name.

“Where did he get enough acid to destroy a body? I can’t even get mustard or pepper on this space station,” I said.

“It’s a grease solvent for spaceships.”

“Oh. What do you want from me, MTB?” I asked.

“The police need to be able to take care of stuff like this. The Navy needs to see we can mete out justice,” he replied.

“Then do it.”

“If I go to Roll Bungalow and try and arrest someone, they’ll end up dissolving me in acid. Or I’ll get in a gun battle. Then the Navy will come settle things. And if Mr. Boshe comes, it will be a whole lot of soldiers kicking a whole lot of teeth out.”

“Yeah,” I said. “That’s why Garm wants nothing to do with this. Want me to talk to Roll? Tell him to give up a fall guy?”

“We don’t need you trying to make deals. We need a real police force.”

“I have no control over that. It’s not like I can bless you and give you legitimacy,” I said.

“Give us your gun. Then people will listen to us.”

“My Rettosian pistol?” I pulled it out.

“Yes,” he said.

“It won’t work for you. It uses the biology of my electricity. And I’m like a big version. Something like that. It’s too early in the morning for science talk,” I said.

“Have Delovoa make us combat gear. Some top-flight stuff.”

“He doesn’t work for me. Ask him yourself.”

“He said he was morally against making weapons for cops.”

“That doesn’t sound right. Delovoa doesn’t have any morals.”

“Maybe he didn’t say ‘morally.’ He said if he arms the police, everyone else will murder him,” MTB corrected.

“He’s probably right. You’re saying you want advanced armor and weapons so the criminals on Belvaille won’t simply ignore you?” I asked.

“Exactly. Right now, we’re outmanned and outgunned. We can’t do any police work.”

“How about plasma guns?” I asked.

“Those Ontakian things you had in the past?”

“Yeah.”

“Do you have any?” MTB asked.

“No. But I might know where to get some.”

He thought it over.

“Yeah, that will work. Belvaille needs police. You have to agree with that.”

“I agree we at least need the appearance of law enforcement,” I said. “But while you guys are getting your new equipment, I have to take care of this dead soldier. The Navy won’t stand for it. And we can’t have them interfering. Especially now.”

“While you’re trying to set up the Skim?” he asked.

MTB was no dummy.

“Yeah. So, are you going to be a hardcase and try and break up the Skim?” I asked cautiously.

“This is Belvaille. It’s not like people are farmers or artists. Everyone here is a criminal of some sort. I figure, the police are to enforce the laws. But Belvaille decides what is illegal and what isn’t. We’re not going to arrest people for spitting on the sidewalk,” he said.

“Do we even have laws?” I asked.

MTB shrugged.

“I figure, if it makes the space station a worse place or endangers our livelihood, it’s against the law.”

“Like murdering a Navy officer.”

“Exactly.”

“Wow, MTB. When did you become such a pragmatist?”

“You either adapt on Belvaille or you don’t live very long,” he said. “So what do you want in return for getting us plasma guns and armor?” MTB asked.

“Like ten favors.”

“What’s a favor? I’m not going to subvert the police force to you.”

“Right now you’re not a police force. You’re already subverted. But in exchange for Delovoa’s help, at least one favor will be a pair of legs.”

“Legs? What do you mean?” MTB asked.

“What guy has the sexiest legs on the station? Delovoa wants them. He busted his last pair.”





CHAPTER 17




“Ugly. Fat. Varicose veins. Ugh, who would wear those slacks? You aren’t very good at taking pictures,” Delovoa said, leafing through my tele.

“No, I’m not very good at it. Because taking pictures of peoples’ legs so we can steal them to fix yours, isn’t something I have a lot of experience with,” I said.

I had sat around for two days trying to program my tele to take leg and face shots. I had also gone to the hospital and threatened the medical technicians so I could get all their x-rays of feet and legs.

We had given up looking for the Dredel Led hackers until Delovoa could make the equipment more sensitive. We had sat in space for a total of sixteen hours. Delovoa said the chances of intercepting a random signal, on a narrow wavelength, in an entire System, was pretty unlikely.

We still didn’t know what happened at Floloria, but didn’t think it was connected to the hack. I spoke to some members of the Society of the Free and Easy, who used to live on Floloria, and they confirmed that if there were any Rettosians still there, they probably would be hostile. Rettosians weren’t the friendliest species at the best of times. Anyone who remained behind when their entire population left were probably a bunch of xenophobic hermits. They recommended we stay clear of Floloria and I had to agree.

“So you said you can build Ontakian plasma weapons, right?” I tried to confirm with Delovoa.

“No, I didn’t say that. I said if you get me one, I can build something similar.”

“I don’t want similar. I told MTB I’d get him plasma weapons for the cops.”

“Your home world, Ontak, is destroyed. Whatever weird elements your species used to create those weapons went with it. It’s not as if there are vast quantities of Ontakonite floating through space for us to use.”

“What’s Ontakonite?”

“Nothing, I just made it up. What I can probably do is make it use Keilvin Kamigan fuel cells just like your spaceship.”

“Will I still be immune to them? I suggested giving the cops plasma weapons because they can’t hurt me,” I said.

“They should work the same. Just the power source will be different. We’ll need to use Zzzho for the energy cells.”

“Poor Zzzho. We’re going to suck him dry,” I said.

It was still hard for me to comprehend how Keilvin Kamigans could simply slough off some of their gaseous bodies so they could be used in batteries.

“He’ll charge you for it,” Delovoa said, still gazing at leg photos.

“What do you give a guy who doesn’t eat, sleep, drink, go to the bathroom, or play bingo?” I asked.

“What do you give him now for driving your spaceship?”

“I pay him credits. But I’ll be damned if I know what he spends them on. He must be rich. I hope he’s not, because no one else can pilot my ship.”

“What do you think of these?” Delovoa asked, holding up my tele to show a pair of beefy legs.

“How should I know? My legs came with my body. Just pick someone. Someone without a lot of firepower or connections,” I added. “Where is Ziggy, by the way?”

“Ziggles. He left when Zeti left. All the Po are gone,” he said absently.

Ziggy was a Po who had been Delovoa’s butler. The Po were the slave species of the Boranjame. The Boranjame were a crystalline species who never stopped growing, who could predict the future, but not directly interfere in it, and were beings of Order. They were the most powerful race in the galaxy. They tooled around in ships the size of planets and periodically wiped out entire solar systems.

Zeti had been relatively small as a Boranjame went, maybe fifteen stories tall. But he had given me lots of scary information that I couldn’t quite forget, despite my desperate attempts to do just that.

He told me that the mere existence of the Boranjame was what made mutations possible. They also made life possible.

Now that was a résumé.

Not only that, but the Boranjame had accidentally made the Creator God, Thad Elon.

Thad Elon had accomplished nearly everything of consequence in the history of the galaxy. He had established the original Colmarian Confederation. He had allowed the Dredel Led to mass produce. He had given technology to the Keilvin Kamigans. He had freed the Ontakians from slavery.

It seemed impossible that one being could do so much. However, I knew for a fact that Thad Elon was immortal. I disintegrated him once and he thanked me for doing it.

I personally witnessed Thad Elon start the Colmarian Confederation civil war, which caused untold billions of deaths. He did it because, essentially, he was bored.

Zeti had stated that Thad Elon was destined to end all life in the galaxy.

Sometimes, hanging out in Delovoa’s apartment, looking at pictures of hairy legs, I would wonder why I even bothered, knowing that all life in the galaxy was destined to die.

It really put things in a horribly depressing perspective. But then my belly grumbled and it was time to get back to work.



“Hello, Master Overseer of Navy and Belvaille Relations,” Garm said. “Won’t you come into my office?”

I was visiting Garm at the telescopes. In the past, all government operations had been in the glamorous one-hundred-story City Hall, but that building was basically empty now. We had little use for such enormous structures ever since Belvaille’s population decline.

Garm’s uniform was now bedecked with medals. They ran down her sleeves and I even saw some on her back as she turned and walked into her office.

The people in the telescope building seemed efficient and competent, hardly sparing me a glance.

When I closed the door, I had to ask.

“What’s with all the medals? Is that one per gray hair that you dye black?”

“I don’t dye my hair,” she said seriously. “I found a loophole that I can nominate people for citations, so I nominated myself. I quadrupled my salary. Why are you wearing a bathrobe?”

“Cliston is on vacation and I can’t figure out my clothes. Also, I’m not really Master Overseer, you know.”

“You are according to the PCC Navy. You told Mr. Boshe that was your title.”

“I said that as a joke,” I replied, alarmed.

“Yes. Because you’re aware of the Post-Colmarian Confederation Colmarian Confederation Navy’s tremendous sense of humor. It’s your job now until someone else gets appointed.”

“How do I appoint someone?” I asked.

“Do what you did: have someone stupid enough to claim it.”

“Can I get paid for the job?”

“You’re not even a citizen. I don’t see how,” she replied.

“This empire sucks.”

“I hear there are some other Ontakians on Belvaille,” Garm stated.

“Maybe,” I said, trying to be cagey.

“Look, there could be a Therezian ballet company, it doesn’t matter to me as long as it doesn’t interfere in my jurisdiction. I run the telescopes. You know, the communications network for this region. I heard chatter that a remote PCC outpost was overrun by a handful of fat, bulletproof people.”

“That could be anyone,” I replied.

“They said the attackers escaped in an ancient Colmarian corvette that had the designation of, ‘Don’t Tell Them It’s The Suckface,’” she continued.

“There’s probably a million ships with that name.”

“I’m trying to explain that it doesn’t bother me. Overrun whatever you want. Just don’t bring any crap back to Belvaille or it becomes my concern,” she said. “So what are these other Ontakians like? It scares me that there may be more Hanks around.”

“No, they aren’t like me. They’re similar to my uncle. But there’s one gal, Najosa, who seems pretty cool.”

“Oh, and let me guess, she just happens to be attractive?” Garm asked.

“What’s that matter? I judge people by their characters,” I said solemnly.

“Yeah, right. You lose all your common sense when it comes to women.”

“I dated you.”

“Like…centuries ago! Why do you always bring that up? We were terrible together and it lasted a handful of months. Why is it some major point in your life after all this time?” she yelled.

Ow.

I took my time responding because I had not been prepared for that attack and, on reflection, I wasn’t sure what to say.

“I don’t date a lot. I’m a big mutant. I guess…why did we break up? I never could really figure it out. I know it wasn’t working, but I don’t know why,” I said.

“Because, Hank, you merely exist. You don’t live. You’re old as hell and you haven’t grown at all, except at the waistline. You make fun of everything because nothing is serious to you.”

“Cancer is serious.”

“See? You were given the incredible gift of a new life and you spent it being a glorified bouncer.”

“I’ve done a ton of stuff to help. The galaxy owes me big time.”

“You only try and do something when you’re forced to, and then you half-ass it. You are the laziest person I have ever met because everything is so easy to you. You don’t age, you don’t get diseases, you’re bulletproof. You keep plodding through life repeating all your same mistakes. Look, you’re good at what you do—you’re great at it. And maybe it’s your personality that makes that possible. But eating and sleeping are probably your two favorite things. I’m romantic enough that I want a bit more in a relationship than that.”

I reflexively looked back to see if anyone outside the office could hear this. But if they did, they kept their heads down.

“You’re going to make it hard for me to enjoy eating if you keep ripping my guts out,” I said.

“I’m sorry. You’re right. I know I have issues, too. A lot. I’m single. You’re single. We’re single for a ton of reasons,” she said, somewhat softer.

“Yeah, but at least you were with someone long enough to have a child. And grandchild. I think my longest relationship was a year. And that’s because she was a crew member on a tanker and I never saw her. Where is your son’s father, if you don’t mind me asking?”

“He’s dead. I…well, I killed him. This was long ago and I was a bit meaner back then. He wasn’t a good guy and he double-crossed me,” she said, appearing somewhat ashamed.

I paused.

“I have an idea. Let’s not talk about relationships ever again,” I said.

I looked outside at the people diligently working.

“So you don’t know if anyone out there is hacking the Dredel Led?” I asked. “Or is that still classified?”

“We broadcast messages for the PCC mostly.”

“Mostly…”

“It would be a major operation to hack a species like the Dredel Led. It would require whole teams of experts. I would know if there was a group here doing that. I can say for certain, there isn’t.”

“Alright.”

“So what are you going to do about Roll Bungalow?” she asked.

“Why does everyone think this is my concern? I’m not a cop.”

“Neither am I. Neither is Mr. Boshe...for now.”

“Ominous,” I mocked. “I’m working on helping MTB get equipment. Maybe he can handle it then.”

“MTB can’t do this. I told him to talk to you.”

“Shouldn’t you have talked to me directly because I’m the Overseer?” I teased.

“Yes. But I know you don’t care and MTB does.”

“So why are you saying he can’t handle it? He’s been a police detective for centuries. I think he was arresting urine in his mother’s womb.”

“Did he tell you who the trigger man was?” she asked.

“It wasn’t Roll Bungalow?”

“That’s just who paid for it. The guy who actually murdered the Navy officer was your old pal, Xandale.”





CHAPTER 18




I wanted to kick in the door at Roll Bungalow’s hotel—make a real entrance. But they had a doorman and he opened it for me.

“Thanks,” I said, handing him two credits and already feeling I was off to a bad start. “Where can I find Roll Bungalow?”

“Who’s askin’?” he said.

I could tell he wasn’t a good doorman. People were trying to maintain a semblance of the swanky conditions that existed in Belvaille’s past, but all the skilled people left. This guy had probably been a janitor on an off-station barge who was now head doorman at one of the best facilities around.

“I’m his lawyer,” I lied terribly, still wearing my bathrobe.

The man pursed his lips and looked me over. Good thing I just tipped him.

“He’s in his office. Want I should tele him?”

“I have an appointment,” I said, walking past.

Inside, the building was massive and most of it was unused space.

There were maybe twenty men and women lounging around on chairs and cots, trying to look sultry and not bored. They were the working people. Gigolos and prostitutes.

The other 90% of the main floor was roped off so people wouldn’t wander around and the staff didn’t have to clean everywhere. There were drop cloths on what looked like hundreds of pieces of furniture.

All the white sheets made it look like some kind of ghost town or cemetery.

“Hey, love, care for a poke?” one woman asked, yawning.

“I weigh over a thousand pounds,” I replied, looking around for Roll’s office.

“Damn, hon. You need to ease off on the cupcakes. But we can still make it work, just don’t lean on me.”

“Thanks. Another time,” I said.

I spotted two security guards looking annoyed at my presence.

They were big, bald guys who wore ill-fitting suits and were sweating. Cliston would have had a fit seeing that purple tie and blue handkerchief. Not to mention their boots. Of course, Cliston would probably murder me if he knew I was walking around in public wearing a bathrobe.

“Hank. Sorry, we can’t let you past,” one of the security guards said.

“Hey, Bort. How’s the head?” I asked.

“What?”

“Didn’t you get shot in the head?”

“I did,” his twin brother replied, turning his head and revealing a scar.

His identical twin’s name was also Bort, and they got annoyed when people mixed them up. In consequence, they were in a perpetual state of annoyed. Which turned out was a great attribute for being a security guard at a brothel, since clients often got unruly.

“I need to see Roll about the Navy guy,” I said.

They looked at my clothes and seemed concerned. Like I really meant business because I just took a shower.

Suddenly, Xandale walked up next to me.

“I’ll take him in, Borts,” Xandale said, smirking.

Maybe he was smirking. I always thought he was smirking. He had those gigantic herbivore teeth and he was a jerk, so he was probably smirking.

Xandale wore tasteful clothes, hip and current. No idea where he got them considering the embargo on the space station. He might have a tailor who was stealing curtains like Cliston.

Xandale led me past the twins.

“I told Roll you’d eventually be coming,” Xandale said, as we walked around a few corners and went deeper into the dark, cavernous building.

“I explained we had to lay low while the Skim is being set up, and then he goes and kills a soldier.”

“I killed him, actually,” Xandale smirked.

It was so dark this far back in the hotel that I felt like we were walking by the glow of Xandale’s white teeth. We were quiet for a moment and I wondered if I was being led into a trap. I kept ahold of Gun in my robe pocket.

“So what exactly do you want here, Hank?” Xandale continued.

“If you don’t mind, I’ll talk to my old buddy, Roll.”

We came to a door and Xandale knocked.

The door was opened and inside it was bright. There were no obvious signs of an ambush. There was a nice carpet, some desks, a statue of an angel, and more armed security guards.

“Stank Delicious!” Roll Bungalow exclaimed.

If I was underdressed in my robes, then Roll was under-underdressed. He was wearing only skintight shorts with his upper body waxed and oiled.

“Candar,” I replied coolly.

Roll Bungalow was my former Super Class glocken teammate and captain, Candar. I forget his real name, but it wasn’t Roll Bungalow. He was golden haired, muscular beyond belief, handsome, and pretty much talented at everything that a person could be talented at. Including becoming a pimp and hotelier.

Our team had kind of…cheated at the Championship, which meant all of us were doomed to never be able to play professional glocken again. To me, that was a blessing. But Candar had been quite skilled and it left him without a job. As team captain of the galaxy’s greatest cheaters, probably a lot of people also wanted him dead.

They wanted me dead, too. But good luck with that. About a dozen people had come in the early years after the Championship to try and punish me for cheating. I’ll just say that they didn’t leave satisfied, if they left at all. Candar could outperform me in nearly every way imaginable, but he wasn’t bulletproof or capable of healing grievous injuries like I was.

So Candar decided to hide out among Belvaille’s dwindling population and try his fortunes here. It hadn’t bothered me until now.

“Why did you kill a Navy officer when I specifically said not to?” I asked.

“I don’t think he was an officer,” Roll/Candar smiled.

When Candar was team captain, he had been beyond reproach and possessed sterling integrity. Now that he changed jobs and was a criminal, all his morals were gone. He even switched personalities like a pro.

“Besides, what’s it to you?” he asked.

“Didn’t you hear? Hank is now Master Overseer of Navy and Belvaille Relations,” Xandale stated.

“Wow. How much does it pay?” Roll laughed.

Everyone in the room laughed.

Except me.

“I need a corpse. I need something to work with,” I said, ignoring them.

“The soldier was drunk or drugged. He was armed to the gills and about to kill one of my ladies. Beauty is a precious commodity on Belvaille,” Roll said.

“Your ‘ladies’ didn’t seem so pretty to me when I came in,” I said, just to be pissy.

“Go fly a transport halfway across the galaxy and then tell me what is pretty and what isn’t. You’d fall in love with the first Gandrine you saw,” he replied. “This is my business. I can’t have every soldier come in and beat up my workers. If even one in a hundred did it, I’d be out of work in a month.”

“And it’s not as if lots of gorgeous prostitutes are stopping by Belvaille looking for career opportunities,” Xandale added.

“So you killed him? A soldier?” I asked, trying to confirm this.

“We tried talking to him,” Xandale said. “He wasn’t listening.”

“But I still don’t see why you care,” Roll added.

“If I go up to that battlecruiser and say, ‘Mr. Boshe, the guy who killed your soldier is on Belvaille and his name is Roll Bungalow, formerly Candar,’ what do you think will happen?” I asked.

Everyone lost their smiles—except Xandale, of course.

“What do you want me to do?” Roll asked. “We burned him up in acid. Nothing was left except slurry and we flushed it.”

“Don’t pour acid down the drain. The system can’t handle that,” I said, drawing on my former sewer-worker experience.

“Next time I won’t. But tell me exactly what your goal is?” Roll asked, finally appreciating the seriousness of the situation.

“He’ll do,” I said, pointing to Xandale. “I’ll take him to Mr. Boshe to face punishment. It’s the PCC. Who knows, they may screw up and give him a reward.”

“Can’t we put our differences behind us, Hank?” Xandale asked. “Besides, I’d like to see you take me anywhere I don’t want to go.”

That made me nervous because Xandale knew my abilities. I got the idea he was about to try something so I pulled out Gun to be safe.

Xandale suddenly turned to smoke! Not like, real smoke. But he looked smoky and sort of black and white.

“Gun, can you see that guy?” I asked.

“Hello! Yes, I can,” Gun answered cheerfully.

“Can you shoot him?”

“Sure.”

“Will it hurt him?”

“No.”

“Why not?”

“I don’t know,” Gun said.

“How do you know if you shoot, it won’t hurt?” I asked, feeling foolish arguing with my gun in front of pimps and thugs.

“Because I just did.”

“Because you just do?” I asked.

“I think.”

I put Gun away and walked forward. I waved my arm around. It didn’t disturb Xandale’s smoke and my arm passed right through.

“You can’t hurt me, Hank. But watch,” Xandale said.

He took out a smoky knife from his smoky belt, and tried to stab me.

Poink.

The knife stopped at my chest.

Poink. Poink. Poink.

He stabbed my shoulder, arm, face. I didn’t even feel it. I could tell from his smoky expression that he was expecting a different result.

“That was super impressive, Xandale,” I said.

“Hank, how else can we solve this?” Roll asked.

“You have to pay a fine. It goes to MTB’s police force,” I said.

“Why?”

“Because of crap like this! We need cops. 100,000 credits. That should dissuade others from killing any government officials,” I said.

Poink.

“Knock it off,” I told Xandale, who I think tried to stab my neck while I was talking.

Xandale changed back to normal, his eyes downcast.

“Also, do you know someone with good legs who isn’t such a great worker?” I asked.

“Good legs? What in Thad Elon Drifting Through Space are you talking about? You mean fast? You putting together another glocken team?” Roll sounded excited.

“No, I mean good legs as in sexy. Er, handsome. You have male escorts, right?” I asked.

“Of course. Male, female, in between. Twenty different species. I’m shooting for fifty,” Roll added.

“Great. A male Colmarian with good legs that I can give to Mr. Boshe,” I said.

“What do you mean?” he asked.

“We have to give them a murderer. I assume you don’t want to give up Xandale, so we have to hand them someone else. If we don’t, Mr. Boshe will come down with his army and look for the killer. That would be bad for everyone.”

“I might be able to give up someone,” Roll said, thinking.

“I know it’s unfair to the poor guy who takes the blame. But they’re going to demand justice for the slain soldier.”

“Does this person with good legs have to be alive?” Roll asked.

“Yes! You can’t go killing some rival and try and pass it off as yours. Make sure they have nice legs.”

“What’s with the legs?” Roll asked. “I don’t get it.”

“I’ll just say this: Delovoa.”

“Oh,” Roll replied knowingly.

Delovoa had been our team doctor when we played glocken. His peculiarities were well known to Roll Bungalow.





CHAPTER 19




“I brought you a knife. It’s forged cobalt and titanium with tungsten serrations,” I said, holding up the gnarly cleaver to Najosa.

She had met me at the door to her apartment and took the blade, confused.

“Why did you buy me this?” she asked.

“Because I’m pretty romantic. It comes from being serious,” I said, trying to contradict Garm’s earlier assessment of me.

Najosa moved to the side to allow me to enter, but her brow was crinkled and I wondered if I had misstepped.

“You sound like a teenage girl. I hope you’re not romantic. It’s a worthless concept,” she said.

“Good. I’m not,” I replied quickly.

Her apartment was filled with weapons and armor and everything military except a tank. I didn’t even see a chair.

“Are you carrying any food?” she asked

“What an odd question,” I laughed. “I mean, yeah, I am. Some rations. A bit of jerky. I need it to heal. And I get hungry a lot.”

“You have to give that up if we’re going to be together.”

“Are we dating?”

“Ontakians can’t breed with any species other than Ontakians,” she said.

“We can’t?”

“No. And we’re almost extinct.”

“Wait, so I didn’t have to use a condom all those times?” I asked, dismayed at centuries of lousy sex.

“No, but you can still get diseases. But since your mutation can cure cancer, you could probably cure those.”

“Well, that sucks,” I said, sitting on a crate of what appeared to be rockets.

“We have a duty to repopulate our species. You are an Ontakian combat model with relatively good genes—at least physically. I am an Ontakian combat model with good genes. The remainder of our people are spread out across the galaxy. We have limited options if we want to reproduce,” she explained.

I sat there. My first instinct was to eat some of my food but she’d already made it clear that was a pet peeve.

“Do you like the knife?” I asked.

“I can sell it. I don’t use knives much.”

“It’s the sentiment that matters.”

“Sentiment about what?” she asked seriously.

“I’m not actually sure at this point.”



“This isn’t what I had in mind when I said we should go out on dates,” I stated, annoyed.

“Who said this was a date?” Frank asked.

“I wasn’t talking to you.”

“We got a lead on two plasma pistols. You said you wanted to get some,” Najosa explained.

I was on my spaceship, the Suckface, and joined by the Society of the Free and Easy. We were headed to do the one thing I had been told was impossible in space: we were going to pirate another ship.

“So how does this work?” I asked.

“We have to board them. It’s a transport moving museum pieces to another planet,” Najosa said.

“And what if they don’t stop? Are we supposed to ram them? Disable their engines with harsh language? My ship doesn’t have weapons.”

“But it looks like it does. We’ll threaten to blow them up if they don’t let us board,” Frank said.

“What if there’s a PCC Navy patrol around? What if they have an escort?” I asked.

“Then we have to run,” Najosa said.

“What if they have guards on the ship itself?”

“They do,” the woman, Alase, replied. “We know that for a fact because we have their departure manifest.”

“So we’re going to get in a firefight on a spaceship? That seems risky.”

“It is. We need to take out the guards without using guns and without them using guns,” Najosa confirmed.

“Good thing I know magic spells. Hopa Badopa!” I intoned, waving my hands around. But no one seemed to appreciate the joke.

“One more Portal jump and we’ll reach target,” Zzzho stated on the intercom.

“Suit up, Hank,” Najosa said.



I could feel my heart beating in my feet as I waited by the Suckface’s exit.

Our ploy had worked. The transport scanned the two turrets on my ship. They didn’t know they were empty turrets, however.

We said we merely wanted to steal a few items and wouldn’t hurt anyone. For a transport carrying as much as they were, it was probably a good deal—compared with getting blown to pieces.

“Does anyone want to hear my safety speech about emergency pressure suits?” I asked.

“No,” everyone replied.

The Suckface was capable of jamming the communications and sensors of other ships, but no one on board knew how to operate those systems. The transport had probably already sent out a distress call. We would have to hurry.

I felt our ships dock together with a hard thud. I took out Gun as I waited for the airlock to open.

“You can’t bring that,” Frank said. “It’s powerful enough that if it feels like it, it could breach the hull of the ship and kill us all.”

“So you want me to go unarmed?” I asked.

“And alone,” Frank said.

He took Gun from my hand and pushed me. When I was on the other side of the airlock door, he flushed the tube.

I went swirling through the connecting tunnel toward the transport ship. I flew inside their boarding compartment, right as the door opened.

I was surrounded by five guards who seemed incredibly surprised at my instantaneous entry.

Man, was I angry.

So angry.

Actually, I was beyond angry. I was in such a transcendent state of hatred that I was hardly aware that I had crippled and battered and otherwise bludgeoned the five guards who met me.

I stood there punching myself in the chest and face because there was no one else left to punch.

Somewhere in my head I knew this wasn’t normal behavior, but I didn’t care. I took off running to find more enemies. More things to hurt and maim.

The pilots.

The engineers.

The loading crew.

All of them eventually suffered broken bones and contusions at my beefy hands. They tried to run, but there was nowhere to go. We were in space.

I stood there for probably three minutes punching what turned out to be my shadow. I punched my way clear through the floor, bloodying my hands in the process.

It was about this time the Society of the Free and Easy, freely and easily made their way onboard to survey the damage.

“Good job, champ,” Frank said.

“Did you give me that drug?” I asked.

“Daxapronal. I figured it was the easiest way to secure the ship,” Najosa said absently.

“You turned me into a shock troop? What if I had been killed?”

“How?” she asked.

“Who knows? I don’t like being used.”

“We’re trying to get you a plasma pistol before the Navy shows up. Start looking,” she replied.

None of the crew were dead that I could tell, but they weren’t much better off. We rounded them up to make sure they were all in the same location and not going to do anything foolish.

As the drug faded away, I became very frightened of the situation I was in.

I had attacked a spaceship and there were plenty of witnesses. I didn’t specifically know if it was against the law, but this was the PCC. It had to be against a thousand laws.

I went back into the cargo hold to find Najosa.

“Look for a box labeled E309,” she said.

“What about all those people? They can describe me to the Navy,” I said.

“What people?”

I heard distant automatic fire from several small caliber guns.

Najosa saw my horrified expression.

“Those were citizens of the PCC profiting from the destruction of Ontak. They were selling our heritage as trinkets and curiosities. You shouldn’t feel sorry for them, Hank. You should be disgusted by them. There’s the box. Get it and let’s go. Wait, what’s that?”

There was a large tarp covering a cone about seven feet long and two feet in diameter. When Najosa threw off the sheet, even I could tell what it was.

It was a warhead.

Frank came in right as we discovered it.

“Holy hell, that’s an Ontakian plasma missile,” he said.

“Please tell me you don’t want to bring that along,” I said.

“Hank, we hit the jackpot. Of course we’re bringing it,” Najosa replied.

“What, are we going to sit on it? You know the Suckface hardly has room enough as it is. And I’m not dragging a nuclear warhead into my ship,” I said.

“It’s not nuclear. It’s a Massive Enhanced Plasma Ordnance. M.E.P.O.,” Frank said.

“How do you know that? How is it you know all this obscure stuff about Ontakians but none of the basic things? Huh?” I yelled.

“It’s written right there on the side,” he said, pointing.

“Oh. Well, what does that mean? I don’t want to get massively ordnanced because I tripped over this thing on our journey home.”

“Plasma weapons don’t harm Ontakians, Hank. You know that,” Najosa said.

“That’s not a plasma gun. That’s a warhead! Don’t tell me when it explodes nothing but Ontakian folk songs burst out,” I argued.

“We’re taking it. We got you a plasma gun like you wanted. This is our payment for the job,” Najosa said.

“I did all the work! What can you possibly do with a massive plasma bomb?” I asked.

“We’ll find a use for it,” she said.

There wasn’t anything else to do. I wasn’t going to stand here and argue, giving time for a PCC warship to come and blast us to hell.

The trip home was relatively uneventful.

Other than yelling at people now and then when they bumped into or leaned against a doomsday weapon that was sitting square in the middle of the crew compartment.





CHAPTER 20




We’d acquired two plasma pistols and I took the newest-looking one.

The Ontakians left with the Massive Enhanced Plasma Ordnance tied down to their car. It was heavy enough that it shattered the front and rear windshields and put a huge dent in the vehicle. The fact they drove across the city towing a warhead, and no one stopped them, was not only a testament to how empty Belvaille was, but how dangerous.

I gave my plasma gun to Delovoa so he could use it as a model. Using it, he built a handful of replica plasma pistols and body armor for MTB’s police force. Delovoa said the replicas were very substandard compared to the original, but the original was broken, anyway.

MTB, with fake plasma pistol and body armor, then pressed Roll Bungalow for the male prostitute—with good legs. That was who we would pin for murdering the soldier and turn over to Mr. Boshe.

The fine of 100,000 credits was paid to MTB, then given to Delovoa for the guns and armor, then paid to me for the original plasma pistol.

It’s good to have friends.

I went upstairs the next day to see how the captive was doing. Presumably, Delovoa was going to take the poor guy’s legs. But then I needed to hand him over to Mr. Boshe so he didn’t vaporize our city.

“What do you think?” Delovoa said, wearing a pair of short-shorts.

His legs were indeed “good,” if you could discount all the surgical scars and metal implants.

“How did you do that in one day?” I asked.

“Oh, it only took a few hours,” he said, twirling around, stumbling, and almost falling down.

“Careful, I broke my back getting those legs. I assume the donor is dead.”

“Nope, quite alive.”

Delovoa chicken-walked into the other room. I couldn’t help noticing that he was having trouble getting used to his new limbs or…

“Hey! You got the legs on the wrong side,” I yelled. The left leg was on the right side and vice versa.

“I did this on purpose. Look, I can sidestep really well.”

Delovoa dodged back and forth with extreme rapidity.

“But you can hardly walk. Why do you always do stuff like this?”

“They’re not your legs, why do you care?” Delovoa sniffed.

I shook my head as Delovoa took me to a covered medical bay. He pulled back the curtains and there was our designated criminal.

What was left of him.

“You said he was alive!” I yelled.

“He is.”

I stood there a moment watching the chest rise and fall. That was all that was left. No arms, nothing below the waist, no head. Just a chest, and even that had scars and depressions where it looked like Delovoa had picked out organs.

“What am I supposed to do with this? How will anyone believe he killed anything?”

“I needed spare parts,” Delovoa said simply. “I don’t get these opportunities a lot and he had good arms, a great liver, and if I ever get tired of this nose I got an extra.”

Delovoa’s giant snout had been leaking mucous this whole time. His face was red and raw from regularly wiping it with a handkerchief.

I was frustrated, but what could I do? I couldn’t exactly ask Delovoa to put the body parts back where he found them—though he probably could.

“If I left him with a head, he would have denied having anything to do with the murder,” Delovoa said, seeing my consternation.

“How do I explain him being chopped into bits? Was it a gun battle and he got perfectly dismembered? The Navy wants to punish this guy, but he’s beyond punishment.”

“Say it was a knife fight. A brutal struggle. We did everything we could to save this man for trial, but his wounds were too grievous,” Delovoa stated dramatically.

I stood there thinking.

“No, that’s too confusing. This is the PCC we’re dealing with. If I say that, they’ll probably make me fill out paperwork until I die. I know how to handle it.”



“You do some pretty screwed-up stuff, but this is up there,” Zzzho said, referring to our dismembered semi-passenger as we flew the Suckface to Mr. Boshe’s ship, the Armage.

“He’s not in any pain,” I said.

“How can you be sure? How do you even know it’s a he?”

“Delovoa chopped him up.”

“That guy is really twisted. You know he kept trying to snort me? Even after I cut him off he had a big long straw that stuck into the cabin—as if I wouldn’t notice.”

“He’s a character,” I said delicately.

“And the Navy wants this thing?”

“Not him specifically. But someone. He was the unlucky choice.”

“Nice,” Zzzho said in what I assumed was a sarcastic response. “I know enough to not ask any more questions. We’re coming up on the ship. How are you going to transfer that cargo?”

“I got straps that I can use to drag him through the tube.”

“Ask Delovoa if he wants a few hits from me,” he said.

“Why? I thought you didn’t want him snorting you.”

“I don’t want him cutting me into pieces! If anyone can figure out how to vivisection a Keilvin Kamigan, it’s that guy.”



“I’m glad you were able to take care of this for us,” Mr. Boshe said.

We were sitting in his office, surrounded by his omnipresent piles of paper. I had delivered my prisoner at the port, so Mr. Boshe hadn’t seen him.

“I think you need to separate these folders, because they seem to be breeding. There’s a lot more since I was here last time,” I joked.

Mr. Boshe smiled ruefully.

“I know the PCC has too much paperwork. It is one of the many inefficiencies I have to deal with,” he said.

“Like that and the fact you aren’t even a soldier in the Navy?” As soon as I said it, I wondered if I had gone too far. I was, after all, standing on his battlecruiser.

“Yes, exactly. If the PCC could just get organized, we would be the most powerful empire in the galaxy. A true force. As it is, we’re just an enormous collection of civilizations that can’t agree on anything.”

I sniffed at my cup of coffee. They had forgotten to add water, so it was just a pile of grounds in a cup. The water would probably come later, after the appropriate requisition.

“How did you ascertain the person you captured was the one who killed our soldier?” Mr. Boshe asked.

“Just the usual way. We have police forces on Belvaille of course. They did the groundwork.”

“You have police?” Mr. Boshe seemed pleasantly surprised.

“Oh, sure. Detectives, officers, guns…whatever police need, they got it.”

“And your population is comfortable having them? I didn’t get the sense that Belvaille was a fan of law and order.”

I thought how to respond to that. I didn’t want to say we loved cops, because then he might be inclined to bring his own troops and assume we wouldn’t care. I also didn’t want it to appear we were a criminal bastion—because he might further be inclined to bring his troops to secure the city’s facilities.

“I wouldn’t say we are entirely comfortable, but the police are all recruited from our citizens, so we don’t begrudge them,” I replied.

“How did the murder of our personnel take place?”

“I don’t know. I wasn’t there. I’m just fulfilling my duty as Master Overseer of Navy and Belvaille Relations and handing over the culprit,” I said uneasily.

Mr. Boshe activated his military tele, which I could see was a lot clunkier and more complicated than a normal one.

“Sector 323. Yes, Mr. Boshe?” I heard answer.

“Have you received the prisoner from Belvaille?” Mr. Boshe asked.

“Yes, Mister.”

“And he is alive?”

There was a pause.

“Yes, Mister,” the voice answered unsteadily.

“Good, then proceed with the interrogation.”

“Uh…”

“Is there a problem?” Mr. Boshe barked.

“No, Mister! We will proceed at once. Over.”

If they could get that torso to talk, I would be in trouble. But I wasn’t concerned.

“I guess I’ll be leaving now,” I said, smiling.

“What’s your hurry? I’d like to talk a bit more,” Mr. Boshe said innocently, which made me nervous.

“I have to Overseer…other things,” I said weakly.

“How is the segregation of the port coming?” he asked.

“Oh, fine. I heard the walls are almost up. We have it set that you all get one third of the berths, and in cases of emergency, you can expand that to half.”

“Excellent. How would we go about signaling this emergency take-over?”

I didn’t like those particular words but I understood what he meant.

“I don’t know. Just ask me?”

“Ask? What forms do we need to fill out?”

“No forms. Just call me up. We’ll get it done.”

“We can’t just ring you personally on the tele to take over the port. What protocols are in place?”

“Mr. Boshe, with all due respect, we aren’t the PCC. You’ve made it very clear we aren’t citizens. We don’t use forms. That’s not how Belvaille does things.”

He seemed to think this over for a long period of time.

“Agreed. But we need a back-up contact.”

“MTB. He’s our police…guy. Policeman. Detective. Police Overseer,” I said, adjusting the title at each cringe of Mr. Boshe’s face.

“Very well. I have other news: there is a ship coming to this System. They made contact several days ago,” Mr. Boshe said.

“Lots of ships come through here,” I shrugged.

“It is a Boranjame vessel.”

I felt a chill.

“Do we need to evacuate?”

“It is not a world-ship, no. Though the occupant is royalty.”

“I met a prince of the Boranjame once.”

“I know. That is why I want you to be our Master Overseer of Non-Post-Colmarian Confederation Colmarian Confederation and Boranjame Relations.”

“I’d grow old saying that title. No, thank you. I mean, thanks, but I’ve had enough contact with the Boranjame for a few lifetimes,” I replied.

“I’m aware of your interactions. This is not officially PCC territory. We can make diplomatic contact, but we can’t lawfully hold long-term negotiations here,” he said. “I wish that we could.”

“Change the law,” I said.

He looked at me like I was a moron. I was asking the head of this PCC Navy fleet, who couldn’t even get an official job title or salary, to go lobby the most backwards legislature in all of creation, and ask it to do something useful.

“That is unlikely,” he replied.

“I don’t want the job.”

“It wouldn’t be a ‘job.’ We couldn’t pay you.”

I huffed, unable to respond to that. This guy sucked at sugar coating things.

“What’s the Boranjame’s name?”

“The Marquor of Lunacy, Gax, the Unfathomable and Disreputable.”

“What? Why would I want to even be in the same System as a Boranjame with that title, let alone hop over and say hi? I had dealings with Boranjame who had nice names and I’m still traumatized from the experience.”

“He asked for you specifically.”

The thousand counter-arguments I had piling up in my throat all crashed into my teeth. If a Boranjame wanted to see me, I didn’t have much choice.

“Okay. But…I’ll need some things.”
