My idea was to request some valuable stuff. They couldn’t give me a salary, but I could still rob them.

“Of course. We have the means of reimbursing you for expenses. But I’m afraid you will have to use our rather complicated procedures.”

He walked to the side of his small office and hefted some papers. When he turned around he was struggling to carry a stack about two feet high.

“You’ll need to fill these out,” he said, grunting.





CHAPTER 21




I came back home from my strange visit with Mr. Boshe, and to my great relief, my door opened as soon as I approached.

“Good evening, sir. Would you care for some dinner?” Cliston asked.

I hugged his hard metal body.

“Thank the void! It’s good to have you back, Cliston. I’ve been eating your frozen dinners, frozen.”

“I left you instructions on how to prepare them, sir.”

I walked inside and Cliston deftly removed my bathrobe.

“I’m not a physicist, Cliston. I didn’t understand your directions.”

“May I ask why you were wearing this out-of-doors, sir?” Cliston asked, holding up my damning robes.

“Um. Was I wearing that? I was just stretching my legs,” I lied.

“I received an invoice from Zzzho for transporting you to the Armage. Sir.”

“Oh, that. That too. But we can talk later. How about some food?” I smiled.

Cliston paused a moment and hurried off to the kitchen.



I was eating a typical Cliston twelve course meal and practically rolling my face in the various dishes. I had almost forgotten how good food could taste.

The textures, the flavor contrasts, the rollercoaster of sensations. Even temperature extremes were weapons that Cliston used to batter my taste buds into submission.

“I give this dinner six stars, Cliston. It was excellent. It’s good to have you back. How was your vacation?” I asked.

“Thank you, sir. Five stars is the maximum, and I must admit that three of the courses were only in the 90th percentile of competency. Your oven needs some minor repairs. As for my vacation…here is your post-meal aperitif,” he said, handing me a strong liqueur.

I sipped at it, my brain pleasantly food-addled.

“I have some more information on the hack against the Dredel Led. I was given some coordinates on where it is originating,” Cliston said, handing me a slip with lots of numbers on it.

“Good. That should help. I’ll get with Delovoa and pinpoint where this is.”

“I’m afraid I also have some bad news, sir.”

“Oh?”

“The Dredel Led, my species, is contemplating declaring war on the Post-Colmarian Confederation Colmarian Confederation.”

“Why? Are they really that mad about the hacking?”

“I can’t say for certain; the vote has been postponed for the moment. But there is a strong push toward full-scale invasion.”

“What would you possibly gain from that? Not even I want to live in this empire.”

“I agree, sir. As far as I can tell, there would be no benefit at all.”

“So what’s the point?”

“There appears to be a sizeable faction within our species who wants war. I’m not sure if it is connected to the hacking.”

“Do you have like a Parliament or King or Big Robot Guy you can talk to?”

“We are a pure democracy, sir. One mind, one vote.”

“One mind? Like a colony or something?”

“No, sir. Every self-aware Dredel Led consciousness is allotted one vote.”

“That’s a weird way of saying it. Why not just one vote per robot?”

“Because there are some Dredel Led with multiple consciousness. It’s only fair they should receive multiple votes.”

“So how many consciousnesses…es do you have?” I asked, curious.

“Just one. The vast majority have one.”

I paused to reflect on this and drink some more.

“If you all declare war, what happens to my fifty-million-credit contract?”

“It is extremely unlikely we would be paid in the event of hostilities,” he said.

“I guess Belvaille will be safe. We aren’t part of the PCC. I’m not even a citizen.”

“I’m afraid if war broke out, the Dredel Led would not recognize the odd peculiarities of this individual space station. They would only recognize that this area is of strategic value, sir.”

“That blows. I can’t vote in the PCC and I’m still going to get attacked? Wait, are you being a traitor by talking to me?”

“Nothing has been decided yet. Those of us who are opposed have blocked the decision temporarily. I was hoping you might come and visit Thaxmultaerea.”

“Me? I can’t stand in the corner and…do whatever you did. I got a ship, but it would take me months, at least, to reach Dredel Led territory.”

“There is a way for organic life to visit our realm. It isn’t done often, but Colmarians have visited in the past.”

“Is it safe?” I asked.

Cliston paused.

“I am not certain. A mutant Ontakian has never visited Thaxmultaerea.”

“So I might fry myself to what purpose?”

“I have notified my people you may be coming.”

“What would they care about me?”

“You are a former Super Class glocken player who made it to the Championship, sir.”

The Dredel Led were diehard glocken fanatics. They couldn’t play the game but they loved it to no end. Cliston had been our team’s general manager and he really knew what he was doing.

“So what do I do, sign autographs?”

“No, sir. You need to try and convince my people not to declare war on the PCC.”





CHAPTER 22




I decided to work on my catastrophes one at a time. The closest one to fruition was the Dredel Led about to use my non-empire as a punching bag.

Cliston knew how to make the device that would let me travel to Thaxmultaerea, but he couldn’t build it. Despite his many, many abilities, and robotic design, Cliston wasn’t technical.

I visited my friendly, upstairs neighbor, Delovoa.

“100,000 credits to build the device,” Delovoa said.

“Mr. Delovoa, I believe the design isn’t very complicated,” Cliston offered.

“How about free?” I replied.

“No way, it will still take me some weeks to build. If you let me come with you, I’ll charge half,” Delovoa said.

“I’m afraid that only Hank is invited,” Cliston said delicately. “While accessing Thaxmultaerea is not difficult, entry is impossible without permission. I will have to accompany him.”

“Yeah, I was doing some research on it and apparently the Colmarian Confederation had a similar…hive mind…some thousands of years ago,” Delovoa said. “But we’re not robots. All those species and brains, anyone who connected went insane. 75,000 credits.”

“I will point out that you tend to make a lot of enemies—because you’re slightly psychotic.”

“I prefer the term, ‘atypical awesome.’”

“Most crazy people do. I make enemies as well,” I said.

“Because you’re ignorant,” he snorted.

“I was going to say, because I let you live in my building for no rent.”

“So what? The city is empty. I could commandeer a forty-story mega-mansion, spray it down with liquid nitrogen, and ice skate until my new legs fell off. Your building is practically the smallest one in the city.”

“In any case, the only reason people haven’t dragged you out of here and hung you up by your stolen neck is because they would have to get past me.”

“And you’re super fat.”

“It’s my girth, my guns, and my reputation that keeps you safe.”

“But I build you a lot of things in return,” he said.

“Like the Thaxmultaerea interface device?” Cliston asked.

“Alright, I’ll do it. But in exchange I want Cliston to cook me a meal. A fancy brunch. With seafood and fruit and champagne and cheese and little embroidered napkins that are too small for your face.”

I looked to Cliston for confirmation.

“Is there any other kind of brunch?” he asked.

“What about our contract with the Dredel Led? I want my 3% of fifty million.”

“2%. If we go to war, they’re not going to pay us. But here, they gave Cliston this. They said it was the position of where the hack was coming from.”

Delovoa looked at the coordinates.

“This is Belvaille.”

“You sure?”

“Of course. I know where the city is located.”

“Well, where in the city is the transmission?”

“It doesn’t say, it’s only a broad estimate.”

“Do you think Garm was lying and it’s the Navy telescopes?” I asked.

“You know her better than I do. Would she lie about it?”

“If she thought it was in her best interest to lie, yes, she would.”





CHAPTER 23




Delovoa needed a few weeks to build the device that would let me visit the Dredel Led capital. He had to go hunting for parts in the city.

I figured I would try and find the hack. If I got fifty million credits, I wouldn’t care if we were at war or not. I could hire my own private army complete with shiny boots and fuzzy hats.

The first thing to do was find out what ships were docked here when the hack took place. I was especially interested in any Navy vessels.

I called in one of my favors from MTB and got the port visitation logs from the dates the hacks occurred.

No one ship was here continuously for the duration, so that kind of ruled them out.

“You need to help me, Cliston,” I said.

“I do help you, sir. I’m your butler.”

“No, I need to scan the city using Delovoa’s machine. We’ll go up on the latticework and try and find where the hacking is coming from.”

“That is hardly an expertise of mine, sir.”

“I’m not so expert at it neither. But if I fall off the roof of the city, I won’t need a butler anymore and you won’t get paid.”

“Should I pack you a lunch?” he asked.

“Cliston,” I said, exasperated. “Of course you should.”



Walking along the latticework was not my idea of a good time. It wasn’t even my idea of a bad time. It was pretty terrifying being at the edge of space.

The walkways along the latticework were tiny, with only half-railings all the way around.

I spent my time clutching that railing and not looking down. Cliston spent his time carrying most of the equipment and wiping off my clothes when they got dirty.

It was about four hours of this nonsense when we finally picked up a signal that matched, as far as I could tell, the one that had hacked the Dredel Led.

“The wavy lines are the same,” I said. “That’s got to be it.”

“Where is it coming from?” Cliston asked.

“It must be right below us.”

We were out in the south side of the city. We had been about ready to go home and had set up the machine one last time. I hadn’t expected anything to be out here, but there it was.

We were on the other side of the city away from the telescopes, so there went that theory.

“This is about ten blocks north of The Club and, what, fifteen blocks south of City Hall,” I said.

“What is here?” Cliston asked.

“Nothing. Nothing that I know of. Let’s go down and check.”

“Would you care to eat first, sir?”

“Not up here. Just my luck I’ll burp and fall off this damn platform. We’ll eat when we get down.”

Crawling around thousands of feet in the air had left me quite hungry.

We found an abandoned building near the elevator to the latticework, and Cliston set up a tablecloth and dishes and I had a nice lunch.

From there, it was about a twenty-block walk to reach the point where we had detected the signal. I wasn’t sure if we could scan it on the ground level and the machines were packed up anyway.

But it turned out we didn’t need a lot of help finding the exact location.

There was a small, round-domed building, with a half-dozen PCC guards standing outside. It was the only building within sight that was occupied.

“So, it’s the Navy,” I said, annoyed. “That’s that. I’m not going to attack a bunch of guards on my home city. Mr. Boshe would send down a battalion to arrest me.”

“Sir, might I suggest a stake-out?”

“What for, Cliston? Do you think they’ll suddenly lie down and go to sleep?”

“You may yet find a way to get past them.”

“Okay, but I’ll need more food. A lot more. Don’t tell Najosa, though.”

I sat myself in a third-story window to watch the building. Cliston went home to return the scanning equipment and to cook me some dinner.

At exactly 2pm, the six guards traded places with six other guards.

They walked around at high attention until midnight and then…they left.

“What the hell?”

I sat staring out at the empty street for four hours, wondering what was going on. Then the original guards returned at four in the morning.

“They’ve got two 10-hour shifts!” I exclaimed to the empty room.

Too bad there were twenty-four hours in a day.

It was a perfect example of the backwards PCC. They had that stupid building guarded for five-sixths of the day and the other four hours it was completely abandoned.

Cliston returned to deliver me an early morning snack.

“They leave at night,” I said. “All we have to do is wait until midnight.”

“We, sir? I am happy to assist you with meals and such, but I am not skilled in gunplay or any form of combat.”

“It’s time you started earning your 15%, Cliston. I can’t check that building by myself, it’s pretty large and I only got a few hours to find the hacking equipment and destroy it.”

“Very well, sir.”

At 12:10, with the street empty, we snuck over to the building.

“This has got to be the strongest door in the city,” I said.

The only way into the building was a security door that looked to be about three feet thick of steel. I’d have an easier time trying to dig through the metal street to get inside.

“There might be some lockpicks in the city who can handle this, but I won’t be able to get them here in time. Not at this hour.”

“Sir, may I try?”

I moved away from the door.

“You’re not that strong, Cliston. I can’t even budge that door.”

“No, sir,” Cliston said. And he began to punch numbers into the security panel.

The light turned green and the heavy bolts unlocked.

“How did you know the combination?” I asked, amazed.

“It was all zeroes. They apparently didn’t bother to change the default code.”

“PCC efficiency yet again.”

I had to put my back into the door simply to open it.

“Should I wait outside, sir?’

“No. I need to find whatever is hacking the Dredel Led. Finding it sooner than later will help us get out of here.”

We split up.

The first room I went to had a lot of forged metal casings. They looked like the covers to generators or transformers. There were an awful lot of them and some were quite large. Seeing them gave me doubts I had the right place.

“Sir, I think I found it,” Cliston said on the tele. “It’s the second door on the left.”

“Coming,” I replied.

The room Cliston indicated was about fifty feet wide and absolutely filled with complicated electronics that reached up to the tall ceiling. There wasn’t a sign that said “Dredel Led Hacking Equipment,” but it looked like it would do the job.

“Oh, but that is here as well,” Cliston said.

There was a robot in the room with Cliston!

It looked kind of like a vehicle. It moved on two sets of magnetic tracks. Like miniature tank treads. But where the cabin or turret should be, it had a boxy armature that was covered in rotating, spinning prongs.

It was like a giant blender.

Cliston was pressed against the wall as far away as he could get from the tank. When I entered the room, the vehicle immediately began beeping and driving toward me.

The prongs were spinning so fast I couldn’t tell if they were sharp or even how long they were.

“Don’t move, sir,” Cliston said.

I stopped and the tank stopped, though its prongs kept spinning.

“What is that thing?” I asked.

“It seems to be a robot defending this area. It isn’t a Dredel Led.”

“Well, that makes it difficult to destroy the room.”

“Yes, sir.”

Because it wasn’t a Dredel Led, it couldn’t think. It simply followed whatever basic defense parameters it was designed to follow.

We stood there quietly for a moment.

“Okay, I’m officially asking for ideas, Cliston.”

“I don’t know, sir. As I said, this isn’t my expertise.”

“I think I’m going to try and grab it by the track. If I can disable its wheels, it won’t be able to come after us. Then we can dismantle this place in our own time.”

I was psyching myself up to lunge at the mini-tank.

“Be careful, sir,” Cliston said.

I absolutely hated when people said stuff like that. I mean, I was standing across from an eight-foot-long tank that was covered with spinning blades. If I was “careful,” I wouldn’t be anywhere near this thing. I’d be sitting back in a well-lit club somewhere drinking vegetable juice and monitoring my prostate gland.

But people didn’t pay you fifty million credits to be careful. People paid you to be reckless. To be dangerous. To be just foolish enough that you didn’t stop and question yourself like I was doing now.

I took a deep breath, made three quick steps toward the tank, and suddenly the armature registered my movement and thrust itself at me.

“Blarrb!” I yelled, as I got picked up, and spat out by the rotating blades.

I shot across the room like I had been fired from a gun. The blades had torn up my clothes, but hadn’t hurt me much that I could tell.

“Sir!” Cliston said.

I looked up in time to see Cliston reach out his arm and the tank swiveled again.

There was a terrible grinding noise and Cliston’s hand was shredded to bits!

“Cliston, stop!”

Cliston froze.

I was staring at his torn limb, expecting it to be gushing blood. But of course, Cliston was made of metal. He wasn’t even gushing oil or windshield wiper fluid.

“Alright, I’m going to come from the other side to pull him away. But you stay still.”

Cliston didn’t respond. He looked like a one-handed statue.

I got to my feet and walked around some machines.

The tank turned and shifted its armature to face me. It beeped a few times, but it was hard to hear over the spinning prongs.

“I’m going to try and grab hold of it. Maybe I can break those blades.”

I didn’t get to take more than two steps when the spinning wheel pushed against me again. I crashed into the wall and began rethinking everything:

Life. Love. My choice of occupations.

Maybe now wasn’t the best time to get philosophical—hey, philosophy. Gun!

From my prone position, I pulled out Gun.

“Do you see that big tank thing?” I asked, pointing.

“Hello. What’s a tank?” he asked.

I put Gun away.

“Sir, are you injured?” Cliston asked.

“No. But I’m having trouble remembering why I’m doing this.”

“Fifty million credits, sir.”

“Oh, yeah. But I don’t know how to fight this thing. I can’t get near it before it flings me away.”

“Maybe you could use a long weapon, sir.”

“I don’t know how to use any weapons other than guns, and Gun doesn’t know what robots are. Wait. I’ll be back, Cliston. Stay there.”

I tried to get up, and the tank mashed me into the ground. I spun around on the floor until I was about to throw up.

“Sir?”

“Give me a minute,” I said weakly.

I managed to crawl out of the room. I think I was moving so slowly that it didn’t register to the tank. Either that or it took pity on me.

I stood up and began running down the hallway. The tank didn’t follow. It was clearly programmed to guard that one room.

I went back to the area I started in. I looked around at all the metal casings. Some were positively huge.

My problem fighting the tank was those prongs got a hold of me and kept spitting me out. But if I could armor myself so that the robot couldn’t grab me, I might be able to damage it.

I found a cylindrical metal casing that was three inches thick of steel. It was about seven feet tall and five feet wide.

I picked it up and put it over my body like a sleeve. I now had armor.

Sort of.

I couldn’t see. And I was trapped inside and couldn’t use my arms or legs. It was like a gigantic trash can covering me. But I was hoping the robot wouldn’t be able to tear through this metal cocoon.

I carried it down the hall and once I was outside the room, I yelled in to Cliston.

“Are you still okay?” I asked.

“I’m not sure that ‘okay’ is the best description, sir. I’ve lost my left hand, and I fear that if I move, I will lose the rest of me.”

“I’m going to come in. But you have to give me directions when I move around.”

“I’ll do my best, sir.”

I could tell he didn’t understand what I was up to, but he’d figure it out soon enough.

I put the cocoon back on me and scooted into the room. I had my hands pressed against the side of the cylinder as I pushed it along.

I heard Cliston say something, but I couldn’t hear inside my protective bubble.

There was a terrible grinding noise and I almost got knocked over. Cliston had been trying to tell me the tank was about to attack.

I leaned into it and found the metal cylinder was not only too thick, but too smooth for the swirling metal prongs to chew through or flick away.

When I stopped moving, the robot stopped grinding at me.

I heard Cliston mumbling something.

“What?” I yelled.

“It seems to be attracted to movement,” Cliston repeated.

“Yeah. But how do I fight it without moving?”

I scooted forward an inch and waited.

Nothing.

Another inch.

“It’s getting ready, sir.”

I stopped and waited.

“Now it is relaxed,” Cliston said. “You are very close to it. Just a few feet away.”

I scooted forward inch after inch, pausing for a minute after each time. Finally, I bumped into the treads of the vehicle.

I got down on my knees inside the cylinder and started slowly raising the side to try and look out.

There were the tank treads.

If this thing only attacked moving objects, I could use that.

I stood up inside the cylinder and slowly raised my arms, causing my metal cocoon to rise from the floor.

“Careful, sir. It’s about to attack.”

I stopped and waited.

“Okay,” Cliston said.

I raised it some more. Then I bent my knees, bent my back, bent my arms. In one quick motion, I rose up and flung the metal cylinder off of me and to the side.

It was so cumbersome it only went about four feet away and I was left standing there defenseless—and motionless.

The robot didn’t attack me and instead pressed its armature full of spinning blades against the fallen, rolling cylinder.

I then leapt forward and landed with both feet on the tank’s right tread.

The armature quickly shifted to me and I once again got flung away like a rubber ball.

But unlike before, the tank could not follow me. I had not only bent the tread, but one of the guide wheels that the tread rolled on.

When the tank tried to move, it could only spin in a circle. Its right side was paralyzed.

“Well. That was totally easy,” I said from my upside-down position against the far wall.

“Can I move now, sir?”

“Let me smash its other side just to be sure,” I said.

I got up and walked over, staying well out of reach of its armature. When I got to its left side, I jumped on it as I had before.

Now it couldn’t move at all.

“I think we’re safe, Cliston. Is your arm going to be fine?”

“I don’t know, sir. I’ve never lost a hand before.”

I took a good look around the room and Cliston had been right that this was clearly the place. There were all kinds of towering antennas and what looked like communications gear everywhere. Much of it was switched on, with glowing control screens and humming electronics.

“We are running out of time, sir. We only have an hour until the guards return for their next shift.”

He was right. I hadn’t thought it took me that long to get beat up by a robot, but we had been in here for a while.

“Hey,” I said, seeing something familiar.

“What, sir?”

“That generator. It’s the same kind that I helped the Ontakians steal from that PCC military installation,” I said.

“Do you know how to disable it?” Cliston asked.

“No. I didn’t pay attention when they took it apart. The problem is I don’t remember if the guy said it was nuclear.”

“Why would that matter, sir?”

“I don’t want to be standing five feet away when I blow up a nuclear generator.”

“Can you destroy everything else?”

I decided I was very unprepared for this. I didn’t even have any explosives.

“Cliston, stand behind me.”

Cliston did so and I took out Gun again.

“Gun, can you shoot everything in this room that’s in front of me?”

“Hello. No, I can’t do that. There are all kinds of molecules and atoms and quarks flying around. I can’t shoot them, they’re too small.”

“By the void,” I said. “Can you shoot the big stuff? Like anything bigger than you.”

“How big am I?” Gun asked.

“A lot of molecules. Like thousands. No. Billions. Maybe more. I don’t know. What can you see in this room?”

“I see the walls. And you told me that thing over there is a door. Though I can’t say why it’s a door and not a floor.”

“So you know what the floor is?” I asked, hopeful he was learning new words.

“It’s not a door, apparently.”

“Can you see that generator?”

“I’m not sure. What does it generate?”

This would take too long to explain so I holstered Gun.

With just my bare hands I began tearing apart the machinery that was out of reach of the tank. Once I had ripped the machines open, Cliston would then twist the guts and internals with his remaining hand.

We did a fair amount of damage and then I stood by the generator, not sure what to do.



“Hurry, sir,” Cliston called back to me, as we ran out of the building.

You’d think I would be pretty good at stealing power plants at this point, but I was banging into walls and doorways.

We were literally a block away when Cliston looked back and saw the PCC soldiers returning.

They didn’t seem to care that two people were running away from the building they were supposed to be guarding. And that one of us was carrying a generator over his shoulder.





CHAPTER 24




I was worried.

Not that we had destroyed equipment in a PCC building and I had a nuclear power plant sitting in my living room. I didn’t care about that so much. But I worried that Garm might have known the PCC was hacking the Dredel Led and kept it secret from me even after I asked her specifically.

True, it wasn’t the telescopes or the port, which were her main concerns, but Garm knew just about everything that happened on Belvaille. Especially since she was once again Adjunct Overwatch.

The idea that there were guards regularly patrolling a building in the city and she wasn’t aware of it, struck me as unlikely. But if she lied to me about the military being responsible for the hack, how much could I trust her on other issues? Had I truly lost her to the PCC bureaucracy?

The next morning, I was eating breakfast and I asked Cliston:

“So am I a multimillionaire yet?”

Cliston did not seem to be inconvenienced by his loss of a hand. He could probably lose both hands and a foot and still be more capable than me at most things.

“Are you joking, sir? My people couldn’t possibly know you damaged the hacking facility already.”

“Well, go tell them before war breaks out.”

“I need to repair myself first. I may require Delovoa’s assistance.”

“Delovoa is good at replacing body parts. Though I’m not sure about for a Dredel Led. Sorry about your hand, by the way. You were right, I probably shouldn’t have taken you along.”

“It’s okay, sir. I’m glad to have been of some use, however small. It also gave me a greater appreciation and understanding for what your career entails.”

“Yeah, getting injured and breaking equipment is pretty much my job description.”



The port was now officially divided.

The Navy was separated from our side by ten foot walls that we had scavenged from the many empty buildings across the city. The PCC had their own warehouse section, their own roads, their own fuel depots, their own workshops, and their own barracks filled with hundreds of armed soldiers.

I hadn’t negotiated for a barracks, but barracks they had.

It wasn’t just walls that were needed to restart Belvaille’s criminal enterprises. We had to build an entirely new set of skills from scratch.

Each Navy ship had a designated notary and a political scientist or two. They even met on Belvaille now and then to debate new laws passed by Parliament. Because as freighters and transports entered Belvaille, they were scanned by the Navy to determine if they were operating “legally.” With hundreds of thousands of conflicting laws and literally trillions of individual planetary codes, it was not easy to come to a conclusion.

Belvaille was now absolutely plugged in to the political system of the PCC. It was all anyone talked about. Who was president of what planet and what bills were inching through government. The reason we cared was because we had to understand, appreciate, and forge every official document that related to shipping.

That was the Skim.

Because of the Source-Destination Doctrine, a planet could only sell a million tons of coal to another planet that was buying a million tons of coal.

So trade worked out fine between giant, diverse, metropolitan planets, but everyone else, such as Belvaille, were starving.

But when those ships docked at Belvaille for fuel, crews, or rest and relaxation, we could institute the Skim. We would forge documents for a small portion of their cargo and siphon that off.

2% is what we figured we could steal. The Navy ships all had the original manifests—we couldn’t change those, as they were sent from the planet itself. But no one was going to go out and count a million tons of coal. Even if the ship had weights and measurements, 2% was within the standard deviation.

But we had to have crooked shippers, crooked pilots, crooked receivers. That was a lot of people to bribe. We even might need some crooked Navy if they started poking around, but that was risky.

We needed flawless forgeries. Forms, stamps, seals, and every type of paper and ink the galaxy could conjure up. We had to know all the rules so we could break them. You couldn’t just fake it and send a transport out for seven months with bad paperwork only to have it confiscated, fined, and the crew imprisoned. No one would take that risk.

All these cutthroats and gang bosses, who could barely write their own names, were now concerned with some planet’s convoluted political system 5,000 light years away.

Also, thankfully, I had a real job again.

I wasn’t holding my breath about getting paid my million bazillion credits from the Dredel Led—it still seemed too good to be true.

So I needed to work.



“Hi,” I said, smiling brightly. “I’m the Master Overseer of Belvaille and Transport Crew Relations.”

The three crew chiefs I was speaking to looked bad. Spaceship crews always looked bad. Even with all the modern amenities on long haul transports, it was just a matter of time before you stopped shaving, slowed down on your bathing, let your clothes become a mess, and overall stopped caring. There was no one to impress in deep space and the constant sea of blackness eventually took its toll on even the most fastidious of people.

It was a universal truth that the longer you were in space, the worse you looked.

These guys looked like they had been exploring a jungle for five years, crawling on their faces. They were grimy and smelly and hairy. They stared at me blankly.

“Huh?” one of them asked.

I could tell he was the captain because of his uniform.

I shook his hand vigorously.

“Welcome. Hank’s the name. It’s my job to show you around and make you at home on Belvaille while you’re here,” I said.

“We’re just refueling,” another guy said. He had a twisted face and seemed immediately suspicious of me.

“Sure. Sure. But you can also stretch your legs and have something to eat.”

“We’re not paying you for anything, buddy,” the sour man continued.

“It’s free! This is what Belvaille does. We’re a hospitality space station.”

“Since when?” the third man asked. He seemed receptive but confused.

“Oh, some time now.”

“I was here five years ago and I can’t say it was very hospital,” the captain fumbled.

“Got to keep up with the demand,” I explained. “But look, it’s free. I’m paid by the city. You don’t have to come. This is all optional,” I explained in my sweetest voice.

I admit I was a hard sell in this role. I was huge. Ugly. And despite my best Cliston wardrobe, I looked more like a professional glocken player than a welcoming ambassador.

Still, the captain agreed and the other crew members fell in behind.

I learned the grumpy man was the first mate and the third man was the head engineer.

I took them to Roll Bungalow’s. It not only had a small bar, but it was a hotel and brothel. Also, I was trying to patch up my relationship with Roll. No use having enemies if I could help it.

That’s one thing that people often don’t get in this business. They take every little thing personally and keep adding more and more names to their hit list. If you live long enough, that list will become so comprehensive the only name not on it will be yours.

About the only person I really had a gripe with was Xandale and that was because of his numerous attempts on my life. It’s hard to forgive a guy for leaving you floating in space.

As we sat at a booth drinking, I could see all the eyes in the bar watching me.

This was the first real attempt at a Skim since we put up the walls and agreed how we would do things. If I ruined this, it meant that all those tens of thousands of hours studying obscure laws and buying forgery equipment and making deals with traders could be in jeopardy. Which would also mean my life was worth considerably less.

“Man, I can’t believe how long you guys stay in space. Must be hard,” I said.

They simply drank their drinks. Except the first mate, who clearly didn’t trust me, and sat with his arms folded, looking like he just swallowed a turd.

“How long were you travelling?” I asked.

“None of your business,” the first mate said.

“From Omni-Baldego. About sixteen months,” the engineer answered.

The captain was staring openly at Roll Bungalow’s prostitutes.

“Hey, Marileent, come on over,” I said.

I had requested ahead of time that the men and women pass back and forth while we were here. Not only did it make the place seem lively, but I could gauge the crew’s interest.

The woman in question was a salty oldtimer. Big, buxom, thick. She could break a man, in a good way—and probably in a bad way, if you crossed her.

“Hey, Hank. Who are your friends?” she asked.

“Captain Reddo-Rait of the Moxbailer 3,” he said, offering his hand.

That was all Marileent needed. She gently pulled on his hand so he stood up, and he followed her out of the bar.

The crew watched their captain leave.

“So, either of you guys have family?” I asked.

“No,” the engineer said.

The first mate was still watching where his captain had gone with consternation.

“Yeah, me neither,” I replied. “Never had the time or inclination.”

I had a bunch of borrowed baby pictures I would have shown them if they said they did have family.

“You guys do a great job. A hard job. Probably aren’t paid enough—not that I’m insulting you. I’m just saying, it’s always the people at the top who make money, never the ones who actually do the work,” I said.

“Yeah,” the engineer replied.

“Look, what is it you want?” the first mate asked. “If you’re really the chief of public relations, I expect to get my kneecaps broken any minute.”

I sighed. My “cover,” as it were, was blown. Should I just backpedal or push on?

“You’re right,” I said. “I’m here to ask you a question: have you ever thought about diverting some of the goods you’re shipping?”

“You’ll get hung for breaking the Source-Destination Doctrine,” the first mate said, standing up.

I noticed people in the bar were whispering. They were watching my lack of progress carefully.

“Hold on. Hold on. I don’t mean go fly your ship to some ass-water of a city and unload. No, you’ll still make your delivery. What if your payload was like 1 or 2% light? No one would ever know.”

“We would know,” the first mate said.

“You’re hauling Chodo fragment tubing. A whole mess of it. You couldn’t count them all if you spent twenty years doing it.”

“You seen all those warships out there?” the first mate said, pointing up to the ceiling. “They have all our records. If we try and fly past them after breaking the Doctrine they’ll blow us to hell.”

“Sit down, Ollo,” the engineer said, curious what I was offering.

But I had to convince the first mate. He was the sticking point and could end it all.

“I know the warships. I live here. I’ve been on the battlecruiser more times than I care to admit. You have been scanned coming into the System. They may scan you again when you leave. If they do, they’ll just see a bunch of tubes, same as when you came in. The scans aren’t accurate enough to detect a few percent drop in cargo.”

“This is a setup,” the first mate said.

I motioned to the bartender for him to turn on the music. I then stood up and put my back to the crowd, blocking the crew from view.

The music came on, loud.

“No one can hear us. No one can see us. This isn’t a trap. This is how you make yourselves rich.”

The men were cautious and Ollo, the first mate, sat down. Not because I had won, but because the booth obscured him from view and he was smart enough to know that he couldn’t push past me.

I motioned to a courier who had been sitting to the side waiting for my signal. He walked over and handed me two things and then left.

The crew must have felt like the only ones in the bar who didn’t know what was going on.

“This is your hotel key. Your room is upstairs. If you want companionship, just ask. This…is a briefcase. Open it when you’re in your room.”

“What’s in it?” the first mate asked, so quiet I could barely hear him over the music.

“Money.”

“How much?” the engineer whispered.

“Count it and find out. If you don’t want it, just give it back tomorrow, and all this is forgotten,” I said. “If you like what you see, simply stay on Belvaille a few extra days—for repairs, or whatever—and give us access to unload. We’ll take 1,423 crates of tubing, and then you will be on your way. That’s it.”

“What if we keep the money and leave?” the first mate asked.

I shrugged.

“You have to have trust in this business or it won’t work. I have to trust you,” I replied.

I didn’t trust him, of course. And I didn’t have to.

In reality, as of now, Belvaille was a supremely dangerous city. Sure, we couldn’t kill Navy soldiers, but these guys weren’t soldiers. They were just a transport crew. There were thousands of crew members on layover here as we spoke. No one would care if one of these guys died. The ship owner, the sender, the receiver, no one would care unless the merchandise was stopped.

If they tried to steal the money they’d be dead within minutes.

There were 400,000 credits in the briefcase. That was a lot of cash. It was certainly more money than they made in a year and probably more like five years. Half a decade’s salary was tough to ignore. Hell, I would only make fifty grand if they agreed to the Skim.

Before they came into this System we knew what they were shipping by checking manifests. We already had a buyer lined up. We had priced the forged papers. We factored in the bribe. We knew my cut. We had the salaries of everyone who was helping out—all thirty or so people. And we had priced the transport and crew who would ultimately leave Belvaille with forged documents, and 2% of the cargo we took from the original ship.

Even accounting for all those expenses, there was about a 350% profit to be made. All because of the stupid Source-Destination Doctrine.

A small city three Portal hops away needed Chodo fragment tubing, but couldn’t afford a planet’s worth. Belvaille could provide it. Assuming these guys took the money.

I didn’t give them time to think or argue. I simply walked away, leaving them with the cash.

The next day, no one offered to return the money. But the crew did open their cargo hold and look the other way while heavy lifters unloaded crate after crate.

Belvaille’s first official Skim was a resounding success.





CHAPTER 25




“Is it supposed to be this tight?” I asked, sitting in my most comfortable living room chair with a giant metal contraption wrapped around my head.

“It was supposed to fit normal Colmarians, not freaks like you,” Delovoa said, adjusting the straps.

It had been a little over a week since we destroyed the hacking equipment. Delovoa had not only foraged enough materials to build the strange device sitting on my head, but he had repaired Cliston’s hand. The hand seemed to be the more complex of the two devices and we had to enlist the services of several machinists to help out.

I paid for the hand since I felt it was my fault for asking Cliston to come along on a mission. The total cost in parts and services was around 90,000 credits. Building a Dredel Led was not cheap.

“So how does this work?” I asked Cliston.

“The device will allow your consciousness to travel to the Dredel Led capital of Thaxmultaerea. I will be with you and communicate normally via talking,” he said.

“Uh, why would you mention that? How else would you be talking?”

“Because Dredel Led do not speak as Colmarians do. Not in Thaxmultaerea. Right now, you just asked me a question and I answered. In Thaxmultaerea, citizens will be able to access your memories and opinions and search for what they want. They won’t have to wait for your answers, or listen to you talk about glocken or complain that your knees hurt, they can search instantly for what they are seeking.”

“They can read my mind? I’m not going! I have a lot of secrets.”

“No one cares about your pathetic life, Hank,” Delovoa said. He was still jealous he couldn’t come along with us.

“They will care quite a bit about Hank. He is the first non-Dredel Led to be allowed inside in more than a century. And he has life experiences that my people will find fascinating.”

“But some of it’s private,” I complained.

“The way the machinery works is that your mind will hide those things you don’t wish to share. You don’t have to worry about that, sir.”

“Well, I am worried.”

“But you don’t have to be,” he repeated.

“Alright. I guess a bunch of robots are going to find out my sexual fantasies,” I said.

Cliston hooked into my brain-blaster machine and then:

“Blah!” I yelled, and fell out of my chair.

“I’m sorry, sir. The interface can be quite jarring for Colmarians, I’m told.”

“I’m not a Colmarian. I’m an Ontakian. I think my eyeballs melted,” I said, patting at my face.

“Let’s try it again, sir. Delovoa, could you please change the transfer markdown to 343?”

“Um,” Delovoa said, fiddling with the back of my head after I regained my seat.

“He doesn’t even know how this works,” I said.

“I know how it works!” Delovoa lied. “343. Done.”

“Ready, sir?”



I felt needles punching through my brain and I saw my life flash before my eyes but at a million frames a second. It stopped now and then on certain images. I saw Zeti, the dead Boranjame. I saw Garm smiling. I saw a big plate of sausages. Another plate of sausages. Thad Elon as Naked Guy. A fist punching my face. A keg of beer. My uncle Frank. The assassin 19-10. Maris-To, the former Arch Minister. Cliston.

“We’re here, sir.”

Thaxmultaerea wasn’t especially impressive visually, but you didn’t interact with it visually. It was all lights and colors and symbols.

Somehow, I just knew how to do stuff here.

I tried to look at myself but I couldn’t. There was no me because I already knew me. Thaxmultaerea didn’t show you things that were self-evident.

Cliston was…there. I tried to “see” what he looked like and I was instantly aware of every facet of his physical appearance. Like the bottom of his feet were a tacky rubberized self-regenerating surface.

I knew the square area of his fingertips and how much weight and speed they could sustain on certain surfaces without slippage—assuming he walked on his hands.

I had to almost physically lurch away to stop learning about Cliston’s fingers.

“Ugh, what’s going on?” I asked.

“You accessed me. You need to control your thoughts or you might become overwhelmed with information, sir.”

“Information I don’t need. So your hands ‘sweat?’ I never noticed.”

“It is a mild adhesive to facilitate gripping items, sir.”

“Oh.”

I had inadvertently downloaded all kinds of information about Cliston that was looping through my head. Just absurd stuff, like the dimensions of his head and the luminosity of his eyes.

“How do I not do that again?” I said, feeling I was already out of brain power.

“You need to think of specific questions. Very specific, sir.”

I absently wondered how many manuals and guides Cliston wrote over the years, feeling that would be a safe question.

“Blaugh!” I threw up.

“Sir. Are you alright? We can leave if you’re ill.”

“So…many…pamphlets! Cliston, you’re insane. How can anyone write so much? I didn’t even read them, just the titles,” I said.

There must have been tens of thousands of them. My brain was in turmoil trying to sort the data. It was too much, too fast. My head ached.

“Just relax, sir. And perhaps stop trying to communicate using the interface. Just talk. I can hear you. I’m standing right next to you at home.”

“Okay. So where are we going?”

“I want to show you around and let you explain to my people that war is unnecessary.”

“How will I do that?” I asked, exasperated.

“Just make yourself available. They will read your experiences and learn about your culture.”

“What culture? Will they know I’m an…you-know-what?”

“No one can hear you talking except me, sir. And Delovoa, who is still in the room.”

“Will they know I’m an Ontakian?”

“They won’t know anything you wish to keep personal. Let’s go to the Main Concordance.”

I saw Cliston move ahead, but I was having trouble following.

“Sir?”

“Yeah?”

“Are you eating?”

“I’m hungry. I just threw up my lunch. It’s only some snacks.”

“It is interfering with the system.”

“Fine.”

We travelled easily and almost instantaneously. We reached an area and there were hundreds of other…blobby things. I couldn’t describe them visually, but I only had to kind of “look” at one, and I immediately knew what it was.

That one was a Dredel Led specializing in accounting, currently operating in System Anoxia. That other one was a Dredel Led manufacturer who created designs for the hyposondric encypto-phlage—whatever the hell that was.

Anything I wanted to know, I instantly knew—as long as someone here knew it and was sharing it.

Of course, not everyone had the same opinions. I wondered where this “city,” Thaxmultaerea, had come from, and a dozen different theories, all extremely complex, shot into my brain. It was like I had always known them. They were part of my memory.

But Cliston was right, if I thought about too broad a topic, I would be flooded with data. My little brain spat it out or ignored it. I ended up with just weird fragments of information, disconnected.

Like I wondered about what the Dredel Led’s conception of Thad Elon was and I ended up with a huge string of numbers, a recipe for heat treating titanium alloy, and a picture of a donkey. A library of answers had been fed to me from thousands of sources, but I couldn’t handle it.

Cliston must have been “talking.” Or somehow getting everyone’s attention, because the other entities were forming around us.

And then I felt myself being accessed.

It was almost like exhaling. I just breathed out my thoughts.

Then another Dredel Led accessed me.

And another.

And five hundred more.

I was about to rip the device off my head, because it was no longer like breathing, it was like hyperventilating or having a stroke.

But then it all stopped abruptly.

I’m not sure how many people were around me. Thousands.

They all represented an individual Dredel Led. As Cliston said, one mind, one vote.

However, something approached that was truly staggering. It was a person, but it was huge. That alone was strange because all the Dredel Led were the same size in Thaxmultaerea. I tried to make a cursory examination of the giant shape, just to see what it was, and that was the last thing I remember.





CHAPTER 26




When I woke up, I was in bed.

“Cake, sir?”

“What?”

“Would you like some cake? It is vanilla with custard cream,” Cliston said.

“How long was I out? Yeah, I’ll have some cake.”

“Two days, I’m afraid.”

I suddenly remembered.

“That thing in Thaxmultaerea. Was that a Dredel Led?”

“It was, as far as I could tell. However, unlike most Dredel Led, it had one million minds. I’ve never heard of such a thing, let alone encountered one. He is named Kai’Un.”

“Kai’Un with a million brains. Is that what knocked me unconscious?” I asked.

“I suspect so, sir. You tried to examine him and the sheer amount of communication may have been too much to sustain. I was only able to partially evaluate him.”

“What did you find out?”

“He seems to be the cause for war. All his minds are pushing to declare hostilities against the PCC.”

“Why would you create such a Dredel Led?”

“I’m not sure that we did. If he was created, it must have been long, long ago and we lost all record of him. We keep track of all the Dredel Led that have been born in the last, approximately, 50,000 years.”

“How are you all born?” I asked, eating my cake.

“We are created by consensus. The members of Thaxmultaerea vote on what units we require. But more than that, we actually create new Dredel Led using our collective consciousness. As far as I know, it should be impossible to create an entity with a million minds.”

“Is he a fake?”

“He can’t be, sir. He exhibits all the capabilities of a true Dredel Led. Again, he may have been created in our primordial past.”

“What about the hacking? Could he be the one hacking?”

“He would have no need to hack into Thaxmultaerea since he can enter at will. Only a non-Dredel Led would have use for hacking into the city.”

“You’re saying you make Dredel Led by consensus? So at some point they voted that they needed you: a butler?”

“I was not created a butler. I chose this career, sir.”

“Oh. Why?”

“Because I enjoy it, sir.”

“Well…good. Did it work having me there? I know a bunch of your people were zapping my brain.”

“You were quite popular and I believe a benefit to the cause of peace. But with Kai’Un there, he casts so many votes that it will overwhelm the consensus.”

“So we’re at war?”

“No, not yet. We managed to put off the vote for a while longer. But I fear it is only a matter of time.”

“I thought there was like twenty million Dredel Led. Can’t they overrule the big brain?”

“Ten million, sir. But at any time there are only thousands in Thaxmultaerea to vote.”

“Voter apathy,” I said profoundly, licking frosting from my fingers.

“Actually, it is part of our design, we take turns voting. We do not need a full population to ballot because a properly-allocated sample correlates to the predilections of the whole.”

“I suppose,” I said, not understanding.

“But I’m afraid you will need to return to Thaxmultaerea, sir.”

“Why? Will that big thing be there?”

“He may. But you need to try and convince more people to vote against war. Kai’Un is convincing others that the PCC is a threat to the galaxy and should be destroyed.”

“Well, he’s probably right,” I said.

“But that would include destroying Belvaille and you, sir.”

“Okay, I’ll go back. I’m a bit tired, though.”

“You do not need to go immediately, sir. The voting occurs in cycles.”

“Did anything else happen while I was unconscious?”

“I asked about the hacking to see if we could get paid for completing the contract. They said there hadn’t been any disruptions in a week, but they want to see if it lasts longer. They aren’t convinced that we really halted the operation permanently.”

“How long do we have to wait? Years?”

“I don’t know, sir. Long enough for them to be certain. Also, while you were sleeping, a Mr. Boshe called.”

“He did?” I said, sitting upright at attention.

“Yes, sir, the message was confusing, but he said a Gax was in the System and you should attend him at your earliest opportunity.”

“Gax the Boranjame?”

“He didn’t say.”

“I suppose there can’t be too many Gaxes that Mr. Boshe would be interested in. I need some really special clothes, Cliston.”





CHAPTER 27




“This isn’t what I meant, Cliston.”

I was wearing another dress. This one was covered in some kind of…

“What are these things?” I asked, gently tugging at one of the shiny circles that were embroidered all over the outfit.

“Sequins, sir.”

“I look like a disco ball. A big, fat, feminine disco ball.”

“I couldn’t take the sequins off because it would leave holes all over the dress.”

“Hah! It is a dress. It’s not a battle skirt of macho-ness,” I said.

“When I was in Thaxmultaerea, I got the designs for new outfits. That outfit is the best approximation I can make of an Oolari-Hosh ceremonial robe. The Oolari are one of the few races that trade with the Boranjame,” Cliston explained.

“Does it look cool? I want to look cool. I don’t feel cool.”

“Gax may recognize and appreciate it.”

“If I know the Boranjame, he’ll tell me I’m going to die, and everyone is going to die, and we’re all going to have a lousy time doing it. He won’t care what I’m wearing.”

“You don’t want to offend him though.”

“No, you’re right. A pissed-off celestial being is not something I need in my life. Tele Zzzho that I’ll be ready for a spaceship ride to my Boranjame friend in thirty minutes.”



The thing about the Boranjame was that they could predict the future.

There were a lot of other things, I guess. They’d never lost a war; they were the most powerful species, by far, in the galaxy; they flew around in ships the size of planets and demolished solar systems; they were responsible for creating all life, Chaos, Order, mutants, flowers, and sunshine. Stuff like that.

But most of that didn’t matter to me—I didn’t even like flowers. But when the Boranjame spoke to me, they often told me the future. Just enough to be annoying.

And it was never a good future.

They didn’t say I was going to win the lottery or get laid tomorrow night. It wasn’t even bad things like my toilet was going to overflow or I was going to get athlete’s foot. No, the Boranjame told me that all life would be destroyed in the galaxy and there was nothing I could do about it.

Picture the gloomiest, most depressing person you know, put them in the form of a demigod, and have them talk down at you for a while. That was my interactions with the Boranjame in a nutshell.

I didn’t want to talk to this new guy whose title was apparently Lord Jerkface of I Hate You.

I suppose on one hand he couldn’t tell me anything that would upset me. It had already been prophesized that I was going to die alone in space, betrayed and forgotten. What could be worse? I would lose my car keys before all that?



I hadn’t seen a lot of spaceships in my time, despite living in space all my life. But The Marquor of Lunacy, Gax, the Unfathomable and Disreputable, had a weird ship.

Approaching it, it was instantly distinguishable.

It was covered in lights. A lot of lights.

It looked like a star.

It was even spherical from what I could tell. Though as we got close I had to shield my eyes.

The only thing that made it clear we weren’t travelling toward a quasar, was the fact the ship wasn’t all that large. It was about the same size as a super freighter.

They made us wait outside to dock for a good hour. Maybe to shine enough light in my eyes that I’d be blind when I finally met Gax.

His electricity bill must be outrageous.



When I finally got inside, it was no less bright. Every panel of every surface was covered in lights. I literally had to walk with my hand over my eyes and allow the Po servants to guide me around.

The Po were the slave species of the Boranjame. They looked like big mops without a handle and moved erratically and at tremendous speeds. When I had played glocken, a Po had been our best scorer despite being the lightest player on the team.

I was worried about how large Gax would be. Zeti, the previous Boranjame that had been in this System, was about fifteen stories tall. But I had once met a prince of the Boranjame who was square miles in dimension.

I entered the room that housed Gax and it was cavernous. Maybe five hundred feet tall and bright as a pulsar. And in the center was a ten-foot black crystal.

Gax was the strangest of the Boranjame I had seen. Instead of his crystal disks flying and moving and rotating around each other like enormous, disembodied clock components, Gax throbbed.

He was more or less spherical, and different segments would bulge and subside. Sometimes the upper right quadrant, sometimes the lower left, sometimes several pieces at once.

Because he was so black, it was hard to see if Gax was one piece or was moving fragments. In this ship of lights, he was the darkest thing here and reminded me of a black hole.

“Hi,” I said, waving.

The Po began setting up electronics for Gax to communicate. Boranjame didn’t have mouths or eyes or anything that could be construed as non-crystalline.

A tele screen was set up, which is the first time I had ever seen them use one. Maybe he wanted to show me an instructional video.

On the screen appeared the following lines:



Dreams of falling, touch the ground.

Hitch a ride home walking the pavement.

Dressed in clothes fit for a vagabond.

Is the Herald of Zeti an imitation?



Huh? Did he just make a crack at my wardrobe?

“Yeah. So I used to work with Zeti,” I said to the screen. “Before he died. Was killed.”



Dice roll of five, fork tines of four.

In times past my people created.

The nature of order and chaos are heinous.

Headphones earjacked into your subconscious.



I stood looking at the words for a moment and then took a picture with my tele.

“Zeti said you guys made Chaos. Thad Elon. And that caused life. Or something. Is that what you mean?”



Our corrections are forbidden.

Be careful who you trust.

The Agglatar were sent to ride the tides.

A breach inside your borders.



“What’s Agg-la-tar?” I asked. I could see why this guy was called an unfathomable lunatic.



Second First, End of Last.

We are all mutants, affronts to Nature.

Take my hand, see the truth.



“You don’t have a hand,” I said weakly.

I looked at the screen for confirmation, but the words faded.

“You want me to touch you? Last time I touched a Boranjame, I got fried.”

No response.

I hiked up my dress with resolve and marched forward. It took quite a while to reach Gax in the center of the room. Long enough that I felt very vulnerable. I suppose I could just run away. A pulsing sphere couldn’t exactly catch me. But his Po could, and they would haul me back.

For some reason, I expected to feel heat when I drew near Gax, but the temperature didn’t change. Still, I was sweating nervously.

The Boranjame hovered a few feet off the ground.

This close, I could see he was indeed solid and not made up of smaller plates or disks. But he still throbbed and bulged, kind of like a big black heart, except not contracting, only expanding, then returning to regular size.

I took in a big gulp of air and reached out and placed my hand gingerly on one of the least-throbbing areas.

Nothing at first.

Then I felt a sensation.

Gax wasn’t black at all. He was…every…shade…of…every…color!

It was too much for me.

I woke up later and about a dozen or so Po were dragging me away by my arms. I was on the floor and nowhere near Gax’s chamber. Had I passed out? Had he knocked me unconscious?

I just remember seeing an endless series of geometric shapes of all sizes, configurations, and colors. I couldn’t look away.

“Thanks, guys. I think I’m okay,” I said.

The Po released me and even helped me to my feet.

I checked my tele and I still had the nonsense words from Gax, the Crazy and Unhelpful.

As the Po escorted me back to my ship, I called Cliston.

“Yes, sir?” he answered.

“The dress didn’t help!”





CHAPTER 28




I was back home with Cliston and Delovoa going over the words of wisdom from Gax.

“I can’t understand why the Boranjame keep contacting you,” Delovoa said.

“Me neither. Maybe they should use you instead,” I answered.

“There’s so much I could do if I knew the future,” Delovoa sighed.

“What are the Agglatar?” Cliston asked, referring to one of the lines Gax mentioned.

“I’m showing you everything he said. I haven’t got a clue.”

“Was there a lot of smoke and lightning?” Delovoa joked.

“Sort of. He made me touch him—”

“Those kinky crystals,” he teased.

“I saw a bunch of weird lights and then got knocked unconscious.”

“You’ve been unconscious a lot lately. You should be careful not to get any brain damage,” Delovoa cautioned.

“Next time someone is going to blast me, I’ll tell them to take it easy.”

“What do you plan on doing, sir?” Cliston asked.

“Nothing! I can’t make sense of this poetry. It doesn’t even rhyme.”

“Yeah, it’s not very good,” Delovoa agreed.

“Ignoring the Boranjame seems…dangerous, sir.”

I sent Cliston a tele copy of what I recorded.

“There. I’m officially making you the Master Overseer of Boranjame-to-Hank Translations. If you can figure out what he’s saying, I’ll take a look.”

“‘Headphones earjacked’ seems to be a reference to your trip to Thaxmultaerea,” Cliston said.

“So? What’s it mean? Should I not visit the Dredel Led again?”

“As your doctor, I recommend not getting knocked unconscious for at least a few weeks. You don’t have a lot of brain cells to start with,” Delovoa said.

“What exactly did you see when you touched Gax?” Cliston asked.

“I don’t know. It was like infinite rainbows. But not as pretty as that sounds. I’m not sure what it was. Maybe it’s what the Boranjame really look like.”

“Might Garm have information about these poetry lines?” Cliston asked.

“I think Garm lied to us about the Navy hacking the Dredel Led. The Boranjame said be careful who I trust.”

“Do you believe that could be in reference to Garm?” Cliston asked.

“I don’t trust a lot of people. You, Cliston. Delovoa—sort of.”

“Thanks,” Delovoa replied.

“MTB. And Garm. But Garm is PCC now. I think we should assume she’s working for the enemy,” I said.





CHAPTER 29




I said I would help Najosa on the next raid of the PCC, even though I didn’t know what it would be.

She kind of tricked me by asking right before we had sex. I not only agreed to attack the PCC, but I said I would go on a diet, brush my teeth more frequently, and I think I promised to donate my organs to science.

Basically, I hadn’t had sex in a long while.

The Society of the Free and Easy were now hanging out in my home, looking like the angry Ontakians they were.

“What are we doing?” I asked Najosa, sure I had misheard her.

“We need a telescope,” she repeated.

“A telescope? One of Belvaille’s telescopes? Those things are mounted on the outside of the space station.”

“No one will expect us to hijack one,” Meltar, one of the technicians, said.

“Yeah, because they’re installations. They’re massive.”

“Let the technicians worry about that,” Najosa said.

“Why do you need one?” I asked.

“It’s for a job. We need money just like everyone else. But we’re hoping we can use it to coordinate activities with the rest of the Ontakians spread throughout the galaxy,” Najosa explained.

I wish I’d had this conversation before I agreed to help.



Sometime later, we were all dressed in fully-enclosed spacesuits, dutifully walking to one of the airlocks on the northwest side of the station.

It took an hour to put on all this gear even with Cliston’s expert assistance.

We clanged along, carrying huge tool chests, torches, collapsible poles, and all manner of equipment.

Exiting the airlock wasn’t easy. We had to go one at a time and it took ten minutes per person. Also, you had to have someone operating the machinery on the inside. That meant someone had to remain here.

I volunteered. At this point we had been waiting around for over an hour and I was hungry again.

But Najosa selected Alase, who was a woman bred to work with hazardous waste. Her expertise wouldn’t be required on this mission. But if we did run into a trash monster outside Belvaille, we were doomed.

Space.

It never ceased to scare the piss out of me.

Here we were, outside of the protective shield of Belvaille, at the literal edge of the space station, with nothing in between us and the void except for a hand railing that I could feel wobbling as I held onto it.

Man was simply not meant to be in space.

We had radios installed in our helmets to let us communicate with each other without cumbersome teles.

“We shouldn’t be out here,” I said.

“Why?” an architect named Shella asked.

I flapped my arms around.

“Look! What if we fall?”

“You can’t fall. You don’t weigh anything here. You’d have to jump off and disconnect your harness,” Frank chided.

“Quiet,” Najosa said. “There they are.”

They were indeed. The telescopes were absolutely enormous. They looked like half-planets squatting on the side of Belvaille, taking a crap over the edge of the station.

“How are we ever going to move that?” I asked.

It was almost beyond comprehension. They must have used a team of a thousand people and all kinds of space machinery to put those in place and now there were six of us.

“Why would we move it?” Nordan, one of the technicians, asked.

“I thought you said you wanted to hijack the telescope.”

“We’re not going to steal it,” Najosa said. “We’re just going to change its protocols so we can operate it.”

“Oh,” I said.

“Did you actually think we were going to pick up and move one of those telescopes?” Shella laughed.

The Society of the Free and Easy were all chuckling at the concept.

“These combat models have a one-track mind,” Meltar said.

“Who is speaking?” a voice suddenly called from my radio. I didn’t recognize the voice.

“Who are you?” Najosa replied.

“I am Captain Vergi-Thal of the Post-Colmarian Confederation Colmarian Confederation destroyer, Avengeance. I repeat, who is speaking?” the man barked.

Holy crap, we’d been talking on a Navy radio frequency—while we made plans to steal a Navy telescope!

“Hi,” I said.

Everyone on the walkway turned around and faced me. Najosa was trying to make military signals with her hands, but I couldn’t read hand-jive. Our face shields were down to protect us from the light and radiation, so I had no idea what she was trying to say.

“Who are you?” the Captain asked.

“Me? I’m Captain…AnkHay of the frigate…Long…Pause.”

There were so many ships out here and a frigate was a small one, so I was hoping he wouldn’t know I was lying. After a moment he replied.

“What are you doing on the emergency frequency, Captain?”

“This…isn’t the emergency frequency any longer.”

“Hello?” a new voice asked. “This is Captain Yolly4 of the cruiser Maridoom. What’s going on?”

Oh, great. It’s a convention.

“I was just saying that a new form came in,” I replied.

I tried to think back to all the various forms and frameworks that came through this System. I knew a lot of it because of my work with the Skim. We had to forge all those documents. But I didn’t know military forms and wracked my brain trying to think of any.

“What form?” Captain Vergi-Thal asked.

“It was a Supplemental Addendum Stroke-77.”

I held my breath. I knew that was a form the military used, but I wasn’t entirely sure what its purpose was. Maybe they didn’t even know. I just recalled the name because it was easy to remember.

“Well? What did it say?” Yolly4 asked.

“It said this frequency isn’t to be used any longer other than for…repair crews.”

“Then what is the new frequency for the ships in Subsector A30?” Vergi-Thal asked.

“Um…”

Najosa flashed her hands at me twice with her fingers spread wide and palms up.

“Twenty,” I said confidently.

“Twenty? There is no such frequency.”

Najosa put up her hands again as if to try and tell me.

“Hand-five. Ten fingers,” I guessed.

“You mean frequency 10F?” Yolly4 asked, confused.

“Exactly. I was reading the form upside down. Sorry.”

“Get your paperwork in order, Captain,” Yolly4 scolded.

“Yes, sir,” I said, and I actually saluted, I was so nervous.

“Avengeance out,” Vergi-Thal said.

“Maridoom out,” Yolly4 added.

“Uh, Long Pause out,” I said.

The radios were silent. We stood there waiting for a warship to fly by and blast us. But nothing came.

Slowly we began to reset the radio frequencies on our headsets.

“Hello?” Najosa tested. “Is anyone on this frequency? Hello?”

After waiting patiently, we figured it was okay to talk.

“That was quick thinking, Hank,” Meltar said.

“What frequency were you trying to tell me, Najosa?” I asked.

“I was trying to tell you to shut up! But Meltar’s right, you did a good job. How did you know all that stuff?” she asked.

“This is PCC territory—sort of. We see all kinds of forms and requisitions come in.”

“Let’s go,” Najosa said. “We’re behind schedule.”

I couldn’t believe she still wanted to do this after we just blabbed to the Navy.

“This seems really risky with what happened,” I said.

“No offense, Hank, but you’re very cautious for a combat model Ontakian,” Shella noted.

“That’s because he was raised as a Colmarian,” Frank said.

If everyone hadn’t had a bad opinion of me before, they did now.





CHAPTER 30




Just getting to the nearest telescope was difficult.

We were in zero gravity, had lots of equipment to move, and were a good half-mile away from the target.

By the time we had reached it and moved all the gear, I was exhausted.

The technicians had a few hours of work ahead of them, so I could relax. I simply stopped and floated. I didn’t need a seat or bed or anything. Zero gravity was actually pretty comfortable if you wanted to take a nap.

I woke up and saw Frank next to me.

“You know, you definitely look like your father,” Frank said over the radio.

“How can you tell? I have the screens down in my helmet.”

“I noticed it a while ago. I hadn’t said anything because…well, it doesn’t really matter. I mean, my brother wasn’t nearly as big as you are, or as ugly, but I can see him there. It’s kind of like he had a bad reaction to some fish, his face puffed up, and he turned into you. His hair wasn’t white, either.”

“So I’m an old, ugly version of my father?”

I didn’t say it to complain. I was simply speaking it out loud to hear the words. I remembered so little of my dad. I remember him being big and adult, probably because my memories of him were mostly as a child. But at this point, I was hundreds of years older than my father when he died.

“Do you know what killed him? You never told me,” I asked.

“They didn’t tell me either. He was still working for the Colmarian Navy. He didn’t have much of a choice, I guess. I think he died in the line of duty. They sent me his things as his last known relative.”

“I was his son. Why didn’t they send it to me?”

“No one knew where you were. You changed your name and moved to the exact edge of the known galaxy. It would have taken the blundering Navy a thousand years to find you. It took me a while to track you down, but then I mailed you his belongings.”

“He only had his uniform and pistol?” It seemed like a paltry life’s savings.

“No, he had a lot more stuff. But I couldn’t exactly mail you his house and I was worried if I sent cash, it would get stolen.”

“You kept everything?” I asked, shocked yet again at my uncle’s dishonesty.

“No, I sold it. I didn’t want his stupid house.”

“How much was it all worth?”

“Who can remember? That was centuries ago.”

I sat there staring over the edge of Belvaille. Nothing but empty space ahead of me. I wondered what would happen if I threw Frank overboard. He’d probably land on a ship and sell Belvaille and its telescopes to some poor sap.

“And you don’t know what happened to my mother?”

“She never escaped Ontak. At least that I know of. Maybe she did and got lost. Or couldn’t find you guys,” Frank said casually.

“What? You said she was dead.”

“She’s probably dead. She wanted to stay and fight. She did stay and fight. But it was a war. Anything can happen in a war. The planet Ontak was destroyed. Anyone on it was killed. I just don’t know she was on it with certainty.”

My mouth was open so wide I was in danger of shattering my space helmet.

“Can you think of anything else you’ve told me that is completely untrue? This is pretty important stuff to me, you know. Are you even certain my father is dead?”

“How could I be ‘certain?’ It’s not like I was standing next to him,” Frank replied coolly.

“You didn’t see his casket or bury him or anything?” I asked.

“No.”

“So you’re taking the word of the Colmarian Navy, which you’ve said countless times was incompetent, to prove my father is dead?”

“If he was alive he would have showed up at his house. Or visited me. Or visited you. Same with your mother. If they were alive they could have found us.”

“Maybe they got captured or injured. Maybe they’ve been rotting in some jail cell all this time,” I yelled.

“Maybe they’re space king and queen ruling a solar system and when we find them, they’ll shower us with credits and happy times. I hate to break your fantasy, but if someone disappears in a war—on a planet that is literally blown to dust—there’s a good chance they’re dead. More than good. Your father was also fighting in that war. On the opposite side. I don’t know when he died or how. Maybe it was on a mission. Maybe it was in the war. Maybe it was in another war. But he was an Ontakian in the Colmarian Navy. You can bet they didn’t have a lot of concern for his safety.”

I floated there, stewing. My uncle was so flippant. I didn’t want to rage at Frank. He was my only connection to my past. If I pissed him off enough, he’d stop answering me altogether. Or at least charge me for the information.

“We’re going to need your help in a minute,” Najosa said. “There is some heavy lifting to do.”

A bit later, I was standing underneath ten thousand tons of plastic, steel, concrete, and ultra-density electrical cables. I had to lift a giant metal box into place on the telescope hundreds of feet up. Despite the weightlessness of space, things still had mass, and it was an effort to move the box in a controlled fashion.

“It’s going to take me six hours to push this thing into place,” I complained.

“Shut up, you aren’t alone here,” Frank said. He was helping with the pulley.

“Be quiet,” Najosa said sternly. “What’s that?”

“What’s what?” I said, turning around. “Oof!”

My back hit the railing and I flew through it at high speed. I looked down and saw a small rocket was latched onto my chest.

“I’m dead,” I said.

I didn’t say it sadly. Or “aww, damn”-ingly. I just stated a fact.

Not only was I now a quarter mile from the city, but this rocket was going to explode any second and shred my spacesuit. I couldn’t even try and pull it off because it looked like it was clamped onto me and I would tear my suit for sure if I yanked at it.

I just tried to put my legs down so they wouldn’t get burnt in the exhaust, and I waited to die.

“Hank!” My uncle cried into my radio.

I paused, certain he was going to ask me for some money before I exploded.

I was so far away the Ontakians were little specks. But I had a great view of the city.

You know, Belvaille was beautiful. I don’t use that word enough—and it’s a little late now. But with the shield, with the glowing stabilizers, with those damn telescopes, it was magnificent.

It was a good sight to experience before I died.

The rocket attached to me sputtered and cut off.

I held my breath.

I coughed.

I then gingerly pried the clasping talons open.

Did it not have a warhead? Was it on a timer? I took the missile in my hand and did my best to throw it.

It drifted slowly away.

My throw had unbalanced me and now I was spinning.

I wished to hell I had a jetpack.

I struggled to swim away from the rocket.

And then I started moving.

“Huh?”

I looked all around to see if a ship had gotten me or another rocket, but there was nothing.

Wait a minute. I could fly! My new mutation!

I concentrated, stretched out my arms, straightened my legs, and…I was flying. I had never thought of trying to fly in space. It’s not like I went spacewalking very often. I was too heavy to fly in normal gravity, but out here was a different story.

I grit my teeth and began pushing myself back toward Belvaille. I could tell I was going faster and faster. I had no idea my speed, but after some time it must have been a hundred miles an hour.

I was closing in on Belvaille and I completely forgotten about the stupid rocket. Who cared, I was flying!

“Hello everyone,” I said on the radio.

“Hank, you’re okay?” Najosa asked. “Is that you out there?”

“Yup. Be back in a second. Let me just zip on over.”

“Holy hell, he’s flying,” Frank said.

I could see the figures on the railing. The telescope looked even bigger from this angle.

“Hank, you need to slow down,” Najosa said.

Right. I had been accelerating this whole time.

Let’s see…stopping. How do I do that?

I tried to think backwards, but that didn’t work. I tried to think slowing down, but that didn’t work either. I never had to slow down before, I simply touched the ground.

The telescope was looming and I was headed right for it.

“Oh, crap.”

Bong!





CHAPTER 31




“Cliston, I flew,” I said weakly.

“Flew where?” Garm replied.

I was in the hospital—my usual room—and Cliston was standing by my bedside. Garm, with her ridiculous uniform covered in medals, was farther away. I hadn’t seen her when I regained consciousness.

“It’s a figure of speech,” I said.

“Does it translate into, ‘someone punched a hole in one of our telescopes after taking it offline?’”

“What’s a telescope?” I answered groggily.

“You know I’m in charge of the Navy on this station, right?” she asked.

“You want to recruit me? I’m in the hospital. How long was I unconscious, Cliston?”

“Um, hard to say, sir. I’d rather not trouble you with such details now. The point is you need to recover.”

“Cliston is smarter than you,” Garm said. “He doesn’t want to say how long you were out because it would correspond with when someone attacked our telescopes.”

“How would someone even ‘attack’ a Navy telescope?”

“You tell me,” she said. “You know, it’s a good thing Delovoa put security systems out there or you could have done some real damage.”

“Delovoa?” I asked, surprised. “Come on, do you think I know how to take a telescope offline?”

“Maybe not. But maybe it was your new friends.”

“I don’t have any friends,” I said.

“I know you’ve been working with those Ontakians. If you want to attack ships and bases and, hell, Mr. Boshe’s battlecruiser with your bare knuckles, it’s not my concern. But if you start messing with the property here, they’re going to make me stop you.”

“Property here? Like the building you used to hack the Dredel Led?” I accused.

“I told you I don’t know anything about your Dredel Led,” she replied. “Are you saying the Navy is responsible for it?”

“Of course not,” I said, not wanting to implicate myself further. “I have nothing to gain from messing with your stupid telescopes.”

“Maybe you wanted to impress your lady friend, Najosa.”

“Ah-hah! You’re jealous. That’s what this is.”

“What?” Garm asked, annoyed.

“I’ve finally found someone who likes me for who I am—because of who I am—and you can’t stand to see me happy. Get over it, Garm, we broke up centuries ago.”

“Why in Thad Elon’s Black Eyes do you think I care about who or what you’re dating? You dated my granddaughter and I didn’t care. I care more about who Cliston dates than I do about you.”

“Technically, I don’t date,” Cliston interrupted.

“You care enough to be keeping tabs on us, apparently,” I said.

“Not close enough, since I missed the part when you attacked PCC Navy property.”

“Oh, give me a break. Like I’m going to go punching holes in a telescope for no reason.”

“How did you end up in the hospital?” she asked.

“I…forget. I was hit on the head. I’ve been unconscious a lot lately.”

“He has, miss,” Cliston confirmed.

“I’m surprised his brain still functions. Look, I’m going to make this really, really clear: don’t screw with anything under my authority. You’re still a friend, a bad one, but a friend. This is a friendly visit—and one I didn’t have to make.”
