“If this is friendly, I’d hate to see you rude. I’m not a PCC citizen. You can’t boss me around,” I said.

“You’re right, you’re not.”

She drew her pistol and pointed it at me.

“No,” I said.

Blam!

She shot me right between the eyes.

“Ow, dammit! My head is already killing me,” I yelled.

“Do you know how much paperwork I would have to fill out if you were a PCC citizen and I just shot you? But you’re nothing. I can flush all of you out into space and I could probably file it under City Renovations. Don’t cross me, Hank. You know better than that.”

Garm turned on her heel and stormed off, her medals jingling.

“You have been unconscious for five days, sir. We really need to get back to Thaxmultaerea.”

“I don’t know if my brain can take it this soon,” I said.

“If you want to prevent a war, it will have to.”





CHAPTER 32




“Did you deploy some security systems around the telescopes?” I asked Delovoa, as he was strapping the Thaxmultaerea device to my head.

“No,” he said.

“There were some rockets that fired at us.”

“Oh, wow, I totally forgot about those. I must have put them out there two hundred years ago. What did they do?”

“Almost killed me,” I said.

“Why were you messing with the telescopes?” he asked.

“Never mind. Were the rockets supposed to have warheads on them?”

“Why would I put explosives around something I was trying to protect? Think for a minute.”

“So Cliston, what am I supposed to do? I can’t have all those Dredel Led reading my thoughts, it will knock me unconscious again.”

“I have arranged it, sir. Normally, people don’t give speeches. Talking isn’t really done in Thaxmultaerea, exactly. But an exception has been made in your case because this is war, and as a Colmarian, you will have greater insights into why your people should be saved.”

“Great, an Ontakian, pretending to be a Colmarian, trying to stick up for the PCC,” Delovoa teased.

“Yeah, but what do I say? What do the Dredel Led need to hear?”

“Right now, the biggest memory of the Colmarians is the civil war. That war hurt the Dredel Led a great deal, as it spilled into our territory. Now the PCC is approaching the size of the original Colmarian Confederation and many are worried what will happen. They need to understand why this empire is worth sparing,” Cliston explained.

“I don’t know if it is,” I muttered.

“You must banish those thoughts when you enter Thaxmultaerea. I can’t stress the importance of this enough.”

“Okay, Cliston. Trust me, I care. I don’t want to be at war. Especially on behalf of a civilization I’m technically not even a citizen of.”



I was in the virtual city of Thaxmultaerea getting ready to give a speech. I couldn’t bring notes, because physical things didn’t exist here.

“Are you ready, sir?” Cliston asked next to me.

“No. But what choice do I have?”

“I’m afraid very little if we want to avoid conflict.”

There was no podium or stage or anything like that. I was just in a sea of Dredel Led…indicators, all around me. I didn’t understand any of it.

Of course, they couldn’t “hear” me talk. Only Cliston and Delovoa could, because they were in the same room with me, back in the real world.

So I was going to talk and Cliston was going to translate. The surrounding Dredel Led would then read his memory to find out what I said.

“When I talk, how long will it take for everyone to hear me? Should I pause and wait?” I asked.

“It will take nanoseconds, sir. You don’t need to wait. It will actually be counterproductive, as most of my people are not used to getting information so slowly: sentences at a time.”

I tried to psych myself up for the most important speech of my life.

“Ahem. So the Colmarian people are not perfect,” I began.

“That is already clear, sir,” Cliston stated next to me. “You need to explain their good points.”

“Do you want to give the speech? Because I’m totally okay with that.”

“My voice does not carry the same weight as someone who is actually part of the empire we may be attacking.”

“Fine. The Colmarian people are perfect—in a way,” I said.

“Sir, I already passed along your statement that Colmarians aren’t perfect. You have contradicted yourself in two sentences.”

“This is why I don’t give speeches.”

“You need to hurry up, people have things to do and this is very irregular as it is.”

“Okay. Um. No race is perfect. Not even the Dredel Led.”

I waited to see if Cliston was going to chastise me for that comment.

“Speak faster, sir.”

“Right. But even though we aren’t perfect, we try. Not to be perfect, but to be better.”

I knew the Dredel Led were huge glocken fans. I thought some sports analogies would help.

“It’s like a Twist in glocken. They are trying to hit a moving target, in-stride, avoiding the Killers, avoiding the line defense, their hands right, not overextending, not straining their muscles, not tripping on their own feet. There has probably never been a perfect throw in the history of glocken. But there have been millions and millions of great throws.”

Having imagined the game, I kind of forgot my point.

“So Colmarians are kind of like that. Trying to make a good throw—even a great throw—but knowing that it won’t ever be perfect.”

“You’re doing well, sir.”

“We’re also like stars,” I said, then I paused.

“No, not really like stars. Maybe the whole Colmarian Confederation, excuse me, Post-Colmarian Confederation Colmarian Confederation is like a star. It’s chaotic. It’s so big that there are black spots on one side and eruptions on the other. But it’s all the same star. It all fits together to…create life. Yeah, stars create life, right? Or make it less dark at least?”

“Maybe don’t talk about astronomy so much, sir.”

“What I do for a career, now that I’ve quit glocken, is I fix things. I fix people. I fix interactions with people. We can’t plug into each other and read minds like the Dredel Led. We have misunderstandings all the time. We get in fights and disagreements. I work to make those better. I work to make us perfect. Okay, not perfect, but I try to make that one problem go away. I partner with a Dredel Led and I would be lost without him.”

“Thank you, sir. But downplay your reliance on me or that will reinforce the weakness of the Colmarians.”

“Uh…so that’s just one example. But the Colmarians are constantly striving to improve themselves. If you judge us today, you will see an imperfect society. But I can only guess that in the future we will be better. Don’t invade us just because we aren’t as good as we could possibly be. Someone told me that without change there is stagnation and ultimately death for a species. We are changing. And for the better. Thank you.”

“Very good, sir!” Cliston commended.

I waited for applause or comments or anything, but that wasn’t how Thaxmultaerea worked. It was just the Dredel Led lights floating there.

“Sir, several important Dredel Led wish to read your thoughts,” Cliston said.

“No, I’ll pass out again.”

“It will only be three minds. They may learn some valuable insights that will help our cause,” he said.

“Well…I guess. But tell them to be gentle.”

I felt my mind racing for a second and it was done. Wasn’t so bad.

“Did they like what they saw?” I asked.

“I don’t know, sir. They are sharing the information with others. Let me see what they learned.”

A moment later, Cliston spoke urgently.

“You need to leave immediately, sir.”

“What? Why?”

“They are passing along your extreme prejudice against the PCC. You should have kept those thoughts hidden!”

“I don’t know how to hide my thoughts. This is all new to me.”

“Delovoa, disconnect Hank immediately.”





CHAPTER 33




That was it. We were at war.

My fat mouth, er, fat brain, had convinced the Dredel Led that the Post-Colmarian Confederation Colmarian Confederation was a dangerous entity that had to be restrained—or destroyed.

Cliston had stayed behind for the vote. He voted for peace, so did a lot of his people, but the big million-mind robot, Kai’Un, had cast all his votes for war and that was that.

“It seems unfair that one Dredel Led should have that much influence,” I said, when Cliston had returned.

“It isn’t just one being. It is a million that happen to be housed in one body, sir,” Cliston said.

“Yeah, but if they’re all individuals, they sure voted the same. That seems convenient.”

“It does, actually. I tried to scan Kai’Un to learn about his history, views, or anything at all. But his entire consciousness was marked private. I couldn’t even determine his primary function or location, which are almost never made secret.”

“As long as that guy was voting for war, it didn’t matter what I said, probably.”

“It may have mattered, sir. You could have held off the vote until more reasonable people came to Thaxmultaerea. We all take turns voting.”

“Way to cheer me up, Cliston.”

“I have three brains and I don’t get any votes,” Delovoa complained.

“I also found out that the hacking has continued and it is still coming from Belvaille,” Cliston said.

“We know the Navy is probably doing it. We wrecked that one building but it’s not like they have a shortage of hi-tech facilities. They probably just moved it. Still, with war on I can’t see any reason to want to stop the hacking. It might give the PCC a chance in the upcoming battles.”

“And even if you were to somehow permanently disable the intrusions, the contract for your services is null and void because of the war,” Cliston said.

“Eh, what was I going to do with fifty million credits anyway? Live out all my dreams?” I said bitterly.

“Part of that money was mine, you know,” Delovoa said.

“Then go talk to the Dredel Led and get them to cancel the war. Maybe you’ll do better than I did. But in other news, what should we do, besides getting a bunch of trashcans and making ourselves Dredel Led disguises in case we’re invaded?”

“I am not eager to suggest it, but I believe you should notify Mr. Boshe of the voting results,” Cliston said. “The military needs to be put on alert.”

“If they’re hacking the Dredel Led, I’m sure they already know. But yeah, call up the Navy and make me an appointment.”

“If I were you, I wouldn’t tell him it’s your fault we’re at war,” Delovoa added.



I flew out to meet Mr. Boshe the next day aboard his ship, the Armage.

He stood there with his arms crossed, scowling. He still wasn’t wearing shoes, which I thought was incredibly odd.

“I was about to have you arrested and brought in,” Mr. Boshe said, when I reached his tiny office.

“What for?”

“Didn’t it occur to you that I would want to be debriefed on your encounter with The Marquor of Lunacy, Gax, the Unfathomable and Disreputable?”

“Oh, sorry. He didn’t say much.”

“What did he say?”

“Not much, just some poetry. It didn’t even rhyme.”

Mr. Boshe looked exasperated.

“What exactly did he say? This is a Boranjame of some rank, sitting in this System, in his own ship. Anything he says is important.”

I took out my tele and flipped through to the words I had recorded. I handed it to Mr. Boshe.

“Forward this to me,” he said.

“I need your tele number.”

“Sorry, you can’t send it directly. Regulations dictate it must go to the Vice Major of Media Affairs and Communications.”

“Of course,” I said, rolling my eyes.

“I don’t make the rules. But is there anything else you want to tell me, Master Overseer of Navy and Belvaille Relations?” Mr. Boshe asked.

I sniffed in annoyance at the title.

“Yeah. The Dredel Led might be—are definitely—going to declare war on the PCC.”

“Yes, their ambassador has told us.”

“Oh. Well, that’s it.”

“That’s it? He also said you were in their capital city for the vote.”

“Well, I left right before. It’s not like I voted or anything. One brain, one vote. Dredel Led brain. I’m not Dredel Led,” I stammered.

“The ambassador also said you were partially responsible for the outcome.”

Uh oh.

“That’s probably misdirection on their part. You know, propaganda. It’s not like I’m some super-secret double agent,” I laughed.

He looked at me hard, trying to determine if I was a super-secret double agent.

“The Dredel Led are no match for us. They’re just dumb robots without the freedoms and abilities we have,” Mr. Boshe stated confidently.

I thought about Cliston. He certainly wasn’t dumb and without freedom.

“I’m not entirely sure I agree with that, sir.”

“I’m no sir!”

“Oh, yeah. Mister. But I know some pretty talented Dredel Led.”

“Are you a collaborator?” he asked.

“What? No. I mean, how can I be a collaborator? I’m not even part of the PCC.”

“The Dredel Led can’t do what we can. Be certain of that. They are just two-dimensional copies of organic life,” he said defiantly, as if convincing me were somehow important.

I knew I shouldn’t prompt him, but I couldn’t help it since he sounded so sure.

“Like what can we do that they can’t?”

“They can’t feel. Or dream. Or hope,” he said. “Or fart.”

I did a double-take at the last part. Was he joking?

“Are you sure they can’t fart? I mean, that’s just waste gas, right? Would robot farts be like heat-exhaust or infrared or something?”

“The point is: war is upon us. War with unthinking, unemotional, fake Colmarians. This is the moment our empire has been waiting for! We will finally become united and do away with all these reams and reams of paperwork and bureaucracy.”

“Well…I’m sure you all will do great,” I said. “If you don’t need anything else, I’ll see myself out.”

I turned to leave.

“Hank. Don’t you want to enlist in the war effort?”

I slowly looked back.

“Um. No?”

“I can assure you that the Dredel Led will be coming here. If not first, very soon in their attacks.”

“Why would they come to—oh, the Portals!” I said.

“Yes. If they can destroy these Portals, our empire won’t be an empire. It will be ten thousand little countries unable to reach one another. We could use you, your ship, and your experience in the coming fight.”

“That sounds…really tempting, Mr. Boshe. I promise you I’ll take it under consideration and will let you know if I decide to join up. Just for the record, if I did enlist, would I become a citizen or be allowed to vote?”

Mr. Boshe looked pained.

“No, you wouldn’t officially be in the Navy. You would be a Foreign Legionnaire.”

“Of course,” I said.

“But with war here, I’m confident I’ll be given an active commission in the military—likely as an admiral. And I will endeavor to not only make you a PCC soldier, but I will strive to make everyone in Belvaille full citizens as well.”

“Why? I mean, what makes you think anyone is dying to be a citizen? Especially if they’d be joining an empire that was suddenly under attack.”

“You have only seen the bad side of the PCC. I know that. I know it sounds hard to believe, but war brings out the best in Colmarians. The original Colmarian Confederation was at war for much of its existence and it was the pinnacle of civilization. When we’re under duress, we have to work together. We have to innovate. Don’t think of it as joining the Post-Colmarian Confederation Colmarian Confederation. You’d be joining the new, strengthened, purposeful PCC,” he said.

“Again, I’ll think very hard about it.”





CHAPTER 34




After starting a war and then refusing to fight in it, I felt like I needed a drink.

When I returned from the Armage, Cliston informed me that Xandale, of all people, had been by to see me. At first that made me a bit nervous, but if Xandale wanted to kill me, he wouldn’t have met my butler and given him his calling card.

I decided to hit up The Club. I dropped a tele to Xandale saying he could meet me there.

Fample was the newest coat check at the establishment. His father was Gample and his grandfather was Pample. Or Dample. I couldn’t keep them straight at this point. But needless to say, when you went into The Club, you would be met by an ‘ample’ with a bad attitude.

“Coat check,” he said.

“I don’t have a coat,” I replied, showing off my latest ballroom gown. I had grown somewhat accustomed to the dresses. They were perfect lazing around clothes. The seats didn’t ride up my backside or pinch my groin and no matter how sweaty I got, I could always hike up my skirts to vent out my legs.

The only thing that might have been problematic was dresses usually didn’t have pockets. But I had Cliston sew in all kinds of pouches and buttons. I now had more pockets than I did on a normal set of pants.

“You have to check something,” Fample said.

The Club was always looking for new ways to earn money. The mandatory coat check was just the latest.

I walked a few feet inside the club, reached down, and grabbed hold of a six-foot potted tree, and put it on the coat check counter.

“Here, checking this in,” I said.

“That’s our tree. My cousin picked that out.”

“What’s his name, Splample?”

“Rample.”

“You want me to coat check or not?”

The amples were a small species of Colmarians. He couldn’t lift the potted tree. But they were also frugal beyond belief and the five credits for checking in something was too hard for him to pass up.

“Okay,” he said, and gave me a ticket for the tree.

Inside, the club was more lively than I had seen it in years. The Skim was in full effect and people were busy doing underhanded things.

It was quite a refreshing change from when I used to come in and people would be moping around, half-heartedly gambling or watching sports.

Now it seemed like no one had any time. People were hustling around, talking rapid-fire, and arguing facts and figures and trade routes.

This was the Belvaille of my memories: lots of gainfully-employed scumbags doing scumbag stuff. It warmed my heart.

“What’s with the kilt?” Xandale asked me.

“What’s a kilt?”

“It’s like a skirt for guys.”

“I think this is just a dress. Look,” I said, lifting my hem.

“I don’t want to see your balls,” Xandale recoiled.

“No, my knees are dry.”

“Do you usually sweat from your knees?” he asked.

“I’ve got thick legs. They rub together.”

Xandale shrugged.

“Where are you sitting?” he asked.

“I just got here. I want something to drink,” I said.

“Is it true you visited with Mr. Boshe?”

“Did Cliston tell you that?” I asked. I was a bit surprised. Cliston should keep my personal business personal.

“No, there are a lot of eyeballs in this System. When a ship goes to the Armage, that’s big news.”

“Oh. Yeah, I went.”

“You really are the Master Seer of Navy-Belvaille Relations. I thought that was just a gag.”

“Overseer. It is a gag. I don’t oversee anything.”

“What’s Mr. Boshe like?”

I thought about that as I ordered some beer.

“He’s one of the most powerful people in this System.”

“One of? Who is more powerful?”

“Gax. The Lunatic.”

“I heard about him! So it’s true? We have a new Boranjame in the System?”

“Yeah, and he has his own ship. It looks like a star. You need sunglasses just to get near it.”

“Wow,” Xandale said, clearly impressed.

“So what is it you want?” I asked. I was still not a fan of Xandale, despite him trying to be all friendly.

“Roll Bungalow might have a problem with a Navy official.”

“You can’t kill him,” I said quickly. “That got you into trouble before, remember?”

“No, it’s not some sailor. This is a Protocol Officer on one of the cruisers by the Portals.”

“Oh. So what’s the problem?”

“He’s smart. Too smart for his own good—for everyone’s good. We think he’s going to uncover the Skim.”

“How? Haven’t you all been careful?”

“He’s one of the guys that watches ship traffic inside Belvaille.”

“You mean Portal Depot #4382.”

“Whatever. But he apparently has a photographic memory and knows every document, every shipping manifest, every form. We barely got past him a few times and that was just by luck.”

“Photographic memory. What a terrible person to have in that job. So don’t use that Portal.”

“It’s one of the least guarded Portals and it links to some of our buyers who we’ve already made deals with. It’s not easy to find people willing to buy skimmed products. That’s a life sentence if they’re caught.”

“Well, tell Roll Bungalow that he’s out of business for a while, until things cool off.”

Xandale was not happy with that notion.

“It isn’t just Roll Bungalow. It’s Podiver Vance. Ulteem. Procon Hobb. And a dozen more. Like I said, there are a lot of buyers beyond that Portal.”

“Can’t you just take a different Portal and reach the buyers through some backroads?”

“Hank, that would add months to the travel time. The Skim operates on a budget, you know. Why don’t you just bribe the officer?”

I cringed and reflexively looked around the club to see if anyone overheard.

Bribe a Navy official. Just the thought of it was scary. Walking with a suitcase full of money onto a Navy cruiser, surrounded by thousands of soldiers. It was not something you daydreamed about—it was the stuff nightmares were made of.

“We knew this was going to happen sooner or later, right?” Xandale pressed.

“Yeah, but I was hoping it wouldn’t be me. You go bribe him.”

“You’re the Grand Overseer of Belvaille-Navy Relations. Go relate with him.”

“If this goes sour, he’ll have to be killed. Because if he rats on me, I’m not going to jail for a century. And even barring that, there would be an investigation, and they could uncover the entire Skim.”

“I don’t know any professional assassins, do you?” Xandale asked.

“Yeah, but she’s part of the military now.”





CHAPTER 35




I was thinking about how to bribe a Navy official when I heard some commotion at the entrance to The Club.

There was a lot of talking and then suddenly the whole club went quiet.

Was it a raid? Was the Navy here? Was MTB conducting some police investigation?

I heard one voice. It was deep. It was loud. And it was…asking for donations?

“—influence peace and stability in the galaxy. I am kindly requesting for charitable contributions.”

I laughed.

Other than a prison for the terminally poor, this was probably the worst place in existence to ask for money. These were not the giving kind of people.

I had to see who this nutcase was and why he had such a profound effect on The Club.

I got up from my beer and walked around the corner. Along the way I passed a lot of people with dazed expressions. It was like they were terrified of giving to charity. Maybe they were.

Then I saw him.

An Agglatar.

The Boranjame Gax had mentioned the Agglatar in his poem. Seeing the man, I knew immediately that’s what he was. I don’t know how, exactly. I just knew. When I had touched the Boranjame and been shown the infinite colors, I think something changed in me. Apparently, I now had the ability to recognize Agglatars when I saw them.

The man stood about seven feet tall. He was an immensely muscled Colmarian male. His body was draped with chains, books, manuscripts, and symbols. It was like a library had exploded right in front of him.

To top it off, surrounding his entire form, was an otherworldly blue flame. Not one that cast light or put out heat. It was as if I was imagining it. It was there, but not quite.

He suddenly turned around and faced me, and I immediately understood why the club had gone so quiet.

I felt fear.

Not any kind of rational fear. Not a fear he was going to hurt or kill me. Just fear. I got goosebumps, a damp chill, and the hair on the back of my neck stood up. I wanted to leave immediately, but my feet were rooted in place.

“Contribute to my fund,” the Agglatar said. “I require money to complete my mission.”

I was too scared to reach into my dress pockets for some cash or I would have gladly heaped it on him.

The Agglatar grabbed hold of a nearby cringing man.

“Give me your money, please.”

The man was too terrified to even blink.

Wham!

The Agglatar punched the poor guy in the face and sent him soaring twenty feet!

“Please give me a donation,” he said to another horrified patron.

Wham!

He sent that one flying with just a backhand slap.

This guy had to be the worst fundraiser that ever existed.

I had to get out of here.

I managed to overcome my fear and take the back exit to the stairs. Seeing me move had spurred others to action and they retreated with me.

I was about to barrel out the front door of the club when I heard Fample.

“Hank!”

“You can keep the tree!” I called back to the coat check.

“No, you have to get that guy out of here. He’s going to wreck the club.”

I stopped. People jostled past me and out the front door.

“I have a firm policy regarding scary mutant-people who are capable of slapping someone across the room: I leave them alone,” I said.

“Free food for life!” Fample cried.

“What?” I paused.

“If you can get rid of him, I’ll give you free food for life.”

“My life or yours?”

“Yours.”

Wow. Food costs were some of my biggest expenses. I mostly had Cliston cook for me, but I ate out now and then. And seven plates of sausages were really expensive.

“Free food and drink and you got a deal.”

“Just beer.”

“No. Anything I want. None of your cheap stuff. Top-shelf liquor.”

Fample bit his lip. We heard the thuds of several more people failing to be charitable and subsequently slamming into walls.

“Okay, deal. Get rid of him,” Fample said.

I drew Gun and headed back inside The Club. I took the stairs because I wanted to see if I could sneak in and take a cheap shot at the Agglatar.

“Gun,” I said.

“Hello,” he chirped as usual.

“I’m going to point you at a big guy covered in books and chains. I need you to shoot him.”

“Okay, but it won’t work.”

I hated this. I was about to go into a firefight and I had to argue with my pistol.

“Why won’t it work?”

“Because he’s going to knock me out of your hand first.”

“What? How can you possibly know—”

I had reached the top of the stairs and the Agglatar was standing there. He swiped at Gun, knocking him from my hands and down a flight of stairs.

I stood with my mouth open and got punched in the nose.

It was a brutal hit, but not one that would cause any permanent damage. I had taken a hundred worse shots in professional glocken.

But the deal was, I was scared again.

I don’t know why, but I was just about pissing myself with fright. It was almost like a nightmare. Nightmares aren’t scary when you think about them the next morning, but they still manage to ruin your sleep while they’re happening.

This was just a large guy with documents chained to him. He was probably looking for contributions to build a book depository so he could stop being weighted down with paperbacks. There was no logical reason I should be afraid of him.

I couldn’t think what to do. I was too scared to run, too scared to fight.

“Hey…” I managed to squeak. “Are you an Agglatar?”

“Give me a donation,” he yelled, and he picked me up and threw me across the room.

I crashed into the thick steel wall.

A desperate realization occurred to me: it seemed that after I banged into the wall, I was thinking more clearly. Was it because I wasn’t looking at him or was it because I just had my brain rattled?

I was on the ground and I turned around to see what he was doing. He was shaking a sad little money lender by the neck.

I still felt fear.

It was possible that this unnatural fear came from visually looking at him, or maybe it came from my brain playing tricks on me. Or both.

I closed my eyes, but I was still afraid. If anything, it was worse, because I didn’t know what he was doing. I decided that he was doing something directly to my mind.

With great defiance and even greater stupidity, I stood up, faced the wall in front of me, and I headbutted the surface with tremendous force.

I felt my forehead bleeding and I was momentarily stunned.

“Give me a donation, please!” I heard, followed by the high-pitched scream of someone who was too frugal or frightened to comply.

I turned around and saw him:

“The Babbladar!” I slurred, full of concussion.

I rushed forward and the Agglatar didn’t even put up his fists.

Having just brained myself against a wall, I was not exactly fighting fit. I reached out for him and grabbed him by the face and started mashing his features, as if I could erase him with my hands.

“Free food,” I chanted.

I saw a glowing light and assumed it was just my skull splitting open. But I looked down and saw a white ball of energy in the Agglatar’s hand.

“Huh.”

He pressed the ball to my chest and I rocketed backwards, smashing aside a half-dozen tables like they were made of playing cards.

I sat there staring, waiting for a follow-up attack, but the Agglatar wasn’t interested in me. He was back to picking up the cringing and cowering club patrons and trying to get their tax-deductible donations.

I stood up and then immediately fell down. My equilibrium was slooply.

Slooply. Is that a word?

I guess everything is a word if you say it.

Wow! What a concept.

“Slooply,” I said.

I just invented a word.

I took a deep breath and tried to stand, getting my slooply feet under my slooply torso.

I charged across the club toward the Agglatar and I managed to hit him square, knocking him down. I moved so slowly that he could have easily stepped to the side. But it’s like he was completely unconcerned—or unaware—that he was in a fight.

With the Agglatar on his back, I wasn’t sure what to do next. I knew scrunching his face hadn’t worked very well.

I reached down and grabbed one of the books on his chest and ripped it off its connecting chain.

“Hah!” I said triumphantly.

A glowing ball of energy began to appear in the Agglatar’s hands.

I held the book up in front of me like a force field.

Kercrash!

I was on the other side of the club and my whole body ached.

The Agglatar finally seemed to notice me.

Another ball of light was growing in his hand and I felt I had better move.

I stood upright as the ball of light struck the wall behind me. The explosion catapulted me a good ten feet up and ten feet forward.

My brain was gone. It must have jumped out of my ear, tired of the abuse it was taking. I couldn’t think up old words let alone make new ones.

But I saw the Agglatar forming another glowing ball and I knew that was…bad.

I rolled twice on the ground, used the momentum to get on my feet, and lunged at him.

I grappled the Agglatar in a bear hug and began squeezing as hard as I could.

His arms were pinned to his sides so he couldn’t throw the energy ball at me, but I saw the light from it, nonetheless.

I hazarded a look down. In his hand, the ball of light was enormous. It was now about three feet in diameter. The one that had knocked me across the club had only been about six inches.

And it was still growing.

Gritting my teeth, I continued to squeeze the Agglatar with all my strength. I didn’t have a game plan at this point, I had a severe concussion. I just knew that I had to stop this guy and then I could go to sleep.

Then the ball of light exploded.





CHAPTER 36




“I need to take your statement,” MTB said.

I was in the hospital again. My old familiar room. It even had a placard with my name on it.

The problem with me and medical devices was they couldn’t scan me, so the medical technicians couldn’t ever tell what was wrong. Even if they knew, they couldn’t exactly operate. My skin was too thick.

So what they did was they gave me industrial-sized feeding tubes, put me on a bed that could support my weight, and turned off the lights and waited for me to die or come back to life.

In that regard, I must have been a pretty easy patient. There was no rushing around or ordering special drugs. Just lots of tubes and lots of food.

I had tubes in my mouth, my nose, and…elsewhere. I really wish they wouldn’t put tubes there, but I guess there’s only so much food you can cram down someone’s mouth.

I mumbled something that sounded a lot like, “mabla bub gra,” because that’s exactly what I said.

It took them about an hour to remove all the tubes.

“Wow, I’m like ten pounds lighter,” I said.

“So, 4,990?” Garm asked.

“What are you doing here?” I said, annoyed.

“This is my station.”

“No, it’s not. This is the non-citizens of Belvaille’s station. You just rent the telescopes.”

“Really? How much do we pay in rent?” she asked smugly.

“So my statement. Statement about what?” I asked MTB.

“About the thing you killed at The Club,” MTB almost shouted.

“Oh. He died?”

“Well, there’s conflicting reports on that.” MTB flipped through his tele. “One person said he ‘blew himself up in a shower of incandescent radiance.’”

If I still had a tube in my nose, I would have snorted it out.

“Who said ‘incandescent’? What’s that even mean? Oh! Is slooply a word? If it’s already a word, I’m going to be really upset.”

“Why were you fighting him?” Garm asked.

“Why do you care?”

“Because I need to know what dangers are around Belvaille that could hurt this installation. That guy walked into The Club, managed to subdue everyone there, put you in the hospital, and then disappeared.”

“He didn’t incandescence?”

MTB and Garm both gave me equally impatient looks.

“Is he a mutant?” MTB asked.

“No, he’s—actually he might be. He’s something called an Agglatar.”

“What’s that?” Garm asked.

“The Boranjame told me about him. He said…I can’t remember what he said. It didn’t make sense, anyway. But he’s called an Agglatar. He’s somehow connected to the Boranjame. Which probably means absolutely nothing good can come from him and I’ll keep ending up in the hospital.”

“He was trying to get money from people?” MTB asked.

“Yeah, it was the weirdest thing. He would ask for donations and then punch people in the skull when they wouldn’t pay. And he also made you afraid just by looking at him. Or him just being around.”

“How did you fight him?” Garm asked.

“I smashed my head on the wall and that was enough to forget I was afraid. I don’t recommend it.”

“Is that what that melon is?” MTB said, pointing.

I reached up and felt my forehead and there was a massive bump there.

“Oh, good,” I said. “I was beginning to worry that I was too handsome.”

“So where did this Agglatar come from?” MTB said.

I shrugged.

“Ask the Boranjame.”

“And you don’t know what his purpose was or if he’ll come back?” Garm said.

“If he blew himself up, I sure hope he’s not coming back. As for what he wanted, he seemed to want money. He was just going about it all wrong. Can’t you track what ship he came from? It’s not like a ton of transports will have records of picking up someone like that.”

“So far we got nothing. Appearing at The Club is our first sign of him,” Garm said.

“Alright. I’ll let you get some rest,” MTB said. “If you think of anything else, let me know.”

MTB left and Garm waited until he was gone, which meant she was going to talk to me, which meant I probably didn’t want to listen.

“I was thinking about what you had asked before,” she said. “About why we didn’t work out as a couple.”

“Slooply!” I yelled, hoping that would shut her up. “I said we should never talk about relationships again, remember?”

“I think the reason we didn’t work was because you’re afraid to care about anything, including yourself.”

“I care about myself. I’m not trying to end up in the hospital on purpose, you know.”

“I take that back. You care about yourself, but you don’t love yourself. You don’t love anything. If you don’t love anything, you can’t get hurt. Everything you do is about making sure you don’t get injured.”

“Um, I’m injured all the time. They got a memorial wing with my name in the hospital and I’m not even dead yet.”

“But you heal. That’s not real pain. You’re used to that. But you never even had a pet because you’re afraid it will die. Yes. They die. Things go away and you feel sad. But you feel great while you have them. You still talk about our ‘relationship.’ We dated for a few months, centuries ago. I’ve had children. I’ve loved people with all my heart. And it broke me. But I’ve lived ten times more in my short life than you’ve lived in your ancient life of caring for nothing.”

“Then tell me, smart gal, why am I constantly fighting for everyone? Constantly getting bloody and busted helping the galaxy if I don’t care?”

“You have a conscience. And you’re a great people person. It’s why I always hired you when I was Adjunct Overwatch the first time. I’m good at being behind the scenes. But you only really use your conscience when some faceless millions are in jeopardy. Then you’re happy to help. You can give your broken bones to the galaxy, but you can’t give one person your heart.”

“I’ll have you know I’m dating someone now and it’s going well.”

“The Ontakian? I’ll believe it when I see it,” she said.

“Do you think maybe we didn’t work out because you’re selfish and cutthroat? You’re working for the PCC Navy. How can I trust you?”

“You shouldn’t trust me. Just like I don’t trust you. But how is it cutthroat to want a steady paycheck? What I guess I’m saying, Hank, is that you’re emotionally unavailable, but a good person.”

“I rob people all the time. I con people. I beat up people. I sometimes kill people. I’m a thug.”

“I didn’t say you have great morals, just good ones,” she amended.

“Then why do I keep ending up in the hospital, knocked unconscious?”

“Because you’re incompetent, lazy, and have the worst luck of anyone I’ve ever met.”





CHAPTER 37




Cliston wanted me to work on trying to figure out who Kai’Un was—the big Dredel Led who had voted for war.

I told him my brain hurt too much to work with robots. That Agglatar had just about taken off my head with his last glowing ball. And that didn’t even count me bashing my skull against the wall.

But I also wanted to prove Garm wrong. That I wasn’t so good morally. I needed to do a dirty job with only self-interest in mind.

So I went around to all the bosses who were affected by Xandale’s photographic memory Protocol Officer and I started taking up a collection for the bribe.

I also negotiated my own salary.

I might have a hospital room with my name on the door, but they still charged me for visits. This job would pay fifty grand in advance with one fifty on completion. That would cover all my bills and give me reserves.

I pulled together an even one million credits for the bribe.

It was an absurd amount of cash. I couldn’t even carry it all in a sack. I had to make trips back and forth.

Staring at this giant pile of currency in my living room, I realized what a stupid mission this was.

I had to fly to a Navy cruiser, walk in dragging three shopping carts full of money, and bribe a guy who had already proven he did everything by the book.

While I was at it, I should just declare myself emperor and make it illegal to pick your nose.

But I had an idea or two.

I told the Navy ship in question that I was the Master Overseer of Belvaille and Navy Relations. It took them two days to confirm with Garm and Mr. Boshe. Once they did, I was allowed on board. Presumably so I could perform my job—whatever that was.

I wasn’t allowed in any high security areas, which was about 90% of the ship, but I could go to the cafeteria and break rooms and major hallways.

There, I introduced myself to Protocol Officer Sylvan-Fre.

He was a non-descript guy in a non-descript military outfit.

“Hi,” I said. “My name is Hank.”

“Navy Code of Conduct Section 32B, Subsection D, Subheading 227, Bullet Point 5: Officers are not allowed to fraternize with civilians,” he said.

He wasn’t rude, just official and efficient. He was the exact kind of guy you didn’t want monitoring space traffic while you were smuggling black market goods.

“I’m not really a civilian. I’m a foreigner. A non-citizen. But I’m Master Overseer of Belvaille and Navy Relations,” I said.

“Good day to you,” he said, walking away.

This was not going to be as easy as I thought—and I’d thought it was going to be nearly impossible.

Back on Belvaille, I tried to convince everyone not to use the Portal that Sylvan-Fre was monitoring until I could make a breakthrough.

The next day I visited the cruiser cafeteria again.

“Hi, what do you plan on eating for lunch today? I heard they have a mean casserole.”

It’s been my experience that if you are genuinely friendly, people have to be real assholes to totally ignore you—regardless of regulations.

Sylvan-Fre wasn’t a bad guy at all.

“I don’t like to eat anything too heavy, it makes me sleepy after lunch and then I don’t work as well,” he said.

Great, I’ll just have a fruit cake delivered to him every day. Put him in a food coma for two hours while we stream transports past him.

“That’s probably a good idea. I have some food allergies and I have to watch what I eat,” I lied.

“Well, good luck,” he said, and went off to get his food.

The next day I caught him in one of the break rooms, reading.

“Sorry, I never got your name, mine is Hank.”

“Protocol Officer Sylvan-Fre,” he said, and shook my hand. Though I could see he wanted to get back to his tele.

“What are you reading, if I can ask?”

“New shipping regulations. They’re constantly changing.”

“Yeah. I don’t know if I could keep them all straight.”

“It’s my job,” he said simply.

“Do you want to play some Oszho?” I said, indicating a simple paddle game that was set up in the break room.

“I’d better get back to studying,” he said.

“Come on, don’t leave me with no partner,” I smiled.

“I’ll play with you,” another officer said.

“Thanks,” I replied, annoyed.

It took five more days but I was finally sitting at Sylvan-Fre’s table, eating lunch with him.

We talked about shipping procedures. I knew a bit about it because of the Skim, but I mostly just asked him questions to keep him talking so I could try and learn about him.

So far, I knew he was single. He had no children. He grew up on a dull planet he had no interest in visiting. And his one sole interest was memorizing the near-infinite number of stamps, practices, forms, and laws that surrounded the movement of goods from one area in the PCC to another. He not only had a photographic memory, but he had an uncanny ability to count and deal with numbers—which made sneaking goods past him extra difficult.

“Sylvan-Fre, what would you do with a million credits?” I asked only somewhat rhetorically.

He didn’t even hesitate.

“Nothing. What could I do?” he replied.

And it hit me that he was right. It took me a week to realize that this guy was beyond bribing. If I gave him a million credits, he couldn’t possibly spend it.

He couldn’t show up wearing fine jewels and expensive clothes, couldn’t buy himself a spaceship, couldn’t even deposit it. A million credits was just a huge bale of lumpy plastic to him. He would have to stuff it under his mattress and hide it until he retired. Not only an impossible task, but one that wasn’t remotely worth the risk.

“So what do you want? What are your hopes and dreams, Protocol Officer Sylvan-Fre?”

Without even pausing he replied:

“I want to be a Protocol Manager. I want to be in charge of people, not just operating controls and inspections all day.”

As nice as this guy was, and as brilliant as his mind was, I didn’t think he would make a good supervisor of people. He was great at taking orders, not so great at giving them, I felt.

Still, I had a million credits to secure his promotion.

I felt if I could get Sylvan-Fre out of the way, whoever took his place would surely be substandard by comparison. Or at the worst, merely above average.

The bosses back on Belvaille were getting impatient with me. They had turned over a lot of cash and they weren’t using this Portal, so a lot of business was on hold.

But I believed I was going about things the proper way. If I had tried to shove a million credits at him, I would be sitting in jail right now. I had to find the weak link above Sylvan-Fre and bribe that person into giving the Protocol Officer a promotion.

This wouldn’t be as easy because higher-ranking officers tended to eat by themselves and socialize with themselves. Despite my Grand Overseer status, I couldn’t reach those inner sanctums.

Still, they had to use the bathroom.

“Hi, I’m Hank. I’m the Master Overseer of Belvaille and Navy Relations. As part of our getting-to-know-you, I was wondering if anyone would be interested in a little card game,” I smiled.

“I don’t gamble,” one Captain said, and he was so offended, he left the bathroom without even zipping his pants.

A Lieutenant and Sergeant Major also left in a huff.

I was beginning to wonder if it was impossible to bribe the PCC Navy, when a toilet flushed.

A bearded Vice Colonel exited the stall and washed up.

“Card game, huh? I’m willing to do my part for Belvaille-Navy relations,” he said with a mischievous grin.

“I have to warn you, I’m not very good at cards,” I said.

“Oh. Then I’ll have to take it easy,” he replied.

It took another week of me losing money hand over fist. I didn’t even have to bribe the Vice Colonel to convince him that Sylvan-Fre’s talents were being wasted.

I just had to point out that someone with a photographic memory, who was capable of counting cards, had better things to do than be a Protocol Officer.

Last I heard, Sylvan-Fre was promoted to aide-de-camp, and became the Vice Colonel’s gambling partner. They made a tour of the fleet, playing cards the entire way. He probably made considerably more than the one million I had been willing to bribe him.





CHAPTER 38




I was on another date with Najosa. Or I think it was a date. It was hard to tell with her.

My car had been fixed from the last time I drove. I kept the car in low gear and never shifted. Going so slow, I figured I would be okay.

Najosa looked nice. Well, sexy. Though she currently had her teeth gritted and her hand on the dashboard.

“If I knew you were such a terrible driver, I never would have gotten in the car with you,” she said.

“I’m not that bad.”

“You were supposed to stop back there.”

“Why?”

“Because there was a light,” she said.

“I didn’t see anyone.”

“It doesn’t matter. You’re supposed to stop.”

“That’s a stupid rule. What’s the point of being in a car if you have to keep stopping for phantom traffic?” I asked.

“What if you run into pedestrians?”

“They’ll get more hurt than we will. They should be looking out for me, not the other way around.”

“I don’t think that’s how the laws work, Hank.”

“You said you hate the PCC. Why are you all of a sudden taking their side when it comes to regulations?”

“Traffic laws are pretty standard. I don’t think the PCC invented them.”

“Well, we’re almost there. Woops,” I said, as I side-scraped a parked car.

I managed to park without much trouble. I bumped into the wall in front of me, but it didn’t do any damage.

Najosa dashed out of the auto and stood by the door to the restaurant, as if she was afraid the car would attack her.

There weren’t a lot of fancy restaurants left on Belvaille. Not only did people not have money to afford it, but good food wasn’t delivered here.

Still, there were two high-end restaurants that bought everything off the black market. They weren’t open every day because they simply didn’t have enough food.

But for two days, and a king’s ransom, you could treat yourself to some pretty magnificent meals that were reminiscent of Old Belvaille.

We got off to a bad start in the restaurant when the doorman tried to take Najosa’s jacket.

He walked up behind her, taking her by the shoulders. She turned around and gave him a chop to the throat.

As the poor man was on the ground gurgling for air, I had to comment.

“How do you know so much about driving but so little about everything else?”

“He grabbed me,” she said.

The whole restaurant was looking at us.

I smoothed things over with the maître d' by giving him two hundred credits. Great, this meal was already going to be expensive.

My goal for coming here was a few fold. I wanted to show Najosa that I wasn’t a huge slob and could eat normal food like a normal person. I wanted to show her that maybe Colmarians weren’t all bad, by taking her to a place with a little class and ambiance. And I wanted to keep stuff in our mouths so we didn’t have to talk much. Talking was our least successful activity up to this point, so I wanted to minimize it.

The restaurant was exquisite. There were glorious artworks everywhere, fine carpets, expensive metals coated every surface, and even the tablecloths were, I don’t know, fancy.

I saw Najosa taking it all in with a critical eye.

When we were seated, I leaned over the table.

“So what do you think of the place?”

“I think if we sold all these useless goods, we could buy a lot of weapons,” she said.

“Don’t go stealing anything,” I cautioned. “Just try and enjoy yourself.”

Najosa put a big satchel on the table.

“That’s for you,” she said.

“You got me a present?” I was shocked. Maybe Najosa was romantic after all.

I opened the brown wrapper and found it was stuffed full of money.

“That’s your share from the work we did on the telescopes.”

I quickly covered the cash.

“Shh!” I hissed. “I thought it didn’t work. You know, after I flew into the dish.”

“It turns out that you smashing into the telescope worked well. The Navy thinks it’s broken so they haven’t discovered that we tapped into it.”

“You guys are broadcasting to Ontakians spread out across the galaxy?” I asked.

“Not yet. It’s being used for other things. But that is our ultimate goal. We’re not sure if anyone will be listening if we broadcast. Your share came to 30,000 credits.”

Nice. Paying for dinner wouldn’t be so hard after all.

I ordered about a third of the food I would normally eat and when it came, I just poked at it daintily.

“You’re eating too much,” she said to me.

“I’m hardly eating anything. I’m surprised they haven’t kicked us out for how loud my stomach is grumbling.”

“That’s your third meat pie.”

“Yeah, but I’m not even halfway done,” I said.

“Well, hurry up. I don’t like this place. Too many Colmarians staring at us.”

“They aren’t staring. Maybe they are admiring you. You’re a lovely woman.”

Najosa sulked.

I began scooping the food into my mouth as I thought how to salvage this date.

It was not long after that I realized I had eaten too much, too fast, and I couldn’t get it down. Finding you can’t swallow is an awkward situation when you’re trying to impress someone.

I made some mild coughs and patted my stomach. I then reached for the water and tried to drink, but it spilled out of my clogged throat all over my chest.

“Are you ill?” she asked me.

Okay, I was really choking!

I hammered the table with my fist and the whole tabletop slammed to the ground along with all the dishes and food.

I clutched at my throat trying to mash the food into a smaller size or otherwise squeeze it down to my stomach, but nothing was working.

“Hank!” Najosa said.

She went around behind me and began hammering on my back with her combat-ready fists. It was like I was drowning and someone started kicking me in the face.

I tried to shoo her away with one arm as I stood up and stumbled around, one hand still on my throat.

I had never choked before and this might be my last time. It was an odd sensation, suffocating on dry land. The mild embarrassment had long ago vanished and now I was completely and utterly panicked.

People were around me. Several women were screaming.

One man was trying to undress me while I heard another say:

“He’s too fat!”

Two men were punching my gut and Najosa was still hammering on my back.

I could only think that I had survived missiles, rockets, napalm, explosives, Boranjame, Therezians, Gandrine, tanks, and more bullets than anyone could possibly count. Yet despite all that, I was going to die from three mouthfuls of a meat pie.

I fell to my knees, knocking over another table.

I was blacking out.

I wanted to tell this cruel, cruel world to Eat Suck, Suckface, but only drool came out.

A metal railing was placed on the ground in front of me.

“Now,” I heard Najosa say.

What felt like a dozen people jumped on my back and shoulders and I was flung forward into the railing, where it hit my abdomen.

Huagh!

I was gasping and looking down at the pile of food that had choked me.

“How did he even get that in his mouth?” someone asked.

When I had recovered, I found that half the restaurant had been destroyed as I groped and flopped around and people took turns beating me.

I could at least partially cover my humiliation by offering to pay for the damages—with cash. I tried to pay everyone who helped, no matter how counterproductive their efforts had been. Despite their protestations, I ended up giving away about 10,000 credits from my satchel. It served me right for choking in a fancy restaurant instead of some greasy noodle joint.

Najosa was mercifully quiet as we went to the car.

“I lost my keys,” I said.

“Did you eat them?”

“No. Can you go back inside and ask if I dropped them?”

“I think if either one of us goes back in there they’ll shoot us.”

We had an eight-mile walk back to my place and I wasn’t looking forward to it.

“We’ll run,” Najosa said.

“I’m not really cut out for running,” I began.

“I have some daxapronal. It will help.”

“Are you kidding? I almost choked to death. I don’t want to go on a rampage.”

“I’ll only give you micrograms. I’ve administered this many times.”

“How do you have so much daxapronal?” I asked.

“We get it from the Rettosians. There’s still some in this System. Though not a lot.”

“Rettosians? Where do they get it?”

“They invented it. Back when we were slaves of theirs, they used to give huge doses to our combat models to help us in battle.”

“Yeah, but I’m a mutant Ontakian. It doesn’t work the same on me,” I said.

“I know. Here.”

She handed me a small tab of paper.

“Put it under your tongue. And save this for later, in case you need it.”

She handed me about two dozen tabs in a plastic bag.

“Need for what? This could drug an army,” I said.

Najosa put a tab in her mouth.

I reluctantly did so. The date couldn’t get much worse.

I had wanted to go play miniature golf on top of Wallow. I set up a little course on his back with holes where his battle scars were. I thought it would impress Najosa to be standing on a dead Therezian.

Instead, we ran home. Screaming and yelling and stripping off our clothes.

We got to my place and we had sex.

Violent, wrathful sex. I’ve had angry sex before. Revenge sex. Payback sex. I-used-to-date-your-grandmother sex.

But this was something different. We were both on the same wavelength. Both of us a jumble of flexing muscles and sweat and fury.

The next day she was gone. My legs felt like I had sprinted across the city while under the influence of an obscene amphetamine. My throat felt raw and ragged, like I had tried to swallow a mess of barbed wire. My whole body was fatigued and I was covered with bruises.

And I had a monster, absolutely monumental headache.





CHAPTER 39




I was in the Ontakian club, the Society of the Free and Easy, having a drink that tasted like dirty socks rinsed in pure alcohol.

At least it gave me a buzz, which was more than I could say for the conversation.

“What am I doing here?” I asked Najosa. We were alone, the rest of the group off doing whatever they were doing.

I didn’t feel especially comfortable in the club, despite being an Ontakian. My original joy at finding there were so many other Ontakians left in the galaxy gave way when I learned that most Ontakians were surly, sour, and prone to punching me in the face when they danced.

My attempts to learn about Ontakian culture and history were met with dull stares. Most Ontakians considered culture and history to be foreign concepts.

Frank was still the only one who seemed to know anything of substance about our past, and he tended to leave out huge sections—like the fact my parents might still be alive.

“You’re here because we’re meeting one of our patrons,” Najosa said.

“Patron? For your music?” I asked.

“No,” she said, without elaborating.

“What the hell is this drink made out of?” I finally asked.

“It’s a traditional beverage. Ethanol filtered through perspiration-soaked cloth that has been worn by combat or labor Ontakians,” Najosa said.

“Holy crap, it is alcohol and dirty socks! Why would anyone invent this drink?”

“When we were slaves, we had to make do with what materials we had. We found that the sweat from certain types of Ontakians enhanced the flavor,” Najosa continued.

“Enhance is a generous word. Maybe my taste buds are mutated too, because this stuff is terrible.”

I hesitantly took another sip. The drink was expensive. I didn’t want to waste it.

Suddenly I realized the club had gone completely quiet. The music was off. The talking had stopped. Everyone looked downcast and introspective.

I panicked and wondered if the Agglatar had resurrected himself and followed me to this club, looking for donations.

But I saw a small form making its way through the crowd.

It was a man. Old. And he was in a wheelchair.

“No way,” I mumbled.

“Greetings, Ontakians. I have come with the details of your next assignment,” the old Rettosian said.

It was him! It was the ancient Rettosian who had mutated the entire System some years ago. Who had created a clone army to fight in the civil war. Who had the ability to teleport things across the System. Who had created mutant glocken teams to play for Thad Elon.

I didn’t know his name—because he hadn’t told me and I didn’t ask. Delovoa had shot and killed him, but we assumed it was only a clone.

I could tell right away it was him.

He wasn’t as old as he had been before, but he was still ancient. So maybe a thousand years old instead of two thousand. He was shriveled, his fingers crooked, and his face was sunken from missing all his teeth.

Rettosian skin is liquid, flowing across their bodies like a colorful ocean. But this man was so old his skin had dried and crusted. Only a few pathetic trickles of skin dripped across his face. It almost looked like he was slowly melting.

“Hello, Rettosian. This is one of the new recruits that we’ve been using,” Najosa said, indicating me.

The old man looked at me and I felt a chill. This was one of the most brilliant people I’d ever encountered. He said he not only built my talking Gun, but that he built me. That he was one of the creators of the whole Ontakian species!

He only looked at me for a second.

“I don’t care who you use or how. I just want results,” the old man said.

“Of course,” Najosa answered, and I could tell she was at least as intimidated by the old man as I was.

“I have your payment…here,” the Rettosian said, feebly pointing to the side of his wheelchair.

Najosa walked to him and leaned down. She took a jug of liquid and a briefcase from a compartment on the side of the chair.

“One million credits and a gallon of daxapronal,” he said.

“And you need us to attack a cargo ship?” Najosa asked.

“No. I need you to deliver three large shipping containers from the transport BA-553J9 out of the U.M.A. System. It is carrying electromagnetic components.”

“Three containers?” Najosa confirmed.

“Three large shipping containers,” he said.

“We’ll take care of it,” Najosa replied.

Without another word, the old Rettosian turned his chair and puttered back out of the club. All the angry, synth-clad club goers scurried out of his way.

“So that’s our next mission,” Najosa said.

I didn’t know where to start.

“That guy…what’s his name?” I asked.

“I don’t know. He’s an old Rettosian.”

“I know that. Do you also know he maybe created the Ontakians? And he mutated all of Belvaille? And he worked with Thad Elon?”

“So you’ve met him before?” she asked.

“Met him? I killed him!”

Najosa clearly didn’t believe me.

“He didn’t seem very angry about it.”

“It was his clone. I don’t know how clones work. Maybe he didn’t know me. Maybe the clones don’t talk to each other.”

“So what are you saying, Hank?” Najosa stated, oblivious.

I was about to blow my brain pan.

“He’s…bad news! A bad, bad person!”

“Bad person according to whom?” she asked.

“Like…the galaxy! He mutated billions of people. He helped start the Colmarian civil war.”

“That hardly makes him bad in my book. The mutations, maybe. Though I wouldn’t mind being able to fly.”

I couldn’t believe Najosa was being so flippant about it.

“I can only fly in space—and then I can’t stop. But that’s not the point. What if he created us? Created us as slaves?”

“You just saw him pay me—in advance. You don’t pay slaves. Once we gained our freedom, the Rettosians have always treated us with respect.”

“So what is he going to do with about 360,000 pounds of electronics?” I asked.

“Is that how heavy the shipping containers are?” she replied.

“Yeah. Wait. So that’s why you wanted me here? I know about ships and cargo because of my work on the Skim.”

“I won’t deny you’re a valuable member of this team, Hank. You have a lot of assets. You’re just a bit overweight.”

“Okay. I get it. I’m fat. But I also know who that Rettosian is. And I know for sure he isn’t going to use those electronics to make…I don’t know, something not incredibly dangerous.”

“He has built us a number of tools. And helped us in our efforts against the PCC. He has earned our trust.”

“You don’t even know his name!”

“What is his name?” Najosa asked.

“How should I know? I didn’t talk to the guy, I just killed him. Wait, is he the one that comes up with all your plans and targets for attack?”

“Not all of them. But our ideals are aligned. Everything you’ve said about him reaffirms he is a powerful enemy of the Colmarians. What is your concern?”

“I guess…that he’ll build a battleship and kill us all. Or something.”

“That doesn’t make sense, Hank. We’ve been working with him for years. Without his support, the Society of the Free and Easy wouldn’t be where it is today.”

“And where is it? I don’t even understand that.”

Najosa held up the briefcase.

“A million credits richer, for one.”





CHAPTER 40




“How much do you trust this Najosa lady?” Delovoa asked.

I had brought him together with Cliston to discuss my recent encounter with the ancient Rettosian scientist.

“I guess I trust her a lot, actually. For once it’s great to be dating someone with no secret motives. I think if she wanted me dead, she’d say, ‘hey, I want you dead.’ Then would kill me. She’s not like Garm where I have to worry about what’s going on behind the scenes.”

“But sir, what does the appearance of the Rettosian mean? You said he was responsible for making Thad Elon’s Championship glocken team?” Cliston asked.

“He did all kinds of stuff. He makes Delovoa look stupid and benevolent by comparison.”

“Hey. I did manage to kill him,” Delovoa sulked.

“Yeah, before he could tell us anything. And as I recall, you had to empty a whole pistol at him because you couldn’t hit a geriatric scientist in a wheelchair.”

“I never said I was an expert marksman. You’re the one with the auto-aiming gun.”

“Perhaps Gun might have information about the man?” Cliston offered.

“Wow, that’s a really good idea. Gun,” I said, holding him up.

“Hello? Yes?” he replied.

“Do you know anything about a really old Rettosian? You met him once before and he…kind of commanded you to shoot me. He might have made the Ontakians.”

“I don’t know what most of those words mean,” Gun answered pleasantly. “You mentioned ‘Rettosian’ before, but I don’t remember if we ever settled on what it is.”

I returned Gun to my holster.

“So back to the scientist,” I said.

“I object to you calling him, ‘the scientist,’” Delovoa said.

“Well, come up with a name. Old Guy.”

“Bird Bones,” Delovoa said.

“Rettosian Clone and Mutant Manufacturer,” Cliston said.

“Wheelchair?” I asked.

“Dumbface,” Delovoa tried.

“Thad Elon’s Assistant?” Cliston said.

“That’s good. Thad’s Friend! I mean, being a friend of the immortal Thad Elon is description enough. And if we just say ‘Thad’ no one will necessarily know who we’re talking about,” I said.

“Yes, Thad is a fairly common name,” Delovoa agreed.

“Okay, then: Thad’s Friend. What should I do about him?” I asked.

“Kill him. Again,” Delovoa declared. “You know he’s doing something. He doesn’t seem the kind of mad scientist who would retire.”

“No, but, first of all, killing him doesn’t seem to do a lot. He’s probably got more clones.”

“Find them and kill them,” Delovoa said.

“Look, I’m not an assassin.”

“Certainly he deserves a good killing for mutating all of Belvaille, to say nothing of all the other things he’s done,” Delovoa argued.

“Oh, no, he deserves being deaderized. I just don’t want to spend the rest of my life hunting super genius clones. All he has to do is teleport me into space once and I’m gone. I think we need to figure out what he’s up to.”

“Yeah, do that and then kill him,” Delovoa said.

“You kill him. You’re the one who shot him before.”

“You said he has an Ontakian army. I won’t be able to get near him,” Delovoa explained.

“We’re not an army. We’re more of an anti-social fraternal order.”

“Be careful, sir. Thad Elon is extremely dangerous and ‘all’ he has is immortality,” Cliston asked.

“So?”

“It seems to me the Rettosian scientist, Thad’s Friend, has a kind of immortality himself, with his multiple clones. And he also has access to vast technological resources. He may be even more dangerous than Thad Elon,” Cliston stated.

“Thad Elon is billions of years old and destined to end all life in the galaxy. It’s hard to get more dangerous than that. Why don’t I do Thad’s Friend’s mission? That way I’ll be able to see what he’s planning. Oh, and I’ll get paid.”

“We could use the money, sir,” Cliston added.





CHAPTER 41




There used to be a handful of Dredel Led in Belvaille. However, they all left when the population cleared out. Cliston was the only Dredel Led that still resided in the System.

So as I ate my breakfast in The Club I was more than a little surprised when I heard a voice declare:

“You are all hereby prisoners of the Dredel Led empire!”

I looked up from my plate of sausages but didn’t see who had spoken. The Club had come to a virtual standstill as everyone gazed around, searching for our assailant.

“You. Obese Colmarian. Drop your weapon,” I heard the person continue.

I turned toward the voice.

Underneath my table stood a six-inch tall, silvery robot. He had glowing yellow eyes, his limbs were mostly rectangular, and he wore a red cape, that I could probably use as a napkin.

“Huh?” I asked dumbly.

A number of people started to gather around to meet our new metallic overlord.

“Yes, you. Drop your weapon or be destroyed.”

I looked around at the thugs and counterfeiters and cutthroats, hoping they might know a suitable response, but they were as confused as I was.

“You asked for it, fleshling!”

I heard a poingk sound, and a little plastic projectile hit me in the knee with about as much ferocity as a kitten’s teardrop.

“I, uh, who are you?” I asked.

“I am the Emerald Crusader,” he stated with gusto.

This struck me as even more odd since nothing on him was emerald unless I was severely color blind.

“Oh,” was all I said.

“What’s he want, Hank?” one of the freighter jockeys asked me.

“How should I know?” I replied.

“You’re coming with me,” he said, and he walked over and tried to grab hold of my ankle.

I lifted my leg and he was still clutching me. I couldn’t even feel him. I shook my leg but he doggedly hung on.

“Stop resisting,” he urged. “You’re only making it worse.”

“Hank, where did you get that?” an off-duty bartender asked curiously.

“I didn’t. I don’t know,” I said. I was getting annoyed that people thought it was mine.

I stood up and stomped my foot on the ground, but the little crusader held onto the hem of my dress.

“Is that a weapon?” a thug asked me.

I reached down and physically pulled him off, ripping a patch from my clothes. I shook my hand and dropped him and hurried to the front exit.

“Stop, fat one! Come back here!” He yelled.

I reached the exit and tried to hurriedly get my coat from Fample.

“Hey, they charged me for my food,” I said. “I’m supposed to have a lifetime supply of free food and drink.”

“You were supposed to get that guy out of here, not destroy the whole second floor,” Fample argued.

“You didn’t say anything about that. Besides, I didn’t destroy it. He did,” I said.

“When we repair the floor, I’ll think about giving you a discount on food,” he said.

“I am placing you under Dredel Led custody. Get on the ground and put your hands behind your back,” the Emerald Crusader said.

“What’s that?” Fample asked, looking over the coat check counter.

“I don’t know. Maybe a Dredel Led,” I mumbled.

“We’re at war with the Dredel Led. Aren’t we?” he asked, alarmed.

“The PCC are at war. Belvaille isn’t part of the PCC,” I said. Then I turned around and addressed the little robot. “Hey, you’re in the wrong city. We aren’t part of the Post-Colmarian Confederation Colmarian Confederation. You have no fight with us.”

“Silence, wet-sack! Do not try and distract me from my purpose.”

“What are you going to do with him?” Fample asked. “It might be against the law to have a Dredel Led.”

“I don’t have him. He attacked my ankle.”

“Well, get him out of here. I don’t want the Navy causing trouble.”

“He’s not…” I started, but realized there was no point in continuing.

I walked as quickly as I could out of The Club and to my car.

Starting the engine, I felt like I had outpaced the tiny robot or he had grown bored of me. I adjusted my side mirror and I saw the Emerald Crusader fighting with my left rear tire.

I opened the door and stuck my head out.

“Move!” I yelled.

“I’m going to destroy your ship,” he said.

I watched him for a moment, and I was pretty sure that even with infinite time, he wouldn’t be able to do more than scratch the paint.

I sighed and got out of my car. I walked over and picked up the Emerald Crusader. He only weighed about ten pounds.

“Unhand me or face my wrath,” he shouted.

I set him down out of the way and got back in my car. However, the little guy ran back to my tire and was punching and kicking at it again. I was pretty certain that if I ran over him, he’d be destroyed. And while I wasn’t terribly unhappy with that prospect, I didn’t want to murder a harmless alien simply because he was slightly irritating.

I exited my car again, picked up the Emerald Crusader, and got shot in the nose with a plastic dart.

“Die!” He screamed.

This time I carried the robot a good fifty feet away and put him on the roof of another car. Maybe he would be satisfied trying to destroy it. If nothing else, he would hopefully need some time to climb down.

I ran back to my car, threw it in reverse, and sped away.

I parked in front of my house and got out. I would need a few drinks before I told Cliston what had transpired. Maybe he would have an idea what it was about.

“Coward! You can’t escape from me that easily,” I heard.

The Emerald Crusader appeared from underneath my car and ran at me, his red cape flapping behind him like a toy flag.

“Well, come in, I guess. Maybe my butler can talk some sense into you,” I said, defeated.

I didn’t have to carry him in because he was latched onto my ankle again.

“What is that, sir?” Cliston asked when I walked inside.

“I was hoping you could tell me. He says he’s a Dredel Led.”

“Join me, comrade. Help me subdue this bulbous Colmarian,” the Emerald Crusader said.

Cliston bent over to take a closer look at the robot, who was currently holding onto my socks and kicking at the back of my shin.

“Excuse me. Am I to understand that you are a Dredel Led?”

“Yes. My primary function is to destroy the Colmarian empire.”

Kick kick kick.

“Colmarian empire?” I asked. “You’re a little late, the Colmarian Confederation was destroyed in the civil war. This isn’t even PCC territory.”

“Do not try and confuse me,” he replied.

“Can’t you hook up to his brain and see what he knows?” I asked Cliston.

“We can only do that in Thaxmultaerea,” he said. “May I ask what your inception date was?”

The Emerald Crusader spat out a series of numbers and letters in rapid succession.

“Sir, this Dredel Led was only created a week ago.”

“So, he’s young. Will he grow bigger?”

“No, sir. What it means is he was created after the vote to go to war. He was likely intended as some kind of military unit,” Cliston said.

“He already made that clear. But I’ve seen combat Dredel Led before, and they were pretty damn dangerous. Do you think he has a bomb or something?”

“Unlikely. But my point is, I think he may be”—and Cliston lowered his voice politely—“flawed.”

“You’re telling me stuff that’s obvious, Cliston. Unless there are another million of him, he’s not going to be doing much harm.”

“But sir, Dredel Led are created from the collected consciousness of Thaxmultaerea. Clearly there was some desire to create a soldier. But why it turned out like…”

“His name is the Emerald Crusader.”

Cliston paused to stare at the struggling Dredel Led.

“Why he turned out like that is the question. He clearly isn’t suited to his stated function. This leads me to believe there was some kind of malfunction during his creation.”

“Like a machine blew a gasket?” I asked.

“I’m speaking of Kai’Un, sir. It was his million minds that led us to war and his million minds would play a significant role in the creation of any new Dredel Led,” Cliston explained.

“I don’t understand. Is Kai’Un defective? Is that why he knocked me unconscious when we hooked up minds?”

“No, I believe his sheer number of consciousness did that. But as I said before, there is no record of the Dredel Led ever creating a being like Kai’Un. If he is ancient, perhaps his minds aren’t suited to building modern Dredel Led.”

“Or maybe he isn’t a Dredel Led?” I asked.

“There has never been an instance where another species has created a Dredel Led facsimile. Especially one capable of operating in Thaxmultaerea.”

“I could go to Thaxmultaerea and I’m not a robot. Maybe it was a million-minded Colmarian using the same tool I did.”

“That seems unrealistic. But perhaps it was a million individuals. That might explain it.”

“But this is actually good. Your army is going to be full of misfits. That should help the war effort.”

“Sir, although I do not wish war upon us, I also don’t wish to see my race grow extinct.”

“You won’t be extinct.” I held up my leg as proof, where the Emerald Crusader continued to scratch at me. “You’ll just be a lot shorter on average.”

It was then we were interrupted by someone yelling on a bullhorn from outside.

“You in there. This is the PCC Navy. We have reason to believe there are Dredel Led on the premises. If you are able, please exit the building with your hands raised. If you are captive, please exit the building with your hands lowered,” the voice demanded.

“Oh, great. This is just what I need,” I said.

“Sir, they will likely kill the Emerald Crusader if they capture him. We are at war,” Cliston said.

“I’m not worried about him, I’m worried about you.”

“Perhaps I could go and explain the situation to them? I am a deductive objector and not taking part in the war.”

I scanned outside the front door and saw what looked like at least twenty military personnel, all armed and taking cover behind their cars.

“I don’t think they’ll care. Let me go out. You find somewhere to hide.”

“One moment, sir.”

Cliston reached down and removed the Emerald Crusader from my leg.

“Traitor!” the tiny Dredel Led yelled.

I adjusted my clothes as best I could and put on my negotiating face. I exited the front door with my hands between up and down, as I couldn’t remember which signified what.

“So guys, here’s the situation—” I began.

“Open fire!”

There were a lot more soldiers than I had thought and they all began peppering me with bullets. I covered my face with my right arm and my groin with my left hand.

It’s been my experience that groups tend to stop shooting after a while when they realize I’m bulletproof. In the immediate aftermath of their shock, that’s when I get to work my diplomacy magic and try and come to a reasonable solution.

But these guys seemed to have an infinity of bullets and an infinity of desire to use them.

I stood there for a good thirty seconds as they took turns reloading and emptying their guns. I was probably putting a dent in the galactic-wide market price of ammunition.

Finally, I realized these guys weren’t going to stop. They had been given an order to shoot, and like good PCC drones, they were going to shoot.

“Can I ask who is in charge here?” I tried to yell over the gunfire. “I think there has been a misunderstanding,” I continued. “Is anyone listening? I’m the Overseer of Navy and Belvaille Relations or something,” I said.

With thirty or so guns firing simultaneously it was probably impossible to hear me. I gave up and went inside, covering the back of my head and my butt as I went.

“Sir! You’re covered in bullets,” Cliston said, alarmed.

“Imagine that. Yeah, help me scrape them off.”

Cliston came over and began to pick off the mashed bullets from my skin. They hadn’t hurt too much, but I’d look like a bruised leopard in the morning.

I noticed that the Emerald Crusader was casually sitting on one of my tables and leafing through a tele screen.

“Is he all tuckered out?” I asked, wondering why he wasn’t attacking me.

“No, sir. Forgive me, but I took the liberty of explaining to him that you aren’t a Colmarian, you are an Ontakian.”

“I’m not at war with the Ontakians,” the Emerald Crusader said.

“Oh. I guess it’s okay you told him. Saves my ankles.”

“What are we going to do about the police?” Cliston asked.

“They aren’t really police. They’re Navy. If they were cops I could talk to them. Hell, I could bribe them. There’s nothing I can do with those guys.”

“Perhaps a show of force would give us liberty to leave,” Cliston said.

“Yeah. Eventually they’re going to try and break in.”

“And that may damage all your furniture,” Cliston said.

I pulled out Gun.

“Gun. You there?”

“I can only guess that I am. I cannot confirm the nature of the universe or even my own existence,” he replied.

“That’s fine. Remember how I explained what guns are?” I asked.

“Vaguely. Non-biological things held by biological things.”

“Well…I suppose.” I didn’t really like that definition, as it included an awful lot, but I remember getting a migraine the first few times we tried to go over this. “If I point you out there, can you shoot the other guns?”

“I do not know. What is a shot? How do I know if I’ve done it or not done it?”

“Come on, Gun. You’ve shot things a million times.”

“A million? That is awfully precise. How do you know I haven’t shot a million and one? Or 999,999 times?”

“Thad Elon’s Runny Nose,” I exclaimed. “I need you to focus, Gun. I can’t go through this right now. Just shoot their weapons, okay?”

“I will make the motions of doing what I think it is you’re saying to do,” he replied.

I contemplated holstering Gun and trying something else, but I figured I would give him a chance.

I cracked open the front door and stuck out Gun, blindly.

Bang! Bang! Bang! Bang! Bang!

Gun rapid fired what must have been two hundred shots. I felt myself sink to my knees and it was all I could do to pull my hand back inside. I was lying on my back, completely drained of energy. I really wanted a nice snack to eat. Something salty.

“Cliston,” I mumbled. “See what happened.”

“You’re blocking the door, sir.”

With significant effort, I managed to roll out of the way.

Cliston peeked out the door and closed it again.

“It seems Gun did shoot the weapons they were carrying.”

“Why did he shoot so much? They didn’t have that many guns.”

“He also seems to have shot off their hats. Their shoes. Their radios.”

“Oh.”

“They appear to be leaving, sir.”

“Nice. Do we have anything to eat?”

“What are you in the mood for, sir? I could whip up a nice six-course pungent savory.”

“That sounds good. No, wait. Something easy. Liquid. I don’t think I have the energy to chew.”

“Sir, your tele is ringing.”
