“Yeah, it is. Can you answer it for me? My arms are kind of jelly.”

“Of course.”

Cliston manhandled me to the side and removed my tele.

“Hank residence. Cliston the butler speaking. Oh, hello. It is good to hear your voice. Yes. Yes. Hank is a bit indisposed at the moment. I believe so. Ah, that is most interesting. What is your recommendation? I see. I do thank you for calling. I’m sure Hank would as well. Goodbye.”

“Who was that? Wrong number?” I asked.

“No, that was Garm. She said the Navy is going to bombard this building.”

“Bombard? What’s that mean?” I asked, getting a boost of adrenaline.

“A cruiser in orbit is going to turn its weapons on this house and destroy it. Garm suggested we leave immediately.”





CHAPTER 42




“Delovoa, get out here now!” I yelled outside his door.

“What’s going on?” he asked, rubbing his three sleepy eyes.

“They’re going to blow up the building! Get your stuff and go.”

“Who? When?”

“The Navy. In a cruiser. Soon.”

Delovoa threw open the door. Inside, I could see his thousands upon thousands of trinkets and inventions and parts and supplies. It was probably all worth a small fortune.

“Help me gather stuff,” he said desperately.

“Screw you, I’m getting my own things.”

“Who cares about your dresses and make-up and food? My gear is irreplaceable.”

“Well, if you consider your life irreplaceable you better hurry up. We’re going to meet between Wallow’s legs. We figure that will be the safest area from the blast.”

“They can’t be using normal missiles. It wouldn’t penetrate the shield. Not to mention the latticework would be in the way.”

“Garm said a cruiser was doing it. I don’t know more than that.”

“Garm? You said you don’t trust Garm,” Delovoa challenged.

“I don’t. But I trust the Navy to obliterate my building just the same.”

“Why?”

“I’m harboring at least two Dredel Led.”

“At least? What’s that mean?”

“They’re making them smaller and smaller. For all I know I got fifty Dredel Led stuck in my carpet fibers. Stop arguing and come on!”

Cliston was carrying his travel case, the helmet that let me connect to Thaxmultaerea, the Emerald Crusader, some of the formula he used to cook my special food, and my bag of daxapronal that Najosa had given me.

I was carrying Gun, a suitcase full of credits, five changes of underwear, a dress that wasn’t shot to pieces, and five of my best bottles of alcohol.

Delovoa was dragging a wheelbarrow literally spilling technical parts and gadgets all along the sidewalk as we hurried toward the safety of Wallow’s crotch.

We all hunkered down and waited.

“This doesn’t make sense,” Delovoa said. “Even if they had a high-pressure tachyon jumbler—which they don’t—firing it at one building on a space station is nearly impossible.”

“I’m just saying what she told Cliston.”

“You didn’t even talk to her yourself?”

“No. I had just fired Gun and was indisposed.”

“Cliston is at war with us, are you going to take his word for it?” Delovoa turned to Cliston, who was standing right next to him. “Sorry, I’m a bit frazzled. I just dragged half my belongings out onto the street.”

“Quite understandable,” Cliston replied.

We waited for another forty-five minutes while Delovoa and I passed one of my bottles back and forth until we were comfortably tipsy.

“Were you saving this?” Delovoa asked, looking at the label.

“Can you believe I got that from Arch Minister Maris-To?” I asked.

“Really? I didn’t think you two were that close.”

“I stole it from his wine cellar when I was invited to a party. I had three other bottles but I drank them.”

“Sir. One does not pilfer from his host,” Cliston chastised.

“Pilfer,” Delovoa taunted. “You big pilferer.”

“I don’t think anything is going to happen,” I said, staring at my non-destroyed building.

“I’m not going back in there until tomorrow. I won’t be able to sleep thinking about a cruiser flying above me getting its accuracy perfect,” Delovoa said.

“Why do you think Garm wanted us to leave the building?” I asked.

Delovoa shrugged.

“Maybe she knew you’d drink up your booze and she was going to sell you some. Who knows with her?”

I was about to debate the logic of that when it suddenly got a bit darker.

“Hey—” I began.

Then there followed an enormous roar. Like an entire five-story steel building was being ripped apart at the molecular level.

“Holy crap!” Delovoa screamed.

It was over in a second, but we sat there, deafened, for some minutes more.

When we were certain it was done, we peeked out past Wallow’s feet.

There was a fine steel dust coating everything for blocks. My building was completely gone! There was no other damage. No holes in the adjoining buildings. No giant crater where my home had been.

It was as if an enormous eraser had come down, scratched out my building, and distributed the particles across the adjoining streets.

“My house,” I said, stunned.

“Well, damn,” Delovoa said. “Someone up there does have a high-pressure tachyon jumbler.”





CHAPTER 43




I was now living in the sky.

Specifically, City Hall. It was the tallest building on the space station, being something like a hundred stories. It was kind of hard to be certain because there were so many elevator banks and I suspected there were hidden floors here or there.

In any case, I figured if the Navy was out to find me, hiding in City Hall would be a good cover. Even if they knew I was here, it would take them weeks to actually locate me, the building was so massive.

I was also happy that I got to move away from Wallow’s corpse.

“What floor are we on again?” I asked Cliston on my tele.

“46th South,” he replied.

We were living in four adjoining rooms that had likely been offices. Delovoa was ten floors above us. He was still upset about our building being destroyed and didn’t want to live on the same floor as me. Though he had no problem being close enough that he could get Cliston to cook for him now and then.

Cliston had done a fantastic job turning the stuffy offices into chic living quarters. He tripled my supply of underwear and managed to make me some extra clothes that weren’t dresses.

We had only been here for three days.

“Everything is gone,” I told Cliston.

I had finally taken the time to scout the empty space that was formerly my house, hoping to find some valuables that survived.

“We’ll manage, sir.”

“How much money do I have in the bank?”

“None, sir. You told me to withdraw it all. I assume most of it was destroyed with the building.”

“When did I tell you to take all my money out?” I demanded.

“About two months ago. You had mentioned something about ‘dirty Ank bastards,’” he explained.

“That sounds like something I’d say,” I agreed.

The Ank were the bankers of the galaxy. They had tried to screw me over a number of times and I vaguely recall meeting one, getting in an argument, and resolving to work on a cash basis.

Which is fine, as long as you don’t get robbed or get your house turned into dust.

I was still unclear on who gave the order and why.

The little Dredel Led, the Emerald Crusader, was still hanging out with us. I think he was a bit infatuated with Cliston. Not in a sexual way—if robots could even get sexual—but Cliston was so experienced and so good at so many things. In contrast, the Emerald Crusader was so bad at everything. Though he was only a week old.

“I’m thinking of getting my primary directive changed and becoming a butler,” the Emerald Crusader said proudly.

“Ah,” I replied. I didn’t want to dash the poor guy’s dreams, but I couldn’t think he would make a good butler unless he could find a species that was six inches tall.

He tried to help around the house but it took all his efforts to carry a set of silverware, and another thirty minutes to arrange them around the table.

We explained the situation about Kai’Un and Thaxmultaerea and the war. That was enough for the Emerald Crusader to put his idea of Colmarian conquest on hold.

That said, it took Cliston to really convince the Emerald Crusader. If I told him which way was up and down, he was immediately skeptical, but if Cliston said that the void of space was made out of black chicken soup, he would take it as gospel.

“So how much money do we have?” I asked Cliston.

“Enough to get by for a while, but we could really use some more.”

“I’m not sure if I should lay low because of the Navy.”

“Aren’t you the Master Overseer of Navy and Belvaille Relations, sir?”

“Yeah. But that didn’t stop them from blowing up my house.”

“Knowing the PCC, perhaps you could just ask them.”

“Ask them what?”

“If they are trying to arrest you for your relationship with the Dredel Led.”

“Why would they tell me?”

“Because you are the Master Overseer of Navy and Belvaille Relations.”

“You know, Cliston, that’s just PCC enough to work.”





CHAPTER 44




I called up to try and make an appointment with Mr. Boshe, but to my surprise no one had ever heard of him. After about an hour of transfers and arguments, I thought I was going crazy. Had I just imagined Mr. Boshe on his battlecruiser?

But no, he was still around.

It seemed his promotion finally came through and he was now the Joint High Wardian and Marshall of the South-by-Southwest Region of the Post-Colmarian Confederation Colmarian Confederation Navy.

That, plus five credits, will get you a gob of spit and some change.

Actually, that wasn’t fair. I was just crabby because I got the runaround for so long. I had only met a Wardian once before, in the old Colmarian Confederation, and they were a big deal. I had to assume they were still a big deal in the PCC.

Though why this area was considered the South-by-Southwest Region was beyond me. Belvaille was almost the exact center of the empire.

The new Wardian wanted to talk to me. But before I was going to fly out to meet him, I wanted some assurances they weren’t going to arrest or try and kill me.

He sent, by space courier, a form. It was Disposition Pergola 1-38-3-3-3. I had them deliver it to The Club and I picked it up the next day.

“It says they won’t arrest you,” Cliston stated, examining the form.

“That’s it?”

“It’s ten pages long, but it quite clearly states they won’t arrest, harm, or kill you. Provided you don’t misbehave.”

“Misbehave? Does it literally use that word?”

“No, sir. It lists just about every possible thing you could do. Knock over trash receptacles; steal office supplies; paint graffiti on the walls; pickpocket Navy personnel; and…quite a bit more,” Cliston said.

“Are there any things I could accidentally do?”

Cliston looked me over for a moment.

“Just keep your hands and feet to yourself and don’t speak to anyone other than the Wardian. You should be fine.”

I had Zzzho drop me off at the Armage.

“Wish me luck,” I said.

“They just disintegrated your entire building?” he asked.

“Yup.”

“I think you’re going to need more than luck.”

There was a holdup when I docked. Security was unbelievably tight on the ship—I guess they were on official war footing.

“I’m a ‘personage,’” I explained, remembering my experiences the previous times I boarded.

“Oh. That’s a whole other form,” the clerk said.

Once they filled it out, I was frisked, and they confiscated Gun. I tried to argue, but debating with PCC officials just wasn’t going to happen, so I gave up.

They handed me two receipts for Gun so I had a slight hope they would give me two Guns when I left.

The new office of the Wardian was impressive. It was everything his old office wasn’t: spacious, uncluttered, imposing.

Mr. Boshe, er, Wardian Boshe, was wearing a crisp blue uniform. Though he only had one medal on his chest. Like the previous Wardian I had met, he also carried an impractical, outdated weapon on his side. The other Wardian had possessed a sword, but Boshe had a metal flail of some kind.

A flail.

He was standing on a battlecruiser, in space, and he had a stick with a spiky metal cap. It was beyond absurd.

“Hey, you got shoes!” I said.

Wardian Boshe was indeed wearing shiny black boots.

“Leave us,” he said to the two officers who had been observing me with a critical eye.

After the men left, he turned his attention on me.

“Are the Dredel Led destroyed?” he asked.

“You blew up my house. You could have asked me if you wanted them.”

“We did ask you.”

“Well, you didn’t ask politely. You opened fire as soon as I stuck my head out.”

“That was a communication error.”

“I’d say it was more than that. If I wasn’t bulletproof, I’d be dead.”

“But you are bulletproof and you aren’t dead, so there is no point in discussing hypotheticals.”

Hmm, that was kind of true. I kept forgetting that Wardian Boshe was cleverer than the average PCC official.

“Seriously, where did you get those boots? I thought you liked to feel the vibration of the ship and the leaking pipes.”

“Never mind my clothes, you’re wearing a dress. But have the Dredel Led been eliminated?”

“Sure,” I said. There was absolutely zero reason for me to tell him the truth. He’d just blow up City Hall, then whatever other building I came to rest at, until Cliston and the Emerald Crusader were dead.

“Would you be willing to take a lie detector test?” he asked.

“Sure,” I said, smiling. Not only was my body impossible to scan, but they couldn’t even check my pulse. I had no fear of whatever detectors they had.

“Good. I’m sorry, but we are at war. I have orders to eliminate all Dredel Led we encounter.”

“So you’re a Wardian now. Of the Southwest Region.”

“I am the Joint High Wardian and Marshall of the South-by-Southwest Region,” he corrected. “I told you that once we were at war, my promotion would be expedited.”

“You also said you would work on getting us made citizens. How’s that coming?”

“I’ve only had my commission for a handful of days. I don’t even have my full uniform, which is held up in Parliament.”

“They voting on how many stripes, and stars, and doodads to put on your chest?” I joked.

“Yes,” he replied seriously. “But things are getting much better because of the war.”

“That’s not a phrase you hear often.”

“Things are improving. They even dissolved the third House of Parliament to expedite the creation of new policies.”

“Really?” I said, honestly impressed. “What was the third House called? I could never figure it out.”

“I don’t believe they got around to naming it,” Wardian Boshe replied. “Have you thought any more about enlisting? I could almost certainly make you a junior officer.”

“I was an Oberhoffman in the Colmarian Confederation Navy at one point.”

“That wasn’t logged on your records.”

“It was centuries ago. And I had the title for like a week. But no, I haven’t thought much about it. I’ve been avoiding getting killed by the Navy.”

“If you were a member of our corps, things like that wouldn’t happen.”

“I’m sure PCC kill each other all the time just because someone filled out the wrong paperwork.”

“No,” Wardian Boshe almost shouted. “Those days are over. We’re going to clean up this government and we need competent people.”

“So I’m competent?”

“You were Governor of the city, you ran the police force, you were a Factotum—whatever that is—and you had a hand in…quite a few very important incidents over the years. Just the fact you’re still alive proves you’re competent.”

“I guess,” I said absently.

“If you didn’t want to enlist, why are you here?”

“Oh, I wanted to see if you guys were really trying to kill me. Or arrest me.”

“The Dredel Led were the target and they have been eliminated. But I need to know everything about them. Their capabilities, their weaknesses, how they died.”

I shuddered thinking of explaining Cliston’s “capabilities.” I would be here for months.

“How they died was easy: you guys disintegrated the building they were in.”

“And they didn’t leave when you did?”

“No. They’re, uh, stupid.”

“You see? I told you the Dredel Led were no match for us,” Wardian Boshe stated triumphantly. “How did you know to leave before the strike occurred?”

As mad as I was at Garm over other things, I wasn’t about to rat her out.

“I was just going to get something to eat. The Dredel Led don’t eat, so they stayed.”

“You were friendly with them both? I was under the impression that one was attacking you.”

“Yeah, but he was tiny,” I said, motioning with my hands. “I’m still not entirely convinced he was Dredel Led. He looked like a toy.”

“Do you think he could have been reconnoitering?”

“I don’t know what that word means. Is it like loitering?” I asked.

“No. Do you think he was gathering information for a further Dredel Led attack?”

“I don’t think so.”

“Did you sustain any injuries?”

“I just said he was like this tall. If I swallowed him, I don’t think it would have hurt me.”

I was admiring the Wardian’s new office while we talked. He had a lot of fancy items in it and the thug in me was appraising how much they might be worth on the black market.

There was an ornate glowing sign with twisty, three-dimensional letters.

“That looks like Rettosian writing,” I said.

“It is. It was given to me by one of their ambassadors.”

I got worried.

“Was he really, really old and wheezy and puttering around in a wheelchair? And a scientist?”

“No. He was young and arrogant. Why would you inquire about a Rettosian scientist?”

“Oh. Never mind. I just had some issues with him.”

“On Belvaille?” he asked, confused.

“No, this was on Floloria,” I said.

“When were you on Floloria?”

“I don’t know. Years ago. He was the guy who mutated the whole System.”

“I see. Though the reintroduction of mutants into Colmarian society may turn out to be a tremendous benefit.”

“I can fly,” I said.

“What?”

“I was mutated so that I can fly—but only when in space.”

He obviously didn’t believe me.

“Watch,” I said.

I concentrated hard and lifted off the ground for a second. Wardian Boshe seemed entirely unimpressed.

“Yes, I heard most mutations are equally pointless. But it still gives us an added element of surprise. I was asking about Floloria because it is a derelict city.”

“So I’ve heard.”

“The Navy may conduct bombing exercises on it.”

“Well, fire away. Just make sure it’s really empty first. So far you guys have a history of blowing up occupied buildings.”





CHAPTER 45




I was walking across the Armage on my way back to the dock. I had been aboard this damn battlecruiser so many times now that I was starting to know my way around.

I noticed the ship seemed to be operating more smoothly. Less backwards and inefficient. Less PCC.

“Psst,” someone said.

I looked around but saw no one.

“Psst,” I heard again.

“Huh?”

“Idiot, over here,” Garm said.

She was wedged between two bulkheads and standing in the only shadows on the entire ship.

“Are you a ventriloquist? I couldn’t tell where it was coming from. I thought I had sprung a leak.”

“It’s these metal walls. Sounds echo. Walk with me. But stay three steps behind,” she said.

“I can’t fit in there.”

She sighed and began walking down a hallway, waving her hand backwards so I wouldn’t follow too closely.

“What are you doing here?” she asked.

“I could ask you the same thing.”

“I work for the Navy. Wardian Boshe is the head of the Navy in this area.”

“Wardian,” I snickered. “He was Mister Boshe a few days ago.”

“He could have been a striped cupcake a few days ago, he’s a Wardian now. So why are you here?” she asked.

“I wanted to see if the Navy was trying to kill me.”

“No, just your Dredel Led. Is Cliston okay?”

“I don’t know if I should tell you.”

Garm stopped, but didn’t look at me.

“Why?”

“You’ve been lying to me this whole time. You even said I shouldn’t trust you. Well, I don’t.”

“I’m the one who warned you to get out of your house. Last time I checked you were alive because of me.”

“You didn’t warn me that the Navy was going to come shooting up my building in the first place.”

“I didn’t know.”

“How did you not know? You make a big point to say you’re in charge of the Navy on Belvaille.”

“I’m an Adjunct Overwatch on a non-citizen space station. I’m just supposed to keep the port open and the telescopes functioning.”

“What about hacking the Dredel Led?”

“That again? I told you I don’t know anything about it,” she said, and either she had gotten better at lying to me, or she was really telling the truth.

“Have you gotten better at lying?” I asked.

“What are you on about? Did you find who was hacking?”

“Yes. It was the Navy. In a Navy building. With Navy guards. On Belvaille. Miss Adjunct Overwatch.”

“Where?”

“About thirteen blocks south of City Hall. That weird domed building.”

“You think I know the architecture of every house on Belvaille? What was inside?”

“Hacking stuff. I don’t know. But we traced the transmissions to that building and it was a Navy facility.”

Garm seemed to think about that.

“Did you attack the guards?”

“Didn’t have to, they left it unprotected for hours.”

“Are you sure it was Navy? It’s not hard to buy a uniform.”

“Yes, I’m sure it was Navy. There’s not many people left on Belvaille and I know them all. The Navy has every reason to hack the Dredel Led—especially now that we’re at war.”

“But you were saying this was happening before the war. And, honestly, the war has taken everyone by surprise.”

“You start hacking people and they get upset.”

“I guess I can try and look into it,” she said reluctantly.

“Golly, don’t hurt yourself. I mean it’s only what caused the war.”

“It didn’t cause the war. And the war hasn’t even started,” she said.

“I got ambushed by a Dredel Led while I was sitting in The Club!”

“I know, but you don’t really count. The Dredel Led could be trying to kill you for all sorts of reasons.”

“And this is why I don’t trust you.”

“Good. Maybe next time I won’t warn you to leave your house.”

“How many times are they going to blow it up?”

“I doubt they would destroy City Hall. It’s too big and important.”

“How do you know where I live?”

“Hank, you aren’t exactly hard to notice. Is the building comfortable?”

“It’s okay,” I said, annoyed. “Hey, so promise me the Navy isn’t doing anything else dastardly. You know, something that might kill me.”

“It’s the PCC Navy. They’re doing a billion things you would think are dastardly. That’s why I don’t tell you. As for it killing you, you’re working on the Skim. It’s not as if you’re Hank the Helpful.”

“I didn’t invent the Source-Destination Doctrine,” I said.

“I didn’t either. You’re doing what you can to get by, and so am I. So far, you’ve gotten your house blown up, ended up in the hospital, and I’ve gotten about a thousand paper cuts. I think I’m doing alright.”

“If you consider working for the enemy ‘alright,’” I muttered.

“What is it with you and the PCC?”

“Oh, I don’t know, maybe the fact that if I hadn’t gotten your call, Cliston would be dead and I would be dead. That’s two really awesome people, gone from the galaxy, just because a miniature robot fired some plastic pellets at me. I can’t say I approve of the PCC’s judgment.”

“But a city full of gang bosses and criminals is okay?”

“They’re just greedy. The PCC are beyond that. I can’t even figure out what they want.”

“To rebuild the Colmarian Confederation,” she said without hesitation.

My head almost popped off my shoulders.

“See? As if the Colmarian Confederation was some shining star of benevolence and organization. You were in it. You know how moronic the Confederation was.”

“Yeah, I do. Moronic enough to let me run an illegal space station while working for them. You better hope the PCC doesn’t get any more competent or they’ll find out about the Skim and crush it.”

“That’s pretty harsh.”

“You want nice and safe you better find another galaxy to live in.”

“All I’m asking is you keep me informed,” I told Garm.

“Okay, then you tell me why the Dredel Led declared war on us. I’m betting you know.”

“Kai’Un.”

“What’s that?” she asked.

“It’s a million-brained Dredel Led that voted for war,” I said, leaving out my involvement.

“Is it their leader?”

“No. Dredel Led don’t have a leader. They vote by consensus and he has a million votes.”

“A million votes aren’t that many,” she said.

“The Dredel Led only have like five million…people.”

“Really? Did a bunch of them die?”

“That’s what I asked. Cliston said there never were that many of them.”

“How did the Colmarian Confederation manage to have a war with them for thousands of years?” Garm pondered.

“Yeah, I said that too. Because the Colmarian Confederation sucked. And the PCC is trying to recreate it.”

“This will be our second attempt at making it. Maybe we’ll do better this time,” she said.

“Yeah, and maybe I’ll paint my face yellow and tell everyone I’m a star.”





CHAPTER 46




“You want to skim from our Skim?” Roll Bungalow asked.

“Not exactly,” I said. “We need to take about 15% of the ship’s cargo.”

I was in Roll’s office. He was wearing his tight, gold shorts, no shirt. With him was his mutant friend, and my mutant enemy, big-toothed Xandale.

I was trying to smooth things over for the Society of the Free and Easy to do the job Thad’s Friend assigned us: to heist transport BA-553J9. But I was meeting some resistance.

“If you take that much cargo we won’t be able to skim at all. In fact, the ship will probably be stopped, inspected, and the crew arrested,” Roll said.

“I know,” I answered weakly.

“We already have buyers lined up,” Xandale said. “We’ve already bribed the crew. Everything is ready to go. That’s our skim!”

“Yeah, I’m sorry about this, but you can’t skim BA-553J9.”

“Says who?” Xandale asked.

“Says me. But also says a lot more guns and fists than you want to deal with. Trust me on that.”

“Trust you? Is that a joke? When you come in here and tell me I’m going to lose a fortune,” Roll said.

I knew this wasn’t going to go over well. What was even more ironic about it was the transport was going to use the Portal that had been monitored by Protocol Officer Sylvan-Fre. So I had gotten him transferred, which opened the Portal back up for business, and then I was saying the first Skim they had planned was cancelled.

“You can get your money back from the crew,” I said.

“And how are we supposed to do that? Ask them politely?” Xandale replied.

“No, I would ask them impolitely. But this needs to happen.”

“We set up this Skim first. We did all the legwork and we did it according to the rules. Your rules, I want to point out. Now you drop in and say you’re skimming it and we have to back off.”

“I’m not skimming it. I’m robbing it. Skim is 2%.”

“I know what a Skim is. I’m asking why your job takes precedence!” Roll said angrily.

“It’s just this one time,” I said.

“We could call the cops on you,” Xandale said.

“The cops? MTB isn’t going to care about this stuff. He doesn’t get involved in settling criminal disagreements.”

“He’ll care that you’re breaking your own agreements on the Skim,” Xandale said.

“You don’t know MTB very well. I swear, he doesn’t care how a bunch of crooks do their crooked crookery.”

“I don’t like being jerked around,” Roll sulked.

“We could call the Navy,” Xandale suggested.

“Nah. Even if you called the Navy, I’m the Master Overseer of Navy and Belvaille Relations. I talk with Mr. Wardian Boshe all the time—he even asked me to join the Navy. You guys are just some non-citizens from a non-citizen space station.”

“If you joined the Navy, they would make you do push-ups,” Roll said.

“I’d have Delovoa give me a doctor’s note excusing me.”

Xandale was stewing but Roll was more pragmatic.

“I want to be reimbursed. If word gets out that you’re cutting in on Skims, that won’t make your life any easier around here.”

“How much?” I asked.

“Four hundred large,” Roll said.

“Be serious. You weren’t even going to clear that much money off your Skim. I can maybe swing five figures.”

“That’s spit,” Xandale blurted.

“Everyone’s on a budget. I’d have to give you half of what I was going to earn just to cover that.”

“You’re not earning very much,” Roll said. “Tell me, is this because you lost your house recently?”

“I didn’t lose it. It’s not like I misplaced it. I moved to a better home.”

“And you blew up your old one?” Xandale asked suspiciously.

“It wasn’t blown up. It’s just not there. I didn’t like the idea of anyone living in it after me,” I explained. I obviously wasn’t going to tell them Boshe did it when I was trying to suggest that I was on good terms with the Navy.

“The crew will report this to the PCC so they don’t get in trouble. Then it will blow back on my gang. They’ll think we did it,” Roll said.

“No. I’m pretty sure it will look like a robbery when we’re done,” I replied.

“What are you going to do about the Navy investigating?” Roll asked.

“I don’t know, honestly. I’m not very worried about the PCC Navy learning anything, but I’m worried if they do increase security overall.”

“You don’t sound like you have this very thought out,” Xandale said.

“I didn’t have a lot of time. The transport is leaving in two days and I didn’t know about it until recently.”

“If you can help us get our bribe money back from the transport crew, I’ll accept fifty grand as a payoff,” Roll said finally.

“How can I ‘help’ you get your money back? Hold your hands while you beat them up?”

“He means for you to get our money back,” Xandale clarified.

“I don’t have a relationship. You’ve already worked with them,” I said.

“If we back out now, they’ll know we’re responsible for your robbery.”

“They’ll know either way. If I take back the bribe money or you take back the bribe money, they’ll know the bribe is off.”

“Okay, let me rephrase it: I’m making you do it because you caused this.”

I sighed.

“Alright, then.”

Xandale was clearly not happy and he leaned in to whisper to Roll.

“No, it’s fine. We’ll make it back,” Roll said to Xandale. “But this won’t happen again, will it?” Roll asked me.

“No, it won’t happen again,” I said.



Getting the money back from the crew wasn’t all that hard.

I have centuries of experience breaking kneecaps and strongly suggesting that I was about to break someone’s kneecaps.

The ship crew needed their kneecaps to pilot the ship, so it was an easy transaction.





CHAPTER 47




I was with the Society of the Free and Easy. And Frank.

We were in my home at City Hall, standing around some blueprints I had procured, planning the mission from the Rettosian scientist, aka Thad’s Friend.

“Frank will be our guard outside the ship. If anyone comes snooping around the docking bay, he can take care of them,” I said, pointing to the blueprint.

“I think Shella would be better at that. Frank’s combat experience will be wasted out there,” Najosa said.

“Frank can talk his way out of most scrapes without resorting to fighting. We want to get in and get out without causing any problems,” I said.

“Why?” Nordan asked.

“Because, that’s what you do. That’s how these jobs are run,” I answered, annoyed. “This isn’t an invasion. We are stealing goods. The less trouble we cause, the less trouble we’ll have.”

Frank shrugged. He didn’t care, as long as he was getting paid.

I motioned back to the blueprint.

“I rented this warehouse. It’s as close to the ship as I could get. Alase, you said you could operate a heavy lifter, right?”

“I’ve run them before, but I’m not great at it,” she explained.

“You’re going to be making three trips, wheeling around 120,000 pounds of electronics each time. You better be great at it. The streets in the dock are wide, but if you go smashing into buildings, this operation is going to fall apart.”

“She’ll be fine,” Najosa said.

“Nordan and Meltar will go into the ship’s cockpit to secure our access to the cargo bay and make sure no one interferes with us. Alase drives the heavy lifter and Shella will be directing traffic. Tell everyone you see that you’re moving hazardous chemicals. That should clear them out.”

“What are you and Najosa doing?” Frank asked.

“We go into the cargo bay and make way for the heavy lifter. This is a massive freighter. There might be crew members in there or security guards. I’m actually expecting some resistance, so most of our combat expertise needs to be there.”

“Why are you expecting resistance?” Najosa asked.

“I just am,” I said. I didn’t feel the need to explain that I had told Roll Bungalow that we were coming. Hopefully, he was okay with the deal I had given him.

I showed Alase where the heavy lifter was stored and gave her the keys.

Cliston brought us coffee and finger food.

“You know, Hank, I had my doubts about you for a while,” Nordan said. “I thought you were a bit of a buffoon. But you’re really good at this.”

“I’ve been pulling these capers since before your great-grandparents were born,” I said.

“I don’t have great-grandparents. My grandparents were grown in a vat,” he replied.

“Then before the vat was molded. Does everyone understand what they’re supposed to do?” I asked.

“What kind of weapons should I bring?” Najosa asked.

“Pistols, submachine guns. Nothing with supersonic ammunition or high penetration. If you damage the containers or the equipment that operates the containers, we’re going to have a tough time fulfilling this contract.”

“What if they’re wearing bulletproof armor?” she asked.

“Then they get to see how much of a combat model Ontakian you are,” I replied.



Everyone was in place.

Najosa and I were standing with Frank in the docking bay next to the rear of the freighter.

Just the back door on this thing was bigger than my house. Well, my old house that got vaporized.

Meltar and Nordan were headed to the control area and would be able to let us in, as well as stabilize the ship for unloading. It was possible that there were some crew members hanging out up there, but they likely wouldn’t be armed, and by the time they figured out it was a robbery, they’d have guns in their faces.

“The ship controls are very archaic. This is an old transport. What planet is it from again?” Meltar asked on the tele.

“Doesn’t matter. Keep the chatter to a minimum, teles aren’t secure,” I replied.

We were all sharing a conference call so we could use our teles like radios. It was a bit clunky to set up and use, but it beat trying to shout across multiple city blocks.

The cargo door began lowering with all kinds of flashing lights and sirens, which made me cringe. It wasn’t exactly the kind of welcome you wanted during a burglary.

“Cut the sirens if you can,” Najosa said, reading my thoughts.

The door paused and then started raising back up.

“No, forget it, just keep going,” she said.

It took ten minutes for the door to open completely. Anyone inside, even if they had been asleep, were now ready for us.

“Don’t die,” Frank said to me, by way of encouragement.

“Thanks. Don’t kill anyone,” I replied.

Najosa was wearing light body armor. It wouldn’t stop any serious weapons, but with a little luck, we wouldn’t be facing anyone.

It took seven minutes just to walk up the ramp that led into the cargo bay. It really was a gigantic ship. It was probably the size of Wardian Boshe’s battlecruiser. But instead of guns and compartments and crew barracks, it was nearly all one big cargo hold.

The ceiling was about 300 feet above us and the far walls in any other direction couldn’t be seen. There was endless row after row of containers, and the machinery to help move, secure, and maintain them.

I tended to look down on transport crews for the most part. It was a dull job with low pay. But seeing the insane amount of storage here gave me a real appreciation for what went on. This ship was capable of moving an entire planet’s trade goods. In the case of this ship, it was carrying food, water, clothing, and our target: electronic components.

If it crashed on an uninhabited planet, it would leave just about everything needed for a small colony to survive and thrive for a good quarter century.

“Quit gawking and come on,” Najosa said at my open-mouthed staring.

I felt like an insect walking amongst all the containers.

“If someone is here, where do you think they’d be stationed?” Najosa asked.

“I haven’t got a clue,” I replied.

We walked side by side, her with a snub submachine gun ready. Me holding Gun.

“See if you can get some more light back here,” Najosa said into her tele.

“Affirmative,” came the reply.

We kept going, straining our eyes at the shadows and looking for ambushes.

Then all the overhead lights powered down.

“Now all the lighting is off,” Najosa said to her tele.

“Hank. You’ve brought company,” I heard Xandale say from somewhere to the front and right of us.

The sirens turned on again. What little light there was, started to completely disappear—the bay door was being closed.

“If we want to get out of here, now is our only chance,” I whispered to Najosa.

“We came for the job and we’ll finish it. Who called your name?” she asked.

“Xandale. He’s a mutant. He can turn…I guess he can make himself into a ghost.”

“What?”

“He can become like smoke. But he can’t hurt me when he’s like that.”

“What about me?” she asked.

It was too dark for her to see me shrug in ignorance.

“Xandale. I have a deal with your boss. This is our transport.”

“No, Hank. You have a deal with Belvaille. With everyone here. It’s called the Skim, remember?”

Najosa fired off a dozen shots in the direction of Xandale’s voice. The bullets ricocheted off several containers.

“You know what a ghost is, right?” I asked her.

“Xandale, I told you why we had to do this,” I yelled.

“No, you didn’t. You just said it had to be done. You said to hell with the Skim.”

“This isn’t about the Skim,” I replied.

“It is!” He said. The location of his voice was moving. “Everything is the Skim. It’s our one chance to make the city grow again. Now you feel like changing the rules, changing the agreements, and we’re all supposed to be okay with it.”

Xandale was right. I beat it into everyone’s heads how the Skim would work. How it had to work. Now I was putting everything at risk, the whole space station’s livelihood, for what? A crusty old Rettosian I didn’t even like and some credits.

Oh, and Najosa. I was doing this because Najosa wanted it.

“Deals change, Xandale, you know that,” I said.

“This wasn’t a deal. Roll Bungalow is just one small part of Belvaille. You have to square this with everyone who is in on the Skim. It affects us all.”

“I can’t do that. There wasn’t enough time,” I said.

“You knew no one would agree. I can’t understand why you’re doing it. If you rob this freighter like you described, it will then be in violation of the Source-Destination Doctrine. Do you think no one will notice that? A freighter comes in, passes all inspections, docks with Belvaille, then loses a significant portion of its cargo. The PCC is bad, but they aren’t that bad.”

Najosa snorted in disagreement.

I touched her hand to indicate which direction we should go.

“So, Xandale, is this your game plan: hope we’re scared of the dark?”

“No. I contacted the Navy and reported there were thieves on board this ship. Knowing the PCC, it will take them an hour to even find the ship, but it should stop you.”

“They’ll catch you too!”

“I can walk through walls,” he said.

“Oh. Well, if you’re worried about the Skim, bringing cops here isn’t going to help.”

“No, but it’s a lot better than a ship breaking the Doctrine when it’s on our soil,” he said.

“Does Roll Bungalow know you’re doing this?” I asked, trying to change tactics.

“No. This is my job. I consider it my last official act as a Factotum. You broke an agreement. An agreement that you yourself set up. If you were me, you would be sitting here doing the exact same thing I am.”

That hurt. Because it was true.

Xandale had been a Factotum along with me. We were official negotiators between nobles and…everyone else. Xandale was maybe better at it than I was. No, he definitely was better. He wasn’t a mutant back then and he still got way more business and way more money than I did.

I respected Xandale. I didn’t like him at all, but I respected him. He had tried to kill me multiple times. But he always gave me the opportunity to back out.

“Is there any way we can come to a compromise?” I asked.

“Yes. Leave,” he replied.

He was doing it again. He’d told me the cops were coming. He was giving me an out.

But I wasn’t the kind of guy who took the easy—or smart—way.

“I can’t do that, Xandale.”

“Then this is happening,” he said.

And nothing happened.

I was standing with my knees bent, my eyes wide, and ready for anything.

“Uh, what’s happening?” I asked, after a moment.

“Wait for it.”

There was a terrific scraping of metal from all around me and I thought the freighter must somehow be caving in. But no, it was the machinery.

The containers were all rotating on their enormous spindles.

The whole inside of the freighter was like a big conveyer belt of containers. Millions of pounds of trade goods were rocking and turning and twisting around past us.

“We need to get to a wall,” I said. “One of these containers hits us and we’re going to be a wet smear on the ground.”

“I can’t see anything,” Najosa said.

We flipped the lights on our teles, but compared to the cavernous interior of the ship, they provided almost no illumination.

I grabbed Najosa by the shoulder and pulled her back, right as a container swung up and came within eight inches of hitting her side.

“I’ve lost my bearings. Which way do we go?” she asked.

I couldn’t see much, but the containers were flipping around vertically, horizontally, and going every direction imaginable. It’s like the machinery had gone crazy. I don’t know how the containers weren’t smashing into each other, but I was at least happy for that small miracle.

Because of the movement of the machines, I was disoriented as well.

“Meltar. Nordan. Shut off the carousels in the cargo hold!” I yelled into my tele to be heard over the noise.

“We didn’t turn it on,” came the reply.

“I didn’t say you did. Just turn it off.”

We shined our lights all around and sidestepped the twisting and turning containers.

“We can’t turn it off. The controls aren’t working,” Meltar said.

“Xandale must be using the controls in here,” I said to Najosa.

“That doesn’t make sense. Why wouldn’t the cockpit be able to override the local controls?” Najosa asked.

“It makes perfect sense. The people down here know what’s happening more than the cockpit. Those are probably back-up controls. Unfortunately, I don’t know where the controls are in here, and even if I did, I wouldn’t know how to use them,” I said.

“Look out!”

I got grazed in the back by the corner of a container. As heavy as I am, I went flying a good eight feet and ended up sprawled on the ground.

I had to roll out of the way as another container came screaming past.

“Let’s go that way,” I said, as Najosa helped me to my feet.

“Why that way? Do you see something?”

“No, but it’s not here.”

We were swinging our tele lights around like they were protective talismans, but the fact of the matter is we got lucky an awful lot of times. The containers were moving tremendously fast. A few missteps would kill us.

I couldn’t make any sense of their movement patterns. The whole system was too big and complicated—not to mention it was almost completely dark.

“There, some lights,” I said.

I pointed and got my arm promptly smacked by a passing container.

“Ow!”

“What are the lights for?”

“How should I know? But it will give us something to move toward and keep our equilibrium. With everything moving, I’m getting vertigo.”

“I see it. It’s a blue light.”

“Yeah.”

We made our way to it, and almost got squashed between two containers. I had three buttons scraped off the front of my jacket.

I thought the light must be far away, until I almost ran into it. It was just a small light, with hardly any brightness. Like a child’s nightlight.

But we had reached one of the walls of the transport.

Shining our teles around, we saw that against the wall we had a good ten feet of safety from even the most recklessly tilting container.

“Now what?” Najosa asked.

“You need to look for the controls down here.”

“I won’t know how to use them any more than you do,” she said.

“No, but you can get Meltar and Nordan to guide you via tele. You also need to call Alase and get her down here with that heavy lifter.”

“The door isn’t even open. And the containers are going crazy! How can she possibly unload anything?”

“That heavy lifter moves maybe five miles an hour. The PCC Navy is going to be here soon. If we wait until we got everything situated, we won’t have time. If the cops come before we fix this, she can get arrested along with the rest of us.”

“What if we don’t fix it?”

“Then she can help identify our corpses,” I said.

“What are you going to do?”

“I have to find Xandale. If he keeps messing with the controls, we won’t ever get out of here in one piece.”

“Do you think you can survive if one of those containers hits you?”

“Hits me? Yeah. Lands on me? No. But I can take a lot more abuse than you can. This is why I’m here, right?”

“Okay. I’ll go along the walls and try and find the controls. Should I just turn the machines off?”

“That’s a start. But also see if you can turn off the artificial gravity.”

“Why?” she asked, shocked.

“Because I can fly.”





CHAPTER 48




I moved along the wall for as long as I could, but I felt Xandale would be hiding amongst the moving containers. If I was a ghost, that’s where I would be.

As I hopped, dodged, and ran my way through the jaws of the giant metal rectangles, I couldn’t help reflecting on how I got here.

This whole situation didn’t feel right. Not only the fact I was about to get flattened by a huge collection of stylish work clothes, but because I had practically known this was going to happen. I worked my ass off helping establish the careful rules of the Skim and then I went to my arch rival and told him I was just kidding about all that.

I think I subconsciously hoped there would be trouble here. That way I could spend my time dodging bullets instead of thinking about how messed up this mission was.

Even though I was thinking about it anyway.

And what exactly was I supposed to do to Xandale once I caught up to him—assuming I could find a ghost in the dark.

Gun said he could see Xandale before, but he couldn’t shoot him.

“Hey, Gun,” I said, taking out my pistol.

“Hello.”

“Can you see in the dark?”

“What’s a dark?” he asked.

I waved Gun around.

“Can you see anyone out there?”

“Yes.”

“Is it Najosa or someone else?”

“How would I know that?” he asked.

“She’s a she. I mean, she’s…” I really didn’t know how to explain the difference between men and women to a pistol that didn’t even know what darkness was.

“She’s smaller than me. A lot smaller,” I clarified.

“Everyone is smaller than you,” Gun replied.

I was used to either strange, metaphysical responses from Gun, or absolute literal truths. So his statement confused me. He had seen plenty of people larger than me.

“Wait. Did you just make a joke, Gun?”

“I don’t know. Was it funny?”

“Hey, don’t fool around. I can’t understand you half the time as it is.”

“I see someone,” Gun said.

“What are they doing?”

“What are any of us doing?” he replied.

That was a more typical Gun answer.

“I want to get to that person you see.”

“Can you walk?”

“Yes. But which direction do I go?”

“Not that way.”

“Not which way? I’m not moving.”

“Not that way either.”

“What are you talking about? I haven’t moved.”

I couldn’t argue with Gun around all these containers. I needed to find a safer place to talk.

“I said not that way,” Gun said, after I took a few steps.

“What?”

I felt my stomach jump into my throat and I almost threw up.

I was weightless.

“Was that you, Najosa?” I asked on the tele.

“Yes. I found some of the environmental systems but nothing that controls the cargo containers,” she replied.

“Keep looking.”

I concentrated, stretched out my left arm above my head, and I flew.

Directly into a container that had been above me.

I was pushed back downward, but instead of smashing me, my weightless body was merely nudged along.

I flew out from under the container and tried to resume my upward direction. But without gravity, it was hard to tell which way was which.

“Not that way,” Gun said.

I was about to question my pistol when I ran into one of the armatures that moved the containers.

“Ah!” I said, rubbing my shoulder.

Okay, flying in the dark maybe wasn’t the best idea I had.

I pulled myself up the machinery, trying not to get my hands caught in the rotating chains and cables.

“This way leads toward the person,” Gun said.

So Xandale—or Najosa—was above me? Or was I so disoriented I only thought I was going up?

In any case, I slowly flew that way. I didn’t accelerate very much, having learned the hard way that I didn’t have the ability to stop.

I bumped into several more containers, but I was brushed harmlessly aside and I continued onward.

After some tense moments, I finally saw a light ahead.

“Hello, Hank,” Xandale said. “Are you flying?”

Xandale was in a control booth. I couldn’t tell its orientation to the rest of the cargo hold, but it made sense if it was high above, so it could provide a better line of sight. Xandale could see me because I was still shining my tele light ahead of me.

I grabbed hold of the control booth and for a second I thought I was going to miss and fly on past. But I managed to catch the railing and haul myself inside.

It was spacious in the control booth, with room for maybe a half-dozen people. There were scores of low-light tele screens which showed the various angles of the cargo hold. He had probably been watching us the whole time.

Xandale was smoky. I couldn’t hurt him and he couldn’t hurt me.

“Yeah, I was flying. It’s my new mutation I picked up during the incident.”

“Wow. Why don’t you fly around all the time?” he asked casually.

“I don’t like to show off,” I said, not wanting to say it only worked in zero gravity.

“Since when were you shy?” he asked.

I figured, while Xandale was smoky, he couldn’t interfere with me. I went over to the controls and tried to sort them out.

There were approximately four billion buttons, ten billion switches, and maybe a quadrillion little knobs.

I started pressing and flipping and twisting.

“Is your goal to blow this ship up?” he asked. “Because I suspect randomly activating controls isn’t going to work out very well.”

“Then tell me how to turn off the containers,” I said, annoyed.

“Sure. This big switch here will shut down all the conveyors,” he said, pointing.

I hadn’t expected him to actually tell me. Was he telling me? Was it a trap? Like the switch was really how to eject the control booth into space.

“Honest,” Xandale said.

I reached out to the switch, watching Xandale’s smoky poker face. He didn’t have a traditional poker face: he was always smiling. But it worked out the same. It was hard to tell what he was thinking.

“Do you swear as a Factotum?” I asked.

“I’m not a Factotum any longer. Neither are you, clearly. Since you feel you can change agreements whenever it suits you.”

I clenched my jaw and flipped the switch.

All the machinery slowed down and stopped.

Huh. It was the right switch.

I heard a click and then all the machinery turned back on.

Xandale had gone from smoky, to not smoky, flipped the same switch I did, and went back to smoky. All in the blink of an eye.

“You’re not going to rob this ship,” Xandale stated.

“Hank, was that you?” Najosa asked on my tele.

“Go ahead and take the call. I can wait,” Xandale said.

“Yeah, it was me. I’m in a control tower…I think at the top of the ship. Xandale is here,” I said.

“Kill him,” Najosa said.

“She’s charming,” Xandale said to me.

“It’s not that simple,” I replied, then hung up.

I flipped the control switch off again and it was flipped back on almost instantly.

I then turned it off and kept my finger pressed on it. I knew I was stronger than Xandale by quite a bit.

The machines all turned back on.

“Got to love these redundant systems,” he said, on the other side of the booth.

I had to toggle the switch on and off again to power down the machines, but Xandale just turned them back on.

“I’d like to say this is fun, but it’s pretty boring, Hank. I’m not going to materialize so you can rip my head off, and you can’t steal anything while the containers are moving. It’s a stalemate—except for the fact the Navy is on its way.”

“Xandale, I’ll give you my entire cut of the heist,” I said.

“You are desperate. So what has you in this situation? Do you owe someone? Is your life on the line? Make me understand why you’re putting the whole space station at risk. Better talk fast, you’re running out of time.”

I thought about what to say.

“It’s a girl.”

“You’re joking. You? Hank? It’s not that one you came in with, is it?”

“No,” I lied.

“I’m happy you fell in love and all but I’m afraid I don’t see how that’s worth it.”

“This job also involves the guy who mutated us,” I said.

Xandale seemed interested in that, if I could accurately judge his smoky expressions.

“Involved how?” he asked.

“This assignment is from him.”

“You’re working for him? Why? Who is he? There’s about a hundred million or so people who want him dead.”

“He’s a Rettosian. A scientist.”

“I figured he wasn’t a magician. Though I’m surprised it was just one person.”

“He’s not just one person. He has a bunch of clones,” I said.

“Clones?”

Xandale clicked off the machines as he stood there deep in thought. He then turned them back on.

“Sorry, I still don’t see that it matters. Even if you were to kill the guy, the mutations are done. We need to get on with our lives. The Skim needs to be maintained. But tell me more about the woman.”

I flipped off the machines and Xandale turned them back on.

“She’s like me,” I said.

“Fat?”

“Why does everyone say I’m fat? I’m heavy. I’m a mutant,” I said.

“I’m a mutant, too. I’m not fat or heavy. Seriously, Hank, you’re going to get arrested when the Navy arrives.”

Xandale was right. I needed a new plan. I couldn’t keep flipping switches forever.

“Hey, Gun,” I said.

“Hello.”

“Can you shoot that guy if he turns solid?” I asked.

“I suppose,” Gun said.

“Okay, Xandale. I’m giving you the warning that you usually give me. Leave the machines off or my gun will shoot you,” I said.

“He doesn’t sound very sure,” Xandale stated.

“Gun.”

“Hello?”

“Will it kill him if you shoot him?”

“Kill him? No,” Gun replied.

My heart sank and Xandale smiled even bigger.

“Why not?” I asked uselessly. I flipped off the machines so I could hear Gun’s answer easier.

“Because he’s already dead,” Gun said.

Across the booth, Xandale turned to normal, switched the machines back on, then:

Blam!

Xandale had a bloody hole in his solid, unsmoky chest. His eyes glazed over and he slumped to the floor of the control booth.

Gun didn’t shoot. I didn’t feel tired. And he didn’t say “bang” like he always does. The sound came from behind me somewhere.

“Do you want me to shoot him now?” Gun asked.

“No,” I said.

I went over to check on Xandale, who I found was very much dead.

“Good work, Hank,” Najosa said, as she climbed up into the booth.

“What happened?” I asked.

“I shot him. I saw him changing back and forth and I timed it right when he had his hand over the switch.”

She activated her tele.

“Nordan, Meltar, get this cargo hold restored to order. The lifter is going to be here any moment,” she said.

“On it,” came the reply.

I couldn’t get over the fact that Xandale was dead.

I’ve killed a lot of people in my life, but almost none of them were worthy of respect. They were just cogs, their faces blurred in my memory. But Xandale, for all his annoyance, was legit. He knew the code and played by the rules—however loosely we defined them. I didn’t like the fact I had a part in his murder.

At least not like this.

The lights turned back on, along with the gravity, and the containers rearranged themselves back into an ordered state.

“We’re here,” Alase said on the tele.

“We need to get three containers. First, we have to find out which ones have electronics in them,” Najosa started.

“We don’t have time. Grab one container and everyone get out of here,” I said.

“Our deal was for three large containers,” Najosa told me.

“Xandale said he called the Navy, they could be here any minute.”

“He could have lied about that.”

“He wasn’t. It takes ten seconds to call the Navy and he knew we were coming as of two days ago.”

“We can take out any Navy. They won’t be well-equipped if they think they’re going to be dealing with robbers.”

“I’m not fighting the Navy on Belvaille. They’ll put the whole station under martial law. I know how they operate.”

“Who cares about this station?” she asked.

“I do!”

“You’re not running this team, Hank. I am,” Najosa said icily.

“You’re right. You are. But if you want to fight the Navy, that’s your business. I’m walking. I’ll help you take one container and that’s it. There’s no way we can make three trips at heavy lifter speed and still escape.”

Najosa was stewing. I honestly didn’t know what she was going to say now that I gave her an ultimatum.

“Alright. But our client won’t be happy. We’ll have to do a make-up job for him. He already paid us,” she said.

“Whatever. Let’s get this done.”





CHAPTER 49




We had convinced the Emerald Crusader to rename himself something a little less ostentatious. He wanted to be called the Green Crusader, but we got him to settle for Ermie.

Ermie had a few jobs around our new apartment in City Hall.

Because he was so low to the ground, he could find all the hidden dusty areas that Cliston missed with the vacuum. He also helped me take off and put on my socks and shoes.

And finally, he was our elevator man. Robot.

When he would find out I was leaving the apartment, he ran out the door, down the hall, and up a little ladder we had installed by the elevator. Then he would punch the button. Once the doors opened, he would hold them for me. He had a similar ladder on the inside so he could select the ground floor.

Socks or no, I couldn’t see that Ermie had a place here long term. It wasn’t terribly hard to hide a six-inch robot on a deserted space station. But the risk vs. reward simply wasn’t there.

I would be absolutely lost without Cliston. In fact, I couldn’t even remember how I ever survived without him. I think I wore the same clothes a lot, only ate out at restaurants, and didn’t know about the mating rituals of Dorponian Herfonites.

But Ermie was no Cliston.

“What are we going to do with him?” I asked Cliston one night.

“I don’t know, sir. But you have to appreciate that he is merely a pawn in this.”

“He’s about the size of a pawn. Harboring him is dangerous. The Navy is on orders to kill all Dredel Led.”

“I’m a Dredel Led, sir.”

“I know. But you make my pancakes. There’s got to be somewhere we can send him.”

“He doesn’t remember how he arrived here and Dredel Led territory is a long, difficult journey away. As I said, I believe he was created erroneously. He is not a typical military Dredel Led.”

“What is he typical of? Was he designed to clean toilets or something and they just sent the wrong robot with the wrong instructions?”

“I’ve never heard of a Dredel Led like him, sir.”

“So he’s like Kai’Un in that regards?”

“Kai’Un has a million minds. Ermie…well, let’s just say he does not.”

I thought back to something that Mr. Boshe had said.

“Hey, do Dredel Led fart?” I asked.

“Excuse me?”

“Do you guys like, you know, toot. Fart? Have waste?” I asked seriously.

Cliston paused.

“Different models work differently. I would not say that, for the most part, we expel gasses like many Colmarians do.”

“And Ontakians,” I added.

“Yes. But we do put out heat and various electromagnetic radiations. I suppose one might consider them waste.”

“Hah. I knew it. Wait, so are your farts going to give me cancer?”

“No, sir. It isn’t that kind of radiation.”

“Getting back to Ermie, can you go to Thaxmultaerea and try and get him a ride out of here? Or at least find out what he’s supposed to do?”

“He was supposed to destroy the Colmarian empire. His programming was clear. He just lacked any ability to accomplish it or any insights into how he might go about doing it.”

“Sounds like my professional glocken career.”

“You were quite skilled at glocken, sir. If you had gone to more practices and we hadn’t been playing illegal mutant teams, we may have won the Championship.”

“Do you think I could go back to Thaxmultaerea? I know you took the helmet device thing before my house exploded.”

“I can’t see you being allowed back, sir.”

“What about if I fixed their hacking problem? Do you think we could still get paid on that?”

“That might actually work. But I worry you would be considered a traitor, sir.”

“Why would the Dredel Led care if I was a traitor?”

“Not my people, the PCC.”

“How can I be a traitor to an empire I’m not even a part of? What are they going to do, make me a citizen and then take away my citizenship?”

“I’m anxious that they might kill you, sir.”

I laughed.

“Just about every job I’ve ever done in the last six hundred years has put my life on the line. Usually for work I’m getting paid thousands of credits to do. This job is for fifty million!”

“I honestly can’t say how the Dredel Led will react while we’re at war. And I don’t believe we have a chance of restoring normal relations until we discover what Kai’Un truly is.”

“He’s a big robot who doesn’t like Colmarians. You said that different Dredel Led take turns voting in Thaxmultaerea.”

“That is correct, sir.”

“So when Kai’Un cycles out of the voting pool, won’t they just vote to cancel the war?” I asked.

“No, sir. It is very difficult to make decisions like that. It takes almost a unanimous opinion to start or stop a war. It’s why Kai’Un wields so much influence. Because he somehow managed to get all his minds to vote the same way.”

“If we found him, maybe we could convince him to vote for peace. Or unplug him.”

“I could not determine where he was located or his primary function. He was incredibly secretive.”

“What do you think the chances are of the Dredel Led sending more Ermies to attack us?”

“I think the concern is that Kai’Un will leave Thaxmultaerea and no longer be corrupting the creation of new Dredel Led. In which case more Dredel Led will come attack, but they won’t be inferior specimens like Ermie.”





CHAPTER 50




I was flying out to meet Gax.

The Marquor of Lunacy. The Unfathomable. The Disreputable.

A member of the Boranjame, the most powerful species in the galaxy.

And I was going without an invitation.

This was the first time I had ever presumed to meet a Boranjame on my terms. It was an entirely arrogant and highly dangerous action. But Gax could see the future and I couldn’t. I couldn’t even make sense of the present.

I was hoping the worst he would do was not see me. Then I would have merely wasted a flight out here. Of course, he might also have weapons on that big glowing ship of his and turn the Suckface into the Junkface.

But they allowed us to dock and the Po let me disembark.

I was taken before Gax where his giant tele screens were hooked up.

I got out my own tele so I could record the complicated and confusing poetry that Gax tended to write.



Leave. You should not have come here.



The screen printed.

That wasn’t very poetic.



“I killed one of the Aggatars,” I said.



Agglatar.



“Yeah, him.”



You cannot end the endless.

You cannot kill the unborn.

Your search for peace will bring about war.

Yellow.



I copied it all down. I should get Gun to talk to this guy. Maybe they would appreciate each other. But I felt it was a bad idea to pull a weapon on a Boranjame. Even if it was just to talk.

“We’re already at war. Is there another war? And what’s unborn?” I asked.



You fight with yourself in a cave of deception.



“A cave? I live in City Hall. I could really use some pointers here. Some clear instructions. A lot is going on and I don’t understand any of it.”



Nothing is clear.

The closer you look, the farther your answers will flee.



I was about to open my mouth and ask more when:



Hank. Leave.



About twenty Po servants streamed out and ushered me back to my ship.

I must have had about ten thousand handprints all over me from their “guidance.”



“Did you learn anything?” Zzzho asked, when I returned to the Suckface.

“I have no idea how the Boranjame became so powerful. If I talked like him, every person who met me would want to kill me.”

“I guess it didn’t turn out so good.”

“I really wonder if when they’re alone, the Boranjame drop the funny talk and have a big crystalline belly laugh at our expense. I honestly think they’re mocking us.”

“Us?” Zzzho asked. “You’re the only one who ever talks to them.”





CHAPTER 51




“So why are we out here? I thought we weren’t going to get paid if we find who is hacking the Dredel Led,” Delovoa asked.

I was currently driving him, and his detection equipment, around Belvaille.

“Cliston thinks that maybe if I find the hack, they’ll let me back into Thaxmultaerea. Then I could argue that I deserve to be paid. And while I was at it, I might try and stop the war with the PCC,” I said.

“Why do you care about the war? It’s not like we’re citizens.”

“The last Dredel Led that came here because of the war promptly attacked me. It’s in my self-interest to have peace.”

“How is the Emerald Crusader doing?”

“Ermie. I guess he’s okay. I think he’s getting a bit of cabin fever since we don’t let him out of the apartment.”

“So you’ve got a Dredel Led prisoner? Not a good first step to peace.”

“He’s not a prisoner. Cliston explained it’s for his own good that he stays indoors. If someone from the Navy spots him, they’ll open fire.”

“He’d be hard to hit—hey, watch that car!” Delovoa yelled.

“Woops.”

“Has anyone told you you’re a terrible driver?”

“No.”

“What makes you think the hacking equipment is still on Belvaille? The Navy could put it on any one of those cruisers floating out there.”

“I’m counting on the PCC bureaucracy. At some point, they must have submitted a million forms and approvals to get the hacking facility constructed on Belvaille. It’s probably easier to rebuild it instead of trying to get approval all over again to put it somewhere else.”

“Smart,” Delovoa said.

“I have my moments.”

We drove past the building where I destroyed the equipment the first time.

“This is where it was earlier,” I said.

“I’m not detecting anything. When you used this scanner before, weren’t you on the latticework?”

“Yeah, Cliston helped me. But it’s a lot safer driving.”

“Safer for whom?”



We had crisscrossed the city a number of times and I was starting to lose hope.

“I think I should have mapped out a systematic route instead of driving in circles,” I said.

“That’s all we need is for you to be looking at a map while driving. You know, if you do get paid by the Dredel Led, I get my 4% of the fifty million,” Delovoa said.

“2%.”

“How is it you forget or fumble every single number you’ve ever seen except when it comes to money?”

“Those are the only numbers that are important.”

“Stop,” Delovoa said abruptly.

I slammed on the brakes and Delovoa smacked against the windshield.

“Dammit!” He shouted, when he flopped back on the seat.

“You said stop.”

“I’m picking up a faint signal that matches the pattern we’re looking for.”

“It’s the hack?” I asked.

“I just said it matches the pattern.”

“Okay, which way do I go?”

“Um, that way,” he said.

“There’s a building there.”

Delovoa stared at me.

“I know this may sound crazy, but you can drive around buildings using something called ‘streets.’”

“Alright. Alright.”

I turned and headed north.

“Keep going,” he said.

“We’re going to run out of space station in a little while,” I replied.

“The signal is getting stronger.”

“Can you tell what they’re hacking, exactly? Like, could you break apart the signal and see what they see?”

“No, it’s far too complicated for that. It’s also not remotely my expertise. Garm might know something about it. She’s a former Intelligence operative, right?”

“Yeah, Garm,” I said absently.

And I stopped the car, because there were no streets left in this direction.

“The telescopes,” Delovoa said, looking where we were.

We had reached the northernmost section of the station. The buildings that controlled the telescopes were right in front of us.

“I knew Garm was in on the hack,” I said.

“She might not know.”

“No way. Garm wouldn’t let an operation like this happen under her nose without being intimately involved.”

“So she lied to you. Is that really anything new?” Delovoa asked.

“No. And the Boranjame told me to be careful who I trusted. But Garm swore up and down she didn’t know anything about the hack. And because of her lies, she kept me from earning fifty million credits, and maybe even helped start the war with the Dredel Led.”

“Didn’t you start it by subconsciously hating the PCC when you were in Thaxmultaerea?” Delovoa asked.

“Yeah, but that was an accident. She’s doing this on purpose.”

“You’re not going to attack the telescope installations, are you?” he asked uneasily.

“I’m not crazy. But I’ll say this, I’m absolutely through with trusting Garm.”





CHAPTER 52




“Let me see if I understand this. You expected to steal three cargo containers from a transport, then hire a different transport to haul the containers immediately afterward? And you thought no one would notice? There aren’t a lot of people in the shipping industry on Belvaille you know,” I told Najosa.

We were in my apartment in City Hall. And I had just learned that we needed to deliver the stolen electronic components off-station. The problem was, we had violated the rules of the Skim.

My rules. The rules I had made everyone agree to at the outset.

It was going to be really difficult finding anyone to help us.

“We only have one container because of you,” Najosa said.

“We aren’t in jail because of me,” I corrected. “Xandale called the Navy and we had limited time to get the goods and get out of there.”

“You have a ship. We can use that to haul the container.”

“The Suckface is a military corvette. It isn’t exactly a freighter. It can barely hold a small crew. Where are we going to put 120,000 pounds of electronics?”

“Can’t you pull it?”

“It’s not a tow truck. Let me get Delovoa down here. Maybe he’ll have some ideas. But you’re going to have to pay him,” I warned.

“We didn’t even complete the mission. We’re going to have to do extra work to earn what we were already paid,” she said.

“That’s fine, but Delovoa wasn’t part of that contract. We need his help.”



A while later, Delovoa came downstairs and was currently scrutinizing Najosa. This was the first time he had met her.

“You’re shorter than I was expecting,” he said after a while.

“Never mind that. How can we move a large cargo container to another station?” I asked.

“Use your ship,” Delovoa shrugged.

Najosa wasn’t the kind of person to say, “I told you so.”

“How can we? The container is too big.”

“It’s space, it doesn’t have to be aerodynamic. Just strap it on top. Or bottom. Or wherever. As long as it doesn’t block the engines or maneuvering rockets you’re fine.”

“So how do I get it to my ship? I can’t push 120,000 pounds.”

“Where is it now?”

“It’s in a warehouse. But we damaged the heavy lifter moving it the first time.”

“The container is a big metal rectangle, right?” Delovoa asked.

“Yeah. It’s metal and painted blue.”

“Oh! Why didn’t you tell me it was painted blue earlier? That changes everything!” Delovoa said sarcastically.

“You going to help or not?”

“I have an invention that can move the container,” he said.

“Is it some anti-gravity device?” I asked.

“I like to call it…a ‘wheel.’”

“What?” Najosa asked.

“Yes. I take these ‘wheels’ and put them on the bottom of things. The angular momentum of the system helps overcome much of the friction caused by trying to drag a heavy object across a flat surface. You see, the wheels turn.”

“How are we going to lift it and attach wheels, smart guy?” I asked.

“I have another invention. It’s called a ‘lever.’ It’s basically a long rod you wedge under something. It allows you to lift heavy objects with minimal effort.”

“Even if it’s on wheels it’s still heavy as hell. How are we going to push it?”

Delovoa thought.

“I can’t say I invented this, but there are things called ‘automobiles’ which have the ability to push and pull tremendous loads.”

“I’m not going to smash up my car trying to push this container.”

“There are plenty of derelict vehicles on Belvaille. Including some heavy-duty trucks. No one took their cars with them when they left the city,” Delovoa explained patiently.

“How much is this technical advice going to cost us?” Najosa asked.

“You consider this technical advice? I guess you Ontakians are all alike. How about you buy me dinner and we’ll call it even,” he said.



Delovoa was right. We used hydraulic jacks to lift the container onto a flatbed trailer. We then used a truck to drive it to where the Suckface was docked.

But this was the hard part.

Not that it was a difficult concept to attach a container to my ship, but we didn’t have expertise at the complicated port controls needed to lift and maneuver the container.

I was really worried we were going to damage my ship, which was by far my most valuable possession since my house got blown up.

It took a good three hours, but we secured the container to the side of my ship with a lot of chains and strap-fasteners.

Zzzho was a good enough pilot that he could fly with no problems, but he said we were limited to 25% of normal acceleration and deceleration.

The Society of the Free and Easy all poured onto my ship.

“So where are we taking this thing, anyway?” I asked.

“The space station Floloria,” Najosa said.

“Floloria?” I asked, surprised.

I thought we were going to have to take Portals and travel quite a distance. Apparently, Thad’s Friend was still living on the Rettosian city. I hadn’t been to the space station since Delovoa and I had been attacked there by guys with weird repulsion rifles.

We made it to Floloria with no issues, though it was a painfully slow trip.

When we were docked, a number of Rettosian workers helped unload the cargo container. It was the first time I had seen actual Rettosians doing something close to manual labor.

I couldn’t understand what they were saying, but something about them struck me as odd. Not just the fact they were Rettosian, and thus their skin was liquid and swirling. But they simply worked…too well together.

I’ve been around blue collar workers all my life. These men didn’t joke around, didn’t have any kind of hierarchy that I could see, and they actually cared about their work. Not only that, they moved as if they were all in a symbiotic relationship. I’d never seen such perfect cohesion.

One of the Rettosians put us in a van and drove our crew deeper into the city as I pondered what I had seen.

“Let me do the talking,” Najosa said sternly.

The car stopped at a nondescript building, though all the buildings here were nondescript, and we got out.

We were met by several guards, who were wearing the same body armor and carrying the same weapons as the guys who had attacked me last time I was here.

I panicked and wondered if they were indeed the ones I had faced before. I didn’t feel like getting pushed through buildings again.

But they simply flanked us as we walked into the building.

Inside, it was really warm. I began perspiring immediately.

We went down a dark hallway and it became instantly clear why the building was so hot. There were electronics everywhere. The floor. The walls. The ceiling. The place was crammed with complicated electrical systems.

I wished Delovoa was here, he might be able to tell what it was. It didn’t look familiar to me, but then again, I wasn’t exactly an ace engineer.

Thad’s Friend met the security guards and they talked to each other briefly in Rettosian. Or I assumed it was Rettosian. It sounded snobby.

As I watched them, I suddenly realized: everyone here was a clone!

It’s why the dock workers had been so cohesive. They were all the same person. They were clones of the Rettosian scientist, Thad’s Friend. It had been so hard to notice because Thad’s Friend was ancient and the other clones were everything from teenager to middle aged.

“You have failed in the task I set to you,” Thad’s Friend said.

“We secured one large container but had to leave before the Navy showed up,” Najosa replied.

“It was my understanding that you not only could deal with such an eventuality, but you looked forward to it,” he answered icily.

Najosa struggled for a response.

“If we attacked the Navy, we would have lost our base of operations—we would have had to flee Belvaille,” I said.

Thad’s Friend looked at me and I wondered if he would finally remember who I was. You know, one of the guys who was there the last time he was murdered.

But he only acknowledged me for a second, as if I was barely worth that much attention.

“Very well. I have another assignment for you,” he said.

Najosa elbowed me.

“Of course. And we’ll do it for free,” she said.

Free? That word was painful. He hadn’t even said what the job was and she already gave it away. Najosa was not the best negotiator.

“There is a ship coming in a few weeks to this System. It contains a representative from the Colmarian Confederation Parliament.”

“The Colmarian Confederation?” I asked, confused.

Najosa elbowed me again. Hard.

“Yes,” Thad’s Friend stated.

“But there is no Confederation. It’s gone.” I was honestly puzzled. Was this guy so old and out of touch that he thought the Colmarian Confederation still existed?

“The name has changed. That is all.”

“We went over this, Hank,” Najosa said through clenched teeth.

“What do you want done?” I asked, ignoring Najosa.

“Kill everyone on board. This will be your chance to strike directly at the empire you despise.”

The Society of the Free and Easy practically cheered at the news.

I was trying to figure out what his angle was. What did Thad’s Friend get out of this? Sure, he had helped Thad Elon start the Colmarian Confederation’s civil war, so maybe he just hated Colmarians. But this wasn’t his style. Assassinating a ship full of people was too small. This guy mutated an entire System. Billions of people. That was his style.

I looked around at the room.

“So, this place. I guess you needed the components for this? Is it some kind of…electrical…like…electrical thing?”

Thad’s Friend didn’t even look at me this time.

“I will send you the detailed instructions on the ship. Do you think you can accomplish this endeavor?” he asked, full of doubt.

“Absolutely,” Najosa said. “It will be our pleasure.”





CHAPTER 53




“You don’t question the scientist,” Najosa said to me, when we were back on my ship. “It’s not our job.”

“Job? I thought jobs were things that paid money. You offered our services for free. Assassinating for free,” I said distastefully.

“We owe him for not finishing the last assignment,” she said.

“You don’t even know his name! Why are you all so enamored of that guy? Just because he’s old?”

“He provides us with information, money, equipment. He’s been a great partner,” Najosa said stubbornly.

“I thought you all hated Rettosians. They created and enslaved our species.”

“These guys are too young to have actually fought any Rettosians in our rebellion,” Frank said. “I hate the Rettosians.”

“You hate everyone,” I told Frank.

“I don’t hate everyone. I’m just old enough to know that most people have plenty of reasons to be disliked,” Frank said.

“And why does a Rettosian on an empty space station want some politician killed?” I asked.

“Who cares?” Nordan said. “It’s our chance to eliminate important members of the PCC.”
