“I care. You should too. He’s up to something. Besides, do you think a Member of Parliament is going to be unguarded? That you can just walk in and shoot him without a fight?”

“I hope there’s a fight,” he replied.

These guys were so annoying!

“But why? What do you, the Society of the Free and Easy, get out of trying to assassinate some very difficult to assassinate people?” I asked.

“Hank, you know we’re trying to destroy the Colmarian Confederation,” Najosa stated calmly.

“It’s already destroyed! It’s gone! This is the PCC.”

“Only the name has changed,” she said.

“You’re mimicking what he said. A whole lot more than the name has changed. Everyone who was in charge back then is dead and gone. It’s a whole new form of government. Granted, it’s a really bad form of government, but how is assassinating this guy going to change things? What if he is the one good politician in the whole place?”

“I don’t care what they call themselves. The government destroyed our home world. Hunted and hounded our people to extinction—” Najosa said.

“We’re not extinct,” I countered, holding my arms up. “I’m on a ship surrounded by Ontakians.”

“Compared to a planet’s population, we are all but extinct,” she said. “Don’t you think that’s worth punishing them for?”

“Punishing who? Do you know who gave the order to destroy our planet?” I asked.

“Grand Wardian Jea-Dahl,” she said immediately.

“Oh. Well, that was a rhetorical question. But okay, why not punish him?”

“He died more than a century ago,” she said.

“Exactly. At some point, you have to move on. Stealing from the PCC is one thing, but murdering random people only sort of connected to our history doesn’t make any sense,” I said.

“Hank. The Society of the Free and Easy won’t rest until we have destroyed the Colmarian Confederation—and its offshoots,” she said.

I looked around the ship and everyone was staring at me like I was some kind of idiot or traitor.

Except Frank. Frank looked bored.



When I had returned home, and was mercifully free of the Society of the Free, I told Cliston about what had happened.

“I’m not an assassin,” I said.

“But sir, you’ve killed people for money before.”

“Yeah, but those weren’t assassinations. That was part of a job.”

“Forgive me, but what’s the difference?”

“There’s a huge difference.”

“It seems to me you’re getting paid for murdering someone in either case. I’m not criticizing, I just wish to understand,” he said.

“Maybe it’s not a huge difference. It’s a subtle difference.”

I thought about how to phrase this.

“If I kill someone who is in my line of work, who is a criminal, that’s not an assassination. They’re crooks. If I get murdered tomorrow, it won’t be some gigantic surprise. The only surprise will be how long it took to happen. A normal person’s everyday work doesn’t involve murder. Mine does. You can’t assassinate a killer. To them it’s simply a career change.”

“So what are you going to do about the Member of Parliament? Will you assassinate him?”

“My gut tells me to have nothing to do with it. Sit this mission out. But if I do that, that politician will die, anyway. If I have an active part or passive part, the outcome will be the same. If I don’t want that outcome, I have to work against the Society of the Free and Easy. And Najosa.”

“And your uncle?” Cliston asked.

“Frank is Frank. As soon as she said we were doing this for free I don’t think he was interested.”

“Would you really work against your Ontakian friends, sir? I know you’ve been happy to have contact with people from your own species.”

“My species sucks, Cliston. I have nothing in common with them. I’m a mutant Ontakian who grew up among Colmarian criminals. The only thing I have in common with the average Ontakian is we can get high off the same drugs.”

“But what about their mission of destroying the PCC? The Society of the Free and Easy seems rather serious about it.”

“The PCC isn’t what they say it is. I haven’t experienced a whole lot of good governments in my life. But compared to anarchy, compared to corporate rule, compared to feudalism, the PCC is damn okay. I mean, what could take its place? How do you govern half of the galaxy in an effective manner?”

“I do not know, sir.”

“I don’t think you can. It’s too big. It’s too complicated. There’s so many planets all with their own identities and problems. Just Belvaille, this tiny little speck of a space station, has all sorts of issues. Multiply that by a zillion and then try and wrap a government around it. Of course it’s screwed up,” I said.

“Sir, far be it from me to suggest a course of action, but I agree with you. I do not think you can allow the Society of the Free and Easy to assassinate that politician. At least until you know more about him and the reasons he is to be eliminated. What if Thad’s Friend is trying to start another war?”

“That’s all we need. As if the Dredel Led aren’t bad enough—no offense.”

“None taken, of course.”





CHAPTER 54




“Wow, where is everyone?” I asked.

The Club was nearly empty. Just a bunch of low level neck breakers and shuttle jockeys stinking up the place.

The waiter shrugged.

“Not sure, but I’ve only made three credits in tips the whole night,” he said, as he deposited my food in front of me, his eyes expectant.

“I would have tipped you fine if you hadn’t begged for it,” I replied.

The waiter scowled but said nothing.

“Hey, here.” I handed him four credits. “There. I more than doubled your salary.”

“Thanks,” he said, leaving.

“Hank,” Rendrae called from out of nowhere, almost making me choke on my sausages.

“Thad Elon’s Black Eyes, what do you want, Rendrae?”

Rendrae was a journalist. The only journalist left on the space station. He was fat and green and old and unbelievably knowledgeable about the inner workings of Belvaille.

He still edited, reported, and published his online tele journal, The News.

“How’s it going?” he asked.

For some reason, Rendrae was wearing a spacesuit, complete with helmet, but the face shields weren’t down.

“What’s with the outfit? You doing interviews in outer space now?” I asked.

“It’s a style. I’m thinking of starting a new column on the details of the Skim. You know, what’s working, what isn’t, new laws. What do you think?” he asked.

“Are you insane? If some Navy official reads it, they’ll throw everyone on this station in jail,” I said.

“I’ll write in code like I used to do back when Belvaille was in Ginland. I’m even planning on having another Most Influential List.”

“Just don’t get anyone arrested,” I said.

“So about my list. I’m thinking of putting you down as number ten. Unless the meeting goes against you. What do you think? Has anyone reached out to you about it?”

“Reached out about what?” I asked.

“The meeting. Your trial,” he said.

“What trial? What are you talking about, Rendrae?”

“Are you serious? Where do you think everyone is right now?”

“They’re at a trial? A trial of what?” I asked.

“Of you, Hank. For breaking the rules of the Skim against Roll Bungalow. I thought you would know. They’re deciding what to do about you.”

“Who?” I asked, alarmed.

“Everyone.”



I got the location of the meeting from Rendrae and practically sprinted to my car.

If I was smarter, I would go home first and get some backup.

But I wasn’t that smart.

The “trial” location was out in the southwest of the station. There must have been about a hundred parked cars out here, so clearly something big was going down.

I ran inside, past the very startled security guards, and kicked open the doors to the auditorium.

There were maybe 500 people inside who all turned simultaneously at my interruption. It looked like nearly every gang boss who had a piece of the Skim was sitting in here.

“Hi. I hope I’m not intruding,” I said.

Their expressions showed everything from guilt to anger to fear.

I put up my hands.

“Look. I’m unarmed,” I said.

“You got that talking gun you always brag about right on your hip,” someone pointed out.

“Yeah, but he’s a philosopher,” I said.

I pulled out Gun.

“Gun?” I asked him.

“Hello,” Gun answered pleasantly.

“Don’t shoot anyone in this room under any circumstances,” I said.

“What room?” Gun asked. I had him currently pointed at the ceiling.

“See?” I said to the group.

“Oh, that room.” I had waved Gun around and apparently he had gotten a better look at the surroundings.

I felt this was a precarious situation and I didn’t want to make it worse by arguing with Gun in front of everyone.

“Forget it. Here. Roll Bungalow?” I called out.

He didn’t stand up. But enough people turned their heads that I saw where he was in the crowd. He probably wondered if I was going to shoot him. Which maybe wasn’t a bad idea.

Instead, I tossed Gun to him and he caught it like the former professional glocken player he was.

“You hold him,” I said.

Roll stared at the gun in his hand until someone beside him objected.

“Don’t point that damn thing at me!”

“Right. So I understand this is my trial. I think it’s only fair that I be here,” I said.

“What’s fair is that we all establish the rules of the Skim and adhere to them,” Ulteem said.

Ulteem was one of the biggest gang bosses on the station. He looked like a regular Colmarian except he didn’t have a head. He wore prosthetic heads to try and appear normal, but they tended to have exaggerated features which were really off-putting. This one had a shaggy blue wig and huge eyes.

“I know the rules of the Skim. I set them up, remember?” I said.

“Then why did you cut in on Roll Bungalow and rob that freighter? You know what that does? What that does to all of us?” Podiver Vance asked.

“I know,” I said.

“That freighter’s crew reported that it was burgled. While on this station. That’s exactly the kind of attention we can’t have. There’s Navy people searching around the docks right as we speak. We’ve had to suspend all Skim activities while they’re nosing around. That’s millions of credits on hold because you had to steal a cargo container,” Podiver Vance continued.

“Just because you’re bulletproof doesn’t mean you’re a god,” a boss said.

Some mumbles of agreement at that.

There were a lot of unhappy faces in the crowd. I had really stepped in it by coming here.

“I know if you all get together you can kill me. I ain’t so tough I can shrug off every guy in this room,” I said. “I came here to try and talk my side.”

“Then what’s your side?” Ulteem asked.

I paused, not sure how I could dig myself out of this.

“You know I’m Master Overseer of Navy and Belvaille Relations—” I started.

“That means nothing,” someone blurted.

“Yeah, the Navy doesn’t care about us. If you die, they’ll just appoint another Master Overseer. And they’ll keep doing it as long as it’s in their best interest to have one. Don’t pretend anyone in the Navy gives a damn about you. You’re just a crook like we are,” Podiver Vance said.

Ouch. Podiver was still probably mad that I got his old store blown up. Everyone with a grudge against me, which was quite a few people, must have been airing their grievances.

This was a lot worse than I had suspected. I shouldn’t have given away Gun.

“I also talk with the Boranjame. I spoke with The Marquor of Lunacy, Gax, the Unfathomable and Disreputable,” I said.

“Did he give you any money?” a young boss heavy into forgeries asked.

“Money? No. He’s a Boranjame. He talks in confusing poetry,” I said.

“Exactly. We’re dirt to the Boranjame. They haven’t done anything for the Colmarians except tell us to leave this or that solar system so they could strip mine it. If you were pals with the Boranjame you wouldn’t even be talking to us,” he said astutely.

“You’re right. I’m just a crook. I’ve been a gangster since before anyone in this room was born. Probably before your parents were born. Maybe some of you have heard of the things I’ve done, maybe you haven’t. But I’ve broken my neck to keep this city whole. To keep our way of life sound. I don’t want to run things, I just want my fair slice of the pie,” I said.

“‘Fair slice.’ You stepped outside that when you broke the Skim,” Roll Bungalow said.

I could tell that they had already decided. That I had lost this trial before I barged in.

“Look, the big thing I do, that I’ve always done, that has kept me alive this long, is I don’t take sides. I’m a bag man. A fixer. A negotiator. I’ve been shot probably a thousand times, and unlike most people, I don’t hold grudges.”

“A dead man’s grudges hardly matter,” a boss said ominously.

“I screwed up. I’m not too big to admit it. I shouldn’t have done what I done. I should have let Roll Bungalow keep his Skim. It was a mistake. I didn’t think it through.”

“What can you give us? I know you’ve done things. Important things for this station over the…centuries. But we don’t want that. We just want to run the Skim. We don’t want the attention of the Boranjame, the Navy, the Dredel Led. Are you worth the trouble you bring, Hank?” Ulteem asked.

“In my experience, trouble comes whether you bring it or not. I don’t know if Belvaille will break without me. It will probably get along just fine. I can’t pay you back for your missed Skim. I don’t have that kind of money. But if you’re not going to look at the major things I’ve done, just look at my work as a thug. You know if I take a job, I’ll give you a fair shake. That’s what being bulletproof allows me to do. The next time someone comes knocking at your door, to collect a debt, or settle a fight, or even rough you up, do you want it to be some paranoid, trigger-happy kid, or do you want it to be me?”

There were widespread grumblings at that, like 500 people were all saying “murber gurber” at the same time. Then they told me to wait outside so they could discuss.

If I wasn’t so lazy, I would have been pacing the halls with nervousness. But as it was, I sat down on the carpet and rubbed my sore knees.

After a while, the doors opened and Roll Bungalow came out holding Gun. I didn’t think he had the bioelectricity to power Gun enough to do me harm, but you never know.

I stood up and he handed me back the weapon.

“That thing is annoying. I had to tell it to shut up at least three times,” he said.

“So I take it I’m not going to die?” I asked.

“We argued for fifteen minutes on how to kill you. I said it could be done, but wouldn’t be easy or worth the effort. It’s not like you’re a terrible guy. You’re just a little slow sometimes. And if we killed everyone that was stupid, there would be three people left on Belvaille,” he said.

“Thanks for sticking up for me, I guess.”

“You got to promise not to do it again, of course. And you have to pay two million credits.”

“To you? I don’t have anywhere near that much cash!” I complained.

“No, not to me. It was kind of vague on who gets paid. Podiver Vance said you probably didn’t have the money since your house got—I don’t know, what happened to your house?”

“It’s gone. So how do we do this? I got to pay someone I don’t know a bunch of money I don’t have.”

“Yeah.”

“I’ll loan you the money, Hank. 5% a month juice,” Ulteem said, walking over. His ugly prosthetic head was grinning.

“1% interest. I still owe you for setting up the Belvaille Glocken Team,” I said, remembering Ulteem’s double-cross.

“You said you don’t hold grudges.”

“Not holding grudges and having amnesia are two different things.”

“Alright. When do you think you’ll pay me back?” Ulteem asked.

“I don’t know. With all these bosses here, I guess I better see if anyone has any work for me.”

“I’d hold off on that for now,” Roll said. “About a third of the people voted to kill you.”

“I guess that’s not so bad. Two-thirds of Belvaille likes me.”

“Not exactly. A lot of people didn’t vote. So maybe a third hate you, a third like you, and a third didn’t care.”

“That’s depressing. After all I’ve done for this city,” I said.

“Most people weren’t around when you were saving the galaxy. And being bulletproof goes both ways. Yeah, it’s great if someone hired you, not so great if you’re coming to get them,” Ulteem explained.





CHAPTER 55




I was asleep in my room when Cliston’s voice woke me.

“Sir, Garm and MTB are here to see you,” he said.

That didn’t sound good, so I turned over and tried to ignore him.

“Get up, we need your help,” I heard Garm say.

I saw a fully armed MTB and Garm standing in my bedroom.

“Ugh, I barely got any sleep,” I said.

“I doubt that,” she said.

“Cliston, how long have I been asleep?” I asked, as proof.

“About thirteen hours, sir.”

“Oh. Well, no wonder I’m so tired. I overslept.”

“Can we talk without him here?” MTB asked.

“Who?” I looked around, not sure who he meant.

MTB pointed to Cliston, which truly surprised me.

“Say what you came to say,” I replied.

“A Dredel Led has landed. We believe it’s here to attack the city,” Garm said.

“Another Dredel Led? Oh, boy!” Ermie declared. He had walked into the room and no one noticed because of his small stature.

MTB and Garm stared at the tiny Emerald Crusader. They had not seen him before.

“How many robots do you have?” MTB asked.

“I don’t ‘have’ them, they work here. That’s Ermie. He tried to kill me.”

“Hi,” Ermie said. “What species are you?”

“Don’t answer that,” I said. Despite Ermie giving up his quest to annihilate Colmarians, he still didn’t hold much affection for them. “So why are you guys here? Let your PCC Navy friends deal with this,” I told Garm.

“They don’t know, yet,” she said.

“Why? Is your tele broken? I can call them if you want. This is their job, right? To protect us.”

“Last time the Navy heard there was a Dredel Led butler and—no offense—insignificant robot on the station, they fired a tachyon ray through the shield and destroyed your house.”

“How many Dredel Led are attacking?” I asked.

“One,” MTB said.

I notice he didn’t say “just” one, or “only” one. I had fought combat Dredel Led before and they beat me silly all the way across the space station. I wasn’t looking to repeat that exercise.

“Can’t your police handle them, MTB? Delovoa built you that armor and weapons,” I said.

“I don’t think our guns are going to hurt it. Besides, I only got two deputies,” he said.

“We have three cops on the entire city? That’s not even enough to give out parking tickets. I thought you had like thirty people.”

“We can’t afford to pay them and no one wants the job,” he said.

“That makes sense,” I agreed. “Okay, so what do you want me to do, talk to this Dredel Led? Or have Cliston talk to him?”

“It doesn’t seem to be much of a talker. We already tried,” Garm said.

“Are you sure it’s a Dredel Led?” I asked.

“We’re pretty damn sure,” MTB replied.



I had Gun and the bag of daxapronal that Cliston had saved from my dying house.

MTB told me to wear body armor if I had some, but I didn’t have any, so I put on three pairs of underwear instead.

Cliston and Ermie came along. I figured it couldn’t hurt to have some Dredel Led on our side.

Garm drove us to the last area the robot was seen. It wasn’t far outside the docks.

“That’s not a Dredel Led,” I said, looking through the windshield, “it’s a damn building!”

The Dredel Led in question took up nearly both lanes of the street. It was about twenty feet long, ten feet tall, and walked on four robotic legs. It had a colossal head, and its mouth looked like a construction scoop.

But other than its legs and mouth, it didn’t resemble anything. It didn’t have eyes or a nose or even a fluffy tail.

We parked on an adjoining street and got out.

The Dredel Led was moving forward, but incredibly slowly. It was clearly beyond heavy, and each footfall sent shockwaves through the road.

“Look at all that armor,” MTB said.

He was right. There were overlapping sheets of metal everywhere. This clearly wasn’t a really large and ponderous candy-making machine. Unless it was designed to manufacture candy at the bottom of an active volcano.

“So do we shoot it?” I asked.

“Excuse me, sir,” Cliston interrupted harshly. “If that was a biological creature merely walking along the street, I can’t believe your first reaction would be to shoot it.”

“We’re at war with your empire. I mean, do you know that thing? Is it your cousin?”

“I do not recognize it,” Cliston said.

“Hey!” I called to the ponderous machine. “Are you Kai’Un?”

The robot did not respond.

“Sir, the chances of it being Kai’Un are astronomical. Kai’Un has a million minds. Despite this Dredel Led’s size, it could not house that many intellects.”

“I only know three Dredel Led by name. Two of them are standing next to me right now. It didn’t hurt to ask, and would have made things a lot easier,” I said.

“Maybe it doesn’t have ears,” Garm asked.

“It is a requirement for all modern Dredel Led to have at least the senses of sight, hearing, and touch,” Cliston said.

“You have a sense of touch, Ermie?” I asked. He was so small it seemed hard to imagine.

“I do,” he said, and he patted me on my ankle as if to prove it.

“Do you recognize that thing?” I asked the Emerald Crusader.

“It looks like a big animal,” he said.

The Dredel Led in question was still thundering its way along at crawling speed.

“What kind of animal?” I asked, not having seen many animals in my life and not able to imagine one that large and slow.

“I don’t know. One that looks like that,” he replied seriously.

“We have to do something,” Garm said. “We can’t let this thing go smashing around the city.”

“It does not appear to be ‘smashing’ anything,” Cliston replied.

“He’s right. Maybe it’s looking for a bathroom,” I said.

“You said yourself we’re at war with the Dredel Led,” Garm countered.

“Does that mean you’re going to shoot me?” Cliston asked.

“No, Cliston. But you’re not a giant robot banging down a residential street.”

“It’s difficult to call Belvaille a residential city nowadays. We are probably the only people within a quarter mile,” Cliston said.

“The robot isn’t going very fast. Maybe we could have MTB’s deputies follow it and call us if it does anything hostile,” I said.

“You want to go back to sleep,” Garm yelled.

“Look, if we’ve been invaded, it seems to be happening very slowly. The robot didn’t even hit any buildings. It drives better than I do. Cliston is right, do we blast the guy just because it looks scary?”

“The Navy officials are going to find out about this. What do you think they’ll do?” Garm asked.

“You know better than me. I suspect they will over-react somehow. But that doesn’t mean we should beat them to it.”

MTB hung up his tele. He had made a call while we were talking.

“My deputies are working on another case. They can’t help out.”

I laughed.

“What is more important than this?” I asked.

“Gangland double-homicide with a follow-up retribution murder,” he said.

“Oh. Yeah, that’s pretty important,” I conceded.

“You all are impossible!” Garm said. “That thing is headed north to my telescopes.”

“By the time it gets there, they’ll be outdated,” I said. “But hey, that makes sense. It’s probably trying to stop the hack.”

“You and your stupid hack. There is no hack! I told you this,” Garm said.

“We traced it to the telescopes,” I replied.

“Who is ‘we?’ You and your Ontakian pals?” she asked.

“Delovoa.”

“That nutjob? What does he know?”

“We followed the signal. The hacking signal. It was coming from one of the telescopes. Just like the previous signal had been coming from a building guarded by Navy soldiers. Don’t pretend you don’t know what I’m talking about. That Dredel Led is here for a reason,” I said.

“With all due respect, we don’t know the reasons, sir.”

“If anyone is hacking the Dredel Led, it was done without my knowledge,” Garm said.

“So there is some mysterious department in one of your buildings, doing stuff without your permission?” I asked skeptically.

Garm thought about it.

“No. I have a project log. Every minute on those telescopes is accounted for,” she said.

“Maybe you have ghosts,” I replied, “operating when you all are off-duty.”

“We have to coordinate ship traffic at all times of day and night. Every telescope is in operation 24/7. Except for the broken one,” she said.

“What broken one? You have a busted telescope?” I asked.

“Yes. The one you broke. Before you feign innocence again, I know it was you. Right after the telescope was attacked, and its security defenses had been activated, you ended up in the hospital. Not to mention there’s a big Hank-sized hole in the dish.”

I stood there with my mouth open.

“The power plant,” I said.

“What?” she asked.

“I went on a mission to steal a power plant from a remote military base, with assistance from the other Ontakians.”

“I thought you said that wasn’t you,” Garm teased.

“You know it was. Zzzho logged the name of our ship as the ‘Don’t Tell Them It’s The Suckface.’ I then stole a similar power plant from Belvaille. From the building that was hacking. I bet it was the same power generator. They installed it on Belvaille after we swiped it from the PCC.”

“Was that where I injured my hand?” Cliston asked.

“Yeah. As for the telescope, the Ontakians said it wasn’t really damaged, it just looked like it. They managed to hijack it. That’s probably why you think it’s broken. And now we traced the current hack to one of the telescopes. I assumed it was your people doing it. But it wasn’t.”

“What are you saying?” Garm asked.

“The Dredel Led were going to pay me fifty million credits for stopping the hack. But I’ve been helping the Ontakians set it up this whole time.”





CHAPTER 56




We were still walking along with the giant Dredel Led.

Cliston was trying to speak to it, but having no success. Ermie was even helping out, but mostly just repeating what Cliston said.

I was still annoyed about my discovery that I had been the cause of the Dredel Led hack this whole time. I wondered if Najosa had known. I never told her I was looking for a hack of the Dredel Led, maybe she would have said, “oh, that’s us.”

“What I don’t get is why the Ontakians would want to hack the Dredel Led,” Garm asked.

“I’m thinking they might not have known. They might have been doing it for Thad’s Friend, and didn’t ask any questions,” I said.

“Who is Thad’s Friend?” MTB asked.

“He’s that old Rettosian who helped Thad Elon with his glocken teams,” I said.

“You mean the one who mutated Belvaille System?” Garm asked, shocked. She knew of the man, having been one of my teammates in Super Class glocken.

“Yeah.”

“Why would they be doing it for him?” Garm asked.

“Well, because we did some other jobs for him,” I answered weakly.

“You’re working for the guy who mutated Belvaille?” MTB asked.

“I mean, not really working for him. I did a few jobs for him. Maybe more than I know. But Cliston, Delovoa and I figured he was up to something and the best way to find out was to play along.”

“So you hacked the Dredel Led and they declared war on us?” Garm tried to confirm.

“I don’t think they declared war on us because of that. They were mad at us and then I went to the capital city and…well, I accidentally convinced them to declare war.”

“Why is it every time something gigantic and horrible happens in this galaxy, you’re right there as the cause?” she asked.

“Luck?” I said.

“What I want to know is, are you going to apologize?” Garm asked.

“Apologize to who?”

“Me! Because you accused me a hundred times of hacking your precious Dredel Led and it turns out you were the one doing it,” she said.

“It wasn’t only me. I don’t know how to hack anything. But I will later,” I said.

“You’ll apologize later?”

“Mreh,” I mumbled.

“Because it will take three hours or something? Just say you’re sorry,” she replied.

“Here come the bots,” I said.

Cliston and Ermie walked over to us.

“I’ve tried every language and form of communication I know and have not been able to elicit a response,” Cliston said.

“I waved at it,” Ermie added.

“Why doesn’t Hank go over and explain that he’s the reason we’re at war?” Garm asked.

“I don’t see how that will help anything,” I replied.

“Maybe it will step on you and make the universe a safer place,” she said.

“There’ll be a lot more food left for the rest of us,” MTB agreed.

“Hey! I didn’t know the Ontakians were working for Thad’s Friend…at first. And I never would have guessed he was hacking the Dredel Led. How could I possibly know that?”

“The real question is, why is he hacking?” Cliston asked.

“He mutated millions of people. Doesn’t he hate Colmarians?” Garm asked.

“I hate Colmarians,” Ermie stated.

We all stared at him a moment.

“I don’t know that Thad’s Friend hates anything. He struck me more as a psychopathic inventor. Like an extreme version of Delovoa—but way more talented,” I said.

“Okay, why would a psychopathic inventor want to hack the Dredel Led?” Garm asked.

“I don’t know. Just a natural, scientific curiosity?” I guessed.

“It was more than idle curiosity that brought this Dredel Led here,” Garm countered.

“And me too,” Ermie added.

“That’s right. Ermie was sent to destroy the Colmarian Confederation,” I said.

“I’m taking a break,” he piped. “I’m trying to become a butler.”

“Sir, you asked if that Dredel Led was Kai’Un,” Cliston said.

“You think it is? I thought a million minds couldn’t fit in there,” I replied.

“They couldn’t, sir. But I also said I had never experienced anything like Kai’Un. That our historical files didn’t have any record of a Dredel Led similar to him.”

“Kai’Un is that Dredel Led that voted for war, right?” Garm asked.

“Yes,” I said.

“He was also extremely secretive and unlike any Dredel Led I have seen in thousands of years,” Cliston said.

“I know this is an odd time to bring it up, but how old are you, Cliston?” I asked.

“15,348 years old, sir,” Cliston said.

“Wow. That’s like…ten times as old as I am,” Ermie added, impressed.

The less than one-month-old Dredel Led had not yet mastered mathematics.

“Damn, Cliston. No wonder you’ve got so many pamphlets and books. You’re an antique,” I said.

“I just celebrated my animation date two weeks ago,” Cliston continued.

“Oh. Happy…turning-on day?” I said.

“So what about this Kai’Un robot?” Garm asked, annoyed at the interruption.

“I think what Cliston is saying is that maybe Thad’s Friend created it?” I asked.

“Nothing of the sort has ever been done before, but if anyone could do it, I suspect the Rettosian would be a likely candidate,” Cliston said.

“But why? You said he doesn’t hate Colmarians. Why would he hate the Dredel Led?” MTB asked.

“Maybe he hates them. I don’t know. It’s not like we ever sat down for tea and crackers and had a talk,” I amended.

“Where is this Rettosian?” Garm asked.

“On Floloria. Hey, come to think of it, I met him in a room with a bunch of electronic devices. Maybe that was Kai’Un?” I asked.

“Kai’Un would have to be much larger than a room,” Cliston said.

“Oh, and we delivered him a cargo container full of electronic components that we stole from a transport here on Belvaille,” I said.

“You did what?” MTB asked.

“Nothing. It doesn’t matter,” I said quickly.

“It matters that you’ve been working for a crazy Rettosian this whole time. I told you those Ontakians were trouble,” Garm said.

“Cliston, how big would Kai’Un have to be?” I asked.

“Think of a million versions of me in one unit, sir,” Cliston answered.

“The construction! Delovoa and I had gone to Floloria because we detected signals coming from it, but they weren’t the same as the hack. When we landed, we found people were knocking down buildings using some advanced construction equipment. We weren’t sure if they were attacking us or what, but we got the hell out of there. Maybe the whole city, the entire space station—” I began.

“Is Kai’Un!” Cliston finished.

Just then, the Dredel Led we were following, slowly tilted forward, opened its giant scoop-like mouth, and took an enormous bite out of the metal street it was walking on.





CHAPTER 57




“What the hell is it doing?” MTB asked.

“It looks like it’s eating the street,” I said.

Indeed, the big, armored Dredel Led had now taken three bites out of the solid metal road. Our roads, and pretty much the whole space station, were steel. And not some cheap-o brand either. It was a hardened steel alloy, that never rusted and rarely needed repair.

And here this robot was, chowing down like it was eating rice crackers.

As we stood watching, the creature ceased chewing and took up a different pose. It lowered its two hind legs and the whole robot began shaking.

“Does it look like it’s about to…” Garm began.

“It’s taking a crap,” I said.

One, two, three hunks of metal fell out of its backside, lying on the street like…well, a pile of turds. They were about two feet in length apiece.

Doooot! A noise blasted from the rear of the Dredel Led and a cloud of brown smoke billowed out.

“Holy hell, it farted!” I exclaimed. “Did you see that? It farted! I told you robots could fart! Mr. Boshe said they couldn’t.”

“Calm down,” Garm said. “Was it simply hungry?”

The Dredel Led righted itself and began to walk again, avoiding the large potholes it had dug out with its mouth.

When it had moved a few feet ahead, the metal turds began to stir.

Each one of them unfolded, springing out arms and legs and heads and hands.

They looked like:

“They’re me,” Ermie said.

They were. They looked like three, somewhat larger, Emerald Crusaders.

The three robots examined their surroundings for a moment, having just been crapped into existence, and then they all ran off in different directions.

“Hey!” I yelled.

“That’s illegal,” Cliston stated.

“What is?” Garm asked.

“Dredel Led are prohibited from creating other Dredel Led. It is one of our most basic tenets. That Dredel Led is in violation of our laws.”

“Well, if we survive, I suggest you write a stern letter, Cliston,” I said. “In the meantime, what do we do about those little…poop robots?”

“We need to stop them. If it keeps squatting out Dredel Led, we could be overwhelmed. There’s an awful lot of road to eat,” Garm said.

“I object to attacking them unless they’ve done something wrong,” Cliston stated.

“You said they were breaking the law,” Garm challenged.

“Dredel Led law. With all due respect, this isn’t Dredel Led territory and you aren’t Dredel Led,” Cliston replied.

“Cliston, Garm, MTB, go after those little robots and see what they’re doing. Stay in tele contact. I’ll follow this thing,” I said.

“How about I follow the big, slow thing and you run after the little ones?” MTB said.

“The smaller robots are too fast for me to follow. Quit wasting time and get after them.”

“You stay with Hank, Ermie,” Cliston said.

“Okay!”

Everyone took off after the robots. They weren’t amazingly fast, their little legs were too small for that, but they had a decent head start.

I stood there with Ermie.

“Should we try and talk to it again?” Ermie asked.

“I don’t have a great track record speaking to Dredel Led. Most times they attack me,” I replied.

“Like me!” Ermie said good-naturedly.

After about fifteen minutes, the Dredel Led had moved maybe half a block. Then it paused and took another bite out of the road.

I got on the tele.

“Hey, it’s eating the street again. What should I do?” I called into my tele.

The only one to answer was Cliston. He could probably multitask better than Garm and MTB. After all, he had 15,000 years of practice.

“Hello, sir. You should instruct him that he is in violation of Dredel Led laws.”

“And how should I do that?” I asked.

“I can give you the proper nomenclature,” Cliston replied.

He started rattling off some legalese and I stopped him.

“I can’t remember all that, Cliston.”

“I’ll tell Ermie, he can memorize it, and then repeat it back to you as you’re speaking, sir.”

“Why don’t I have Ermie say it instead?”

“I think that perhaps your voice might carry more weight. If you understand me, sir.”

“Ermie, listen to this and remember it,” I said.

“Okay.”

I handed Ermie the tele and Cliston told him what to say. When he was done, he handed me back the tele.

“What is the poop-bot doing, Cliston?” I asked.

“I object to that derogatory term, sir. It is merely a Dredel Led.”

“Sorry, Cliston.”

“It seems to be a scout. It is moving across the city, looking into doors and windows and vehicles. I’m not sure what it’s searching for.”

“Probably the telescopes,” I said.

“If that’s the case, it is going the wrong direction. We’re now seven blocks away from you.”

“Alright, I’m going to try and talk to this thing.”

“Good luck, sir.”

I took a deep breath, shook out my arms and legs to warm up, and walked over in front of the enormous Dredel Led, whose feet were twice the size of me.

“Come on, Ermie,” I said.

“Coming.”

“Ahum,” I started, clearing my voice. “Dredel Led! I have to inform you that you are in violation of the laws of Thaxmultaerea.”

I turned to Ermie.

“What are the next lines?” I asked.

Ermie told me.

“I just said that. What are the ones after?”

As he told me, I got the sense I was playing a childhood game of tele. Where you have a string of people whisper the same phrase to each other and it gets garbled as more and more people translate it. I don’t think Ermie had the best memory processing unit in the galaxy.

“You need to cease and delist your parent occupations. The grasping words of the silver monitor make your actions regal. If you do not stop in your mother father brother, you will be reformed to the barren middle school,” I said.

The Dredel Led paused. It was probably as confused as I was.

The big robot leaned back a bit and I wondered if I had succeeded. Then it thrust out its left leg and slammed it on top of the Emerald Crusader, who disappeared from sight.

“Ermie!” I shouted.

But I didn’t have much time to react beyond that, as the Dredel Led kicked me with its right front leg.

I woke up some time later from where I had cratered into a building. I saw the Dredel Led had moved another half-block and had just pooped out two more robots.

Presumably, I had been unconscious for a bit.

I was wedged into a building. My butt and back were squashed into the metal of the building’s wall. My face was bleeding and my chest was killing me.

I had to wiggle for a bit, but I managed to extricate myself from the building.

I hobbled over to where I saw Ermie get stepped on. A flattened Emerald Crusader was clearly seen in outline. He almost looked like a drawing, he was smashed completely two dimensional.

I can’t say I knew Ermie well, he hadn’t been alive that long, but he was a good guy as far as robots went. The best elevator attendant I ever met. Very courteous—to non-Colmarians. And he had a childish, infectious enthusiasm.

I pulled out Gun and pointed it at the backside of the Dredel Led.

“Gun, can you see that thing?” I asked.

“Hello. What thing?”

“That non-floor, non-wall, non-door, big, moving, metal thing.”

I stood there, my arm outstretched as Gun thought.

“What’s metal?” he finally asked.

I put my arm down and exhaled. I had a huge urge to throw Gun at the Dredel Led, but I knew that would be a useless gesture.

“Gun! You can see movement, right? Things that are…I don’t know, moving.”

“I think so. Not floors, right?”

“No, not floors.”

I held out Gun again.

“You see…the only thing that is moving. It’s big. It’s…it’s…not a person.”

“Is it alive?” Gun asked.

“I… Why does that matter?”

“I don’t know. I’m wondering what it means to be alive. Am I alive?”

“I can’t do this right now, Gun. I need you to concentrate. Can you shoot that moving thing?”

“I believe so.”

“Will it hurt it?”

“I don’t know what it is. I can’t tell if it will hurt it. Or it can be hurt. Or if it’s alive. Or if you’re alive.”

“Forget it. Can you shoot at full power straight ahead? Wait!” I said.

I realized that if he shot at full power it might drain every ounce of my bioelectricity and kill me. Simple linguistic mistakes like that could be fatal with Gun.

“Let me correct that. Can you shoot straight ahead at 85% power?” I said, aiming carefully at the Dredel Led.

“I don’t know. What’s an eighty-five? And a percent?” Gun asked.

I was growing terribly frustrated, but I realized there probably wasn’t a big need for an intelligent gun to know about numbers and percentages. It would just be useless information.

“Gun, how do you know how powerful to shoot? Like, how much electricity do you use?”

“I shoot how I need to shoot.”

“Okay. I think. Can you shoot that moving thing so it is damaged, but not really, really damaged?”

Gun was quiet a long while.

“Well?” I asked.

“I believe I understand what you mean,” Gun said. “Bang!”

I fell to my knees with a thud and flopped over on my back. I couldn’t move my limbs or speak or do anything. But at least I wasn’t unconscious.

I saw one of the armor panels of the Dredel Led had bent and cracked, which made moving its left rear leg difficult. It stopped walking and turned its head back.

“Gluh,” I managed to say from my prostrate and entirely helpless position.





CHAPTER 58




“Sir, are you okay?” I heard Cliston say.

After shooting Gun, I was too tired to turn my head, so Cliston obliged by walking into my field of view. He was holding one of the small robots he had pursued earlier. It was struggling vigorously.

Cliston tried to get me to my feet, but he couldn’t manage and hold onto the robot at the same time. He stopped helping me and, to my great shock, he twisted the head off the small robot! He then dropped the remains to the ground and dragged me up.

“Cliston?” I asked. I wasn’t sure what to think. Here he had been touting the rights and liberties of Dredel Led and he just murdered one.

“That wasn’t a Dredel Led. It was a mindless automaton,” he said.

“A what?”

“It was simply a machine. A drone,” he stated angrily.

Cliston never got angry.

“So what’s that mean?” I asked. Wondering if he might snap off my head next.

“I told you it was against our laws to create Dredel Led. We can only be created by the consensus of Thaxmultaerea. It is wholly against our philosophy to create drones. We strive to show the galaxy that Dredel Led are beings of substance and individuality. To stamp out brainless robots is an affront to all we stand for.”

“So that huge robot is a lawbreaker and a heretic?” I asked.

“Something like that. Did you shoot it, sir?”

“Yeah. You see in the hindquarters there. Almost knocked me unconscious. Stupid Gun.”

“Why did you shoot it? You didn’t know it was creating drones until just now. Where is the Emerald Crusader?” he asked.

“He got smashed. He’s gone. And that bastard kicked me into a wall. That’s why I shot it.”

The large Dredel Led, unable to walk properly, bent over again and took two bites out of the street.

“You need to destroy it before it creates more drones,” Cliston urged.

“I can barely stand up. How am I going to destroy it? I don’t have enough electricity to shoot Gun again and I don’t want to get near that thing.”

“Did you explain to it that its actions were in violation of Dredel Led laws?”

“More or less.”

“I’m going to tele MTB and Garm and tell them they can destroy the drones they’re following. Please think of a way to disable that Dredel Led.”

Cliston let go of me to use his tele and I promptly slid to the ground.

Every time I get injured, I get hungry. I could really go for some Club sausages. Drench them in syrup. Have some crispy toast to use as a sponge. Maybe even get someone to pre-chew it all for me, so I could just slurp it up with a straw.

Yeah.

I heard Cliston talking on the tele.

“I don’t see why you attacked it without knowing it was a drone.”

He must have been speaking to Garm. She didn’t care one way or the other if a robot had a brain, two brains, or none. If she viewed it as a threat, she would act accordingly.

The giant Dredel Led squatted and began to shake again. Great, it was going to poop out more drones.

But instead, a large, flat piece of metal came out.

It reached back with one of its hind legs and some fingers jutted out from its foot. It grabbed hold of the metal and its leg swiveled and stretched and lifted the piece up.

Then the busted plate of armor I shot, popped off the Dredel Led and fell to the ground with a clang. The Dredel Led then snapped on the newly formed piece and righted itself.

Even Cliston seemed amazed.

The Dredel Led had just repaired its damaged body by eating some roadwork. How the hell could I dream of fighting this thing?

MTB called me on the tele.

“What’s up, MTB? You know you can shoot the robot you’re chasing?”

“Yeah, Cliston said. The security forces by the telescope took care of it for me. This thing made a straight line to one of the buildings and started raising hell inside. It’s safe to say the Navy knows something is going on down here.”

“That’s just great. This thing might crap out some more robots. Why don’t you stay there and shoot anything that comes near the telescopes?”

“Sounds good. What do I tell the Navy?”

“Don’t tell them anything. You’re Police Overseer.”

“I am?”

“Yeah, didn’t I tell you? That’s what Wardian Boshe knows you as. You’re also the back-up coordinator when it comes to the Navy commandeering the ports in case of emergency.”

“Why are you telling me this now?”

“Because I just remembered.”

“Do these jobs have a salary attached to them?”

“I’m like five different Master Overseers and I don’t get paid. I don’t see how you would.”

MTB hung up.

“Sir, do you still have the daxapronal?” Cliston asked.

“Yeah. But I don’t want to fight that thing hand-to-hand. Its hands are huge.”

“I was thinking, does the drug give you more energy?”

“Sure. I go crazy, remember?”

“No, I mean you might be able to fire Gun multiple times.”

I thought about that. Did the drug literally give me more bioelectricity or did it just make me feel that way? It could be risky being half-crazy and shooting off a half-intelligent Gun.

“I’m not sure that’s a good idea, Cliston. Besides, the robot will just rebuild itself.”

“Do you have any other methods of stopping it, sir?”

“I do not,” I said plainly.

I reached into my pocket for my baggie of drugs.

“Cliston, this stuff makes me insane. In a few instances, I’ve gone screaming down the street and attacked police cars. You need to keep me calm and focused or this won’t work.”

“I will do my best, sir.”

I stared at the drugs a moment.

“You know, Thad’s Friend, the Rettosian scientist, probably manufactured this drug.”

“Sir?”

“Nothing. I was just thinking how circular things can be.”

I pulled four tabs of paper out of the bag and put them in my mouth. This was an overdose, but I didn’t think I had time to dial in the perfect administration of daxapronal.

It didn’t take long for me to get to my feet and begin hyperventilating.

I was so angry. At everything. Anything I looked at made me furious. I hated the roads. The buildings. The way the buildings connected to the roads. I wanted to destroy it all!

Before I could bodily throw myself at my surroundings, I heard a clear, baritone voice.

Singing.

It was Cliston!



The lights, shining on Belvaille,

They make me feel so well.

When I'm down and gloomy,

This town she tries to soothe me.

City Hall with its spire,

Fills me with desire.

It's that pulse, that beat, that rhythm of the street.

That lifts you around, till the latticework goes down.

Belvaille's in my heart. Belvaille is a song.

Belvaille is the dream, that makes us get along.



Here I was, so high I was about to pop out my own eyeballs using my toes, and all the anger suddenly vanished.

That little song had drawn a picture. I could see the city. Belvaille. See it with greater clarity than I ever had before. See it, touch it, smell it, taste it. It was all-encompassing.

Right when I was about to be overwhelmed with sensation, I felt a firm grip on my arm.

“Sir. You need to shoot the Dredel Led with Gun,” Cliston said.

“I need to shoot the Dredel Led with Gun,” I repeated.

“Yes, sir. Pick up Gun. It’s right there.”

“Pick up Gun,” I said, and I reached down and took hold of the pistol. I looked back to Cliston to see what the next step was.

“Aim it at the Dredel Led.”

I pointed Gun.

“Instruct the weapon to fire now, sir.”

“Gun. Kill that damn thing! Kill it! Kill it! Kill it!”

“Alrighty,” Gun said. “Bang!”

Next thing I knew, Cliston was looming over me. I must have passed out.

“How long was I unconscious?” I asked.

“About four seconds, sir.”

“Oh. Were you singing, Cliston?”

“Yes, sir.”

“I’m really mad. But I’m also really tired. I’m mad that I’m tired. Help me up, Cliston.”

Cliston raised me to my feet and I could see that I hit the Dredel Led and it was in the process of eating the road again.

“It’s rebuilding itself,” I said.

“Can you shoot it again, sir?”

“I don’t think I have enough juice.” I rolled my head around on my neck and flexed my shoulders. “I’m getting my energy back, but even on this drug it’s not instantaneous. I might need you to sing at me again if I lose control.”

“I haven’t finished the lyrics, I’m afraid.”

“That’s okay. It was a nice song, Cliston. Did you write it?”

“Yes, sir.”

“When do you write songs?”

“I composed that one just now, sir.”

That made me angry. That Cliston could multitask writing a song while preparing to fight a monstrous robot, and I could barely tie my shoes and hum at the same time.

I felt the rage growing. And believed I might be able to hazard another shot without killing myself.

“Gun, shoot that thing again.”

“Bang!” Gun said.

The blast completely shot off a right rear piece of armor. The Dredel Led immediately took three more bites out of the metal street.

I was momentarily stunned with fatigue, but I didn’t fall down. Cliston was right about the drug.

Garm suddenly came running up. She had a pistol in each hand.

“What’s going on?” she asked.

“Ermie is dead and that thing is a criminal,” I replied.

“What?”

The Dredel Led hunched over and four metal logs plopped out the back, along with a cloud of brown gas.

It made me furious that the robot was farting at me and my efforts to destroy it.

As the Dredel Led continued to eat the road, four little robots sprung up from the pile it had excreted and began running straight toward me.

“Yes! Come on, you sons of bastards! This is for Ermie,” I yelled.

The robots swarmed and I immediately fell on my back.

“Help!”

I struggled to get the robots off, but they were too quick and weren’t interested in a fair fight.

“Sir, they are a distraction while the Dredel Led replaces its damaged components,” Cliston said.

“Well, they’re working!” I said. I couldn’t stand up and couldn’t dream of shooting at the robot again. Every time I tried to rise, the robots pushed me over.

Blam! Blam!

Garm shot one. She did some kind of showoff, handstand, twirly-kick thing and another robot went flying.

With my hands free, I was able to grab a robot and squeeze it to death.

Cliston took hold of the last one and twisted off its head.

But the Dredel Led had finished its repairs and was back to walking.

“Cliston, how should we fight this thing?” Garm asked.

“Hey, whoa. I’m the resident expert at fighting monsters and robots. Not him,” I complained.

“I can tell you’re high on that weird drug. Cliston was our glocken General Manager and he knows more about Dredel Led than either of us.”

“Give me your keys,” I said, suddenly flushed with a brilliant idea.

“Why? You want to steal my clothes? None of my dresses will fit you.”

“Hah, hah. No, the keys to your car.”

She threw them at me and I failed to catch them.

“That really inspires confidence,” she added.

“Watch this,” I proclaimed.

I got up and ran back to her car.

When Cliston and I had fought that robot with the spinning blades in the government building, I had protected myself by hiding inside a metal cylinder.

I had a car! A big, metal car. It gave me speed and all the protection I could want.

I got into the vehicle and started it up. Already I felt impervious.

The Dredel Led itself was too big to ram, but I could shoot at it out the window. And as it farted out more drones, I would just hit-and-run them.

I felt so foolish for not coming up with this plan earlier. Use my brain for once instead of punching my way out of every problem. Ironic I had to get hopped up on psycho pills before I started thinking straight.

I drove the car from the side street and joined the road the Dredel Led was slowly moving down.

Garm and Cliston were cheering at me on the sidewalk. They probably realized what I was planning to do.

I didn’t even see what I ran into.

The car came to an abrupt stop and I flew through the windshield. I skidded along the street like a fleshy bowling ball and finally ended up about thirty feet behind the Dredel Led.

It all happened so fast and I was so high on daxapronal, that I was still trying to steer the car for a few seconds as I sat on the pavement.

Alright. Alright. So that didn’t work.

The Dredel Led began to squat again and I jumped to my feet, wobbled a bit, and ran forward.

I was going to crawl up this thing. It couldn’t be armored both outside and inside.

I think Garm and Cliston were cheering at me again as I fought my way past the dropping robot turds and the gaseous exhaust.

Everything went black.

As my knees were pushed up to my chest and my hands were pressed to my face, I suddenly felt this wasn’t a very good plan.

I had just climbed inside a Dredel Led’s butt.

Unasked, I might add.

This damn drug. What was I thinking?

I felt my confines twisting and bouncing and growing tighter. I got the sense the robot was walking, probably cockeyed since it had a mutant Ontakian shoved up its rear end.

But the idea of ripping it apart from the inside was a bit far-fetched. There were tons and tons of metal pressing against me from all sides. I couldn’t even wiggle my toes.

I didn’t have Gun. I had left it in the car. Or on the ground.

I could feel plenty of fresh air. But it was so tight that I couldn’t take more than little hiccup breaths.

I was going to die in the colon of a giant, a proctologist’s nightmare.

There was a terrible grinding noise and vibration. The Dredel Led was eating the road again. I wasn’t sure if I was going to get digested or what.

A tremendous blast of heat hit me and then my prison suddenly expanded dramatically. I almost fell out of the robot’s butt, except I held on for some reason.

He was trying to poop me out, along with several new robots which were sitting to my left.

I turned myself sideways and braced my legs and back. I was wedged solid.

I could see the street to my right and all I had to do was let go and I would be free.

“Hank? What are you doing?” I heard Garm say.

Garm and Cliston were standing behind the robot watching me.

“Nothing,” I said, straining. Trying to be as nonchalant as possible while stuck in a Dredel Led’s rectum.

The drones the Dredel Led was trying to expel were stuck. Because I wasn’t moving, they couldn’t drop out. I was a living, breathing constipation.

Garm fired several times past me, but the bullets ricocheted harmlessly.

“Watch it,” I said.

“Sir, what are you attempting to accomplish?” Cliston asked.

“I’m not really sure at this point, Cliston.”

“Would you like me to recover your gun for you?” he asked.

“Yes!”

Cliston walked away while I tried to ignore my aching body.

“Is that comfortable?” Garm asked.

“Super comfy. Yeah. I’m thinking of living here. This robot is flexing its sphincter and my legs are going to snap in a second. Do you have any bombs or grenades or anything?”

“No,” she said.

“So what was your plan on fighting this? Have me kill it?” I demanded.

“Pretty much. You know, this is the first time I’ve spoken to someone who was literally up the ass of another creature.”

“You kidding? I do this all the time. Ow, that was my knee!” I said, feeling a joint pop.

To add insult, a horrid brown gas wafted past me with a hiss.

Cliston returned a few moments later holding Gun.

“I’m going to count to five and throw it to you, sir.”

“Count to one!”

He threw Gun, but I didn’t catch it. It slid out and fell back on the ground.

Cliston threw it two more times and I caught it on the third try.

“Eat suck, suckface!” I yelled, and I pointed the gun to my left, which was up the digestive tract.

“Oh, yeah,” I mumbled. “Gun, can you hear me?”

“Yes. Hello.”

“Hi! Um, I need you to shoot at full power straight ahead. Right now.”

“Sure. Bang!”

The walls of the Dredel Led closed on me.





CHAPTER 59




I was lying down in my bathtub, recovering from the thirty-minute ordeal of being dragged out of the Dredel Led.

The bath was filled with salts, spritzes, effervescent, aloes, acids, and probably everything except axle grease. My muscles couldn’t decide if they should spasm, tingle, or be numb.

Cliston stirred the bath and added ingredients he deemed appropriate.

“You shot the Dredel Led’s head off. So it can’t eat anymore and it seems to be walking randomly,” Garm said, leaning against the bathroom wall.

“It’s still walking?” I asked.

“Yup. The Navy has it surrounded, but they haven’t destroyed it. I think they’re using it to film a propaganda video. You know, to show that the war with the Dredel Led is necessary. Frankly, I don’t think they have any weapons big enough to hurt the robot.”

“Tell them to shoot it with that big tacky gun that creamed my house,” I said.

“Tachyon. That’s one of the main weapons on Wardian Boshe’s Armage. It’s meant to shoot planetary installations. Your house was probably the smallest target it could hit. As big as that Dredel Led is, it’s not that big—and it’s moving. Besides, at this point it’s somewhat near the telescopes, so I don’t think they’ll risk it.”

“Sorry for wrecking your car,” I said.

“No, it’s my fault. I know you can’t drive at the best of times and you were high on that Ontakian drug. Though I’m not sure how you managed to run into a twenty-foot tall support beam,” Garm said.

“Sucks about Ermie,” I added.

“He died for a noble cause, sir.”

“What cause was that, Cliston?”

“Protecting this city from a Dredel Led that should never have been created,” he said.

“Those drones, as you called them, looked a lot like Ermie. Do you think the Emerald Crusader was a prototype?” I asked.

“I’m not certain. Thaxmultaerea has been behaving erratically since the start of hostilities,” he said.

“What do you think we should do about the hacking going on at the broken telescope?” Garm asked. “The Dredel Led might send more attackers.”

“The first building that Cliston and I went to was a PCC facility. Even if the generator I stole was the same one I took from that military base, how did it get back to the Navy? Why were there Navy guards there? The Ontakians hate the PCC. I can’t believe they were working together.”

“I don’t know. Maybe they were Ontakians wearing Navy uniforms as a disguise?” Garm suggested.

“That doesn’t make sense. Marching around in the streets brings all kinds of unnecessary attention. If they wanted to make it hidden, they would stay inside.”

“I control the port and the telescopes. We aren’t technically supposed to have a military presence anywhere else. We aren’t authorized.”

“How would you get authorization?” I asked.

“Like anything else: request it. Submit a thousand pieces of paperwork. And then wait.”

“Could one of the ship commanders have troops down here?”

“If they had authorization, sure. They wouldn’t have to clear it with me either, because it’s not the port or the telescopes,” she said.

“Well, that sucks. There’s a million ships and ship commanders out there,” I said.

“Perhaps the Navy didn’t know they were guarding a building that was hacking my people,” Cliston offered.

“That sounds right. The telescope that is currently hacking wasn’t known to us,” Garm said.

“Not known to you, but what about the rest of the Navy?” I asked.

“It’s far-fetched to think there are a whole bunch of Navy factions working against each other,” Garm said.

“The more I think about it, the more I believe the Ontakians didn’t know about the hack at all. They have nothing to gain from it. They do odd jobs for Thad’s Friend. I believe he’s the one behind all this,” I said.

“I can’t see a Rettosian scientist on Floloria having any ability to coerce the Navy to guard a building here,” Garm said.

“I can’t either,” I replied. “But we already guessed that Floloria might not be a space station at all. It might really be the million-mind Dredel Led, Kai’Un.”

“But sir, what would Thad’s Friend get out of creating Kai’Un? That isn’t clear to me.”

“None of this is clear to me, Cliston.”

“If the Navy really had a hand in any of this, I can try and find out,” Garm said.

“How would you do that?” I asked.

“You said there were troops guarding a building. The soldiers have to eat. They have to sleep somewhere. They need guns, water, blankets, boots, and a regular paycheck. All of that creates paperwork. I just have to find proof of it.”

“How long do you estimate for that?” Cliston asked.

“Somewhere between now and before I die. This System creates an awful lot of forms, you can imagine.”

“I think I’ll run that mission with the Society of the Free and Easy,” I said.

“To assassinate the Member of Parliament? I thought we agreed you weren’t going to do that, sir,” Cliston said, surprised.

“What? Assassinate Parliament? Are you nuts?” Garm asked.

“A ship carrying a politician is coming here. Thad’s Friend wants us to kill him.”

“Why?” she replied.

“I don’t know. I’m hoping if I do this, I can find out more about what Thad’s Friend is doing.”

“You’re going to murder a Parliamentarian?” she asked.

“No, I’m going to double-cross the Ontakians. I’ll need Frank’s help.”

“Betray the Ontakians, sir?”

“They’re zealots, Cliston. You can’t reason with people like that. You can’t negotiate with them. If you gave them a billion credits, they still wouldn’t budge.”

“Whereas you’ll cut out your own tongue for a hundred credits,” Garm said.

“More than a hundred. But it would grow back, anyway.”

“What about your girlfriend?” Garm asked.

“You know me, I think I’m doomed to be single.”





CHAPTER 60




“What do you want?” Frank said at his door.

“Hi, Uncle!” I answered with mock cheerfulness.

He opened the door but didn’t invite me in. I entered after him. This was the first time I had come to Frank’s home on Belvaille.

It was wall-to-wall with stolen junk. I assumed it was all stolen. People with stolen tele screens and clothes and power tools tend to stack and arrange them a certain way.

“Are you a fence or a thief?” I asked, at the array of junk.

“Man’s got to make a living,” he said, not answering my question.

He was drinking beer and didn’t have on his usual breathing apparatus.

“Aren’t you worried about poison air?” I asked.

“Got more on my mind. Besides, I can’t drink with it on.”

I had never met anyone who drank as much alcohol, as fast as my uncle. It was like he was permanently in a beer-drinking contest where his life depended on the results.

“Why are you here, Junior?”

“Junior?” I asked. “I’m like twice as big as you.”

“And six times heavier, you always leave that part out.”

“I need you to not help the Ontakians attack that politician,” I said.

“Oh, that. I wasn’t going to do it anyway.”

“How were you going to get out of it? Just tell them you weren’t interested?”

“They’re a bit harder to convince than that. I was going to say I had diarrhea. If they came over to check, I have a gallon of Creamy Koona milk I was going to drink.”

“Does that give us Ontakians diarrhea?” I asked.

“No, it gives me diarrhea. It’ll probably just make you fatter.”

“Why weren’t you going to help the Society? I thought you were in good with them.”

“I’m in with them when it’s a paying job. I don’t work for free. But they’re getting weirder than usual. Attacking Parliament? That’s suicide.”

“You think they’ll fail?” I asked.

“No, I think they’ll succeed. It’s the PCC after all. But you can’t knock off their top politicians and expect them to leave you alone. That’s what, a quadrillion or so cops chasing you for the rest of your life? No, thanks.”

“Do you really hate the PCC like they do?”

“Kid, I honestly don’t give a crap. As long as I can do what I want to do, who cares what bubbleheads are in charge?”

“I gave this a lot of thought last night. I believe I honestly like the PCC. It’s vapid and terrible and it surrounds you with a protective cocoon of imbecility.”

Frank laughed and drank another beer in two seconds, crushing the can to get the liquid out faster.

“You know the PCC won’t harm you,” I continued. “It wants nothing more than to be confusing and ineffective. It lets you be your own person as it limps along. Because the government can’t solve anything, and it can’t really break anything, you’re given almost absolute freedom. Belvaille is still here and it’s still full of criminals. Yeah, we have to jump through some hoops, but it’s not difficult.”

Frank finished two more beers as I talked.

“I said for years that I despised the PCC. I was sick of the endless laws and rules. But the point is—”

“You can’t possibly follow them all,” Frank finished.

“Exactly. There’s too many. They’re contradictory. So in a way you get to pick and choose which rules you want to obey,” I said.

Frank put his breathing mask on.

“The way I think of the PCC,” Frank started, “is that they got all these rules to cover their ass. If something bad happens, they can point and go, ‘see, they were breaking the law.’ But unless you’re really in someone’s face causing problems, no one is going to bother you. It’s a big joke in a way.”

“We seem to view it kind of the same. So you’ll help me rescue the politician?”

“Oh, no. I didn’t say anything about that,” Frank replied. “I said I’ll sit on the can and pretend I got the runs. That’s a big difference from fighting the Society of the Free and Easy with just the two of us.”

“Only Najosa is a combat model. But I’m hoping we won’t have to fight them,” I said.

“They have a lot of very powerful hardware.”

“You mean like that plasma warhead we recovered on the ship we boarded?”

“Their plasma weapons won’t do much to Ontakians. But they got a whole lot of machine guns, mortars, and everything else. And like I said, I don’t work for free. How much are you paying?”

“I guess I’m already in debt. I can borrow more money. Then maybe Ulteem will hire you to break my kneecaps for not paying him back.”

“The Society is not a group I want mad at me,” Frank said.

“Let me figure out how to smooth things over with them. Maybe I can offer them some free bullets as a make-up gift. What else do Ontakians like?”

“You could break up with Najosa. That would be a present.”

“I think stopping them from killing the politician will be my way of breaking up with her. It’s easier than a direct confrontation about it. Though I hate being passive aggressive.”

“Only an Ontakian would consider a gun battle passive aggressive,” Frank said.

“Let’s hope it doesn’t come to that.”

“It’s too bad. I bet Najosa was great in bed.”

I sighed.

“She was violent. It was stressful. Look, I’m not a great lover and I don’t pretend to be. But even I know that I shouldn’t have to check myself for broken bones immediately after sex.”





CHAPTER 61




I was enjoying some discounted sausages and beer, courtesy of The Club. Fample had stayed sort of true to his word, and decided to give me a price reduction on food and drink for fighting the Agglatar.

Half off. I wasn’t complaining.

“What are you doing here?” I asked, looking up from my food.

Najosa sat down at my table.

“We have to talk,” she whispered. “The Member of Parliament is here.”

“In The Club?” I scanned around but didn’t see anyone who fit the description of a PCC Parliamentarian. Though I doubt I would recognize one if I saw him.

“No, on Belvaille,” she said.

“How did you get in here? This club is for members only,” I said.

“That’s what the guy at the door told me. Until I broke his jaw.”

“What did the security guards say?” I asked.

“One didn’t say anything. One of them screamed a lot when he tried to touch me.”

“Najosa, you can’t…” I started to explain, but then I thought better of it. I was already going to double-cross her on this mission. No need to pester her about little details like who she should and shouldn’t beat up. “It’s only one politician?” I asked.

“Yeah, just one. Apparently, he’s important. We’re putting together a strike team and we need your help. Let’s get out of here, I don’t like talking with all these Colmarians around.”

The Club didn’t have to-go bags, but I picked up my remaining sausages and carried them, after downing half my beer.

Najosa scowled at me.

“You’re supposed to be on a diet,” she said.

I didn’t reply.

She wanted to plan the attack at my place, but I decided we should go to Frank’s.

From our last talk, I had a major concern that Frank wasn’t going to help me with the Ontakians. Being there in person made it harder for him to get out of it. And if he tried to pull the diarrhea gag, I would call up Cliston and get him over with some home remedies.

Frank’s apartment was small. Especially with all his stolen goods inside. It was a tight fit with the Society of the Free and Easy there.

“You know, Frank, like 90% of the city is unoccupied. Why did you have to pick the smallest apartment?” I asked.

“I like to have the walls close by,” he replied.

“Never heard that being a selling point of a home,” I said.

“Enough chitchat,” Najosa barked.

Nordan handed me a slip of paper.

“This is where we tracked the hated politician to. There are two guards outside, rotating on schedules. At least four more inside. They aren’t Navy guards. They are either private hires or Parliament staff,” he said.

The slip of paper didn’t even have an address. It was a black-and-white drawing of the block. But quite a detailed drawing. I could immediately place where it was.

“I know this building. It’s an old Nobleman’s home,” I said. “Who drew this, by the way?”

Shella, the architect, raised her hand.

“Good stuff,” I said.

“Since you know the building and area, we’re going to rely on you to formulate our attack vectors,” Najosa said.

“I don’t know what that means,” I started, but then I got an idea. “Oh, we’re going to have to split into groups.”

“Why?” Nordan asked, skeptical.

“Because…the hallways are really narrow. And winding. We won’t be able to make a good…what do you call it…shooting line. You know, we’ll be blasting each other in the back. Also, he could be in one of three areas,” I explained.

“So? We’ll neutralize the guards and search them all,” he replied.

“I know you guys think you’re hot stuff, but if we go storming into a Parliament Member’s home, the Navy is going to descend on us. Time is a factor.”

“We’ll take them out, too,” Nordan said dismissively.

The other Society members seemed to agree.

“This is a mission, right? The mission is to kill the Member of Parliament. Getting in a street fight with a hundred Navy guards armed with heavy weapons isn’t part of the mission,” I said.

We all turned to Najosa, who was deep in thought.

“Agreed,” she said finally.

“Okay, I’ll go with Frank,” I offered.

“No. We split up the combat models. You, me, Frank,” she said.

Ack.

“I go with my nephew or I don’t go at all,” Frank stated.

He had been silent the whole time. I thought it was pretty crafty of him to say that. Not because he wanted us to go together. He didn’t want to go at all. This was his way of trying to get out of the mission.

Najosa was fuming, and I thought she was going to cut him loose. Then I’d have to double-cross the Society all by my lonesome.

“Alright!” She barked. “Hank, can you get us detailed floorplans of the inside of that building?”

“No. How could I possibly do that?” I asked.

“You said he could be in one of three rooms,” she demanded.

“Yeah, but I can’t remember exactly where they are,” I lied. “I think one is the second floor, one is the ninth floor, and one is the twelfth floor.”

“It only has nine floors,” Nordan said.

“See? I can’t remember it perfectly.”

Good thing the Society didn’t know much about Colmarians. Or nobles. Or housing. Every rich person that ever existed, lived on the top floor of their mansion. Made it easier to look down on people. I knew that’s where all the master bedrooms were located in that building, and that’s likely where the politician would be.

I wanted to try and take a break. That way I could warn the politician to leave and maybe rendezvous with him later.

But Najosa had us all march over to the Society of the Free and Easy headquarters. Which was the bar I had originally met them at.

Upstairs, they had a suite of rooms they stayed in, and a considerable armory.

“Boy, I never get tired of standing next to this,” I said, patting the plasma warhead delicately.

The Massive Enhanced Plasma Ordnance rested in the middle of the armory, where anyone could, and would, bump into it while they were outfitting themselves.

“Everyone suit up. Light armor. I want this a quick operation,” Najosa said, then she pulled me aside. “What’s wrong with you?” she asked. “You’re acting really weird.”

“Me? I’m always weird. I pick my nose and paint my toenails. Sometimes I pick my nose with my toenails,” I said.

“Are you sure you’re okay with this assignment? You expressed some reservations before,” she tried to confirm.

“Nah, let’s do it. I want to assassinate a Parliament guy. What’s his name, anyway?”

“What does it matter?” she asked.

“How will we know it’s him?”

“It’s not as if he’ll tell us his name when we’re attacking,” she said.

“If I know big shots, he probably will.”





CHAPTER 62




The Society of the Free and Easy was certainly well armed.

It seemed every member was carrying approximately 500 weapons apiece. They had machine guns, rifles, shotguns, pistols, knives, whips, grenades, nets, clubs, landmines, and I think I saw a few slingshots.

I don’t know how well that politician was protected, but unless he had the actual God of Ass-Kicking guarding him, he was probably in for a lot of trouble. Frank looked decidedly uneasy with the prospect of betraying this group.

I personally didn’t bother with armor, which would only slow me down, or any weapons other than Gun.

The Ontakians only had one car and it was all mangled and cramped from hauling the plasma warhead some months ago.

Our gun-toting strike force could only fit about three people in their car.

“I can drive my car,” I volunteered.

“No, you’ll jeopardize the mission,” Najosa said. “You’re a horrible driver.”

“We going to walk across the city, murder a Member of Parliament, and then walk away?” I asked.

It took a little while, but we managed to hire two taxis to shuttle us to our assassination.

“We’re going to a costume competition,” I tried to explain to the driver.

“I didn’t say nothing,” the driver replied worriedly, seeing our arsenal of weapons.

The good part was we were riding in style, because the taxis were limousines. Since so many people left behind their cars when they abandoned Belvaille, there wasn’t a huge need for taxi services. The handful that remained were either heavy trucks for hauling items or limos.

We were just seven Ontakians, covered in body armor and loaded with weapons, driving along in limousines.

Totally inconspicuous.

It was close to midnight when we finally pulled right up to the front door of the building we were about to attack.

Everything was telling me to abandon this ridiculous mission and Frank was also making it clear he wanted no part of it.

But I felt I had a semi-moral obligation to protect the politician of the empire I wasn’t a part of from the maybe-diabolical plans of the Rettosian scientist.

What was I doing here? I asked myself for the tenth time.

“Go! Go! Go!” Najosa called out.

I’d like to say we poured out of the limos like trained Ontakian combatants. But people were tripping over their guns, or forgot to take off their seat belts, or otherwise got tangled and held up.

When we finally collected ourselves on the sidewalk, I was the first to speak.

“I think we should just ring the doorbell at this point,” I said.

“Shh,” Najosa hissed.

We headed to the building.

The mansion was one of the smaller, but more modern structures the nobles had created back when Belvaille was thriving.

The building was various shades of blue and seemingly composed of nothing but long columns and cylinders. The blue coloring gave the building a wild, optical-illusion feel that showed up a lot more in the daylight.

But we weren’t here to admire the architecture.

The front door was our first obstacle.

“You’re kidding. Out of all this hardware no one has a lock pick?” I asked.

“I can pick it, but this place has a security system too,” Frank said.

“Move,” I said.

I looked under the welcome mat, in the fake, potted plant, and on top of the door frame, but there was no keycard.

“Okay, I’m out of ideas,” I stated.

“Kick in the door,” Najosa commanded.

“Back up,” I said, clearing a path.

I got a big running start and I plowed into and through the front door, slid about halfway down the adjoining hallway, knocked over two tables, and came to a messy stop inside a closet.

These floors sure were buffed and polished.

“I told you to kick in the door,” Najosa said, when she had entered after me.

“I’m not a kicking kind of guy,” I replied.

“He isn’t,” Frank confirmed. “He can’t even raise his leg that high. He’s in terrible shape.”

“Thanks, Frank,” I said, getting to my feet.

“Anyone that’s here probably heard that commotion and is calling for help. We need to split up and execute the assignment. Form into your groups and get going,” Najosa said. But then she quickly pointed and shouted, “Target!”

A half-dozen guns opened fire.

I didn’t even see what they were shooting at because I had been facing the other direction.

After a moment, they were done firing and I saw him.

It was just one guy standing there.

He was about seven feet tall. Muscular. And he wore robes that, besides the copious amount of bullet holes, were covered in books and symbols and papers. He was also wrapped in thick, loose chains.

The fact that he was totally unfazed by the gunfire wasn’t the most amazing thing. It was that he was completely wreathed in an eerie, flickering, blue flame.

And then I felt it: fear.

Irrational. Unreasonable. Overpowering. Fear.

“It can’t be,” I stammered.

Najosa threw down her submachine gun and drew a pistol from her thigh. She flicked on the power and a high-pitched scream emanated from the weapon. It was like a million fingernails dragging across a million chalkboards.

Najosa fired and a blinding flash of orange light shot out. The entire front lobby of the mansion was now gone.

But the man was still standing there, oblivious.

“The plasma didn’t hurt him. He’s an Ontakian!” Frank said, and even his voice betrayed the fear he felt.

“No,” I began.

“Would you please give me a donation?” the man said stupidly.

“He’s an Agglatar,” I finished.

The Agglatar’s fists glowed with two energy balls. He threw them one after the other.

Kercrash!

I blinked myself back to consciousness as Frank helped me to my feet.

Where was I?

Then Frank started shaking me.

“What the hell is that thing?” he yelled.

“What thing?” I asked. I tasted blood.

“The scary guy with the books!”

I heard two more explosions but they seemed distant. Had I gotten blown into another mansion? Had Frank carried me to safety?

“Oh. He’s an Agglatar,” I said.

“What’s that?”

“He’s like…I don’t know. The Boranjame told me about him. I killed him once. But I don’t think he can die.”

“What is it with you fighting immortals?” Frank yelled, and he let go of me so that I fell to the floor. My knees were weak. “First it was Thad Elon and now this guy. Screw it. I’m out of here.”
