Frank started to storm off and I noticed that he was bleeding. Everywhere. The rear and right side of his armor were completely destroyed and he was covered with burnt, bleeding flesh.

“Frank, you have to help me with…the Society,” I whispered.

He turned back and almost hit me.

“They’re dead!”

“What?”

“Well, probably they are. They’re fighting your demigod. And they’re welcome to it.”

“Frank, I need you,” I implored.

“You haven’t paid me! I told you I don’t work for free. I’m going to a hospital. I suggest you do the same.”

And he left.

He just walked away.

I couldn’t believe it.

I know I joked around a lot about my sleazy uncle, but here he was ditching me. Right when I needed him most. If he was anyone else, I would have shot him in the back.

I pulled out Gun to see if he was ready to fight an Agglatar.

“Gun, you there?”

“I don’t know. Am I?”

I dropped Gun and examined myself for the first time. I was bleeding. Both my legs. That’s why I had fallen.

I wasn’t sure how bad they were. I tried to stand up and I felt an extreme tightness and pain, but no broken bones.

Another explosion erupted.

Yeah, I was still in the mansion. But I wasn’t in the same hallway. Frank indeed must have pulled me to safety. Pulled himself, too, of course. But he could have left me there.

I guess I’m glad I didn’t shoot him.

I was tired. I was beat. I didn’t feel I could safely shoot Gun. If I passed out in front of the Agglatar he might easily kill me.

And that would suck.

I needed to help the Society of the Free and Easy. Help them get a shorter name. But also help them fight that…whatever it was.

As I hobbled back toward the explosions, I couldn’t help thinking how bad I was at double-crossing people.





CHAPTER 63




I made my way to the front of the mansion.

The floor was littered with shell casings from every kind of gun imaginable. As well as pockmarked with small craters from explosions. The walls were completely blown apart. It’s amazing the upper floors hadn’t collapsed.

I saw what was left of the Society of the Free and Easy. They had tried to make a stand. Tried and failed.

“Hank,” I heard a weak voice call out.

I ran over to her.

“Najosa!”

She was lying in a pool of blood and her armor and weapons were all but melted and gone.

“Shoot…” she tried to say.

“Don’t talk. I’ll get you to a doctor,” I said.

“No! No Colmarians,” she replied.

“Now? You’re going to take a stand against Colmarians now? I’m not going to lie, you’re in bad shape, Najosa.”

“No Colmarians!” She coughed, and blood splattered across my face.

“O-okay. But you know there’s not a big medical community on Belvaille. I don’t think we have any Qwintine or Gandrine doctors.”

“Listen,” she said, gripping my chest tightly. “Shoot…the light.”

“I…uh…alright. I’ll shoot the light. I promise,” I said, patting her arms gently. I assumed she was talking gibberish.

“No. That man. He throws…light. Burns. Explode. Shoot it.”

“Oh! The Agglatar. Shoot the energy balls in his hands?” I asked.

“Yes,” she said, and her body relaxed noticeably.

“Hey, uh, Najosa. There’s something I’ve been meaning to tell you,” I began.

Her eyes were bloody. She had two cracked teeth. A patch of her hair was smoldering. She tried to focus her attention on me as I spoke.

“You know, never mind. This isn’t the time,” I said.

She was shivering now but attempting to say something.

“Fuh…” she mouthed.

“What?” I asked, putting my ear closer.

“Fuh…” she tried again.

She was fading fast.

“Fat,” Najosa said finally.

Then she exhaled and was gone.

Did she just call me fat with her dying breath?

Najosa and I hadn’t seen things the same. Remotely the same. And, yes, I was going to break up with her and even double-cross her. But I didn’t want her dead.

She was a strong person. She certainly stood up for what she believed in and was willing to sacrifice for her ideals. Not many people could say that.

I straightened my pants and took out Gun.

“Gun!”

“Hi.”

“Can you shoot at blobs of energy that are created in an Agglatar’s hands? You need to shoot them in the air or while he’s still holding them.”

I waited for a response, preparing myself for the inevitable argument that would follow.

“I think so,” he said after a moment.

“Um. Good. I’ll…no, I’m not going to say anything more. Let’s leave it at that.”

I heard an explosion upstairs. The Agglatar was still in the mansion. Probably killing the poor security guards who were with the politician.

I was going to avenge Najosa. And the Society of the Free and Easy. And save the life of a Member of Parliament. But mostly avenge Najosa.

But what about his fear aura? I couldn’t fight someone if I was throwing up from terror.

One of the downstairs bathrooms had been demolished, and I saw a metal wastebasket lying in the rubble.

I walked over and examined it. It was about eighteen inches in height. Perfect.

I put the trashcan on my head like a helmet. I then crunched the bottom of it so it was snug around my neck.

Now I couldn’t see the Agglatar and hopefully wouldn’t be afraid of him. I didn’t need to see, I just needed Gun to see.

“Gun, can you hear me?”

“You sound odd,” he said.

I took a few steps and ran into the wall. Then I felt my way to the stairs, managed to walk up a dozen, then I stepped on some debris, tripped, and rolled back down to the ground floor.

Okay, this was stupid.

I untwisted the trashcan from my head.

I went back up the stairs, trying to get a sense of where the Agglatar was in the building. He shouldn’t be hard to find.

Shouldn’t be.

But after creeping around for ten minutes, I didn’t see or hear him. I saw some evidence of his passing, as there were more spent bullet casings on the ground, some walls had unsightly holes in them, and I found two security personnel who were no longer providing security.

I had been taking the stairs and I was winded. My legs hurt. I was hungry. I was really hungry.

Was I fat?

Najosa said that before she died and that kind of gave it a sense of importance. What a crappy thing to tell someone as you’re about to hitchhike to the afterlife.

“Give me a donation, please,” I heard a deep voice say behind me.

“No!” I whirled around.

I was scared. Petrified.

The Agglatar stood there. He didn’t have a scratch on him even after wiping out a small army of Ontakians and parliamentary police.

He looked upset.

A ball of light began to form in his right hand.

“Gun, shoot the energy!” I blurted.

“Bang!”

Gun fired and the Agglatar exploded to the left. The ball of light was gone.

“Pah!” I cried out with joy. “It worked!”

Gun had actually done it.

I wasn’t scared. I was excited.

The Agglatar stood up from where he had fallen. He didn’t look hurt. He looked angrier than ever.

“Give me a donation,” he said.

“No. Throw another energy ball at me,” I taunted.

The Agglatar put down his arms and began to run toward me.

“Hey!” I said. “Throw the thing. The light!”

He hit me square in my stomach with his right shoulder.

I doubled up over his back and he lifted me like a wet towel, throwing me behind him.

I cartwheeled in the air and landed flat on my back.

I turned over and lay on my chest, holding Gun with both hands.

The Agglatar faced me and created a glowing ball in each hand.

Before I could even speak:

“Bang! Bang!” Gun said.

The energy balls exploded, knocking the Agglatar backward where he hit the wall. His upper body actually protruded outside of the mansion.

The explosions had hit me as well and there was not only force, but heat. I rubbed my eyes and blinked them. My eyebrows had been burnt away. I couldn’t see out of my left eye, which only saw spots, but I could see out of my right.

I saw the Agglatar running at me.

He picked me up from the floor.

“Give me a donation!” He screamed.

His breath smelled minty.

“Shut up,” I said.

He punched me with a haymaker and I went tumbling down the hallway. My nose was broken and blood was smeared all over my face.

I pointed Gun again, waiting for the glowing balls, but the Agglatar had apparently learned not to do that. He came at me again and this time kicked me square in the neck.

I flipped feet-over-head.

I splatted on the ground a short distance away and coughed and gasped for air. How was this guy so strong? I weighed thousands of pounds and he was tossing me around like I was a feather.

He walked over and stood on my chest as he looked down at me.

“Give me money, please!” He said.

He took turns punching me with each fist. My head was whipping back and forth and I was so dazed I didn’t have a chance to be afraid.

He picked me up and rammed me into the wall. Once. Twice. Thrice. Green. Blopery. Gnerfuper.

I went limp. I felt myself passing out of consciousness.

He turned me over and screamed at me.

“Give me a donation!”

I don’t know where I got the energy. Frustration? Oncoming death?

“Donation for what?” I yelled.

And amazingly, he stopped.

“I must complete my purpose,” he said.

I could barely see him from my compound concussions, skull fractures, seared eyeballs, and copious amount of blood in my face.

“What is your damn purpose? What do you want a donation for?”

I didn’t mind dying so much. I just wanted to know why.

“I must destroy the First and Last,” he said.

I paused a second.

“Thad Elon?” I asked.

“It is known as that as well. Yes.”

“You want a donation to destroy Thad Elon?”

“Yes.”

“Well, shit, why didn’t you say so?”

“You will give me a donation?” he asked.

“Hell, yeah,” I said, slumping out of his grip. “I don’t…have any money on me.”

“Give me a donation,” he said, cocking back his fist.

“I will! I’ll totally give you all my money. Just…you know, stop punching me.”

The Agglatar helped me to my feet, but I fell back down.

“Give me a donation,” he said. But the malice was gone from his voice. I think I must have been the first person who ever agreed and he didn’t understand what to do next.

“Okay. We need to go to my house. Do you have a car? I guess you don’t have a car. It’s okay, here, hold on, don’t kick me or anything.”

I took out my tele.

“Cliston! Hi. I really need your help.”

“Sir, you look horrific! What happened to you?”

“Never mind that. I need you to get my money. The loan I got from Ulteem. And—” I looked back to the Agglatar. “How much money do you need?” I asked him.

It was clear he didn’t know. He probably didn’t even know what money was.

“Hey, forget it, buddy. Cliston, bring all my money.”

The Agglatar suddenly leaned over me and stared into the tele screen at Cliston.

“Give me a donation,” he demanded of the image.

“My goodness!” Cliston exclaimed.

“Yeah, Cliston, do you have any money I can borrow from you? He’s going to ask when you get here.”

“I…do, sir. But I don’t like to spend it.”

“It’s for a good cause, Cliston. I swear. This guy is the Agglatar.”

“Oh. From the Boranjame poem?”

“Yeah. He’s not very lyrical, though. Can you bring the money fast?”

I saw the Agglatar eyeing me suspiciously so I decided to reword it.

“The donation. Bring the donations fast.”

“Yes, sir. Just give me your location.”

I told Cliston where I was and I hung up.

I sat there a foot away from the Agglatar, waiting for an hour.

“Give me a donation,” he said for the eightieth time.

“I know. I know. It’s coming, remember?” I said, holding my hands up in a sign of submission.

Cliston walked up the stairs carrying a satchel.

“Hello, sir.”

“Give me a donation, please,” the Agglatar immediately said, his voice angry.

“It is here, Mr. Agglatar, sir.”

Cliston unzipped the bag of credits and put it on the ground.

The Agglatar cautiously walked over, keeping an eye on my broken form and Cliston.

He looked inside the bag and then quickly snatched it up and clutched it to his chest.

He kept staring back and forth between Cliston and me.

It was clear he didn’t know what to do next.

“That’s all my money,” I said. “I can’t give you any more of a donation than that.”

A ball of light began to glow in his hand.

“Oh, come on,” I complained.

He hurled it at the wall and it blew a hole to the outside.

The Agglatar then ran and jumped out of the sixth story and was gone.

“Sir, you need medical attention,” Cliston said, kneeling next to me.

“I do, Cliston. I really do. But I need to see the politician in this building first. If he’s still alive.”





CHAPTER 64




Cliston and I took the elevator up to the top floor of the mansion, as I was too beat up to walk the stairs.

“The main living quarters are up here,” I said.

“I really think we should get you to a hospital, sir,” Cliston replied, as he helped me along. “Your eyebrows are missing, by the way.”

“Yeah. But don’t worry, I’ll be fine,” I said, sniffling some blood from my broken nose.

There was no damage up here that I could see, so the Agglatar presumably hadn’t made his sales pitch this far.

The first two rooms we went into were empty. But on entering the third room:

Blam! Blam! Blam!

A man in a fine suit emptied a pistol at us. He missed so badly that it was pretty obvious who he was.

“I take it you’re a Member of Parliament?” I asked.

The man was handsome, middle-aged, and had absolutely perfect hair. He was standing with a taller, older man, who was wearing long robes.

“Who are you?” the gun-shooter demanded.

“Usually people ask that before they open fire,” I replied.

The guy was scared. The tall man in robes didn’t look concerned at all. He seemed completely oblivious. Almost relaxed.

Since neither of them were talking, I guessed it was up to me.

“Hi. I’m Hank. I saved your lives from a very aggressive non-profit.”

“What’s going on? Who was that?” the shooter asked.

“You know, I really don’t have the time or inclination to go over it. Besides, you probably wouldn’t believe me. Let’s just say I’m here to help you.”

“Is that…is that a Dredel Led?” he asked of Cliston.

I looked at my robot butler. They were technically at war with the PCC.

“No,” I began. “He’s not. He’s…something else.”

“What is he?” the man asked suspiciously.

“Does it matter? You’re alive. There’s a bunch of people in this building who aren’t. I need some answers,” I barked.

“What kind of answers?”

That was a good question.

“Well, why is a Member of Parliament here? Oh, and what’s your name? Name first, I guess.”

“I’m not a Member of Parliament. He is,” he said, pointing to the tall, robed man beside him, who still hadn’t moved or shown any emotion.

“Does he not speak?”

“That is correct.”

“Oh. Who are you?”

“I am his translator and assistant, Jer. He is Chief Minister of the Sub-Subcommittee on Extra-Non-Military Expenditures and Evaluations. His name is The Honorable Nanea Quoffus.”

“Did you get all that?” I asked Cliston.

“Yes, sir.”

“Good. Alright, Jer. What are you all doing here?”

“That’s Parliamentary Prerogative and none of your concern,” he said stiffly.

“Heh. Alright. Let’s forget I just saved your life for a second,” I said.

I pulled out Gun and pointed in the general direction of Nanea and Jer.

“Again, what are you all doing on Belvaille?”

Jer looked to Nanea, who nodded, raised an eyebrow, flared his nostrils, and made a few other very subtle gestures.

“Chief Chair Nanea refuses to communicate while at gunpoint,” Jer said.

I put Gun away with a sigh.

“This guy is kind of an ass, huh, Cliston?”

Nanea’s face twisted into a scowl.

“Oh, he can still hear?” I asked.

“Yes, his senses are fully functional, he merely chooses not to speak. He has taken a vow of silence,” Jer said.

“Of course he has,” I mumbled. “Right. Sorry about that, Mr. Minister. I’m not used to dealing with, you know, such men of principle.”

“Now explain who you are and what’s going on here,” Jer said.

“I’m…I’m the Master Overseer of Navy and Belvaille Relations. Hank. You were attacked by various groups,” I said. “I saved you.”

“Master Overseer, is it safe for us to remain here?”

I thought about that, and while I didn’t feel like moving anytime soon, I figured the Navy might show up with some soldiers to find out what all the explosions were about.

“No, it’s not safe here. We’ll go to my place,” I said.

“Sir, our home isn’t prepared to receive guests of this stature,” Cliston said urgently.

“They just survived a bloodbath, Cliston. I think they can handle some dirty carpets and finger food,” I replied.





CHAPTER 65




Cliston drove us back to City Hall.

Me, so beat up that Life Itself was going to put me on the Injured Reserve list. Nanea, the Minister of Parliament who didn’t talk. And his translator, Jer.

When we got to the elevator, I felt bad again about Ermie being gone. How he loved to press the buttons.

Now Najosa was gone as well.

It sure was dangerous being my friend.

“Do you live here?” Jer asked, after getting some non-verbal prompts from Chief Chieftain, Nanea.

“This is where we can talk,” I said. Not sure why I was being coy. I just didn’t trust these guys yet.

Cliston was a blur.

He cleaned up every room we stepped foot in, prepared some food and drinks, bandaged me, and managed to follow every diplomatic protocol that was expected. Of course, he did it at like fifty miles an hour.

We were all seated in my makeshift living room. I was not bleeding…much. And we were enjoying some tea and cookies.

When Cliston zipped out of the room, Jer leaned over to whisper to me.

“Do you realize our empire is engaged in open aggression with the Dredel Led?” he asked.

“Not my empire. I’m a citizen of Belvaille.”

“Where?” he asked, confused.

“Oh, right. Portal Depot #4382,” I corrected.

Nanea made some hand gestures.

“Yes, the Minister is concerned about this region’s dubious legal standing,” Jer said.

“That’s nice of him. But I need to know why you’re here,” I said.

“As we stated, that is Parliamentary Prerogative,” Jer replied.

“You don’t get it. People are trying to kill you. They almost succeeded. All your security guards are dead. I can help you, but I need to know what this is about. For instance: why are you in this System?”

Jer and Nanea went back and forth nodding and blinking and smirking and tilting their heads.

I realized I was too tired for this. My body was trying to repair itself and I needed food. Cliston sensed my troubles and he brought me a lot of cookies. About two pounds of them. I ate them all and slurped down my tea.

“The Minister has instructed me to tell you that he is here to investigate some irregularities that have been going on in this System,” Jer said.

“Irregularities?” I laughed.

I couldn’t begin to add up all the weird stuff that’s happened in Belvaille over the centuries.

“You know there’s a Boranjame sitting in this System?” I asked.

“That isn’t one of the Minister’s concerns. He’s here about the Navy and their procurement procedures.”

I stopped stuffing my face for a second.

“That’s it?” I asked, annoyed. “You’re here for bureaucratic niceties? What, is someone not using the right forms?”

I rubbed my forehead. I probably should get to a hospital.

“It’s an extremely important issue,” Jer said.

“Yeah, I bet.”

“You said yourself that someone was trying to kill us. That proves there are parties who don’t want to be discovered.”

“Discovered doing what? Stealing ink and file folders?” I asked.

“There are a tremendous number of accounting discrepancies going on here,” Jer said.

Man, I hope he wasn’t talking about the Skim. If he was, I might have to kill him myself.

“What discrepancies? Give me an example. To help figure out why you’re a target.”

“Well, for one, the payroll grossly exceeds the allotted budget,” Jer continued, after consulting with Nanea.

“Alright. Fat paychecks. Anything else?” I asked. “Hey, Cliston, can you make me some food? Some real food?”

Cliston appeared immediately.

“Sir, one does not yell into other rooms while in the presence of a Parliament Minister. Please excuse him. He’s suffered some injuries.”

Nanea waved his hand magnanimously.

“So that’s it? Payroll issues?” I asked.

“No, there is quite a list,” Jer said.

“I’m not getting any younger,” I replied.

“Purchases. Improvements. Non-employee payroll. Modifications. I’m not sure where to start.”

“What’s a non-employee payroll? Is that like to the people who live on Belvaille?” I asked.

Jer took out his tele and began sifting through.

Cliston thankfully brought me some porridge. I practically dove into it, until Cliston smacked me, wiped my mouth, and handed me a dainty spoon.

“Floloria,” Jer said.

“What?” I asked, mouth full of porridge.

“There is a substantial non-employee payroll to people on a city called Floloria. Do you know of it?”

“You guys are paying people on Floloria?” I asked, amazed.

“Our government is, yes.”

“Who are you paying? There’s no one there.”

Jer went flipping through his tele again.

“There are hundreds of people listed,” he said.

“What are some names?” I asked.

“Let’s see. Zydolium 48. Zydolium 49. Zydolium 50. And on, and on,” Jer said.

“Holy crap,” I said.

I bet those were the clones! They didn’t even bother to hide it. So Thad’s Friend was really named “Zydolium”?

“Who authorized those payments? Who in your government?” I asked.

“The Navy, of course. The Minister is on the Sub-Subcommittee on Extra-Non-Military Expenditures and Evaluations,” he said, like that should make sense.

“Okay. But who authorized it? Who gave the money?” I asked.

“The Head Account Auditor,” he said simply.

“Oh. Yeah. I don’t know who that is,” I said, stumped.

“He reports to the Supreme Requisition Clerk.”

“Well, of course he does.”

“Who reports to the Joint High Wardian and Marshall of the South-by-Southwest Region,” Jer finished.

“The Wardian? Wardian Boshe?”

“He is the only Joint High Wardian in this System,” Jer said.

“So you’re saying that the Wardian is paying people—those names and numbers—on Floloria?” I tried to clarify.

“No,” Jer replied, like I was stupid.

I slumped. I didn’t understand this.

“The Wardian orders his subordinates to pay them. He wouldn’t pay himself,” Jer said.

If I wasn’t so tired, I would have punched the guy.

“Okay. Okay. What else? You said modifications? Purchases?” I asked.

Jer went back to his tele.

“There was one listing that was extremely odd,” he said, flipping through the screens.

I was on the edge of my seat waiting.

“Here. There is a large expenditure for Non-Employee Pharmacological Materials,” he said.

“What’s that mean?”

“That’s what the Minister wants to find out. I did some research and I’ve never heard of a drug called daxapronal,” he said.

“No way!” I started laughing.

Mr. Boshe was actually paying Thad’s Friend, the old Rettosian scientist, Zydolium, to manufacture the Ontakian drug? And he made paperwork for it? It was almost too insane to imagine.

But I guess I was used to criminals. We could just hand someone a bag filled with money. Where would the Navy get the money? Where would they get the bag? They had to fill out forms.

“Wait a minute. Hold on. I’ll be right back,” I said.

I hopped out of the room and into the pantry that Cliston used as a kitchen.

“Sir, one does not leave his guests before the main course is served,” Cliston said.

“Cliston, go entertain them. I have to call Garm.”

“I can’t entertain them, sir. I’m a butler! Besides, they suspect I’m a Dredel Led.”

“Can you do some magic tricks or something?” I asked.

“I know quite a few illusions and sleight of hand I can perform, sir.”

“Really? I’d kind of like to see that. No, wait, you can show me later. Just give me a minute to make a call.”

Cliston took off his apron and left the room.

“Garm! Garm! You won’t believe this. The Navy is behind the hack. They’re behind everything. They’re behind the Ontakians. They’re behind the Rettosian.”

“What are you talking about?” Garm answered. “What happened to your face?”

“It’s Mr. Boshe! They have files that show he requisitioned this stuff,” I said.

“But why?”

“Why what?” I asked.

“Why would he hire the Rettosian? Or the Ontakians? If he wanted to hack the Dredel Led, for whatever reason, he could have instructed my team to do so.”

“To start the war. He gave me some big long speech a while ago about how the Colmarians were at their best when they were fighting. He really believed it. Besides, he was Mister Boshe before the war.”

“And now he’s Joint High Wardian and Marshall of the South-by-Southwest Region,” she said.

“Or whatever.”

“Do you think he also created that big Dredel Led you were talking about?” Garm asked.

“Kai’Un. No, I think he paid the Rettosian to do it so it would vote for war.”

“You said they have paperwork to prove that?” she asked.

“Yeah, tons. So can we get him fired and get this stupid war called off?”

The tele was quiet a moment.

“Are you serious?” she asked.

“Sure. Boshe was working with crazy Rettosians. And crazy Ontakians. And he hacked the Dredel Led. And maybe I can get my fifty million.”

“Hank. It took years for him to get that job. Even if you have a thousand eyewitnesses, it would have to go to trial. There would be investigations. There would be lawsuits.”

“But why? We have proof! You can probably find the paperwork where he hired the Ontakians to assassinate the Minister!”

“This is the PCC. It took that minister six months just to fly out here. It would take a decade to get Wardian Boshe fired.”

“That will be a decade of war,” I said.

“If Wardian Boshe is really behind this—I believe you—and he would go so far as to try and murder a Parliament Minister—”

“Not to mention start an intergalactic war,” I added.

“Right. He’s not going to sit around and let this go to trial. Or for us to investigate. You need to get him out of office immediately. Kill him.”

“Me? You’re the assassin.”

“But you’re Hank,” she said.

I moved the tele all around my face and body so she could get a better view.

“Look at me. Right now I’m half a Hank.”

“Maybe you don’t have to kill him. But you need to stop him before he does any more damage. Talk to the Minister. That politician has civilian oversight.”

“Okay, but it will probably have to wait until tomorrow. It’s like 4 am.”

“Fair enough. But the longer he has, the more he can entrench his position.”

“What’s that even mean?”

“There’s a war on, Hank. After a while it doesn’t matter who started it or why.”

I went back to my visitors and they were busy cheering and applauding at Cliston. Even the mute Minister was holding his sides and belly laughing.

“What? What did I miss?” I asked.

“Nothing, sir. I’ll prepare rooms for our distinguished guests to sleep in.”

“That reminds me. I don’t know how to put this delicately, but I have to consider the Minister’s safety above all else,” Jer said.

“Which is why tomorrow, he has to go speak to Wardian Boshe and have him sacked from his job,” I said.

“Sacked? You mean fired? That’s not why we’re here,” Jer said. “But I was talking about the immediate future. You said you can protect him, but…you look rather injured yourself.”

“Oh, don’t let the blood and scabs fool you. I have a talking, intelligent gun that never misses,” I said.

Jer looked at me like he suddenly realized I had dementia.

“No, seriously. That’s how I survived your attackers. Look.”

I took out Gun and put it on the table in front of us.

“That’s an ancient alien artifact,” I lied.

“May I?” Jer asked.

“Sure. It only shoots if you tell it to,” I explained.

Jer turned over Gun in his hands. At first he held it gingerly, but then he seemed confused.

“It has no trigger. It has nothing mechanical at all,” he said.

“No. It’s like energy. Gravity. Gravity energy. And bioelectricity.”

Jer kept examining Gun skeptically.

“But how does it fire?”

“Bang!” Gun said.

Jer dropped the pistol and sat there with a small hole in his forehead. He had been looking down the barrel a moment before.

My mouth hung open, dumbfounded. The Minister silently shrieked.

I cleared my throat and gently picked up Gun.

“Uh, Gun?” I asked.

“Hello.”

“Why did you shoot him?”

“He said fire.”

“No, he asked how does it fire?”

“Like that,” Gun said simply.





CHAPTER 66




Cliston gave something to Parliament Minister Nanea to calm him down and help him sleep.

Not sure what it was, exactly. But after a day where the Minister’s security team got murdered by a rampaging Agglatar, and his assistant committed suicide with a talking gun, Cliston must have administered some hardcore narcotics.

I slept by the door.

Not because I was afraid of anyone attacking. I was afraid of Nanea sneaking off in the night. I wouldn’t exactly blame him, but I couldn’t let him leave.

The Honorable Nanea Quoffus was the only means I had of removing Wardian Boshe from office, and bringing an end to this pointless war with the Dredel Led. Unfortunately, Nanea didn’t talk, and his translator was dead. As we ate breakfast the next day, these facts weighed heavily on my bandaged head.

The night had been good to me, for the most part. I no longer felt like a wet sack of meat that had been dragged behind a big truck.

I felt like a dry sack of meat that had been dragged behind a smaller truck.

Cliston was kind enough to draw some eyebrows on me since mine had been burnt away fighting the Agglatar.

“So, Nanea,” I began. “Are you ready to go to the Armage and confront Wardian Boshe?”

Nanea shook his head vigorously in the universal sign of “no.”

“That question was kind of rhetorical. You have to confront him.”

“Perhaps a small drink might steady Your Honor’s nerves?” Cliston offered.

“Give him whatever it was you gave him last night to sleep,” I said.

“That’s for sleep, sir.”

“Can I talk to you for a second, Cliston?”

We went into the other room as the Minister sat there looking terrified and confused.

“Cliston, Wardian Boshe is going to find out that his Ontakian assassins failed. We can’t simply wait here in City Hall for the next hit squad.”

“I realize that, sir. But the Minister appears to be in no shape to give testimony. And he has lost his translator.”

“Yeah. That’s bad. But give him something to stay relaxed. I think I can work with this.”



I was flying in the Suckface with Zzzho and the pleasantly buzzed Minister Nanea.

I felt naked. But only figuratively. Cliston had dressed me in my fanciest gown. It was an iridescent violet with green fringes.

But I didn’t have Gun with me. I didn’t have any weapons.

When I tried to bring Gun, Nanea, even in his drugged state, would panic and scream. I guess seeing his translator blow his brains out with Gun didn’t make him especially comfortable around it.

So I was unarmed, headed to a Navy battlecruiser to arrest the top-ranking official in the entire System, using a drugged and mute Parliament Minister, and I was wearing a dress.

It could be worse. I could have cancer. Or literally be on fire.

“Do you smell smoke?” I asked Zzzho nervously.

“I don’t have a nose,” he replied. “But I don’t detect any irregularities in the compartment.”

“Okay. Just checking.”

I had all of Nanea’s official documentation that proved he was a hotshot Member of Parliament. I hoped it was enough, since Nanea couldn’t exactly explain himself.

“You look nice,” Zzzho said to me.

“Thanks,” I said. “This is a new outfit.”

“No, I mean your eyebrows look different. Kind of glittery.”

“Yeah. It’s make-up,” I said.

“I wish I could wear make-up,” Zzzho buzzed.

“You’re a gas cloud,” I said.

“Am I really? Golly, I thought I was a tree this whole time,” Zzzho said, annoyed.

“Hey, sorry.”

“It’s okay. It’s…seeing everyone wear all their different clothes and styles makes me a little jealous,” he said.

“Dude. You eat radiation. You don’t sleep. I saw you get rammed by a spaceship and not get hurt. And you can electrocute peoples’ brains. I wish I was like you,” I said.

“You do?”

“Sure. You have any idea how much time I spend sleeping? Or going to the bathroom?”

“Yeah, but I can’t do anything. Unless there are special receptacles for me to plug in.”

“Doing nothing is underrated! I want to do nothing. But then I run out of money. Or food. Or someone tries to kill me. I’m telling you, you got it good.”

“Huh. Thanks. We’re coming up on the Armage. Ten minutes.”



I was standing in the visitor processing area of the battlecruiser Armage and it was taking longer than usual. Apparently, no one was exactly sure how to handle a visiting Parliamentarian. Half the people were saluting. Half were bowing. And half were busy reading the various instruction manuals.

“And who are you?” one of the soldiers finally got around to asking me.

“I’m the Master Overseer of Parliamentary Translations. I shall be speaking for Nanea Quoffus,” I said with authority.

The soldier scrutinized Nanea’s various paperwork.

“Now be gone, and summon the Wardian. He’s still in charge, right?” I asked.

“He is.”

“Good. Then bring him here immediately.”

“You don’t want to visit him in his office? Instead of here?” the soldier asked, looking around at the open hangar.

“Yes. We will go to his office. That is what we choose to do,” I stated confidently. “Lead us to him.”

I looked over at Nanea, who was aghast, shaking his head. I guess his soothing drugs had worn off on the trip over here.

“Um, the Minister is thanking you all for performing such an excellent job,” I said.

All the soldiers were pleased and congratulating one another.

As we walked through the corridors, Nanea kept grabbing at officers and mouthing words. But they all looked to me for the meaning.

“He said he likes your haircut,” I translated.

“Thank you!”

Nanea took someone’s tele and began typing frantically.

“I’ll read that for you, sir,” I said, wrestling the tele from him.

“Nanea asks that we should have some food. Sausages, beer, and sandwiches. In fact, food and drink for everyone,” I said, deleting the original message which had stated he was my captive and I didn’t have permission to speak for him.

“In here, sir,” one of the soldiers said at a doorway, but Nanea tried to run away from us.

“This is an exercise he likes doing. He calls it, ‘resisting arrest.’ Can you please bring him here? He likes to put up a fight.”

The soldiers did as they were told and grabbed the silently protesting Minister and hauled him into the room.

A soldier stared at me skeptically during all this.

“Don’t be so narrow-minded,” I said to him. “The cultural norms of every species are dramatically different. For example, on my home planet, it is common to wear a dress, bandages, and burn off your eyebrows and then draw them back on.”

The soldier nodded dimly.

“Is your nose broken, sir?” he asked.

I reached up and felt I had blood dripping down my face.

“No. This is a fashion accessory. I find it insulting you would ask me,” I huffed.

“I’m terribly sorry! This is my first time hosting a politician’s staff,” he stammered.

We entered the room. Wardian Boshe stood in front of us looking terribly confused.

“Hank?” he asked.

“Master Overseer of Political Translations,” I corrected.

Minister Nanea was pantomiming desperately.

One of the soldiers stepped forward.

“May I introduce the Chief Minister of the Sub-Subcommittee on Extra-Non-Military Expenditures and Evaluations. The Honorable Nanea Quoffus,” he said.

“And I’m his translator,” I added.

“No, you’re not,” Boshe stated.

The Minister pointed at Boshe and nodded his head vigorously in agreement.

“Nanea says that I am. And that you’re a jerk.”

“Everyone,” Boshe began, “this person is a con man and a liar. He is a thief and is likely collaborating with the Dredel Led.”

Nanea was flailing his arms with frustration.

“The Minister declares that you are under arrest,” I said.

“Pah! On what grounds?” Boshe demanded.

“For aiding…Rettosian scientists. Buying narcotics. And starting a war,” I stated.

“Arrest that man! I order you,” Boshe yelled.

“No. Arrest him. I—Nanea orders you,” I replied.

The soldiers didn’t look especially keen on arresting anyone. This was clearly not in the manual and clearly outside their paygrade.

“Nanea says to take his weapons,” I added.

One of the soldiers got smart.

“Is that true, Minister?” the soldier asked.

Nanea jumped up and down and shook his head in the negative.

“That means yes,” I said.

It was obvious that this was failing fast. My plan hadn’t been very good to start and it was getting worse by the second.

“Hey, give me your gun,” I said to the man nearest me. I was unarmed and felt keenly aware of it.

The soldier recoiled like I had tried to kiss him.

“Present arms!” I yelled.

The soldier snapped to attention and whipped his rifle up. Got to love that military training.

I reached out and took the rifle from him. He tried to resist, but he didn’t weigh thousands of pounds—and I did.

“Okay, Boshe, freeze,” I said.

“See? He is threatening the High Wardian and Marshall of this region,” Boshe said.

“I’m not threatening you. I was just telling you to stand still.”

I squeezed the trigger on the rifle. Or at least I tried to. I got fat fingers. And, apparently, these guys carry around fully automatic rifles.

Blap! Blap! Blap! Blap!

I emptied the magazine. That wasn’t my intention. I just wanted to scare Boshe so he would shut up. But I must have shot him at least four times, the other eleven shots missing completely, as the gun fired out of control.

“Woops,” I said. “I’m not used to a gun that doesn’t talk back.”

“Arrest him! Now!” I heard an unfamiliar voice yell with conviction.

The soldiers drew their weapons and pointed them at me. Two men were on their teles calling for backup.

“Holy crap,” I said, looking at the furious Minister Nanea. “You can talk.”





CHAPTER 67




I was in the brig of a battlecruiser.

Brig.

That translates into “unbelievably tiny prison” in Naval-ese. I don’t think there was enough room in here for me to sneeze.

The frustrating part was that I could push against these bars and pop them right out of the wall.

But then what?

There was something like ten thousand soldiers on this ship. Even if I managed to headbutt my way past them all, what was I going to do next? Open a window and throw rocks at the couple hundred other warships floating around me?

I was a prisoner of the Post-Colmarian Confederation Colmarian Confederation. The funny thing was, they read off my rights when they arrested me. I now had more personal rights and freedoms than I did as a citizen of Belvaille.

I technically got a promotion.

It just didn’t feel like it.

The food in prison was actually pretty amazing. They had a master chef just for me. Well, for the entire brig, but I was the only person locked up.

I wasn’t sure what the penalties were for impersonating a translator, lying on behalf of a Parliament Minister, and assassinating a Wardian, but I had to suspect they were pretty substantial.

I spent my time wondering how they would try and execute me. Firing squad wouldn’t work. Hanging wouldn’t work. They couldn’t get a needle through my skin to administer a lethal injection. Unfortunately, I felt they would keep trying until they got it right.

It was my third night in prison. My drawn-on eyebrows had washed away, but the hair was starting to come back. I was even getting used to my cramped conditions and learned how to sleep while leaning against the wall.

The next day, they suddenly took me out and transferred me to a large room. There were about forty armed guards and six protocol officers shuffling papers.

I guessed they were going to officially charge me. But no. Much to my amazement, Cliston was led into the same room with me!

“Cliston,” I said, shocked.

One of the protocol officers spoke.

“Do you accept this person as your designated barrister?”

I was almost too stunned to answer.

“What?” I asked.

“Do you want me as your lawyer, sir?” Cliston said.

“Sure. Yeah!” I blurted.

“Duly noted,” the protocol officer replied, and they all began stamping forms and writing on documents.

“In that case, my client demands he be transferred to cell 240 on Jano level. He also requires the air be continually filtered with a Maxomatic Scrubber. As his barrister, I also need to share his cell so that I may provide legal consultation.”

The protocol officers huddled up and began to argue.

“I think you’ll find the Penal Systems Handbook version 84.3.2 under the section on Prisoner Well-Being makes all this legal. Failure to grant these requests could put any future prosecution in jeopardy,” Cliston added.

I had no idea what was going on, but at least Cliston was here. He’d get me out of this if anyone could.

“Transfer the prisoners,” the officer said.

“Prisoners?” I asked.

Handcuffs were placed on Cliston and we were taken to a different holding cell.

The new room was much larger and it didn’t have metal bars. Instead, it had a solid steel door.

“What’s going on, Cliston?” I asked.

“We can’t talk yet, sir. We have to wait,” he replied.

I sat there silently for what seemed like hours. Finally, they wheeled into our cell a giant metal contraption, hooked it up, and turned it on.

It was loud!

“The Maxomatic Scrubber,” Cliston explained, once the guards had left.

“How did you know all those legal things about the PCC?”

“I learned it from you, sir.”

“Me? I don’t know any of that stuff. I know some laws about shipping because of the Skim, but not about criminal justice.”

“I mean I made it all up. I lied. I learned that from you.”

“You didn’t know how to lie before you met me?” I asked, wondering just how bad an influence I’d been.

“I did. But I didn’t know how to lie convincingly. I figured that with all the PCC legislation, they couldn’t possibly know if I was quoting legitimate procedures. They would err on the side of caution—provided I didn’t ask for anything too outrageous.”

“So are you my lawyer? Are you going to get the charges dropped?” I asked.

“I am representing you, sir. But you are accused, last I saw, of 2,648 infractions of the PCC Legal Code. It is unlikely those will be reversed,” Cliston said.

“Why are you in handcuffs?”

“Garm arrested me, sir.”

“I knew it! That bitch. Didn’t I say we shouldn’t trust her? What was your crime?”

“Being a Dredel Led, sir.”

“Terrible! She probably figures I’m a lost cause and is trying to suck up to her bosses. That’s just like her. Things get tough and she bails on her friends. Hah! ‘Friends.’ She doesn’t have any friends,” I ranted.

“Actually, sir, this is all part of her plan to break you out of prison.”

“Oh,” I said. “How?”

“Garm has a copy of the blueprints of this vessel. Our current room is in a section that was not formally completed. There is no armor on the outside.”

“So?” I asked.

Cliston removed his handcuffs with ease. He then opened his chest cavity, which I didn’t know he could do, and removed several small items.

“I smuggled these inside. It’s why Garm ‘arrested’ me. We are going to cut our way out with this torch. The air scrubber is to mask the noise.”

“Cliston, you might be able to hold your breath in space, but I can’t.”

Cliston held up two handles and what looked like an attached rubber blanket. I recognized it immediately.

“An emergency pressure suit! I have those on my ship,” I said. “Do you want to hear the safety instructions for them?”

“Another time, perhaps, sir. This suit will protect you once we’re outside and provide you with air.”

“Yeah, but are we going to walk back to Belvaille? It took hours to get here in a spaceship. And those suits only have ten minutes of oxygen,” I said.

“The Suckface is sitting cloaked a short distance from us. Garm is operating the stealth controls. We will cut our way out and you will use your mutation to fly us to your ship,” Cliston explained.

“Should I put it on now?” I asked.

“You need to wait until I’ve finished cutting or you will run out of air,” Cliston said.

“How did you guys learn I was in jail?”

“News spread quickly that you gunned down Wardian Boshe. Then Garm got us together to devise a plan on how you might escape.”

“I was…upset when I spoke about Garm earlier. She’s good people. A good friend,” I said.

“Yes, sir. We will attempt the escape after dinner and lights out,” Cliston said.

Dinner was brought to me and I had to point out a few things.

“See how the chef put sauce in the little cupcake thing? So I can use it to dip and then eat it. Assuming we get out of this, you should do that with your sauces.”

“Yes, sir.”

When I had gone to the bathroom and it was bedtime, we made our move. The first thing we did was spot-weld the lock on the door to slow down anyone in case they heard us.

Next, Cliston began to make cuts in the wall with the little torch. The incisions were shaped like a hexagon with the corners not connected. Then he made a larger hexagon outside of that with the corners connected but not the sides.

“You should put on the spacesuit now, sir,” Cliston said.

“Are we going to get sucked out when you finish?”

“I’m already finished. The torch is depleted. You will have to run into the wall to break it free.”

“I have to kick open the side of a battlecruiser?”

“As I said, this wall should be unarmored.”

“Should be?”

“Delovoa made the calculations. He found that with your mass, and those incisions, the outline should break free with the first hit,” Cliston explained.

“Now I’m trusting my life on Delovoa’s calculations? What kind of plan is this?”

“Sir. Please put on your suit and try,” Cliston said calmly.

Despite having practiced my safety speech on emergency pressure suits about fifty times, I had never actually used one.

I mouthed the instructions to myself as I grasped both handles, held my arms wide, and then pressed the plungers with my thumbs.

There was a tremendous rubber stretching noise and I found my body completely swathed with a tight, synthetic suit and my head capped with an inflated helmet that was snug at the neck. It happened so fast it was quite startling.

“I can barely see. Why isn’t it transparent?” I asked.

“I believe the coating helps block light and radiation, sir.”

The rubber clothes fit…well, they fit perfectly. The only downside was I was still clasping the plastic handles and my fists were fully enclosed. I couldn’t use my fingers at all.

“Do I look stupid in this bubble hat, Cliston?”

“Yes, you do, sir. But I’m afraid that isn’t important right now.”

“Alright. Here goes,” I said.

I took off at a run, hit the wall with my shoulder and:

Bonk!

I fell to the floor. The wall was perfectly intact.

“Ow.”

My bubble helmet swayed back and forth from the collision.

“Try again, sir,” Cliston encouraged, as he helped me back up.

I hit the wall again. Then again. Then again.

Then I tried ramming it with my left shoulder since I think I dislocated my right. But I couldn’t get enough power.

“This isn’t working, Cliston. I can’t bash my way out of a battlecruiser.”

“You’re going to run out of oxygen soon, sir. This is our only plan to get you out.”

“Yeah, but if they put me in a prison on an actual planet, it’d be a lot easier to escape,” I said.

“That doesn’t seem likely, sir. Garm suggested they would poison your food.”

“What? Why did you let me eat dinner?”

“It won’t be for several weeks while they await authorization.”

“The Navy doesn’t seem like the poisoning type.”

“It’s considered a compassionate means of execution since the victim doesn’t know it’s coming,” Cliston explained.

“Now I do. I won’t be able to eat anything. And they have a really good cook.”

“Try the wall again, sir.”

I kicked at it, pushed it, I even yelled at it once.

“It’s no use, Cliston.”

“Use me as a battering ram, sir.”

“Huh?”

Cliston put one arm over his head to protect himself. He then made a loop out of his other arm and raised one leg at a weird angle. Even with my hands bundled up inside the pressure suit, I could hook my arms through and swing Cliston.

“Okay. If you’re sure.”

I lifted Cliston and banged him against the wall.

“Harder, sir.”

Bong!

“Harder!”

I swung him forward and the next thing I knew, I was in outer space! Cliston was quick enough to grab hold of me before we floated apart.

We twirled like leaves in a tornado.

I was completely at a loss what to do until I remembered that I could fly.

I closed my eyes and concentrated. I not only righted myself, but managed to start puttering forward.

Cliston tapped and tugged at me. After a bit of this, he managed to get me going in the right direction. He must have memorized the location of the ship because I didn’t see it. Though I couldn’t see much at all with this colored helmet.

We were travelling forward when I started to get lightheaded.

Oh, crap, I was running out of oxygen!

I could feel the temperature rising as I began breathing more and more of my exhaled air.

I felt I was about to suffocate!

From pure fear, I started fumbling with my helmet, trying to take it off.

Cliston locked my arms behind my back so I couldn’t move. Huge splotches of color began to appear in my vision and I think I was hyperventilating.

This was my prophesy.

The Boranjame had told me exactly how I would die:

Betrayed and murdered by my best friend. Unloved, my corpse deserted in the coldness of space, where memory of me would swiftly become less substantial than the faded light of a long-dead star.

My muscles cramped. I stopped struggling. And I passed into complete and utter blackness.





CHAPTER 68




“Is he dead? Can I have his gun if he dies?” I heard Delovoa say.

I was lying on the floor of the Suckface staring at the ceiling.

Around me were Delovoa and Cliston. Garm was busy at the ship controls.

“What happened?” I asked.

“You’re quite safe now,” Cliston said.

“I wouldn’t say ‘safe.’ We still need to get out of here. If that battlecruiser decides to scan this area, for whatever reason, we’re dead.”

“Setting course for Belvaille,” Zzzho said over the intercom.

“Take it really slow,” Garm replied. “I have to keep scrambling our signature and I can’t do that if you go fast.”

“Garm,” I said, “you’re here.”

“Yeah, I’m here. Did he suffer brain damage or something?”

“I’m here too, Hank. Remember your good buddy, Delovoa?”

Delovoa stuck his rancid face a few inches from mine. His mucous nose dripped on me and his mouthful of metal teeth smiled hideously.

“Ugh, move,” I said, sitting up.

“You’re famous again,” Delovoa said.

“Why?”

“On account that you murdered Wardian Boshe, in cold blood, on his flagship, in front of a hundred armed soldiers.”

“There weren’t a hundred,” I replied.

“Oh,” Delovoa answered, clearly disappointed.

“It was like…two hundred.”

“And now you may be the first person to ever escape from a Navy prison ship.”

“Brig,” I said.

“What?”

“Brig is what they call Navy prison. It’s like a special word.”

“I know what a brig is,” Delovoa said.

“Then why did you ask?”

“Sir, sorry to interrupt, but there is still a major problem. The PCC and Dredel Led remain at war.”

“What else can I do? I killed Wardian Boshe.”

“Kai’Un still exists. You may have eliminated the main reason the Colmarians wanted war, but that doesn’t change the Dredel Led side.”

“Well, point me at Kai’Un and give me a machine gun. I’ll shoot him, too,” I said.

“Seriously, check him for brain damage,” Garm said.

“We know Kai’Un is on Floloria,” Cliston stated.

“That’s what we suspect, but are we really sure?” I asked.

“We’re sure. I researched the invoices and paperwork that Boshe generated. He had a ‘Project Kai’Un’ based on Floloria. It’s been going on for almost five years and consumed over three billion credits worth of Navy funding,” Garm said.

“Wow. No wonder he was being investigated by Parliament. I guess Boshe was planning the war for a long time. But that blows. I risked my life to kill the Wardian and it didn’t accomplish anything because Kai’Un is still around? Did it at least stop the hacking?”

“I stopped it,” Garm said. “After you murdered Boshe, I had some technicians go out to the ‘broken’ telescope and remove the hacking equipment. Simple.”

“What was the hack? Did we ever find out exactly?”

“Sir, I personally feel it was how Thad’s Friend, Zydolium, learned to build Kai’Un. As intelligent as he may be, it is not an easy task to create one of my species. As Garm stated, the Kai’Un project was going on for five years,” Cliston said.

“Okay, but before we mess with a giant Dredel Led, can I get a few days off at least? I still haven’t recovered from fighting the Agglatar. Then I was in prison—”

“The brig,” Delovoa said.

“Then I almost died flying here—hey, when you’re telling people about this story, mention that I flew here.”

Garm cleared her throat loudly.

“No one is going to breathe a word about this escapade.”

“Why not?” I asked.

“Because if it is hinted that I, the Adjunct Overwatch, helped an assassin escape from…The Brig…then my life won’t be worth a drop of piss at a urine factory.”

It was deathly silent in the Suckface for a while.

“People are going to ask how I got away,” I said.

“Tell them it’s none of their damn business. Keep your fat mouth shut for once. I’m serious, Hank. Right now, you’re a wanted felon. The PCC is going to be searching for you and anyone else involved,” Garm said. “I saved your life. This is the tiny repayment I ask.”

“Fine,” I sulked. “But it was a pretty glorious escape if you ask me.”

“Sir. What about Kai’Un?” Cliston said.

“There’s a bunch of clones with high-tech weapons on Floloria. Can’t we use one of these cruisers to blast Kai’Un from space?”

“Not after you murdered Wardian Boshe, no. I’d say we need some kind of explosives,” Garm said.

“Explosives? I know where we can get a tactical warhead,” I replied.



We were in the upstairs apartments of the Society of the Free and Easy back on Belvaille.

“Talk about a gun fetish,” Delovoa said, as we walked past row after row of guns, rockets, grenades, and all manner of weaponry.

“You sure no one will come in on us?” Garm asked, her hand on her pistol.

“They’re all dead. Unless they come back to life, we should be fine,” I said.

I was carrying an armful of sausages I picked up at The Club before we came here.

“There it is,” I said.

The warhead rested in the middle of the room.

“Goodness,” Delovoa said. “A Massive Enhanced Plasma Ordnance.”

“How is it everyone knows about this secret Ontakian weapon?” I asked, annoyed.

“It’s written on the side,” Delovoa said.

“Oh, that’s right.”

“Is this capable of being used against Kai’Un?” Cliston asked.

“It’s capable of destroying all life in an entire city,” Frank said, having entered the room behind us.

Garm drew her pistol. Delovoa practically jumped on top of the warhead in his fright. And I was about ready to throw a sausage.

“Frank, what are you doing here?” I asked. I was still mad at him for leaving me when the Agglatar showed up. He had on his breathing mask as usual, but I could tell it was him from his voice, his build, and the fact he was the only person on Belvaille who wore a breathing mask.

“I’ve been watching this armory to see if any of the Society survived. You’re the first people to visit,” he said.

“Do you know anything about this weapon?” Delovoa inquired.

“It was built during our slave rebellion. I heard it would create an enormous plasma pulse. The idea was we could detonate the bomb right in the middle of our own troops and it wouldn’t hurt us, because we’re immune to plasma weapons, but it would kill the Rettosians.”

“Was it ever used?” Delovoa asked.

“I don’t think it was even tested,” Frank said.

Delovoa examined the warhead with a new appreciation.

“So you’re back,” Frank said to me.

“No thanks to you. That Agglatar almost killed me,” I said.

“I meant from the Armage.”

“Yeah.”

“I heard you fought your way past three hundred guards, spat in Wardian Boshe’s face, then ripped his head off,” Frank said.

“Pretty much.”

“How did you escape from the ship?”

I looked over at Garm, who remained stone-faced and impassive.

“I don’t know. I forget.”

There was a large metal bang and we all turned to see Delovoa had taken the cover off the warhead.

“You’re going to blow us all up!” I yelled.

“Relax. I’m a weapons designer, remember?” Delovoa said.

“What do you think? Could this be used against an extremely large Dredel Led on Floloria?” Cliston asked.

“Give me a moment to check it out,” Delovoa complained.

“Are you simply here to loot this apartment?” Garm asked Frank.

“Yes,” he replied.

“You can’t have the warhead,” I said.

“I don’t want anything to do with it.”

“This is very shoddy construction,” Delovoa said.

“It was made by slave scientists during a slave rebellion. They only had access to materials that they stole,” Frank explained.

“Then it’s extremely advanced construction, considering,” Delovoa amended.

“Will it work?” I asked.

“It’s in bad shape, but most of it’s here,” Delovoa said.

“Can we use the Suckface and drop it on Floloria?”

“It wouldn’t make it through the shields,” Garm said.

“It’s a warhead. Can we put it on top of a missile?” I asked.

“Sure. If you give me a few years and a few million credits to build an intergalactic rocket,” Delovoa said. “Barring that, it would have to be rigged as a bomb.”

“So we need to be standing on a city that gets destroyed?” I asked.

“That’s not what this weapon does. It would emit a low-intensity plasma wave. It would be strong enough to kill most biological lifeforms—except Ontakians. But the buildings should be fine.”

“What about Dredel Led?” Cliston asked.

“A byproduct would be a strong electromagnetic field that disrupts all electronics. It may wipe your memory clean,” Delovoa said.

“I wasn’t speaking of me. I was referring to Kai’Un. He has a million minds.”

Delovoa lifted some pieces out of the warhead.

“Here’s a big problem. The plasma core has deteriorated,” Delovoa said.

“What’s that mean?” I asked.

“I’d say this weapon originally had the blast radius of a small city. But now, if it works, it might only kill everything within a dozen city blocks,” he said.

“Only. That’s way more than enough,” I said.

“Actually, sir, Kai’Un may easily be that size,” Cliston replied.

“So we’re going to have to land a spaceship on Floloria. Look around for where Kai’Un is hidden. Then drag this bomb over to him?”

“Floloria is deserted. I doubt they ‘hid’ Kai’Un. Especially if it’s as large as Cliston says,” Garm replied.

“We also have to make sure they don’t disarm the bomb,” Delovoa added.

“How would anyone know to disarm it?” I asked.

“Because on that city is a scientist and his clones. He’s mastered teleportation, mutation, cloning, and self-aware handguns. He’s probably not going to see a plasma warhead and think it’s a big, harmless piece of candy.”

“There has to be an easier way to destroy a million-mind computer,” I said.

“I’ll help you with this Floloria mission,” Frank said.

I believe everyone was shocked. Even the people who didn’t know my uncle very well.

“I…don’t have any money to pay you. I gave it all to the Agglatar,” I said.

“No problem. How long will it take you to make this bomb operational?” Frank asked Delovoa.

“I need at least 24 hours,” he replied.

“Alright. Call me up when you’re ready to go,” Frank said.

“Wait. Wait,” I stammered. “Let me see if I understand. You’re volunteering to help drag this plasma bomb over to Floloria. Where we might be attacked by Rettosian scientist clones.”

“Yeah. Sounds about right,” Frank said.

“Do you even know why we want to do this?”

“No,” he shrugged.

Was this really my uncle?

“Delovoa. The warhead. It doesn’t explode into gold or jewels, does it?”

“No. Just a lot of radiation,” he said.

“Frank, do you know of anything valuable on Floloria? Like Kai’Un is made out of…mountains of credits?”

“I don’t even know what Kai’Un is,” he replied.

“It’s a false Dredel Led that must be destroyed,” Cliston stated.

“Right. We’ll get to that. But Frank, can I ask you why you want to help?”

He sighed deeply.

“I kind of figure I owe you after last time. Besides, you’re my nephew. You’re probably the only relation I have left.”

“Probably,” I said. “So you don’t expect to make any money out of this?”

“Oh, I’m going to sell every weapon in this armory—except the warhead. I figure, since you murdered Wardian Boshe, the PCC will declare martial law. Hardware like this is going to be at a real premium.”

“Okay! That makes sense,” I said.





CHAPTER 69




We were back at City Hall thinking of how to proceed.

The idea of wandering around the city of Floloria, dragging a plasma bomb, searching for Kai’Un, was not very appealing.

Then I, of all people, had a technological breakthrough. Or at least an idea.

In the past, Delovoa and I had detected signals coming from Floloria. They weren’t the same ones as the hack, but we investigated anyway. When we did, we were attacked by the Rettosian clones. I was pushed through buildings and Delovoa got his legs broken.

That signal we detected had to be something, right? It wasn’t just clones listening to the radio.

If the hack was how Thad’s Friend got the information to create Kai’Un, then how did Kai’Un actually connect to Thaxmultaerea? Kai’Un was no good sitting on Floloria by himself. He had to visit the Dredel Led capital to actually vote and influence decisions.

Maybe that signal we detected the first time was Kai’Un connecting to Thaxmultaerea. Like what Cliston did when he took a vacation to his home city. Or what I did when I put on the Dredel Led helmet.

“It makes sense,” Delovoa said. “The signal was powerful. It takes very little bandwidth for Cliston to connect and very, very little bandwidth for a brain like Hank’s to connect. But a being like Kai’Un would need a tremendous signal to patch into Thaxmultaerea.”

“I have some slight familiarity with the specifications of Thaxmultaerea,” Cliston said. “I might be able to assist you in creating a scanner that could find the signal again.”

“And that should lead us to Kai’Un, right?” I asked.

“It seems reasonable,” Delovoa confirmed.

Delovoa was rigging a plasma warhead to be a bomb and modifying a scanner to pinpoint the location of Kai’Un’s signal.

I spent the time eating and sleeping, mostly.

I figured it was the best I could do to prepare. My legs still hurt from fighting the Agglatar. My shoulder hurt from ramming my way out of a battlecruiser. I probably suffered carbon dioxide poisoning swimming through space. And my eyebrows still weren’t full grown. They were too thick to draw on, and sparse enough that it looked like I had mange.

I also did some exercises with Gun.

I had Cliston stand with a rifle or pistol facing me, and I would try and get Gun to identify what part was Cliston and what part was the weapon he was carrying.

“I’m not especially comfortable with this, sir,” Cliston stated, while staring down at my chromium, talking handgun.

“He won’t shoot you. Will you, Gun?”

“I can’t predict the future. I don’t think,” he said.

I was concerned about the first time Delovoa and I had met the clones. They used repulsion rifles on us that flung me backwards and flung him upwards. Probably because of our differences in mass.

The weapons themselves hadn’t seemed to cause any damage, but getting pushed through a building or flung up into the latticework would be bad.

I was trying to train Gun to destroy the repulsion rifles before they could be used.



The next day we got Zzzho to fly Frank, Garm, Delovoa, Cliston, and me to Floloria.

As we walked down the street, Frank took point. He wore full body armor and carried an assortment of frightening weapons.

Then came Delovoa, monitoring the scanner that was tracking Kai’Un’s signal.

Garm held the left side, also wearing body armor and carrying two heavy pistols and probably a bunch of smaller blades.

Cliston kept an eye on the right. He was unarmed, but Cliston was extremely fast, extremely strong, and, knowing him, he probably wrote several books on hand-to-hand combat just for fun.

I was in the rear.

Pushing the bomb.

The bomb was typical Delovoa handiwork. The metal cover had been removed from the warhead and in its place, there was countless strips of tape, string, dodgy-looking mechanical devices, and I think some bent coat hangers.

“Couldn’t you at least find a cart that had four working wheels?” I asked.

I was pushing the bomb on a large dolly and two of the wheels stuttered and squeaked. Every time I hit a pebble or joint in the pavement, the whole thing would jerk to a halt and a plasma warhead almost fell to the ground.

“It’s the only one I had,” Delovoa replied. “Now be quiet.”

“How can I be quiet? Anyone can hear this cart from three blocks away,” I said. “Not to mention your stupid scanner.”

Delovoa had designed the scanner so that it blorped louder and more rapidly as we neared Kai’Un. So we had a perfect reminder of how close we were to danger.

It was also not exactly precise. We had to go down entire streets, wait for the sound to get louder or dimmer and then turn around and go another direction.

Blorp! Blorp!

“I swear, can you at least throw a towel on that thing? My nerves are frayed enough,” I said.

Garm shushed me.

“Oh, right. So now I’m the bad guy because I want a little peace and quiet as I push along ten megatons of joy over every bump and crack in this damn city.”

“Sir, your voice does carry quite a distance,” Cliston echoed.

“I’m sorry. Am I speaking too loud? It’s hard to hear over my imminent heart attack.”

Blorp! Blorp! Blorp! Blorp!

I was about to say something again, when Frank held up his arms and crossed his hands at the wrists. I wasn’t sure what that meant, but I supposed he wasn’t cheerleading for an invisible glocken team.

One by one, we crept forward to the corner of the building ahead and peeked around it.

The space station had been completely leveled at this point forward. There were no buildings whatsoever. In their place, there seemed to be an ocean of humming electronics. Every kind of capacitor, resistor, processor, adapter, module, circuit, battery, fuse, inverter, and an encyclopedia of things I had never seen. All of it connected by endless cords and cables and punctuated with millions of flashing, multi-colored lights.

“Do you think that’s Kai’Un, Cliston?” Garm asked.

“You bet your ass, ma’am,” he replied. Which was such an uncharacteristic statement it had to be true.

“I don’t see anyone, do you?” I asked.

“No, but I smell a lot of people,” Delovoa said. “Something’s around here.”

We moved as stealthily as a robot, two Ontakians, a mutant, and a Colmarian with backwards legs could move while pushing a squeaky trailer.

Which was not very stealthily.

“Where should we put this?” I asked Delovoa.

Delovoa looked at some of the electrical equipment with uncertainty. Compared to his jury-rigged bomb, a city-wide supercomputer was clearly beyond him.

“What are those raised areas over there?” Cliston asked.

Most of Kai’Un was relatively flat, the machinery at ground level. But in the distance were some taller portions maybe eight feet in height. Going on the assumption that bigger meant more important, we slowly made our way over.

“I know what those are,” I began.

“They’re cloning tubes!” Delovoa finished.

They were. Hundreds of cloning pods like we had seen in the past. They looked exactly like we had seen in the past because they were made by the same Rettosian scientist, Zydolium.

When we approached, we saw there were a handful of Rettosians standing among the pods. Only two had weapons, but I think they were the same repulsion rifles that had sent me spiraling across half the city.

“Hello,” Frank said to the clones.

It was clear they were clones, just of different ages. Their liquid Rettosian skin boiled and bubbled and streaked across their exposed faces, necks, and hands.

They began speaking to each other in Rettosian but took no other actions.

I put my hand on Gun.

“Gun. I might need you to shoot some weapons. Are you ready?” I asked.

“Will Cliston be holding them?” Gun replied.

“I told you. Sir,” Cliston chastised.

“So what do we do?” Garm asked. She was also tense, her hands by her pistols.

“We do what we’re here to do,” Frank said. “I say we prepare the You-Know-What and get out of here.”

“Is this a good spot, Cliston?” I asked.

“We aren’t at the exact center of Kai’Un, but if the electromagnetic field is as large as Delovoa believes, it should be sufficient,” he said.

“Alrighty, then. Delovoa?” I said. I stepped away from the plasma warhead and moved next to Frank.

Delovoa put down his scanner and began fiddling with the bomb.

“Do you think they can understand us?” I asked Frank.

“They can,” I heard a wheezy, amplified voice say.

Zydolium puttered along in his electronic wheelchair, passing between cloning pods.

“I see Delovoa has returned. Such determination,” Zydolium chuckled.

“I guess killing you once wasn’t enough,” Delovoa taunted.

“Silly man. You couldn’t possibly kill me. But I assume your feeble intellect can’t quite fathom that concept.”

“You’re the silly one!” Delovoa shouted. “This whole thing is silly. Big deal, you made a Dredel Led. It’s silly! I detected it with no problem.”

“Yes. No doubt you had the mighty Cliston’s help in that regard. I’ve read quite a lot about you, my friend. Your pamphlet on the interrelationship between galactic species I found extremely interesting—if incorrect,” Zydolium said.

“I wrote that many centuries ago,” Cliston replied humbly.

“And the…Adjunct Overwatch, as I believe you are called. Amusing that a member of the Navy power structure is here. I worked so well with your kind before.”

“Wardian Boshe is dead,” I said.

“Yes. Unfortunate. He was a vital resource. None of this would have been possible without him,” Zydolium said. “Is that—let me see if I can remember—Frank?”

Frank sniffed, but didn’t reply.

“I can’t say I’m surprised to see you here. I never fully trusted you Ontakians. A limited, but extremely resourceful species.”

We all stood there silently, facing off.

“Ehem. Do you know me?” I asked.

“No.”

“I’m Hank. I, uh, helped Frank. And Delovoa. And Cliston. I was there when we killed you last time.”

“Yes? What do you want?” he asked, mildly annoyed.

“Nothing. Just. Saying hi,” I said, feeling rather foolish.

“You look familiar,” he conceded. “I assume…you all want…to hear about…my plans?” he stated very slowly.

“Sure,” Garm said. She nodded to Delovoa so that he would continue work on the bomb.

“It seems…Mr. Boshe…learned of me…through the Historians. They compile…so very much…information.”

I whispered to Frank.

“Is he talking slow or is that warhead leaking radiation?” I asked.

Frank ignored me, interested in Zydolium’s story.

“Mr. Boshe felt…that his species…and new empire…The Post-Colmarian Confederation Colmarian Confederation…needed war…to keep itself intact. Without an external…enemy…it would grind itself…down…with bloat…and wastefulness. He was impressed…with my work…mutating the Belvaille…System. And asked…if I could help…start a war.”

“So you created Kai’Un,” I said, trying to speed this along.

Zydolium shrugged.

“We agreed…on the Dredel Led. An ancient enemy…of the Colmarians. But I needed…information… I tapped into…Thaxmultaerea…and monitored…their communications. I even…accessed…creation schematics. Protocols. Voting. Their whole…society.”

“That is sacred data!” Cliston blurted.

Zydolium smiled with his crusty lips.

“The Dredel Led…are fascinating. I do not believe…I uncovered…even one percent…of your species. However, I had…enough to…begin.”

He moved closer in his electronic chair and clearly saw Delovoa, who was frantically trying to set the timer on the bomb.

“With funding…from…Boshe…and the PCC…I hired…my Ontakians…to procure…materials. So that I might…construct…Kai’Un. It is not…truly…a Dredel Led. It is not…self-aware…at all. There were…errors. So many…corrections…I had to make. It was one…of the most…difficult tasks…I have ever…undertaken.”

I kicked at the electronics all along the road.

“So this is Kai’Un?” I asked.

Zydolium sat quietly for some time.

“Yes,” he said finally. “I told…Boshe…that Kai’Un…would eventually be…found out. It is not…a very convincing…Dredel Led…I admit. But war…has come…once again. And war…has a will…of its own.”

“Why are you talking so slow? Did you just have a stroke or something?” I asked.

“I am…stalling…for time. You moron.”





CHAPTER 70




There was a tremendous crunching noise and Frank called out.

“Behind us!”

I turned around to see a giant running toward our group.

It was a clone of Zydolium, liquid skin and all, but he must have been twenty-five feet tall.

We couldn’t fight that thing in addition to those repulsion rifles. I had to get rid of one or the other. I stepped forward, so that I was safely past Frank and Garm, and I pulled out Gun.

“Gun, shoot and destroy all their weapons,” I said.

“Okay. Bang! Bang!”

The clones carrying the weapons were suddenly carrying fragments of weapons. Their repulsion rifles had been sliced into pieces.

Zydolium looked startled and he began to speak. On one of our previous encounters, another clone of Zydolium had turned Gun against me. I wasn’t going to let that happen again.

“Gun, shoot to kill that old man in the wheelchair,” I said, pointing.

“Bang!”

Zydolium slumped in his chair and his liquid skin slowed and stopped.

“Yeah, maybe now you’ll remember me,” I shouted defiantly.

To his corpse.

Those handful of shots had really drained me of energy. However, I didn’t have long to contemplate my tiredness because I was suddenly airborne.

I felt the distinct presence of that old familiar friend of mine: unbearable pain. The giant had stormed up and punted me like a ball.

I touched down a moment later and went tumbling through a mass of electronics and cloning tubes, smashing it all into hi-tech confetti.

I came to rest face down, my nose pressed against some flashing and flickering circuit boards. It was at this time I decided I would take a quick breather. A little break.

I deserved it.

“Hank! Hank! Get the hell up,” I heard Garm yell from seemingly inside my brain.

“Just resting my eyes,” I slurred.

I got up, brushed off the broken electrical components, and took a look around.

The battle had certainly begun.

There were now dozens of Rettosian clones running around. Most were armed with wrenches, hammers, and more complicated tools, but a few carried firearms.

Garm was fighting them all. Despite their vastly superior numbers, and the fact some were in the prime of their lives, the Rettosian clones were still clones.

Clones of a scientist.

It was clear they had spent a lot of time studying mathematics and physics and not a lot of time studying martial arts or working out at the gym.

Garm flipped over one clone, kicked a second one in the face, and shot a third. All while she was in the air. They swung their weapons and fired their guns, but didn’t come close to hurting her.

Frank had it a bit harder, as he was squared off against the giant. But he unloaded a submachinegun into the ankle of the creature, causing it to squeal and stumble.

Delovoa was still by the bomb and Cliston was doing his best to protect the both of them. I could see Cliston didn’t know much about fighting, but he was fast and strong and made of metal. A clone came by, swung a baton, and it clanged harmlessly off Cliston’s skull. Cliston then reached out, grabbed the man by the arm, and twisted. The result was more than enough to discourage any further attempts for the time being.

Unfortunately, I didn’t think I could use Gun in this situation. Things were too chaotic and crowded and I didn’t know how I could describe my targets to Gun without jeopardizing my friends.

Frank seemed like he needed the most help so I hurried over to him, tromping through the electrical systems.

Frank sensed my approach—or heard me cursing as I tripped over Kai’Un.

“Attack his right ankle,” Frank yelled. “Let’s see if we can get this bastard limping!”

I didn’t have a weapon I could use, so I pulled out one of Frank’s belt pistols. I fired seven shots, hitting the enormous target only three times.

“Taxes and Tariffs,” Frank swore. “Don’t you know your right from your left?”

Frank fired at the giant’s other ankle.

“I thought you meant my right,” I said. Actually, I had just shot at the ankle closest to me and even then, I hadn’t been very accurate.

The giant reached down with his open palm and took a slap at us. Frank easily dodged to the side, but I stood there flat-footed and got swatted.

I only flew backward about five feet, but the impact was enough to daze me.

When I tried to stand up, the giant stepped on me. Then stepped on me again.

When he lifted for a third stomp, I actually stuck to the bottom of his foot.

Frank shot at him some more and managed to push the monster back.

As I dragged myself up, I realized this giant was the worst possible thing I could fight. I was big and strong and really, really slow. But I wasn’t big or strong compared to that giant. I was just an easy target because I couldn’t get out of the way.

I looked around for something to use as a weapon. Like, a lance. Something to keep it at a distance.

As I was gazing around, I saw the clones fall back and, amazingly, form themselves into a competent battle formation.

They had a leader.

He was a tall man with a brutish face. His arms were bare and he was incredibly muscular. He clearly wasn’t a Rettosian, because he didn’t have their colorful, liquid skin. He looked competent, aggressive, and extremely angry.

He barked out orders in Rettosian and gestured with his thick arms where everyone should go.

“That’s you!” Frank yelled.

At first I thought Frank was having an existential crisis of some kind. But then I saw what he meant:

It was me!

It was the me of like five centuries ago. A young Hank!

The Rettosians had bioengineered my species, the Ontakians. Zydolium had hinted that he personally had a part in that process.

Was that really me? Or some version of me? I always heard I was a “combat model” Ontakian. But was I really some template they stamped out on an assembly line? One of many?

It was eerie seeing myself. Man, I’ve gained a lot of weight since then.

And I used to have more hair.

My doppelganger glanced in my direction, but if he recognized his future self, he made no obvious reaction.

“Hank, I need help over here,” Garm said.

She stood her ground and was facing the clones alone. They were no longer a mishmash of disorganized geeks. They were a structured army.

“Coming,” I called to her.

I hurried over as best I could, bumbling through Kai’Un’s electronics.

“Walk on the street!” Garm yelled.

Oh, yeah.

I jumped over to the narrow road and zigzagged over to Garm.

“I’ll take the clones, you fight that guy,” I said to Garm.

“I’m not fighting your clone! You fight him. I’ll hold off the clones,” she replied.

“Alright. Follow my lead,” I said. “Yaaar!” I screamed, charging forward.

I stopped and looked back, seeing Garm remaining where she was.

“What?” she asked. “That’s not how I fight. Go do your thing.”

I didn’t have time to get annoyed as some of the clones began shooting me. They weren’t especially accurate, and the guns weren’t powerful. I got hit in the arm and leg. It was nothing compared to being stepped on by a giant.

The clones also swung at me with their various construction tools, but they did no damage. I think in some cases, I hurt them more than they hurt me, as the weapons rattled out of their hands.

But I was now confronted with…myself.

“Zeetso Bledono,” he said in Rettosian.

“Hi,” I replied, upbeat. “I know this may seem crazy, but I’m you. We’re Ontakians.”

I smiled.

Hank 2 then did the weirdest thing. He flipped forward, did a cartwheel, landed in a crouch, and grabbed me by the groin with one hand, and reached up and grabbed me by the neck with the other hand.

“Uh. What are you doing?” I asked.
