He roared! Like a real, honest roar. Very different from the scream I made in my aborted charge over here. It sounded manly and powerful.

He then lifted me over his head.

I was like, I don’t know, two thousand pounds. Okay, more than that. A lot more. And he lifted me up and threw me.

It wasn’t a very big throw. It was more like a forward drop. But no one could deny the fact he had done a handstand flip and lifted me from the ground.

I wasn’t hurt. I was shocked.

From my position on the ground, I turned back to Garm.

“Did you see that?” I asked her. But she was busy doing her Quadrad assassin routine, dancing around kicking clone necks and breaking kneecaps.

I started to get up when Hank 2 hit me with a full-on haymaker punch. My head bounced off the road and left a sweat stain that perfectly outlined my features.

Man, this guy made me feel old.

I tried to rise again and he somehow did a running kick with both legs that sent me flipping over backward.

The blows I was taking hurt, but weren’t likely to cripple me anytime soon. The problem was, I hadn’t hit him once. I hadn’t even swung at him.

“Hey, give me a second to get to my feet,” I said.

But apparently, Hank 2 didn’t speak Colmarian, or didn’t understand sportsmanship.

He kicked my arm out from under me and then slammed his foot on the back of my neck.

I tried to cover myself with my arms but he reached in and jammed his thumb into my right eye, pressing and twisting.

“Ahh!” I screamed.

I flailed out with my arm to try and knock him away. He grabbed hold of my wrist. Then he grabbed me by my ankle.

Then he turned, turned, and I was flying. He was swinging me around like I was a little kid playing airplane.

Except he let me go.

Very much like an airplane, I flew across the landscape a short distance. I made an emergency landing somewhat near Delovoa and Cliston.

“Quit screwing around, Hank!” Garm yelled at me.

I took this moment to stand up and reevaluate things.

Garm looked like she had incapacitated or killed a half-dozen clones. But what seemed like a dozen had taken their place. We were surrounded by cloning tubes. Hundreds. If there were that many clones on this city, it was just a matter of time before they all converged here. Not even Garm could fight off that many angry scientists.

Frank was, surprisingly, doing okay against the giant. He would aim and take a measured shot at some vital part of his opponent. And while it was clearly painful, the giant wasn’t remotely in any danger of dying.

Frank was conserving his ammunition. Eventually, he was going to run out of guns and bullets and then he would be easily overmatched by the creature.

After tossing me around for a while, Hank 2 didn’t even look like he was winded. He was back to giving orders to the group of clones.

“So, uh, Delovoa. Do you think you can get this bomb working?” I asked.

“Now? It will kill us all!”

“You said the warhead doesn’t affect Ontakians,” I replied.

“Maybe. But if you hadn’t noticed, most of us here aren’t Ontakians. It would kill Cliston, too, in case you don’t care about me.”

“Set the bomb and we’ll all run away,” I said.

“The Rettosians might be able to disarm it, sir,” Cliston answered. I notice he had a few dents and scratch marks on his body case.

I wasn’t sure what to do. It seemed clear that we couldn’t win this. There were too many of them and they were too powerful.

Frank must have heard our conversation as he gave us some advice.

“Set the bomb. Hank and I will guard it. We’ll meet back at the ship if we survive.”

I could hardly believe he said it. A last stand. Was I ready to do that?

“Yeah. Okay. It’s for a worthy cause, right? Erasing Kai’Un. Ending the war. Killing Zydolium and his damn clones. And it won’t affect us.”

“Maybe, I said. You’ll be at ground zero,” Delovoa explained, and his sociopathic voice was filled with emotion. Which really let me know this was not something to be undertaken lightly.

But I noticed more clones exiting their tubes. They formed a thick mob and were forcing Garm to back up. The giant Rettosian punched at Frank and caught him on the shoulder, spinning him around like dice at a casino.

It was clear to me that we were going to lose this battle.

I turned to Delovoa and said with all seriousness:

“Sometimes the bad thing is the best thing.”

“What the hell does that mean?” he answered.

“Just set the bomb and get out of here. Don’t make it like four hours or anything, we won’t be able to hold off that long.”

“Two hours?” he asked.

I motioned around with my arms, in case Delovoa was blind.

“Make it like three minutes,” I said.

“I can’t run that fast. My legs are backwards,” he reminded me.

I went to the warhead, wrapped my arms around it, and set it on the ground. The squeaky cart was now empty.

“Cliston—ow, my back—Cliston, push Delovoa on the dolly,” I said.

“Yes, sir.”

“Did you hear any of that, Garm?” I called out.

Garm was out of bullets, she hadn’t brought as many guns as Frank. She did a feint rush forward, threw a handful of blades, and dropped some clones to the ground.

“Just give the signal,” she said anxiously.

“Hank, the clones can understand us,” Delovoa whispered. “They must know this is a bomb. They’re going to make for it at all costs.”

“I got it,” I said.

I pulled up my pants, sniffled a bit, damn, my nose was bleeding again. What was my problem?

I slowly walked back toward Hank 2 and the mass of clones. I took out Gun and held it ready at my side.

“Okay, go!” Delovoa yelled, and he hopped onto the dolly.

Cliston began pushing him at around fifteen miles an hour. The squeaking was so obnoxious it gave me goosebumps.

Garm withdrew, and ran to catch up to Delovoa and Cliston.

“You got five minutes,” Delovoa yelled.

Five minutes? I told him three.

There must have been about fifty clones now and they all smiled and began talking in their pompous Rettosian language as they slowly moved toward me.

“Yeah. See, Garm isn’t in the way anymore,” I explained. “Gun. Shoot, um, ten of those people. To kill.”

“Okay. Bang! Bang! Bang! Bang! Bang!”

Ten Rettosian scientists slumped to the ground. That wiped the smile clean off the rest of their faces.

The drain of my bioelectricity made me so weak that I went down on one knee and almost lost consciousness.

Okay. I’m not doing that again! I’ll save Gun as a last resort.

Hank 2 gave some commands and the Rettosian clones opened fire on me. I covered my face, but those guys had worse aim than I did.

Still, a number of bullets hit. Of course, I was bulletproof and the shots did no harm.

Hank 2 saw that and seemed confused.

“We’re the same guy,” I yelled to him. I pointed at him then back to me.

Hank 2 ran forward, and it was clear he wasn’t going to embrace me with brotherly love.

He was attempting a diving tackle. But he telegraphed the move ahead of time, so I raised my arms and turned my hips, and he merely bounced off of me.

You know, if I beat Hank 2, that will prove that I’m getting better with age. How often do you get a chance like this? To match skills against a younger, dumber version of yourself. I’ve accumulated centuries of experiences that this boy can’t even dream of.

If I win, it will show that maturity and wisdom triumph over strength and youth.

The clones rushed in to follow Hank 2, but they weren’t combat model Ontakians.

The first clone stuck a rifle in my face like he was going to pick my nose with it. I grabbed the barrel and pulled the weapon out of his hands.

“Now I got a gun,” I said to no one in particular.

I fired as fast as I could pull the trigger and hit several clones. I was literally surrounded by attackers and I somehow missed with a dozen shots. There simply had to be something wrong with me. No one could be this inaccurate.

Maybe my head was misaligned with my arms so I was always shooting in the wrong location. I’ve never been good with guns. Or darts. Or billiards. Or tossing dirty socks into a hamper.

“Hey, Frank, do I seem kind of cross-eyed to you?” I yelled at him.

“What?” he asked, right as the giant brought his fist down in an overhead slam and battered my uncle into the ground.

Hank 2 gave some new instructions to the clones and they rushed past me and headed straight for the bomb. They were going to try and disarm it.

I hurried over, caught two clones by the backs of their heads and bashed their brains together until they went limp.

Suddenly, a hand darted out and grabbed me around the throat.

“Hank 2,” I gurgled.

I tried to punch him several times, but I was too slow to get any momentum behind it, and my feeble blows did nothing.

No, don’t try and fight his game. Use my brain. He had me by the throat, but I had him by the hand.

I wasn’t just a combat model Ontakian. I was a mutant combat model Ontakian.

I took hold of his wrist with both hands and started walking backwards. My idea was to drag him around by his arm. He didn’t let go and kept choking me.

As my face turned blue, I realized this was a rather stupid idea. Hank 2 walked faster than me and could hop over and around all the things I was tripping on.

I pulled him close and then I pushed him as hard as I could. That at least got me free of him, as he fell over backwards.

The clones were huddled around the bomb again.

I kicked and swung and shooed them away. Was it five minutes yet? It felt like five hours. I had to hope that Delovoa, of all people, had set the bomb right. What if he had screwed up? What if it was fifty minutes?

One of the clones shot me with a pistol, but I broke his arm and took the gun from him.

Hank 2 jumped up and was about to slug me again when I shot him twice in the stomach and three times in the empty space around him.

If I lived, I was definitely going to take a marksmanship class.

Hank 2 winced at the shots, but was undeterred. I quickly held up my hand for him to stop.

Amazingly, he did.

I took the pistol and shot myself in the stomach.

“See? We’re the same. We’re both Ontakians. We don’t have to fight,” I explained.

Hank 2 actually snorted with laughter and then punched me in the jaw.

The blow knocked two of my teeth loose. I instinctively covered my mouth so he wouldn’t knock them clean out. I could regrow teeth, but it took a long while, and it made eating difficult in the meantime.

Right.

You know, if Hank 2 and I are evenly matched, that will show there are pros and cons to getting older. Sure, I have more intelligence and skill, but there’s no denying the stamina and enthusiasm of youth.

Hank 2 had learned that I was a lot heavier than him, so he wasn’t going to wrestle with me.

He planted a roundhouse kick to my knee, rocketed me with an uppercut under my chin, gave me a wicked kidney shot, and then kneed me in the groin.

I’m not sure, but I think all this happened in about one second. My body didn’t even have time to register pain from one hit before I was getting hit again.

I felt this was not the path to success.

“Frank,” I said, my mouth swollen, “we got to do something else.”

Frank went hurtling through the air and landed not far from me. He looked bad. Worse than I felt. His body armor was torn away like it had been made out of soft cheese. His face was pouring blood like a punch bowl that had cracked in a thousand places. When he tried to stand up, his legs were as weak as uncooked sausages.

“Man, I’m hungry,” I said. “All my analogies are food.”

Hank 2 was not sympathetic, and kicked me over and over in the face and torso as I was bent over on the ground, spitting blood.

“Stupid kid probably going to break his foot,” I laughed, as my two loose teeth fell out.

Frank was punch drunk and dazed. He shook his head and blinked his eyes rapidly, trying to regain focus.

“The bomb,” he said.

Those damn clones were back, and they had their tools out this time.

With great effort, I climbed to my feet and Hank 2 punched the back of my head. The blow sent me wobbling to the bomb and I collapsed on the clone nearest the controls. His bones crunched beneath my weight.

“One down,” I said.

I grabbed hold of the bomb and lifted it up, using my back instead of my knees, because my knees were shot.

“What are you doing?” Frank asked.

“I don’t know.”

“Run,” he said.

Yeah. They can’t disarm it if I’m holding it.

I made like a glocken puffer, put my head down, and took off. I wasn’t going very fast because I was running over clones, kicking through Kai’Un, and the warhead was heavy.

As I teetered dangerously with an armful of Massively Enhanced Plasma Ordnance, I felt things might be okay. I just had to outrun the timer.

Frank took up the role as a glocken wedge and chopped, hacked, and otherwise maimed the clones chasing after me.

I even saw him get a great shot at Hank 2 and knock him down from behind.

We should have done this from the start: run around in circles carrying a city-killing plasma bomb.

But the good times didn’t last very long.

It was Hank 2. I swear, that guy just didn’t give up!

He swept out my legs and caused me to fall, dropping the warhead.

When Frank tried to intervene, Hank 2 did a one-two-three combination punch and before he hit the ground, I saw Frank was unconscious. I think the shockwave from Hank 2’s fists were enough to break some windows a few blocks over.

I covered the bomb with my body.

Hank 2 was not impressed with my efforts. He clutched and twisted my head until I finally let go. He then dragged me away by my feet, kicked me twice in the face, and then walked back to the bomb.

Okay. So, I lost to my younger self.

Of course someone a tenth my age was going to kick my ass. It’s not like we’re debating. He was physically ramming his fists into my nose. That’s not something you get better at as you get older.

Hank 2 waved for the scientist clones to come over and work on the bomb.

I stood, fell down, then stood up again.

“Hey,” I yelled boldly. “Do you want to fight or—” and that was it. I had nothing clever left to say. It had been beaten out of me. It was all I could do not to swallow my tongue.

Hank 2’s brow furrowed as he looked at me.

I wanted to see some sign that he was hurt. Or tired. Or had a sore throat. Anything.

But he walked past the Rettosian clones and stepped forward with the clear intention of making my life a lot shorter.

Killed by my youth in old age. I guess that was something.

However.

You would think that being thirty feet away from a detonating Massive Enhanced Plasma Ordnance bomb would be a really bad thing.

But it was just light.

A lot of light!

Pure white. Like the universe had been erased and nothing was left but a blank piece of paper.

There wasn’t even a sound.

No smells. No burnt flesh. No smoldering electronics.

Just a flash and then I was blind.

I stood there waiting.

For what, I’m not sure. An explosion? Death?

After a while of absolutely nothing dramatic happening, my stomach grumbled.

I rubbed at my eyes to try and restore my vision.

Shadows begin to slowly appear in the whiteness.

A form here and there.

They were all dead. The clones. The giant. Kai’Un.

Actually, I didn’t know if Kai’Un was “dead.” It didn’t do anything like groan or gurgle up some vacuum tubes. It still looked like a massive electronic computer that stretched away for blocks and blocks. But all its glowing, bleeping, colored lights were now dark.

“Frank. Frank,” I said.

Frank was curled in an unnatural position, but I could see his chest rising and falling.

As for Hank 2, I guess in the end, age and wisdom triumphed. And teamwork. Teamwork was a key—

Wham!

Hank 2 punched me full-on in the gut, doubling me over.

“Come on!” I gasped.

He lifted my head to get a better angle and then smashed me across the cheek.

“Everyone is dead! Your computer is broken. There’s no reason to fight me!” I implored. “I won. Your boss is gone.”

Punch. Kick. Throw.

This was like the…dumbassedness of youth. Had I been that stubborn when I was his age?

Now that everything was done and finished, I really, truly didn’t want to die. It would be pointless now.

I still had Gun. I pulled it out and pointed.

“Gun! Shoot that guy to kill. Give it everything you got!”

Nothing.

No hello. No bang. No questions.

The electromagnetic pulse! It may have fried Gun.

I knew what I had to do. Sort of. I turned and ran. Fight or flight instinct had kicked in and I was practically flapping my arms to try and get away.

I couldn’t beat my younger self in a fight. He had proven that beyond a doubt in those soul-crushing five minutes.

Of course, he caught up to me. Tackled me.

I pushed him off, got up, and started running again.

Hank 2 could beat the everloving crap out of me if I stood still and fought. Like when I was protecting the bomb.

But I was a moving target now.

Admittedly, I wasn’t moving very fast. Still, it was hard to run after someone and punch them with much force.

He tried, however.

I was battered, bludgeoned, wailed on. I suffered contusions, sprains, bruises, cuts, and concussions.

I was merely prolonging the inevitable because I couldn’t outrun him and I couldn’t outfight him.

Earlier, I had thought of my prophesy. The one the Boranjame had told me.

Was it a good thing to know how I would die? If I ever found myself in dire circumstances, if they didn’t match the prophesy, then it couldn’t possibly be fatal.

Could it?

Were the Boranjame lying to me?

In any case, the prophesy gave me an idea.

After running for what felt like hours, I finally reached the edge of the port.

Hank 2 tackled me for the one millionth time.

“Hank?” I heard Garm ask.

The whole gang was there. Delovoa. Cliston. Garm.

“We saw the flash,” Delovoa began. “Do you need help?”

I struggled to my feet as my younger self grappled with me.

“Call Zzzho. Tell him to get the Suckface ready for takeoff. Now!”

I pushed off Hank 2 and ran past them.

It was a quick few blocks to reach the Suckface, but the port was crammed full of useful weapons for Hank 2 to use.

He threw boxes at me. Whacked me with a crowbar. He even tried to push over a heavy lifter on me, but it barely missed.

I hopped on board the Suckface followed closely by my twin.

“Hank?” Zzzho asked over the intercom.

“Take off! Now!”

I was now crammed inside my rather small spaceship with Hank 2. I couldn’t escape him.

He took full advantage and punched and kicked at me until I curled up in a ball on the floor. We obviously hadn’t strapped ourselves in, so the inertia of departure from Floloria made us slide down the floor.

The ship was now in space.

The place where my prophesy said I would die. I felt like, this could really be it. I had forced this situation. If I died, it would be my own fault.

My younger self regained his footing, picked me up, and threw me the length of the Suckface, even with the artificial gravity on.

I landed in the aft of the ship and I reached into the storage compartment and grabbed something.

Then I put my head down and ran forward, ramming Hank 2 and pushing him backwards due to my mass.

He didn’t mind. He kneed me repeatedly in the face.

But I had done what I wanted, and moved us to the front of the ship.

I reached out, punched five buttons on the wall and pulled a lever up, then down, then up again.

Hank 2 looked at me confused.

“That’s right. Eat suck, suckface,” I said.

He replied with a heavy accent.

“No, you.”

Could Hank 2 speak Colmarian? Had he understood me this whole time? It so startled me that I almost didn’t hit the glowing red plunger.

But my arm was already in motion.

The airlock slammed open and then woosh!

We were sucked into outer space.

Hank 2 held onto me with a few fingers but I swiped at him and broke his grip.

I did my best to remain calm despite the unbearable surroundings.

When Hank 2 kicked me to the rear of the ship, I had grabbed something from the storage compartment. This object was now my only hope.

I took hold of the two handles, depressed the buttons with my thumbs, and I was instantly encased in an emergency pressure suit.

I hadn’t been sure if they worked while actually in space, but the fact I could breathe and wasn’t dying a horrible death, proved that they did.

Hank 2, on the other hand, was not so fortunate.

He was only about ten feet away from me now. Clasping and clawing and…dying.

I couldn’t see well out of the colorized bubble helmet, but I watched a younger version of me die.

Betrayed and murdered by my best friend—me. Unloved, my corpse deserted in the coldness of space, where memory of me would swiftly become less substantial than the faded light of a long-dead star.

That was my prophesy.

Had it ever truly been about me? Or had it been that Hank?

Had the Boranjame foreseen that I would get in this fight and need those words to escape?

I don’t know.

But Hank was deserted in the coldness of space. Hank 2 at least.

Zzzho turned the ship around for me.





CHAPTER 71




Two weeks later, I invited Garm back to my apartment in City Hall.

“The Dredel Led are taking a wait-and-see approach to the war, sir,” Cliston explained. He had just returned from another visit to Thaxmultaerea.

“That may be true, but the PCC have the war on the books and are allocating funds to the effort. I assume that means we’re still at war,” Garm said.

“All that work for nothing?” I asked.

“Sir, you destroyed Kai’Un. He was a corrupting and unnatural influence on my people. Merely a puppet of Zydolium and Wardian Boshe.”

“Well, Boshe is dead for sure. Hopefully, we killed all of Zydolium’s clones.”

“MTB hired two new police officers and they are patrolling Floloria,” Garm said.

“Two beat cops walking around a city isn’t much. But I guess we did drop a plasma warhead on everyone. I didn’t see anyone alive after that. The warhead even broke Gun,” I said.

“So you don’t have a talking, philosophical pistol?” Garm asked.

“Delovoa said it might restore itself with time. But I can’t see how.”

“Why are you still carrying it?” Garm asked, pointing to Gun in my holster.

“I’ve bragged about my talking, never-missing handgun for years. No one else knows it’s broken,” I explained.

“How is your uncle?” Garm asked.

“Frank? He busted something like twelve different bones. He doesn’t heal nearly as fast as I do, but I suspect he’ll be fine. He wants me to pay his hospital bills.”

“Speaking of which, when I was on Thaxmultaerea, I inquired about the fifty million credits for ending the hack,” Cliston said.

“And?”

“I’m afraid we won’t be getting paid. They said the war nullified all existing contracts with citizens of the Post-Colmarian Confederation Colmarian Confederation.”

“I’m not a citizen!”

“Unfortunately, they don’t see it like that, sir.”

“The Agglatar would have probably come by and taken all my money, anyway.”

“Do you know what happened to him, sir? He depleted nearly all of our cash reserves.”

“I’m hoping he bought a one-way ticket to wherever Thad Elon is. There’s no use hanging around Belvaille. Everyone here is poor and Thad Elon is long gone.”

“You think the Boranjame created the Agglatar?” Garm asked.

“Yeah. From what I understand, the Boranjame can’t like, directly interfere in things,” I said.

“But they strip mine entire solar systems. Populated solar systems,” Garm replied.

“Yeah. That’s a Boranjame being ‘indirect.’ I think the Agglatar is the same thing. They created something to fight Thad Elon on their behalf…but they screwed it up. They seem to do that a lot.”

“I have difficulty believing the very blunt Agglatar could ever defeat the very subtle Thad Elon,” Cliston observed.

“Thad Elon can’t be defeated. He’s going to kill all life in the galaxy, eventually.” I shrugged. “But hopefully we’ll all die of natural causes long before that. So how about my legal status?” I asked Garm.

“You mean for murdering the Joint High Wardian and Marshall of the South-by-Southwest Region?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“You’re a wanted criminal,” she said.

“Even though Wardian Boshe broke the law? He started a war!” I said.

“But he was never tried for it. As far as the Navy is concerned, you killed an extremely important governmental employee.”

“Does the PCC have a most wanted list?” I asked.

“Yeah. It has 53,814 names on it,” she said.

“That’s an oddball number.”

“It’s an oddball government.”

“How high am I on the list?” I asked.

“You’re not on it. Maybe on the next cycle they’ll add you, but the list has people accused of genocide and destroying entire continents. You simply murdered one man.”

“That sucks. I don’t even get any notoriety out of it.”

“No, but they’re still looking for you. I can’t be seen with you in public and you had better stay away from any PCC facilities,” Garm said.

I stood quietly for a moment.

“This has to be the worst outcome of any set of missions I’ve ever been on,” I said.

“Didn’t you break your prophesy, though? You found out that it wasn’t you who was destined to be betrayed and die out in space.”

“Who knows? That was crazy Boranjame talk. I mean, I’m going to die sometime, right? If it’s not out in the coldness of space it will be in the warmth of some bomb Delovoa created or in the stinky palms of some monster intent on destroying the city.”

“So you giving up now?” Garm asked.

“No. But…can’t I complain for a little while? I lost all my money. My girlfriend was murdered. My talking gun is defective. And I’m a wanted criminal in a civilization I’m not even a part of.”

“You still have me, sir,” Cliston said.

“Yeah, I got you, Cliston. And honestly. Sincerely. I’m very happy about that.”

“And I don’t hate you,” Garm added.

“No, you’re a bundle of laughs, Garm. So, what are your plans?” I asked.

“If there’s one thing the Dredel Led hack showed me it’s that the telescopes have a lot of extra capacity we aren’t using.”

“You going to hack some other species?”

“No. Those telescopes can communicate across the entire galaxy and we use them as glorified traffic lights. I’m going to sell bandwidth on them.”

“Bandwidth for what?”

“For whatever and to whomever will pay for it,” she replied. “What about you? You got a spaceship. You going to stick around Belvaille?”

“Yeah, I like it here. I know this city so well. Even when it changes. You could blindfold me and I would still know my way around. But with my criminal status, I’m not sure if I can keep working on the Skim. Which is bad news, because I owe people a lot of money.”

“What will you do, then?” Garm asked.

“I think I might go back into the private sector,” I said. “Private investigations.”
